```markdown
# Marketing Analytics: What Consumers Want (Week 2 - Session 1)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee, VGSoM, IIT Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course, Week two. This week's overall topic is "What Consumers Want". We will explore how to find out what consumers want over six sessions.

## Basic Marketing Concepts: Need, Want, and Desire/Demand

In basic marketing (Marketing 101), the concepts of Need, Want, and Desire (or Demand) are distinct and crucial for marketers to understand.

*   **Need:** Basic necessities required for survival.
    *   Examples: Food, shelter, physical health, safety, financial support.
    *   Modern needs can also include connectivity, mental well-being, quality education.
    *   Something you *cannot* survive without.
*   **Want:** A higher version of need, not something you cannot survive without, but something you desire or find pleasurable.
    *   Example: For a child, basic food is a *need*, but candy is a *want*.
    *   Example: For a traveler, a safe place to sleep is a *need*, but a hotel with a comfortable bed, good bath, hot water, Wi-Fi, etc., is a *want*.
*   **Desire/Demand:** A want targeted towards a specific product or service, usually with a price tag.
    *   Marketers often focus on converting *wants* into *desires*.
    *   When a consumer's willingness to pay matches the price of a *desire*, it converts into *demand*.
    *   Example: A traveler wanting a hotel might *desire* a specific type of room at the Hilton Tokyo, and if their willingness to pay matches the price ($432), it becomes a *demand*.

Understanding this conversion process (Need -> Want -> Desire -> Demand) is vital for companies to know what customers value and how to offer it at the right price.

## How to Find Out What Consumers Want

There are various sources of information to understand customer preferences:

1.  **Directly Asking Customers or Gathering Explicit Feedback:**
    *   **Qualitative/Quantitative Surveys:** Directly asking customers about their satisfaction, pain points, desired features, likes, dislikes, etc.
    *   **Review Websites:** Platforms like Amazon or Tripadvisor where customers post reviews (verified or unverified) detailing their experiences. Analyzing these reviews (often using text mining) provides insights into what features or aspects matter most.
    *   **Complaints Websites:** Sites like Consumercomplaints.com where customers post negative reviews or issues. While negative, these highlight major reasons for dissatisfaction and areas for improvement.
    *   **Online Forums:** Communities where users discuss products, services, issues, and suggest improvements. Users might vote on suggestions, indicating collective wants. Examples include forums for software like Salesforce, Power BI, or Watson.

2.  **Inferring from Customer Behavior:**
    *   **Choice Models:** Quantitative modeling of customer decisions (choices).
        *   **Binomial Models:** When there are two choices (e.g., continue service or churn out). Example: Netflix predicting customer churn based on behavior. Identifying drivers of churn helps create targeted offerings.
        *   **Multinomial Models:** When there are more than two choices (e.g., stay with current plan, upgrade, downgrade, or churn). Example: WordPress users choosing different subscription plans based on features and price. Modeling these choices reveals customer preferences.

3.  **Inferring from Experiments:**
    *   Creating artificial situations or scenarios for customers and observing or asking about their potential behavior or preferences. This is useful when the product or feature being studied doesn't exist in the market yet.
    *   **Conjoint Analysis:** A technique where multiple product/service options (with varying attributes and levels) are presented to consumers, and they are asked to rate, rank, or choose among them. This helps determine the relative importance of different attributes and attribute levels.

In this session, we will focus on quantitative data sources, particularly from review websites, building upon the hotel review data from the previous week.

## Practical Demonstration Using R: Hotel Review Data

We will use R to analyze the hotel review dataset and extract insights about customer wants based on reported satisfaction levels.

### Setup

Load the necessary file and clean the environment. We will use the `sample_hotel_data.csv` file.

```R
# Set working directory to source file location (Assuming the data file is in the same folder)
setwd(dirname(rstudioapi::getActiveDocumentContext()$path))

# Load the data
data <- read.csv("sample_hotel_data.csv", header = TRUE, sep = ",")

# Display data structure
str(data)

# Display first few rows
head(data)

# Clean missing values and outliers (code from previous session)
# Removing rows with any NA values in key rating columns
data <- na.omit(data, cols = c("Rating.Value", "Location", "Sleep.Quality", "Value", "Rooms", "Cleanliness", "Service"))

# Assuming outlier removal was also performed in a similar way, e.g., based on Z-scores or IQR for ratings.
# (Details of the exact outlier removal code were not shown explicitly but mentioned as done in the last class)
# Example (conceptual - adjust based on specific code from previous week):
# data <- data[data$Rating.Value >= lower_bound & data$Rating.Value <= upper_bound, ]
# data <- data[data$Location >= lower_bound & data$Location <= upper_bound, ]
# ... and so on for all rating attributes
```

After cleaning, the data size was mentioned to be around 872 observations.

### Attribute Importance (Regression on Scaled Attributes)

We want to find the relative importance of the six hotel attributes (Value, Location, Sleep Quality, Rooms, Cleanliness, Service) in determining the overall `Rating.Value`. Since the attributes are on a 1-5 scale but might have different distributions, we scale them to make their coefficients comparable.

```R
# Scale the variables (Overall rating and the six attributes)
data$Rating.Value.scaled <- scale(data$Rating.Value)
data$Location.scaled <- scale(data$Location)
data$Sleep.Quality.scaled <- scale(data$Sleep.Quality)
data$Value.scaled <- scale(data$Value)
data$Rooms.scaled <- scale(data$Rooms)
data$Cleanliness.scaled <- scale(data$Cleanliness)
data$Service.scaled <- scale(data$Service)

# Run linear regression with scaled variables
model1 <- lm(Rating.Value.scaled ~ Location.scaled + Sleep.Quality.scaled + Value.scaled + Rooms.scaled + Cleanliness.scaled + Service.scaled, data = data)

# Summarize the model results
summary(model1)
```

**Interpretation of `model1` results:**

*   **F-statistic:** 189.6 (highly significant, p < 0.05), indicating the model as a whole is statistically significant.
*   **Adjusted R-squared:** 0.5651, meaning about 56.5% of the variance in overall scaled rating is explained by the scaled attribute ratings. This is considered reasonably good in a marketing context.
*   **Coefficients:** The standardized coefficients indicate the relative importance of each attribute.
    *   `Value.scaled`: 0.28 (Highest coefficient)
    *   `Service.scaled`: (Next highest, check summary output)
    *   `Rooms.scaled`: (Next highest, check summary output)
    *   `Cleanliness.scaled`: (Next highest, check summary output)
    *   `Sleep.Quality.scaled`: (Next highest, check summary output)
    *   `Location.scaled`: 0.05 (Lowest coefficient)
*   **Significance (p-values):** All six attributes are statistically significant (p < 0.05).

**Finding:** Based on this model, customers consider **Value for Money** to be the most important factor influencing their overall satisfaction, followed by Service, Rooms, Cleanliness, Sleep Quality, and finally, Location is the least important among these six factors.

### Impact of Co-traveler (`review_type`)

Does who you travel with affect your satisfaction? We add the `review_type` variable (a factor variable) to the model.

```R
# Check levels of review_type
levels(data$Review.Type)

# Run linear regression including review_type
# R automatically handles dummy variable creation, dropping the first level alphabetically ("as a couple")
model2 <- lm(Rating.Value.scaled ~ Location.scaled + Sleep.Quality.scaled + Value.scaled + Rooms.scaled + Cleanliness.scaled + Service.scaled + Review.Type, data = data)

# Summarize the model results
summary(model2)
```

**Interpretation of `model2` results:**

*   The importance of the six scaled attributes remains similar, with Value for Money still being the most important.
*   **`Review.Type` Coefficients:** These are interpreted relative to the dropped level, which is "as a couple" (alphabetically first).
    *   `Review.TypeBusiness`: -0.18 (Significant, p < 0.05). Business travelers report significantly lower satisfaction compared to couples.
    *   `Review.TypeSolo`: (Insignificant, p > 0.05). Solo travelers report similar satisfaction to couples.
    *   `Review.TypeWith family`: -0.13 (Significant, p < 0.05). Travelers with family report significantly lower satisfaction compared to couples, but slightly higher than business travelers.
    *   `Review.TypeWith friends`: (Insignificant, p > 0.05). Travelers with friends report similar satisfaction to couples.

**Finding:** Travelers in smaller groups (couples, solo, with friends) tend to report higher satisfaction than business travelers or those traveling with family. Business travelers are the least satisfied segment among those with significantly different scores. This suggests hotels might need to "pamper" business and family travelers more.

### Impact of Time Distance Between Visit and Review

Does the delay between the hotel visit and writing the review affect the satisfaction score?

**Handling Date Data in R:**

We need to convert the `Month.of.Visit` and `Date.of.Review` columns, which are factors, into actual date objects in R. The `as.Date()` function is used, requiring a specified format string matching the text format in the data.

```R
# Convert Date.of.Review to character first
data$Date.of.Review.char <- as.character(data$Date.of.Review)

# Convert character dates to Date objects
# Handle slash format first
data$Date.of.Review.date <- as.Date(data$Date.of.Review.char, format = "%m/%d/%Y")

# Handle dash format for values that were NA after the first conversion
data$Date.of.Review.date[is.na(data$Date.of.Review.date)] <- as.Date(data$Date.of.Review.char[is.na(data$Date.of.Review.date)], format = "%m-%d-%Y")

# Convert Month.of.Visit to character
data$Month.of.Visit.char <- as.character(data$Month.of.Visit)

# Paste '1-' at the beginning to create a valid date string (assuming 1st of the month)
data$Month.of.Visit.char_with_day <- paste("1-", data$Month.of.Visit.char, sep = "")

# Convert character dates to Date objects
# Format matches the '1-Aug-14' structure (%d- for day, %b- for abbreviated month, %y for 2-digit year)
data$Month.of.Visit.date <- as.Date(data$Month.of.Visit.char_with_day, format = "%d-%b-%y")

# Calculate time distance in days (Review Date - Visit Date)
data$timedist <- data$Date.of.Review.date - data$Month.of.Visit.date

# Convert timedist to numeric (it's a 'difftime' object by default)
data$timedist <- as.numeric(data$timedist)

# Remove rows where timedist is NA (where date conversion failed or visit date was missing)
data_clean_timedist <- na.omit(data, cols = c("timedist"))

# Check number of observations after removing NAs
nrow(data_clean_timedist)
# Original was ~872, now ~806 (around 66 dropped)
```

**Regression with `timedist`:**

Now, add the calculated `timedist` variable to the regression model.

```R
# Run linear regression including timedist
model3 <- lm(Rating.Value.scaled ~ Location.scaled + Sleep.Quality.scaled + Value.scaled + Rooms.scaled + Cleanliness.scaled + Service.scaled + Review.Type + timedist, data = data_clean_timedist)

# Summarize the model results
summary(model3)
```

**Interpretation of `model3` results:**

*   The coefficients for the scaled attributes and review types remain largely consistent.
*   **`timedist` Coefficient:** There is a small but statistically significant positive coefficient for `timedist`.

**Finding:** A positive coefficient for `timedist` means that as the time distance between the visit date and the review date increases, the overall satisfaction rating tends to be slightly higher. This suggests that reviews given later are generally more positive. This finding prompts the strategic question: should companies encourage customers to leave reviews immediately or after some time delay?

## Conclusion

Through analyzing explicit feedback (via regression on attributes from reviews), we can identify the relative importance of different service/product features (e.g., Value for Money is most important for this hotel dataset). By including demographic or situational factors (like co-traveler type), we can see how different customer segments vary in their satisfaction drivers or baseline satisfaction levels. Furthermore, analyzing temporal factors (like time distance to review) can reveal interesting biases or patterns in how feedback is provided.

The instructor mentioned related research on how factors like co-traveler, time distance, and traveler expertise can influence customer focus on process vs. outcome attributes in service reviews. An older version of this paper might be shared for further reading.

This concludes Session 1 of Week 2. We will continue exploring how to understand customer wants in the next sessions.

Thank you.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 8: What Consumers Want (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome to the marketing analytics course. This is week two, and this is session two, which will be a smaller session compared to the others. In this session, we are continuing our discussion on "What Consumers Want". This session is presented by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSoM IIT, Kharagpur.

## Understanding Consumer Preferences

In the previous session, we discussed how you can use consumer review data, where consumers express themselves, to find out what they are asking for.

However, there is another method that can be used, called **Conjoint Analysis**.

### Conjoint Analysis: Goal and Principle

*   **Major Goal:** To identify what consumers want.
*   **Principle:** We break down the available options (products or services) into a combination of multiple attributes. We then find out how customers value *each* of these attributes. Finally, we combine the values of these attributes and the consumers' valuations to understand the overall value or preference for a specific combination (product).

This might sound textual for now, but we will delve into what exactly this means.

### Economic Context: Consumer Surplus and Company Dilemma

The very basic thing a customer wants to do is to get the maximum **consumer surplus**. He wants to maximize his consumer surplus.

*   This is a microeconomics concept: a consumer gets a certain utility or value from an offering when they purchase it. When they purchase it, they also pay a price.
*   **Consumer Surplus ≈ Utility (in monetary terms) - Price**
*   The customer wants to maximize this: **Value - Price**.

Now, from a company's perspective:

*   To create value for the customer, you often have to increase your cost. The more value you create, the more costs the company will incur.
*   On the other hand, you cannot just charge any price.
*   If you want to make customers happy (increase Value - Price), you can either increase value or decrease price.
*   **Problem with Decreasing Price:** Price significantly affects your profit function. Your profit is roughly `(Demand * Price) - (Demand * Cost)`. If your price is very high, demand will come down. So, you cannot keep the price very high.
*   **Problem with Increasing Value:** If you increase value, your cost will go up. Your price must be higher than your cost to make a profit.
*   **Dilemma:** Should I increase value? Should I increase price? If I increase value, cost goes up. If cost goes up, price has to go up. If price goes up, demand comes down. It becomes difficult to know if profit is maximized.

Therefore, we need to find an optimal solution that gives customers the highest value at the lowest cost for the company, and allows the company to sell it at the *right* price (not necessarily the highest or lowest). This combination of information is difficult to obtain using ordinary techniques.

### Limitations of Traditional Market Research

What are the various ways we currently find out what customers value (so we understand `Value` as a function of what)?

1.  **Market Research (Qualitative):**
    *   Go to customers and ask questions.
    *   **Focused Group Discussions (FGDs):** People discuss what they want, like, dislike. You can capture their talk and do text analytics, content analysis, or tone analysis.
    *   **Online Reviews:** Analyze text from review websites.
    *   *Limitation:* These are primarily qualitative techniques. They tell you *what* people talk about, but not precise trade-offs or valuations in monetary terms, or preferences for hypothetical products not yet on the market.

2.  **Market Research (Quantitative):**
    *   **Regression:** As discussed in the last class, you can relate features to sales or ratings.
    *   *Limitation:* This relies on existing data. You cannot get information about options that are not available in the market or that customers haven't experienced or reviewed.

3.  **Direct Questioning about Features/Attributes:**
    *   You identify important features (e.g., brand, processor speed, screen size for a smartphone) through qualitative study.
    *   You then directly ask customers: "Which brand do you like?" (Samsung). "How much screen size?" (Up to 4 inches). "Which Android version?" (Latest). "Storage capacity?" (More than 64 GB). "RAM size?" (More than 4 GB). "Price?" (Less than 20,000-30,000 rupees).
    *   *Limitation:* These answers are often vague ("as much as possible", "more than", "latest", "less than"). They don't tell you *how much* preference changes if, for instance, the price increases from 20,000 to 40,000, or if you combine a non-preferred brand with a preferred RAM size versus a preferred brand with a non-preferred RAM size. You don't know how attributes interact.

4.  **Importance Rating/Ranking:**
    *   You ask customers to rate features on a scale (e.g., 1-7, 1=least important, 7=most important) or rank them.
    *   You calculate average ratings/ranks. Example: Processor 6.2, Brand 6.2, RAM 5.9.
    *   *Limitation:* You get relative importance (e.g., RAM is slightly less important than Processor/Brand). But the key question is "So what?". How much less important? How does a specific *level* of RAM (e.g., 8GB vs 16GB) impact preference compared to a specific *level* of processor speed? This method doesn't tell you how much importance to place on increasing RAM size from 32 GB to 64 GB versus 64 GB to 128 GB, or how it trades off against price or brand.

**Conclusion:** These classical market research methods cannot answer the crucial questions about customer valuation of specific attribute *levels* and how they make trade-offs. This is where Conjoint Analysis excels.

## Conjoint Analysis: Method Details

*   **Etymology:** Conjoint means "joined together" or "combined". The marketing phrase is "**Fea**tures **CON**sidered **JOINT**ly". (CON + JOINT = CONJOINT).
*   **History:** Started around the early 1970s.

### Applications of Conjoint Analysis

*   Learning more about potential customer segments and their preferences.
*   Heavily used in new product development.
*   Predicting market share for a new product introduction.
*   Finding the optimal positioning of products.
*   Determining customers' Willingness to Pay for specific features or product configurations.

### Steps in Conjoint Analysis

1.  **Decide on the Product/Service:** Choose what you are analyzing (e.g., laptop, smartphone).
2.  **Decide on Attributes and Levels:** Identify the key features (attributes) and their specific values (levels) that define the product.
3.  **Create Combinations:** Generate hypothetical product profiles by combining the chosen attribute levels.
4.  **Show Combinations to Customers & Collect Data:** Present these product profiles to a sample of target customers and ask them to provide their preferences (rating, ranking, or choice).
5.  **Analyze Data:** Process the collected preference data.
6.  **Interpret Results & Apply:** Derive insights from the analysis to inform marketing decisions.

Let's look at Steps 2, 3, and 4 in more detail.

### Step 2: Deciding on Attributes and Levels

Choosing the right attributes and their levels is crucial.

*   **Choosing Attributes:**
    *   Attributes should be **independent** of each other. You cannot choose attributes that are highly dependent.
    *   *Example:* For a laptop, weight, screen size, and overall physical size (e.g., 21 inches monitor size, though monitor size usually refers to PCs, let's use screen size like 14 or 16 inches for laptops) are related. If you say a 16-inch laptop has very low weight, it might be an unrealistic combination for standard laptops. You might need to consider thickness as a separate attribute if you want to disentangle weight and size.
    *   You can check for independence using correlation for continuous variables or chi-square test for categorical variables. If they are highly correlated or dependent, choose only one or reconsider your attributes.
*   **Choosing Levels:**
    *   Attributes must have **varying levels**. If an attribute has no variety across competing products or realistic options (e.g., all standard laptops from major brands come with a certain basic feature), it's not useful for a conjoint study.
    *   *Example:* If you are analyzing smartphones and most realistic options are either Android or Mac OS, you have two levels for OS. But if a specific brand (like Apple) only offers Mac OS, then 'Brand' and 'OS' are dependent for that brand. You might have to choose one over the other as an attribute or handle this dependence during combination creation.
    *   Levels must be **unambiguous**. Avoid subjective terms.
    *   *Example:* Instead of "Popular Brand" or "Unpopular Brand", use specific brand names like "HP", "Lenovo", "Dell". Instead of "High Price", "Medium Price", "Low Price", use specific price ranges or values like "$500", "$1000", "$1500". What is "popular" or "high" varies between people.
    *   Levels must be **mutually exclusive**. A product profile should clearly have one level for each attribute.
    *   *Example:* For storage, levels could be "500 GB", "1 TB", "2 TB". A single product cannot be both 500 GB and 1 TB internal storage. For color, levels could be "Red", "Pink". A product cannot be "Red and Pink" if those are separate levels. If "Red and Pink" pattern is a distinct option, it should be listed as a separate level.

### Step 3: Creating Combinations (Product Profiles)

Once attributes and levels are defined, you combine them to create hypothetical product profiles.

*   If you have Attributes A, B, C with Levels a1, a2, a3; b1, b2, b3; c1, c2, c3 respectively, the total number of possible combinations is 3 * 3 * 3 = 27.
*   It's tempting to include many attributes and levels, but this increases the number of combinations exponentially. A large number of combinations makes data collection and analysis difficult.
*   **Recommendation:** Stick to a manageable number of attributes (typically 3 to 5). Ensure the number of levels for each attribute is more or less balanced (e.g., not one attribute with 6 levels and another with 2).
*   **Important:** Ensure the generated combinations are **meaningful** and realistic. As discussed, an "Apple brand + Windows OS" combination is not meaningful and should not be included.

For space constraints here, I couldn't list all 27 possible combinations for the 3x3x3 example, but they are generated from all permutations of the attribute levels.

### Step 4: Data Collection Methods

Once you have the combinations, you present them to your sample of customers and collect their preferences. There are three main methods:

1.  **Ranking based:** Customers rank the presented product profiles from most preferred to least preferred.
2.  **Rating based:** Customers rate each presented product profile on a scale (e.g., 1-10, 1-7) indicating their likelihood to buy or overall preference.
3.  **Choice based:** Customers are shown sets of product profiles and asked to choose the one they would most likely buy (simulating a purchase decision).

The analysis techniques used in Step 5 depend significantly on which of these data collection methods is employed.

## Moving Forward

In the next video, we will talk about the analysis step (Step 5) and how different statistical tools can be used based on the data collection method (Ranking, Rating, or Choice) to get what consumers want. We will go through a case study to illustrate this.

## Conclusion

We will stop here for this particular session and will come back with a case study in the next video. Thank you.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 9 : what consumers want (contd.)

# Marketing Analytics
## Lecture 9: What Consumers Want (Continued)

### Introduction

Hello everybody, this is Professor Swagato Chatterjee from VGSoM, IIT, Kharagpur, taking the Marketing Analytics course for you. This is week two and session three is something that we will start with.

In session 1 and two, we talked about what customers want.
*   In session 1, we showed how from customer review data you can find out the preferences of the customers.
*   In session two, we started briefing about **Conjoint Analysis**. This is another quantitative technique which you can use to find out what are the various preferences of the customers. You can also get data about the preferences for various attributes and the attribute levels. For example, if somebody is trying to buy a laptop, you can find out how much is the preference for 2GB RAM, 4GB RAM, or 8GB RAM. You can try to find out the exact attribute level preference.

In this session, we will do a hands-on exercise to see how we can find out all of these things.

### Problem Setup: Car Buying Example

Let's say you are trying to buy a car. In this particular case, we are assuming we are trying to buy a car. You probably go and ask some people what are the major features, factors, or aspects they think are important when buying a car.

Suppose we find that these three things are considered important:
1.  **Fuel**
2.  **Capacity** (sitting capacity)
3.  **Price**

These are the three important factors, and we also find that there are three levels for each:

*   **Fuel:** Diesel, Petrol, CNG (represented as 1, 2, 3)
*   **Capacity:** 8-seater, 6-seater, 4-seater (represented as 1, 2, 3)
*   **Price:** 12 Lakhs, 8 Lakhs, 4 Lakhs (represented as 1, 2, 3)

It's ideal for these attributes to be mutually exclusive (not highly correlated). While Price might be correlated with Capacity (higher capacity often means higher price) or Fuel Type (diesel engines can cost differently), in this specific example, we'll proceed with these attributes. It's important to note that if attributes are too highly correlated, it can be problematic for the analysis.

The levels chosen are objective and concrete, reducing ambiguity (e.g., you know what a diesel engine or an 8-seater means).

With three factors and three levels each, the total number of possible combinations is 3 * 3 * 3 = 27.

### Data Collection and Orthogonal Design

After identifying the attributes and levels, we ask customers how they prefer these combinations. Giving a respondent all 27 combinations can be overwhelming and lead to inconsistent or heuristic-based ratings rather than carefully considered preferences.

Instead of a **Full Factorial Design** (all 27 combinations), we often use a **Subset of the Design**. A common type of subset used in Conjoint Analysis is an **Orthogonal Design**.

*   **Orthogonal Design:** A subset of the full factorial design where the attributes (columns) are uncorrelated with each other across the selected combinations. For example, in the 9 combinations shown to a respondent, Fuel and Capacity should ideally have a correlation close to zero, Fuel and Price should have a correlation close to zero, and Capacity and Price should have a correlation close to zero.
*   Creating orthogonal designs requires specialized software. This will be covered in a different session or video.

In our example data (`conjoint_data_price.xls` or the corresponding CSV), we have given **9 choices** (an orthogonal subset of the 27) to each respondent.

The data shows:
*   **Serial Number:** Respondent ID
*   **Fuel:** (1, 2, or 3)
*   **Capacity:** (1, 2, or 3)
*   **Price:** (1, 2, or 3)
*   **Rating:** Customer's rating for that specific combination (e.g., on a 1 to 10 scale, 10 being most preferred).

This is a **Rating-Based Conjoint Analysis**.

**Rating vs. Ranking:**
*   Instead of rating, you could ask for **Ranking**. For example, rank the 9 options from 1 (most preferred) to 9 (least preferred).
*   If you use ranking, you need to convert it to a **Reverse Ranking** for analysis to align with preference (higher number = higher preference). For a 1-9 ranking, reverse ranking would be `10 - Rank`. A rank of 1 becomes a score of 9 (`10-1`), a rank of 9 becomes a score of 1 (`10-9`).
*   **When to use Rating vs. Ranking:** Rating is generally better if customers are familiar with the product category and levels. Ranking is useful when customers know they prefer A over B but aren't sure *how much*, making rating difficult.

Our dataset contains data from **60 respondents**, each rating 9 options. This gives us 60 * 9 = 540 observations. This demonstrates a key strength of Conjoint Analysis: creating a substantial amount of data (540 rows) from a relatively small sample of people (60 respondents) by asking them about multiple hypothetical product combinations.

### Data Analysis in R

We need to analyze this data to understand how Fuel, Capacity, and Price influence the Rating. Since Fuel, Capacity, and Price are categorical variables (1, 2, 3 representing distinct types/levels, not numerical values), we need to treat them as such. We will use **dummy variables** in a regression.

Let's use the provided R script (`week2_S3_four.r`) and CSV file.

1.  **Set Working Directory and Load Data:**
    ```R
    # Set working directory to the location of your files
    # setwd("path/to/your/folder") # Uncomment and modify if needed

    # Read the data from the CSV file
    data <- read.csv("conjoint_data_price.csv")
    ```

2.  **Check Initial Data Structure:**
    ```R
    str(data)
    ```
    Initially, R reads the columns as integers because it's a CSV file. We need to convert Fuel, Capacity, and Price to factors.

3.  **Convert Attributes to Factor Variables:**
    We'll use a loop to convert the relevant columns (columns 2, 3, and 4) to factors.
    ```R
    # Convert Fuel, Capacity, and Price to factor variables
    for (i in 2:4) {
      data[,i] <- as.factor(data[,i])
    }
    ```

4.  **Check Structure After Conversion:**
    ```R
    str(data)
    ```
    Now you should see Fuel, Capacity, and Price listed as factor variables with levels '1', '2', '3'.

5.  **Run Simple Linear Regression:**
    We will model the Rating as a function of the factor variables Fuel, Capacity, and Price.
    ```R
    # Run a linear regression model
    conjoint_model <- lm(Rating ~ Fuel + Capacity + Price, data = data)

    # Get the summary of the model
    summary(conjoint_model)
    ```
    A simple linear regression is sufficient here. While more complex econometric techniques exist, the marginal return on complexity might not significantly improve business insights in this context. The goal is business-oriented analytics, balancing sophistication with practicality.

### Interpreting Regression Results

The `summary(conjoint_model)` output provides the coefficients for each level relative to a reference level (the first level for each factor in R by default), their significance, and overall model fit statistics.

Here is an example of what the output might look like (values will match the transcript):

```

lm(formula = Rating ~ Fuel + Capacity + Price, data = data)

-0.025370 -0.002364  0.001370  0.003704  0.027630

            Estimate Std. Error   t value Pr(>|t|)

Capacity2   -2.40000    0.00774  -310.125   <2e-16 ***
Capacity3   -4.60000    0.00774  -594.633   <2e-16 ***
Price2       2.60000    0.00774   335.980   <2e-16 ***
Price3       3.40000    0.00774   439.317   <2e-16 ***
---
Signif. codes:  0 ‘***’ 0.001 ‘**’ 0.01 ‘*’ 0.05 ‘.’ 0.1 ‘ ’ 1

Residual standard error: 0.01807 on 533 degrees of freedom
Multiple R-squared:    0.9982,	Adjusted R-squared:    0.9982 
F-statistic: 4.788e+04 on 6 and 533 DF,  p-value: < 2.2e-16
```

**Interpretation of Model Fit:**
*   **F-statistic:** 4.788e+04 (very high) with a p-value < 2.2e-16 (highly significant). This indicates that the model as a whole is statistically significant and the attributes collectively explain a significant portion of the variance in ratings.
*   **Adjusted R-squared:** 0.9982 (very high). This means that ~99.82% of the variation in ratings is explained by the model. *Note:* In real-life marketing data, such a high R-squared is uncommon; values > 0.3 or 0.4 might be considered good. This high value likely indicates simulated or ideal data.
*   **Significance of Coefficients:** All coefficients have `***` which means their p-values are < 0.001, indicating that each attribute level (relative to its reference) has a highly statistically significant impact on the rating.

**Interpretation of Coefficients (Part-Worth Utilities Relative to Reference Level):**

*   **Reference Levels:**
    *   Fuel Level 1 (Diesel)
    *   Capacity Level 1 (8-seater)
    *   Price Level 1 (12 Lakhs)

*   **Fuel:**
    *   `Fuel2` (Petrol): -0.26667. Choosing Petrol instead of Diesel decreases the rating by 0.26667 units, holding other attributes constant.
    *   `Fuel3` (CNG): -4.33333. Choosing CNG instead of Diesel decreases the rating by 4.33333 units, holding other attributes constant. CNG is the least preferred fuel type among the three.

*   **Capacity:**
    *   `Capacity2` (6-seater): -2.40000. Choosing 6-seater instead of 8-seater decreases the rating by 2.4 units.
    *   `Capacity3` (4-seater): -4.60000. Choosing 4-seater instead of 8-seater decreases the rating by 4.6 units. 4-seater is the least preferred capacity among the three.

*   **Price:**
    *   `Price2` (8 Lakhs): 2.60000. Choosing 8 Lakhs instead of 12 Lakhs *increases* the rating by 2.6 units.
    *   `Price3` (4 Lakhs): 3.40000. Choosing 4 Lakhs instead of 12 Lakhs *increases* the rating by 3.4 units. Lower price is preferred. 4 Lakhs is the most preferred price among the three.

These coefficients represent the change in utility (or rating) when moving from the reference level to another level, keeping other attributes constant. These are sometimes called **part-worth utilities** or **part-worth coefficients**.

### Calculating Attribute Importance

While the coefficients tell us the utility of different levels *within* an attribute, they don't directly tell us the overall importance of one attribute compared to another (e.g., is Fuel more important than Capacity?). The importance of an attribute is related to the *range* of utility values across its levels.

To compare importance across attributes, it's helpful to center the part-worths so the sum of utilities for each attribute's levels is zero.

**Calculating Centered Part-Worths (Alpha Coefficients):**
1.  **Get the raw coefficients:** Include the reference level which has a coefficient of 0 in the regression output.
    *   Fuel: Level 1 (Diesel) = 0, Level 2 (Petrol) = -0.267, Level 3 (CNG) = -4.333
    *   Capacity: Level 1 (8-seater) = 0, Level 2 (6-seater) = -2.400, Level 3 (4-seater) = -4.600
    *   Price: Level 1 (12 Lakhs) = 0, Level 2 (8 Lakhs) = 2.600, Level 3 (4 Lakhs) = 3.400
2.  **Calculate the average coefficient for each attribute:**
    *   Fuel Avg = (0 - 0.267 - 4.333) / 3 = -1.533
    *   Capacity Avg = (0 - 2.400 - 4.600) / 3 = -2.333
    *   Price Avg = (0 + 2.600 + 3.400) / 3 = 2.000
3.  **Subtract the attribute average from each level's coefficient:**
    *   **Fuel:**
        *   Level 1 (Diesel): 0 - (-1.533) = 1.533
        *   Level 2 (Petrol): -0.267 - (-1.533) = 1.266
        *   Level 3 (CNG): -4.333 - (-1.533) = -2.800
    *   **Capacity:**
        *   Level 1 (8-seater): 0 - (-2.333) = 2.333
        *   Level 2 (6-seater): -2.400 - (-2.333) = -0.067
        *   Level 3 (4-seater): -4.600 - (-2.333) = -2.267
    *   **Price:**
        *   Level 1 (12 Lakhs): 0 - 2.000 = -2.000
        *   Level 2 (8 Lakhs): 2.600 - 2.000 = 0.600
        *   Level 3 (4 Lakhs): 3.400 - 2.000 = 1.400

These centered values represent the utility (or preference) for each specific level, adjusted so the average utility for each attribute is zero. Plotting these (level vs. centered utility) gives a **Part-Worth Plot**.

**Calculating Attribute Importance Scores:**
The importance of an attribute is the range of utility it covers, i.e., the difference between the highest and lowest centered part-worth within that attribute.

1.  **Calculate the Range (Max Utility - Min Utility) for each attribute:**
    *   **Fuel:** Max (1.533, 1.266, -2.800) - Min (1.533, 1.266, -2.800) = 1.533 - (-2.800) = 4.333
    *   **Capacity:** Max (2.333, -0.067, -2.267) - Min (2.333, -0.067, -2.267) = 2.333 - (-2.267) = 4.600
    *   **Price:** Max (-2.000, 0.600, 1.400) - Min (-2.000, 0.600, 1.400) = 1.400 - (-2.000) = 3.400

2.  **Calculate Relative Importance Scores:** Divide each attribute's range by the sum of all ranges.
    *   Sum of Ranges = 4.333 + 4.600 + 3.400 = 12.333
    *   Fuel Importance = 4.333 / 12.333 = 0.35
    *   Capacity Importance = 4.600 / 12.333 = 0.37
    *   Price Importance = 3.400 / 12.333 = 0.28

**Interpretation of Importance Scores:**
*   Capacity (0.37) is the most important attribute.
*   Fuel (0.35) is the second most important.
*   Price (0.28) is the third most important.

These importance scores are derived from how customers *behaved* (their ratings), not necessarily what they *verbally stated* about importance. This can reveal inherent preferences that customers might not explicitly articulate.

### Applications of Conjoint Analysis

Conjoint analysis has many applications. Let's look at one: pricing decisions.

#### Application: Pricing Decision based on Utility

We can use the derived part-worth utilities to understand how changes in one attribute level can be compensated by changes in another attribute level (like price) to maintain the same overall utility.

Let's use the Capacity and Price attributes.
*   Changing Capacity from 6-seater (Level 2) to 4-seater (Level 3) results in a utility *drop* of -4.600 - (-2.400) = -2.200 (using the original regression coefficients relative to the 8-seater reference).
*   To compensate for this utility drop, we need a price change that *increases* utility by 2.200.

Let's look at the Price utilities relative to the 12 Lakh reference:
*   12 Lakhs (Level 1): Utility = 0
*   8 Lakhs (Level 2): Utility = 2.6

We want to find the price `P` that gives a utility increase of 2.2 units relative to the 12 Lakh price. We can approximate this using interpolation/extrapolation based on the available price points and their utilities.

Assume a linear relationship between Price and Utility within the observed range. We know:
*   Price 12 Lakhs gives Utility 0
*   Price 8 Lakhs gives Utility 2.6

This is a difference of 4 Lakhs in price corresponds to a 2.6 difference in utility.

We want a price change from 12 Lakhs that gives a utility change of 2.2. Let the required price reduction be `x`.
Using proportions (based on the slope of the price-utility relationship between 12 and 8 Lakhs):
`x / 2.2 = 4 Lakhs / 2.6`
`x = (4 / 2.6) * 2.2`

Calculate x. The new price would be `12 Lakhs - x`. This is the price reduction needed to compensate for the utility loss of moving from a 6-seater to a 4-seater, assuming the 8-seater is the reference point and we're starting from the 12 Lakh price.

This method relies on the assumption that the relationship between price and utility is linear between the tested price points and can be extrapolated/interpolated.

#### Application: Pricing Decision based on Willingness to Pay (WTP)

A more direct approach for pricing using Conjoint Analysis is to change the dependent variable from Rating/Ranking to **Willingness to Pay (WTP)**.

Instead of asking customers to rate/rank product profiles, you ask: "How much would you be willing to pay for a car with [Attribute levels of the profile]?" Customers would provide a monetary value (e.g., in Lakhs of Rupees).

You then run the same regression:
`WTP = Intercept + b1*Fuel2 + b2*Fuel3 + c1*Capacity2 + c2*Capacity3 + d1*Price2 + d2*Price3 + errors`

*Note: If you are directly asking for WTP in monetary units, Price should *not* be one of the attributes included in the profiles presented to the customer, as that would confuse the task.* Let's assume the third attribute is something else, like "Engine Type" or "Features".

In this case, the coefficients (b1, b2, c1, c2, d1, d2) directly represent the *monetary value* (in terms of WTP) that customers associate with each specific attribute level compared to the reference level. The intercept would represent the base WTP for the product with all reference levels.

For any given product configuration (a specific combination of Fuel, Capacity, and the third attribute's levels), you can plug in the corresponding dummy variables (0 or 1) and the intercept to calculate the average WTP for that configuration:

`Average WTP = Intercept + b_Fuel_Level + c_Capacity_Level + d_ThirdAttribute_Level`

This provides a direct estimate of how much customers are willing to pay for specific product designs.

### Conclusion

We have seen how to use Conjoint Analysis to:
1.  Estimate the utility (preference) associated with different levels of product attributes.
2.  Calculate the relative importance of different attributes based on the range of utilities.
3.  Explore applications like pricing decisions, either by converting utility differences to price equivalents or by directly modeling Willingness to Pay.

This concludes the current session. I will come back with the next video with further applications of Conjoint Analysis.

Thank you.
'''

```markdown
# Marketing Analytics - What Consumers Want (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome to Week 2, Session 4 of the Marketing Analytics course. This session continues the discussion on the applications of conjoint analysis, led by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSoM IIT, Kharagpur. In the previous video, we touched upon pricing decisions using conjoint analysis. We will now explore further applications.

## Applications of Conjoint Analysis

Conjoint analysis provides valuable insights into consumer preferences, which can be applied to several marketing decisions.

### 1. Pricing Decisions (Recap)

As discussed previously, conjoint analysis can help determine how much consumers are willing to pay for different product attributes. The coefficients obtained from the conjoint model for the price attribute can be used to understand the price sensitivity of consumers and inform pricing strategies. More detailed pricing models will be covered in a separate module later in the course.

### 2. Brand Premium

Another significant application is calculating **Brand Premium**.

*   **Definition:** Brand premium is the extra amount of money consumers are willing to pay for a product solely because of its brand name, keeping all other attributes constant.
*   **Measurement using Conjoint Analysis:**
    *   Include 'Brand' as an attribute in the conjoint study with different brand levels (e.g., Brand A, Brand B, Brand C, Unbranded).
    *   When analyzing the conjoint data (often through regression), the coefficients for the brand levels indicate the relative preference or utility associated with each brand compared to a base level (often the unbranded option or one of the brands).
    *   If the conjoint was designed to measure willingness to pay directly (e.g., the dependent variable is price), these coefficients directly represent the monetary value consumers place on the brand difference.
    *   **Example:** Using the car attributes example, if 'Fuel Type' were replaced by 'Brand' (Maruti, Tata Motors, Ford), the coefficients (-2.66 for Tata, -4.33 for Ford, assuming Maruti is the base) would indicate that consumers are willing to pay, say, 2.66 lakhs more for a Maruti than a Tata (keeping other attributes same), and 4.33 lakhs more for a Maruti than a Ford. This difference in coefficients quantifies the brand premium.

### 3. Market Share Modeling

Conjoint analysis is crucial for predicting market share for existing products and forecasting market share for new product introductions.

*   **The "So What?" Question:** After determining customer preferences (part-worth utilities or ratings), the key question for a marketing analyst is "So what?". How do these preferences translate into actual market outcomes like demand or market share?
*   **The Model:** We make an assumption about how individual preferences translate to choice and aggregate demand. A common assumption is:
    *   **Utility:** Utility derived from a product is related to its preference rating (e.g., Utility = Rating ^ alpha, where alpha is a coefficient).
    *   **Choice Probability:** The probability that a consumer chooses product `i` from a set of available products is proportional to the utility of product `i` relative to the sum of utilities of all available products.
    ```
    Probability(Choose Product i) = Utility(Product i) / Sum(Utility(Product j) for all products j in the choice set)
    ```
    *   This probability, when aggregated across the target market, is expected to approximate the product's market share.

*   **Finding the Optimal Alpha:**
    *   We need to find the value of `alpha` that best fits the observed market share data.
    *   **Steps:**
        1.  Identify existing products in the market (Competitor 1, 2, 3).
        2.  Determine their actual market shares from market research.
        3.  Calculate the expected *preference rating* for each existing product based on its attributes and the conjoint regression model's coefficients (part-worth utilities), plus the intercept.
        4.  Assume an initial value for `alpha` (e.g., 5).
        5.  Calculate the *predicted market share* for each existing product using the formula:
            ```
            Predicted MS_i = (Rating_i ^ alpha) / (Rating_1 ^ alpha + Rating_2 ^ alpha + Rating_3 ^ alpha) * 100
            ```
        6.  Calculate the error between the actual and predicted market shares (e.g., squared error: (Actual MS - Predicted MS)^2).
        7.  Calculate a measure of overall error, such as Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE), by averaging the squared errors and taking the square root.
        8.  Use an optimization tool (like Solver in Excel) to find the value of `alpha` that minimizes the RMSE.
    *   **Solver Setup:**
        *   Set Objective: Minimize the cell containing the RMSE.
        *   By Changing Variable Cells: The cell containing the value of `alpha`.
        *   Constraints: Can set alpha to be non-negative or allow any value depending on the model assumptions. (In the example, allowing any value was preferred).

### 4. New Product Introduction

Once the optimal `alpha` value is found (which validates the market share model based on existing products), we can use this model to forecast the market share of a *new* product concept.

*   **Process:**
    1.  Define the attributes and levels of the new product concept.
    2.  Calculate the expected *preference rating* for the new product concept using the conjoint regression model (coefficients + intercept).
    3.  Include the new product in the set of available products for market share calculation.
    4.  Recalculate the market share for *all* products (existing and new) using the same formula:
        ```
        Predicted MS_i = (Rating_i ^ alpha_optimal) / Sum(Rating_j ^ alpha_optimal for all products j, including the new one) * 100
        ```
    5.  Analyze the results:
        *   What is the predicted market share of the new product?
        *   Which existing competitors lose the most market share when the new product is introduced? (Often, the competitor whose product is closest in attributes/utility to the new product will be most affected).
*   **Limitation:** While this predicts demand/market share, deciding whether to launch the new product and at what price requires further analysis (e.g., incorporating cost, profit optimization) which goes beyond basic conjoint analysis but builds upon its demand prediction capabilities. This is an example of sequential analytics.

## Other Types of Conjoint Analysis

The method discussed so far (based on rating all possible combinations) is sometimes referred to as 'Full Profile' or 'Ratings-Based' conjoint. Other approaches exist:

### 1. Choice-Based Conjoint (CBC)

*   **Approach:** Instead of rating many product combinations, respondents are presented with *sets* of 3-4 product options (including sometimes a "none" option) and asked to *choose* which one they would buy. This simulates a more realistic purchase decision process (awareness set -> consideration set -> choice set -> choice).
*   **Data:** The data consists of choices (categorical: Product A chosen, Product B chosen, etc.).
*   **Analysis:** Typically uses conditional logit models to estimate the utility of attributes based on the choices made.
*   This method will be discussed in the next video.

### 2. Adaptive/Hybrid Conjoint

*   **Approach:** The questions presented to the respondent *adapt* based on their previous answers.
*   **Goal:** To efficiently gather information about preferences, especially to differentiate between options that are very close in utility for that specific respondent. It helps pinpoint where the respondent falls on different preference scales.
*   **Analogy:** Similar to adaptive testing (like the GRE) where question difficulty adjusts based on performance to precisely estimate ability.
*   **Implementation:** Most effective in online environments where the system can perform real-time calculations to select the next set of questions.

## Conclusion

This video covered key applications of conjoint analysis, including calculating brand premium and modeling market share. We saw how to use conjoint results and actual market data to predict market shares and evaluate the potential impact of new product introductions. In the next video, we will delve into Choice-Based Conjoint analysis and choice modeling.

Thank you.
```

# Marketing Analytics: Lecture 11 - What Consumers Want (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course, week 2, session 5. This session, led by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from Vinod Gupta School of Management, IIT Kharagpur, continues the discussion on "What Consumers Want."

## Choice Modelling Basics

We will build upon the previous session's introduction to conjoint analysis, specifically focusing on the premise for choice-based conjoint. To do this, we first need to understand **choice modelling**.

Choice modelling is the process of creating quantitative models (econometric or statistical) to empirically understand how customers make choices.

In this video, we focus on **binomial choice**: a yes/no decision, such as whether a customer will buy something or not buy something.

*   **Binomial Choice:** Two outcomes (e.g., buy/not buy, switch/not switch).
*   **Multinomial Choice:** Multiple outcomes (e.g., choosing between product A, B, C, or D). We are setting this aside for now.

## Case Study: Mobile Number Porting

We will use a dataset related to **mobile number porting**. This is a facility, common in countries like India, where a customer can keep their existing mobile number while switching from one service provider to another.

### Context and Process

*   The mobile number porting facility allows users to switch providers without changing their number.
*   The standard procedure involves sending a porting request to the current provider to obtain a porting code.
*   This code is then given to the new service provider to initiate the transfer.

### Data Collection (Exit Interview Analogy)

*   Mobile operators often try to retain customers who initiate porting, especially profitable ones (e.g., postpaid customers).
*   They contact customers to understand their reasons for leaving, offer incentives (discounts, new plans), and try to resolve service issues.
*   Sometimes, they conduct surveys (similar to HR exit interviews) asking about service quality, price perception, and other factors.
*   This data on *why* customers leave or consider leaving provides insights into what switchers and non-switchers value.

### Switching Barriers

A key concept in retaining customers is **switching barriers**. These are factors that make it difficult or costly for a customer to switch from one provider to another. Marketers can strategically create or emphasize these barriers.

There are four main types of switching barriers:

1.  **Economic Switching Barriers:** Related to financial costs or loss of benefits when switching.
    *   *Example (Mobile Porting):* Losing pre-paid balance upon porting, breaking a long-term contract and losing associated benefits.
2.  **Social and Psychological Switching Barriers:** Related to social ties or psychological comfort/habit with the current provider.
    *   *Example (Mobile Porting):* Having family and friends on the same network (for free calls within the network), being habituated to the current provider's services and processes.
3.  **Procedural Switching Barriers:** Related to the difficulty or complexity of the switching process itself.
    *   *Example (Mobile Porting):* Complex paperwork, multiple steps, delays, or hassles in getting the porting code or completing the transfer.
4.  **Option Related Switching Barriers:** Related to the limited availability or attractiveness of alternative options.
    *   *Example:* If there are very few other service providers or the perceived quality of alternatives is low, switching becomes less feasible.

## Data Set Description (`switching.csv`)

We will use a dataset named `switching.csv`. This dataset contains information about customers who *expressed intention* to switch (e.g., by asking for a porting code) but may or may not have ultimately switched.

Key Variables in the Dataset:

*   `gender`: Gender of the customer (Categorical).
*   `age`: Age of the customer (Numeric).
*   `profession`: Profession of the customer (Categorical).
*   `urban_rural`: Whether the customer is from an urban or rural area (Categorical).
*   `recipient`: The service provider the customer intended to switch to (Categorical).
*   `SInt`: Switching Intention (All customers in this dataset had switching intention, so this is likely a constant or indicator).
*   `servqual`: Perception of service quality (Numeric rating).
*   `satisfaction`: Overall satisfaction with the current provider (Numeric rating).
*   `PSB`: Procedural Switching Barrier perception (Numeric rating).
*   `ESB`: Economic Switching Barrier perception (Numeric rating).
*   `OSB`: Operational Switching Barrier perception (Numeric rating) - *Note: In this dataset, 'Operational' includes both Social/Psychological and Option Related barriers.*
*   `price`: Perception of price/value (Numeric rating).
*   `SwBh`: Switching Behavior (Dependent variable, 1 or 0).
    *   `SwBh = 1`: Customer ultimately switched (left the current provider).
    *   `SwBh = 0`: Customer stayed back (did not switch despite intending to).

Our goal is to study how these factors influence the final switching behavior (`SwBh`).

## Choice Modelling in R: Finding Relationships (Explanation)

When our objective is to understand the relationship between predictor variables (X's) and a binary outcome variable (Y), we use the entire dataset to build a model and interpret the coefficients.

Here, we use **Logistic Regression**, a type of Generalized Linear Model (GLM), which is appropriate for binary dependent variables.

### R Code: `glm()` with `family=binomial(logit)`

```r
# Set working directory (assuming script is in the data folder)
setwd(dirname(rstudioapi::getActiveDocumentContext()$path))

# Read the data
df <- read.csv("switching.csv")

# Check data structure
str(df)

# Build the logistic regression model using GLM
# SwBh is the dependent variable (0/1)
# All other relevant variables are predictors
# family=binomial(logit) specifies logistic regression
result <- glm(SwBh ~ age + gender_MF + profession + urban_rural + recipient_op + servqual + satisfaction + PSB + ESB + OSB + price, data = df, family = binomial(logit))

# Get the summary of the model results
summary(result)
```

*(Note: The variable names in the formula `gender_MF`, `recipient_op` suggest these might be dummy variables created from original categorical variables like `gender` and `recipient`.)*

### Interpretation of Results

The `summary(result)` output shows the coefficients, standard errors, z-values, and p-values for each predictor.

Interpreting the significant predictors (low p-values):

*   **Age:** Significant and positive. As age increases, the log-odds of switching increases. This implies older customers are *more* likely to switch *after expressing intention*. (Contrary to an initial slip in interpretation in the video, the coefficient sign matters).
*   **Satisfaction:** Significant and negative. As satisfaction increases, the log-odds of switching decreases. Higher satisfaction makes customers *less* likely to switch.
*   **Service Quality (`servqual`):** Significant and negative. As service quality perception increases, the log-odds of switching decreases. Higher perceived service quality makes customers *less* likely to switch. The magnitude (-1.00) is larger than satisfaction (-0.77), suggesting service quality has a slightly stronger impact *among those considering switching*.
*   **PSB (Procedural Switching Barrier):** Not significant.
*   **ESB (Economic Switching Barrier):** Not significant.
*   **OSB (Operational Switching Barrier):** Significant and negative (-1.41). As operational switching barrier perception increases, the log-odds of switching decreases. If customers perceive high social/psychological/option-related barriers, they are *less* likely to switch. This barrier seems most effective *among those already considering switching*.
*   **Price (`price`):** Significant and positive (2.38). As price perception (likely, that the current service is expensive) increases, the log-odds of switching increases. Customers who feel the price is high are *more* likely to switch. The large positive coefficient suggests price perception is a very strong driver of switching *among those considering it*.
*   **Categorical Covariates (Gender, Profession, Urban/Rural, Recipient Operator):** Some levels might be significant (e.g., Videocon recipient operator is significant in the output shown). These indicate differences in switching propensity compared to the reference category for that variable.

**Key Findings for Retention (among customers considering switching):**

Based on this analysis, to retain customers who have expressed interest in porting:
*   Focus on improving **service quality** and **satisfaction**.
*   Strengthen **operational switching barriers** (social ties, network effects, perceived lack of better options).
*   Address **price concerns**, as price perception is a major driver for this group.
*   Procedural and Economic barriers seem less effective *at this stage* because the customer has already overcome the initial hurdles by expressing intention to switch.

### Theoretical Interpretation of Logistic Regression

Logistic regression models the logarithm of the odds of the event occurring. The model equation is:

```
ln(p / (1 - p)) = β₀ + β₁X₁ + β₂X₂ + ... + βₖXₖ
```

Where:
*   `p` is the probability of the dependent variable being 1 (e.g., switching).
*   `p / (1 - p)` is the odds of the event.
*   `ln(p / (1 - p))` is the log-odds or logit.
*   `β₀` is the intercept.
*   `βᵢ` are the coefficients for predictors `Xᵢ`.

The coefficients (`βᵢ`) represent the change in the log-odds of the outcome for a one-unit increase in the corresponding predictor, holding other predictors constant. Exponentiating a coefficient (`exp(βᵢ)`) gives the odds ratio – the factor by which the odds change for a one-unit increase in `Xᵢ`.

### Interpretation of Dummy Variables

When a categorical variable has `k` levels, logistic regression typically includes `k-1` dummy variables. One level is chosen as the **reference category**, and the coefficients for the other levels represent the difference in log-odds compared to this reference.

*   *Example (Hypothetical Urban/Semi-urban/Rural):* If Rural is the reference category, and you have dummy variables for Urban and Semi-urban:
    `ln(p / (1 - p)) = ... + β_Urban * (IsUrban) + β_SemiUrban * (IsSemiUrban) + ...`
    *   For a Rural person: `IsUrban=0`, `IsSemiUrban=0`. log-odds = `...`
    *   For an Urban person: `IsUrban=1`, `IsSemiUrban=0`. log-odds = `... + β_Urban`
    *   For a Semi-urban person: `IsUrban=0`, `IsSemiUrban=1`. log-odds = `... + β_SemiUrban`
    *   `β_Urban` is the difference in log-odds between an Urban person and a Rural person (controlling for other variables).
    *   `β_SemiUrban` is the difference in log-odds between a Semi-urban person and a Rural person (controlling for other variables).

The differences between any two non-reference categories can be calculated from their respective coefficients (e.g., the difference between Urban and Semi-urban is `β_Urban - β_SemiUrban`).

## Choice Modelling in R: Making Predictions

When the objective is to predict the outcome for *new, unseen* data, we split the available data into training and testing sets.

### Training and Testing Data Split

*   **Training Data:** Used to build the model.
*   **Testing Data:** Used to evaluate the model's performance on data it has not seen during training. This gives a more realistic estimate of how the model will perform in the real world.
*   A common split is 70% for training and 30% for testing, though this can vary.

*   **Analogy (Fortune Teller):** You give the fortune teller most of your past (training data) to understand their method and build their prediction skills. You hold back a small part of your past (testing data) that they haven't seen. If they can accurately predict *that unseen past*, you trust their ability to predict the future.

### R Code: `sample()`

```r
# Get the number of rows in the data frame
n_rows <- dim(df)[1]

# Randomly sample indices for the testing set (30% of rows)
# The number 120 is approx 30% of 409 (number of rows)
set.seed(123) # Optional: set seed for reproducibility of the random split
test_indices <- sample(1:n_rows, 120)

# Create the testing data frame
test_data <- df[test_indices, ]

# Create the training data frame (excluding test indices)
train_data <- df[-test_indices, ]

# Check the dimensions of the split data
dim(train_data)
dim(test_data)
```
*(Setting `set.seed()` ensures that the same random sample is selected every time you run the code, making your results reproducible.)*

### Building the Prediction Model (`glm()` on Training Data)

We train the logistic regression model using only the `train_data`.

```r
# Build the logistic regression model on the training data
r_train <- glm(SwBh ~ age + gender_MF + profession + urban_rural + recipient_op + servqual + satisfaction + PSB + ESB + OSB + price, data = train_data, family = binomial(logit))

# (Optional) Summary of the training model - useful for understanding, but less focus than for explanation models
# summary(r_train)
```

### Improving the Model: Stepwise Regression (`step()`)

For prediction, we often want the simplest model that performs well. Stepwise regression can automatically select predictors by iteratively adding or removing them based on a criterion like AIC (Akaike Information Criterion), aiming to minimize AIC for the best fit-complexity trade-off.

```r
# Perform stepwise regression on the training model
# direction="both" allows adding and removing variables
r3 <- step(r_train, direction = "both")

# Summary of the final stepwise model (r3)
summary(r3)
```

*(The output of `step()` shows the process and the final selected model. The goal here is the resulting model `r3`, not necessarily the interpretation of its coefficients, although that can still be done.)*

### Making Predictions on Test Data

Now we use the final model (`r3`, trained on `train_data`) to make predictions on the `test_data` (which the model has never seen).

```r
# Make predictions on the testing data
# type="response" gives probabilities (values between 0 and 1)
predictions <- predict(r3, newdata = test_data, type = "response")

# Display the first few predictions
head(predictions)
```
*(The instructor encountered an error `error in model frame, recipient operator has new labels` during the demonstration. This can happen if a categorical level present in the test data was not present in the training data. Re-running the data split and model training usually resolves this by getting a different random sample where all test levels are also in the training set.)*

The `predictions` variable now holds probabilities for each customer in the `test_data` indicating their predicted likelihood of switching (SwBh=1).

### Evaluating Prediction Accuracy

To evaluate how well the model predicts the binary outcome (0 or 1), we need to convert the predicted probabilities into binary predictions (0 or 1). We do this by setting a **cutoff probability**.

*   **Cutoff:** A threshold probability. If the predicted probability is above the cutoff, we predict 1 (switch); otherwise, we predict 0 (stay).
*   A common cutoff is 0.5. Another approach is to use the mean probability of the outcome in the training data.

```r
# Set the cutoff probability
# Option 1: Use 0.5 as the cutoff
cutoff <- 0.5

# Option 2: Use the mean probability of switching in the training data as cutoff
# cutoff <- mean(train_data$SwBh) # Or use the mean of predictions if preferred for this dataset

# Convert probabilities to binary predictions based on the cutoff
predicted_class <- ifelse(predictions > cutoff, 1, 0)

# Get the actual switching behavior from the testing data
actual_class <- test_data$SwBh

# Create a Confusion Matrix to compare actual vs. predicted classes
confusion_matrix <- table(Predicted = predicted_class, Actual = actual_class)

# Display the confusion matrix
confusion_matrix
```

### Confusion Matrix and Accuracy

The `confusion_matrix` table looks like this:

```
         Actual
Predicted   0   1
        0  TN  FN
        1  FP  TP
```
Where:
*   `TN` (True Negative): Predicted 0, Actual 0 (Correctly predicted they stayed).
*   `FN` (False Negative): Predicted 0, Actual 1 (Incorrectly predicted they stayed, when they actually switched).
*   `FP` (False Positive): Predicted 1, Actual 0 (Incorrectly predicted they switched, when they actually stayed).
*   `TP` (True Positive): Predicted 1, Actual 1 (Correctly predicted they switched).

From the example output shown:
```
         Actual
Predicted  0  1
        0 61  0
        1  9 50
```
*   TN = 61
*   FN = 0
*   FP = 9
*   TP = 50

Total instances in test data = 61 + 0 + 9 + 50 = 120 (matches our test data size).
Actual 0s = 61 + 9 = 70
Actual 1s = 0 + 50 = 50

Overall Accuracy is the percentage of correct predictions:
`Accuracy = (TN + TP) / Total`
`Accuracy = (61 + 50) / 120 = 111 / 120 ≈ 0.925`

This model achieved approximately 92.5% accuracy on the test data.

## Summary and Next Steps

*   We applied **logistic regression** (a type of choice modelling) to understand and predict customer switching behavior (a binomial choice).
*   We highlighted the difference in methodology depending on the objective:
    *   **Explanation:** Use the full dataset to build the model and interpret coefficients to understand relationships.
    *   **Prediction:** Split data into training and testing sets. Train the model on training data (optionally using stepwise selection) and evaluate its performance on the unseen testing data using metrics like accuracy derived from a confusion matrix.
*   We saw that for customers already considering switching providers, price and operational switching barriers (social, psychological, options) were highly significant factors influencing their final decision.
*   We can use other machine learning methods for prediction, but the train/test data splitting procedure remains similar.
*   Using `set.seed()` helps ensure reproducibility of random processes like data splitting.

In the next video, we will move on to discuss **Choice Based Conjoint**, which uses similar choice modelling principles but in a different survey design context.
