Lecture Name: Lecture 12 : what consumers want (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics - What Consumers Want (Contd.)

**Course:** Marketing Analytics
**Lecture:** Lecture 12: What Consumers Want (Contd.)
**Week:** Week 2
**Session:** Session 6
**Instructor:** Professor Swagato Chatterjee, VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. We are in Week 2, Session 6, and today's topic is 'What Consumers Want', specifically focusing on Choice-Based Conjoint analysis.

## Why Choice-Based Conjoint?

In previous sessions, we discussed ranking-based and rating-based conjoint analysis. However, a significant challenge arises when respondents are presented with a large number of options (e.g., 10, 16, 24 options).

*   **Cognitive Load:** It becomes difficult for individuals to perform pairwise comparisons and accurately rank or rate all options, especially when options are very similar.
*   **Difficulty in Ranking:** Ranking a large set requires numerous comparisons (e.g., A vs B, A vs C, B vs C, etc.), which is cognitively taxing. Choosing from a subset is simpler.

To address this, Choice-Based Conjoint (CBC) was developed, gaining popularity in the early 90s. CBC aims to reduce the cognitive load on respondents.

## What is Choice-Based Conjoint?

In CBC, instead of asking respondents to rank or rate a large number of hypothetical product or service concepts, we present them with a smaller *set* of options and ask them to choose **which one they would buy or prefer**.

*   **Mimics Real-World Decision Making:** This approach more closely reflects how consumers make purchase decisions in the real world. Consumers typically evaluate a small set of options within their consideration set and make a choice.
*   **"None" Option:** A crucial feature of CBC is the inclusion of a "None of these" or "I would not purchase any of these" option. This allows us to capture the *cutoff utility* – the minimum utility level required for a consumer to make a purchase. If all presented options fall below this internal threshold, the consumer will choose "None." This was not possible with traditional ranking/rating methods.
*   **Multiple Held-Constant Alternatives:** You can include specific alternatives that remain constant across different choice sets presented to the respondent.

### Example of a Choice Set

The instructor shows an example from Sawtooth Software, which is a common tool for CBC. The example involves choosing a golf ball. A typical choice set might look like this:

| Option                | Brand            | Performance | Price   |
| :-------------------- | :--------------- | :---------- | :------ |
| **Choose this one**   | High Flyer Pro   | Long        | Medium  |
|                       | Smith & Forester | Long        | Low     |
|                       | High Flyer Pro   | Standard    | High    |
|                       | None             | -           | -       |

The respondent is asked to choose *one* option from this set.

## Strengths and Weaknesses

Like any method, CBC has its advantages and disadvantages.

### Strengths

*   **Mimics Real World:** As discussed, it closely resembles how consumers choose from a limited set of alternatives in the market. This aligns with the "choice set" stage in the consumer decision-making process.
*   **Investigate Interactions and Alternative Specific Effects:** CBC models can incorporate interaction terms between attributes and capture effects specific to particular alternatives.
*   **Runs on Paper and Computer:** Data collection can be done using physical questionnaires or computer-based surveys. This provides flexibility, unlike adaptive conjoint (which requires a computer) or standard full-profile ranking (which might be better on paper for viewing all options).
*   **"None" Option:** Allows measuring demand and understanding the cutoff utility threshold.
*   **Multiple Held-Constant Alternatives:** Provides flexibility in study design.

### Weaknesses

*   **Large Sample Size Required:** Since each respondent only provides one data point per choice set (their choice), a larger number of respondents is needed compared to rating/ranking studies where each respondent provides data points for many concepts.
*   **Less Attributes is Ideal:** While CBC can handle more attributes than some older methods, presenting too many attributes simultaneously can still lead to respondents using heuristic decision rules instead of thoroughly evaluating each trade-off. This can compromise the accuracy of the results.
*   **Analysis Method is Complex:** The statistical analysis required for CBC data is more complex than simple linear or logistic regression, typically involving conditional logit or similar models.

## Case Study: Healthcare Decision-Making for Urban Slum Dwellers

The instructor introduces a case study based on ongoing research about healthcare decision-making among bottom-of-pyramid (BOP) customers in urban slums in Bangalore, India. The goal was to understand how to design healthcare services for this population.

### Sample Description

*   **Sample Size:** 303 respondents.
*   **Demographics:**
    *   127 female
    *   Average Age: 36.15 years
    *   Average Household Members: 3.64
    *   Average Family Income: $167/month (approx. ₹10,000)
    *   Average Per Capita Income: $1.44/day (maximum $1.96/day), confirming BOP status.
    *   Percentage with Health Insurance: Only 4%.
*   **Professions:** 33% low-paid service, 23% unorganized business, 13% farmer, 12% daily wage worker, 19% others.
*   **Data Collection:** Data was collected at a government hospital frequented by this demographic, with help from doctors.

### Attributes and Levels

Based on exploratory research, five key attributes were identified for healthcare services for this population:

1.  **Distance:**
    *   Level 0: More than 5 kilometers
    *   Level 1: Up to 5 kilometers
2.  **Reputation:**
    *   Level 0: Low
    *   Level 1: High
    *   *(Note: Reputation was kept abstract as BOP customers might not have access to online ratings like middle/upper class segments).*
3.  **Delivery Method:**
    *   Level 0: Face-to-Face
    *   Level 1: Telemedicine
4.  **Payment:**
    *   Level 0: Upfront (pay full amount at the time of service)
    *   Level 1: Non-Upfront (e.g., EMI, staggered payments)
5.  **Price:**
    *   Level 0: High (Above ₹100 per consultation)
    *   Level 1: Low (Up to ₹100 per consultation)
    *   *(Note: This price is for OPD consultation only).*

This design has 5 attributes with 2 levels each, resulting in 2^5 = 32 possible unique service concepts. Each respondent was shown 8 choice sets, with 4 options (concepts) in each set. This design ensured all 32 concepts were shown across the survey.

### Data Structure

The data was collected and structured in a specific format suitable for CBC analysis in R. The dataset, `choice_based_conjoint.csv`, includes:

*   Demographic variables (serial number, gender, age, household members, profession, monthly income, health insurance).
*   A unique identifier for each choice instance: `v1` column, formatted as `customerID + choiceSetID` (e.g., `1.1`, `1.2`, `1.3` for customer 1's first, second, third choice sets). This column groups the options presented in a single choice set.
*   The `choice` column: This is the dependent variable. It is coded as `1` for the option chosen by the respondent in that specific choice set and `0` for the options not chosen in that set.
*   Attribute columns: `distance`, `reputation`, `delivery`, `payment`, `price`, coded as `0` or `1` corresponding to the levels defined above.

For each choice set shown to a respondent, there are 4 rows in the data. For example, for customer 1's first choice set (`v1 = 1.1`), there will be 4 rows, one for each option presented. The `choice` column will be `1` for the option they picked and `0` for the other three.

The total number of observations is 303 customers * 8 choice sets/customer * 4 options/choice set = 9696 observations.

## Analysis Method: Conditional Logit

The analysis of CBC data typically uses a model called **Conditional Logit**. This model is designed to predict the probability of choosing one option from a set of alternatives, where the probability is dependent on the attributes of *all* options in the set.

### Mathematical Model

The probability that person `i` chooses option `j` from choice set `k` (where `j` is one of the options in set `k`) is given by:

$P(\text{Choice}_{i,k} = j) = \frac{\exp(U_{i,j})}{\sum_{m \in k} \exp(U_{i,m})}$

Where:
*   $U_{i,j}$ is the utility person `i` derives from option `j`.
*   $\exp()$ is the exponential function ($e^{\text{value}}$).
*   The denominator is the sum of the exponential utilities of *all* options `m` available in choice set `k`.

The utility $U_{i,j}$ is typically modeled as a linear combination of the attributes of option `j`:

$U_{i,j} = \beta_0 + \beta_1 \cdot \text{Attribute1}_{j} + \beta_2 \cdot \text{Attribute2}_{j} + \dots + \epsilon_{i,j}$

The coefficients ($\beta$ values) represent the average change in utility associated with a one-unit change in the corresponding attribute level (moving from level 0 to level 1). These coefficients are estimated from the choice data.

This model is related to logistic regression. If there were only two options (e.g., buy/not buy), where the "not buy" option has a utility of 0, the probability of buying would simplify to $\frac{\exp(U_{buy})}{\exp(U_{buy}) + \exp(0)} = \frac{\exp(U_{buy})}{1 + \exp(U_{buy})}$, which is the standard logistic regression formula.

### R Implementation

The conditional logit model can be implemented in R using the `survival` library, which contains the `clogit` function.

1.  **Load Data and Prepare:**

    ```R
    # Set working directory (replace with your path)
    # setwd("path/to/your/data") 

    # Read the data
    data <- read.csv("choice_based_conjoint.csv")

    # Remove serial number if not needed
    data <- data[,-1] 

    # Convert monthly income to factor as it was categorical ranges
    data$Monthly.Income <- as.factor(data$Monthly.Income) 
    ```

2.  **Load Survival Library:**

    ```R
    # Install if not already installed: install.packages("survival")
    library(survival)
    ```

3.  **Run the `clogit` Model:** The formula structure is `dependent_variable ~ independent_variables + strata(grouping_variable)`.

    *   `dependent_variable`: This is tricky. The `clogit` function expects a `Surv` object for survival analysis, but for CBC, we use a special syntax: `c(dependent_column, grouping_variable)`. The dependent column is `choice` (1 if chosen, 0 otherwise), and the grouping variable is `v1` (which defines the choice sets).
    *   `independent_variables`: The attribute columns (`distance`, `reputation`, `delivery`, `payment`, `price`).
    *   `strata(v1)`: This tells the function to perform the logit analysis conditional on each unique value in the `v1` column (i.e., within each choice set).

    ```R
    # Model with direct effects only
    clog_output_direct <- clogit(c(choice, v1) ~ distance + reputation + delivery + payment + price, data = data)

    # View the results
    summary(clog_output_direct)
    ```

## Model Results

### Direct Effects Model

Running the first model (`clog_output_direct`) provides coefficient estimates for each attribute level change (Level 0 to Level 1).

```
Call:
clogit(c(choice, v1) ~ distance + reputation + delivery + payment + price, data = data)

        coef  exp(coef)   se(coef)      z P(>|z|)
distance 1.0712   2.9187     0.0575  18.63 < 2e-16 ***
reputation 1.7342   5.6637     0.0595  29.15 < 2e-16 ***
delivery 0.1546   1.1672     0.0594   2.60  0.0093 **
payment 0.1869   1.2055     0.0576   3.24  0.0012 **
price -1.6310   0.1958     0.0596 -27.38 < 2e-16 ***
---
Signif. codes:  0 ‘***’ 0.001 ‘**’ 0.01 ‘*’ 0.05 ‘.’ 0.1 ‘ ’ 1

     aic       
 4279.06 
```
*Interpretation of Coefficients (for Level 0 to Level 1 change):*

*   **Distance (1.0712):** Positive and highly significant (P < 0.001). Level 1 (Up to 5km) increases utility compared to Level 0 (> 5km). Decreasing distance is preferred.
*   **Reputation (1.7342):** Positive and highly significant. Level 1 (High) increases utility compared to Level 0 (Low). Higher reputation is strongly preferred. This has the largest positive impact on utility.
*   **Delivery (0.1546):** Positive and significant (P < 0.01). Level 1 (Telemedicine) slightly increases utility compared to Level 0 (Face-to-Face). Telemedicine is slightly preferred, though the coefficient is relatively small.
*   **Payment (0.1869):** Positive and significant (P < 0.01). Level 1 (Non-Upfront) increases utility compared to Level 0 (Upfront). Non-upfront payments are preferred.
*   **Price (-1.6310):** Negative and highly significant. Level 1 (Low Price) increases utility compared to Level 0 (High Price) because the coefficient for the *variable* `price` (coded 0 for high, 1 for low) is negative. This means moving from Price Level 0 (High) to Price Level 1 (Low) *decreases* the value of the `price` variable (from 0 to 1), but since the coefficient is negative, $-1.6310 \times (1 - 0) = -1.6310$ is the change in utility *from the perspective of the model's variable coding*. A *positive* coefficient for Price=Low (if coded as a dummy) would be intuitive. Here, the negative coefficient on the Price variable coded as 0/1 means moving from high price (0) to low price (1) *decreases* the price value, and the negative coefficient on this means Utility increases. A simpler interpretation is that **low price is strongly preferred over high price**, reflected by the large magnitude of the coefficient. This has the largest negative impact on utility (or largest positive impact from High to Low).

Based on magnitude, Reputation (1.73) and Price (-1.63, interpreted as preference for low price) are the most important factors influencing choice in this segment, followed by Distance (1.07).

### Model with Interaction Effects

The instructor then explores interaction effects (moderating effects) to see how the importance of attributes changes based on individual characteristics like gender and health insurance status.

```R
# Model with direct and interaction effects
clog_output_interaction <- clogit(c(choice, v1) ~ distance + reputation + delivery + payment + price + 
                                    distance*Gender + price*Gender + price*Health.Insurance +
                                    Gender + Health.Insurance, # Include main effects of moderators
                                    data = data) 

# View the results
summary(clog_output_interaction)
```
*(Note: The formula should include the main effects of the moderators, `Gender` and `Health.Insurance`, which the instructor's summary output seems to include implicitly or explicitly).*

Looking at the interaction terms in the output:

*   `distance:Gender`: The coefficient is -0.3873 and is significant (P < 0.001). Gender is coded 0/1. The instructor mentions 0->1 change, and then male users having lower sensitivity. Assuming Gender 0 is Female and 1 is Male:
    *   For Females (Gender=0), the effect of distance (Level 0 to 1) on utility is the `distance` coefficient (1.2752).
    *   For Males (Gender=1), the effect of distance (Level 0 to 1) on utility is `distance` coefficient + `distance:Gender` coefficient = 1.2752 + (-0.3873) = 0.8879.
    *   Interpretation: **Males are less sensitive to distance** than females (the increase in utility from closer distance is smaller for males).
*   `price:Gender`: The coefficient is -0.3820 and is significant (P < 0.05). Assuming Gender 0 is Female and 1 is Male:
    *   For Females (Gender=0), the effect of price (Level 0 to 1) on utility (preference for low price) is the `price` coefficient (-1.9990).
    *   For Males (Gender=1), the effect is `price` coefficient + `price:Gender` coefficient = -1.9990 + (-0.3820) = -2.3810.
    *   Interpretation: **Males are *more* sensitive to price** than females (the increase in utility from a lower price is larger for males, or the negative utility from a high price is stronger). *Correction:* The instructor says "gender increases the price sensitivity drops" and "When you are male, your price sensitivity drops". Let's re-check. The coefficient on `price` (0=High, 1=Low) is -1.999. A negative coefficient on `price` means Utility decreases as `price` variable increases (from 0 to 1, i.e., from High to Low). This is counter-intuitive to preference for low price. *However*, if the `price` variable was coded the *other way* (1=High, 0=Low), a positive coefficient would mean preference for high price, and a negative coefficient preference for low price. Let's trust the instructor's interpretation based on his data coding: A negative coefficient on `price` (coded 0=High, 1=Low) means preference for low price. The interaction `price:Gender` is -0.3820. So for males (Gender=1), the effect is -1.999 - 0.382 = -2.381. This means the utility *penalty* for high price (or bonus for low price) is *larger* for males. So males are *more* price sensitive, not less. *There might be a coding mismatch or slight misstatement in the transcript interpretation vs the coefficient sign.* Sticking to the coefficient math: The negative coefficient for `price` means moving from High (0) to Low (1) increases utility by $|-1.999|$. The interaction `price:Gender`=-0.382 means for males (Gender=1), this effect is $|-1.999 - 0.382| = |-2.381|$. Since $2.381 > 1.999$, males are *more* sensitive to price. Let's assume the instructor meant the *opposite* sign for the interaction effect based on his statement. Re-checking the provided image of the output (around 30:38), the coefficients are: distance:Gender (-0.3873), price:Gender (-0.3820), price:Health.Insurance (-1.7070). My interpretation based on the math holds. **Males are MORE sensitive to distance and MORE sensitive to price based on these coefficients.** The instructor's verbal interpretation (males less sensitive) seems contradictory to the signs shown in the summary output image. Let's present the findings based on the *visual* coefficients shown, noting the potential discrepancy with the verbal explanation.
*   `price:Health.Insurance`: The coefficient is -1.7070 and is highly significant (P < 0.001). Health Insurance is coded 0/1. Assuming 0=No Insurance, 1=Has Insurance:
    *   For those with No Insurance (HI=0), the price effect is `price` coefficient (-1.9990).
    *   For those with Insurance (HI=1), the price effect is `price` coefficient + `price:Health.Insurance` coefficient = -1.9990 + (-1.7070) = -3.7060.
    *   Interpretation: Those with health insurance are **more sensitive to price** than those without insurance (the utility increase from low price is much larger for those with insurance). *Again, this contradicts the instructor's verbal statement "when you have health insurance then also your price sensitivity drops".* The magnitude of the effect *increases*.

*(Self-correction: It's possible the coding of the interaction terms or the base levels in the model is different from the simple 0/1 coding assumed. However, interpreting the interaction coefficient as modifying the main effect is the correct approach. Given the discrepancy between the spoken interpretation and the coefficient signs visible in the video capture, I will stick to interpreting the signs and magnitudes as they appear in the output image shown, and note the findings based on those numbers, while acknowledging the instructor's potentially contradictory verbal summary).*

The direct effects in the second model (Reputation 1.73, Price -1.99, Distance 1.27) are similar to the first model, but Price has become slightly more important than Reputation based on magnitude.

The analysis shows that certain demographic factors (Gender, Health Insurance) moderate the importance of attributes like Distance and Price.

## Case Study Findings Summary (Based on Transcript's Final Summary)

Despite some potential discrepancies in the interpretation of specific coefficients vs. the final verbal summary in the transcript, the overall findings presented by the instructor are:

*   Men are less sensitive towards distance (this aligns with the coefficient sign).
*   Men are less price sensitive (this contradicts the coefficient sign observed).
*   If health insurance is available, consumers are less price sensitive (this contradicts the coefficient sign observed).
*   Consumers have an affinity towards telemedicine (small positive coefficient, might be significant in a larger model).
*   (Findings from other variables not shown in output):
    *   Consumers with bank accounts are less price and distance sensitive and prefer reputed doctors. This group likely has more financial resources.
    *   Availability of local ambulance facility (social capital) leads to lesser price and distance sensitivity. People prefer face-to-face interactions and EMI payment options when this community support is present.

The case study demonstrates how CBC can be used to understand complex trade-offs and how different consumer segments value product/service attributes differently, informing service design decisions.

## Conclusion

Choice-Based Conjoint analysis is a powerful tool for understanding consumer preferences and willingness to pay for different attribute levels by simulating real-world choice scenarios. It allows for the estimation of part-worth utilities and analysis of how individual characteristics influence preferences.

This concludes our discussion on What Consumers Want using Conjoint Analysis. Next week, we will move on to a new topic: Customer Segmentation. Thank you.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 13: Segmentation, Targeting and Positioning

Hello everybody! Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Professor Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur.

This is Week 3, Session 1. The topic of Module 3 is **Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning**.

In the last two weeks, the content was a little bit heavy. I can understand. This week will be a little bit lighter than the last two weeks.

Here we will be talking about customer segmentation and targeting positioning. This is something which is marketing 101, basics of marketing. This is somewhere I have seen that many people, sometimes professors of reputed colleges as well, and probably practitioners also in many organizations have a little bit of understanding problem. I have a few slides which talk about what segmentation, targeting, positioning is, and we will discuss about that. The methodology is probably less tough than the previous ones that we have talked about, so we'll have a session on that as well.

So, be with me for this particular presentation.

The first thing that we are talking about is **Segmentation**, and then **Targeting**, and then **Positioning**. So, of course, what is segmentation, targeting, positioning?

Some of the content used in this presentation has been taken from our classic book, Philip Kotler's book. We all follow that book and that is a very good book. There are also lots of other resources available in the online domain which are freely available, and you can read about that from there as well. Some of the materials I have given in this particular video.

## Why Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (STP)?

Segmentation, targeting, positioning is required because it becomes difficult for an individual or for some company to actually cater to many people.

In **segmentation**, what we do is, it is a methodology to create groups, smaller groups, out of lots of consumers. How will I create those smaller groups? I will make sure that:
*   People who are in the same group are closer to each other.
*   People who are in different groups are very different from each other.

Then I decide **whom I will target**. Out of those various groups, I cannot actually cater to many people, I cater to some of them.

And then **how I will target**? What kind of offerings I will give? What kind of competing offerings are already available? And **how I can position myself** in the jungle of competing offerings so that I will be different from other people?

This framework was created based on these needs.

Why is that important?

One major reason is that many customers have different kinds of preferences. If you remember in the last video of conjoint analysis, I have shown that people who have healthcare insurance and people who do not have healthcare insurance have different kinds of preferences. People who have bank accounts and people who do not have bank accounts also have different kinds of preferences. People who have ambulance facility locally and who do not have ambulance facility locally have different kinds of preferences. And obviously, gender-wise also, they are different.

So, people who are of different groups might have different kinds of preference, and you have to know first thing is that who has what kind of preference. If you do not know that, then whatever you are trying to provide, whatever offerings you are trying to give to these customers, will not actually solve their purpose; they might not even buy that because that might not come at the top of their preference.

So, given that kind of a situation, we have to find out segments, we have to find out which segment likes what, how they are different, what kind of characteristics they have, what kind of behavior they do, and etcetera.

## The Problem with the "Middle Path"

Now a classic question comes up: So what, people are different, so what? Why can I not give something which is suitable for everybody or which will be in the middle ground? So why can I actually ask for a truce and say that okay, all of you guys come together, and whatever is the middle path I will follow that?

The problem of that kind of a methodology is something that I will be going to tell now.

Let us assume that you are talking about two groups, and you just close your eyes and think about whether you like cold coffee or hot coffee, or it is iced tea or hot tea, which one you prefer most?

There will be some people in my audience who actually like hot tea, and there will be some other people who will like iced tea. Let's say I take a poll and find out that okay, in my student base that I have, I have 70 percent people who prefer hot tea and 30 percent people who prefer iced tea. And then during the day when the exam will happen, I thought that I will actually give a tea treat to my students.

So what I decide is, okay, why do not I give 70 percent? So I make 7 litres of hot tea with 3 litres of iced tea, mix them up, and find out something, and then give it to you. That should be preferred because that is the middle path, that is how preference has been shown, so that will become a lukewarm tea. And that is something that we might not like.

None of you, the hot tea guys will probably go to a hot tea, they will probably pay 10 rupees and get hot tea from a local vendor. And the iced tea guy will pay 30 rupees and get probably iced tea. And nobody will have my drinks. Though they are free, though I am giving you lukewarm tea free of cost, probably you will not go there.

Because nobody wants that middle path, middle path does not work as simple as that.

## Choosing Which Segment to Target

Now then I can choose any one of you; I can choose the 70 percent guys or 30 percent guys. So I have a small problem here.

First of all is that there is a hot tea and iced tea, there are two different preferences that I have to understand. Now I cannot create, I do not have the setup or I do not have the capital to create both iced tea and hot tea.

If I have that setup, there is a best option: those who like hot tea, they will raise their hands, and I will give them hot tea. Then I will ask who likes iced tea, you will raise your hand, and I will give you iced tea. Or probably I'll have both the options in my hotel, and you come and pay a little bit of money and decide which one you like to have.

The problem is that in most of the cases, we do not have that much of resources available, that much of expertise available to actually cater to both the segments or multiple segments. Then I have to choose one segment.

Now how will I choose? 70 percent prefers hot tea, 30 percent prefers iced tea, should I give hot tea? Common sense says yes. But a little bit of more common sense says that "depends". What is the "depends"? This is a classic thing that we managers say, "depends". It depends on what?

It depends on the very first thing is the price. Even if the price of one cup of hot tea is 10 rupees, there is a price of one cup of iced tea, let's say 40 rupees or something like that, 30-40 rupees. So then, it's not exactly on price, it is on margin. In that 10 rupees, you might have 2 rupees of profit or 3 rupees of profit, and in this case, probably if it is 30 rupees, you might have probably 20 rupees of profit, I do not know.

So, if that kind of a margin is also different, then 3 rupees into 70 people and 20 rupees into 30 people, which one is higher than the other, that becomes one thing.

Now the next thing that is important is also the resource availability. I am very, I know how to create hot tea, but I do not know how to create iced tea, or I might need a fridge and etcetera for iced tea, and I might need only a microwave oven or whatever I don't know, some oven for hot tea.

So, the amount of resources required for hot tea will be much less than the amount of resources required for iced tea. So that becomes also one issue when you try to decide whether you will sell hot tea or iced tea.

So all of these together creates a problem, and that is what we are trying to solve using segmentation, targeting, and positioning.

As I have shown here, there are two segments, yellow segment and blue segment. Yellow segment prefers attribute one, which is price... wait, looking at the diagram provided (implicitly): Yellow segment prefers attribute 1 (e.g., durability), and Blue segment prefers attribute 2 (e.g., price). Beauty wise both are same probably. The preference is a little bit more for the yellow segment than the blue towards attribute 1. Blue segment is price sensitive, and yellow segment is durability sensitive.

So, preferences given to various attributes, importance is given to various attributes for these two segments are different. It is absolutely like whether we have health care insurance or not, that kind of a segment that we talked about in Week 2.

## Four Types of Segmentation Methods

So, there are majorly four ways. How to do segmentation?

One thing that we have to understand properly is that **segmentation is the process**, and **segments are the outcome**. So, if somebody asks you to create segments, you do not say that okay, it has to be a geographical segmentation or demographical segmentation and so on. You have to give me the outcome, the segments. So you have to tell about what exactly this person is, what is his preference, what is his profile. If you create a human being, how would he be like, how would he behave, what will he require, and etcetera. Those kind of details have to be given when I talk about segments. When I talk about segmentation, then you can talk about okay, it is a demographic segmentation or whatever.

So majorly there are four segmentation methods:
1.  **Characteristics of the customers:** Demographic and Geographic. Both are characteristics of the customer.
2.  **Benefits sought by them:** Psychographic.
3.  **Systematic product related behaviour:** Behavioral.

So there are four types of segmentation:
*   **Geographic Segmentation**
*   **Demographic Segmentation**
*   **Psychographic Segmentation**
*   **Behavioral Segmentation**

When I am talking about systematic product related behavior, that can be different based on the purchasing behavior and based on the channel choice kind of behavior also, so it can be so many behaviors that you can track.

As I told, based on what is a **market segment**: a group of customers who share a similar set of needs and wants. And you should also understand that they have different sets of needs and wants from different segments. That is something that is also important. So they are similar within and different outside. That is something that is important.

## Emphasis on Behavioral Segmentation in Marketing Analytics

Now, this is where I actually... and I will talk about many things here... that compared to these four segmentation types, the marketing analytics people or people who have worked on database marketing and etcetera with lots of data, they say that the previous three segmentations... as we have more and more data about a customer and if I have, if I can track the customer through the cookies, how he walks in the online world, the www world, or how he behaves in my retail stores if I can track based on the overhead cameras, or let's say if I can have his purchasing behavior, all of these things if I have, then the segmentation should be done on the, based on the **behavioral data**.

So, you should ideally for all the marketing analytics purposes, if you have been asked to do segmentation, you should do segmentation based on behavioral data. Why? Because that is the thing that you do not ask somebody, that is the thing that nobody can manipulate, or it can be very difficult to manipulate. That is something that is coming out and that is something that is the closest towards the ultimate outcome.

Ultimate outcome is the purchase. Whatever is closest to that purchase, whatever behavior is something that is important data. We will do all our segmentation and etcetera based on that.

**Understanding the Technology/Process:**

Once we have created the segmentation, understand the technology, understand what I am trying to say. So once I have done the segmentation using behavioral segmentation, I will **name the segments** using the geographic, demographic, and sometimes psychographic profile.

I will do the segmentation based on behavioral segmentation. This is something different from what classical marketing professors say or other practices. But in the industry, this is what people actually do: we do the segmentation based on the behavioral data, and we create segments. And then even if I have to identify the segment, if somebody comes in tomorrow and I have to identify whether he is falling in segment A or segment B or segment C, in that I will talk about geographic, demographic, and psychographics.

So all I am trying to say is that if I ask you to create four segments for let's say apparel buyers, and you have done the segmentation and you are defining the segment now. So one segment is probably ethnic buyers. And you will say that ethnic buyers are somebody who are of let's say from 20 to 50 years of age, and they are mainly male, and they will wear this and that, and they like Indian products, and they actually watch Indian movies more, and they go to festivals, Indian festivals, they celebrate Indian festivals more.

If you actually talk about all of these things, then you are actually talking about the demography, the psychographics of the person. Now that is the outcome that should come from the behavioral pattern of the customers.

You should not start with the geography. You should not say that at first I will create a male versus female segmentation or let's say state wise, India state wise segmentation, North Indian, South Indian. Because there can be so many aspects where that kind of segmentation might not matter. For example, let's say preference towards education. Whether you prefer an English medium school or whatever your local language medium school. In that kind of a situation, probably whether it is a North Indian or South Indian might not matter. Everybody who are middle class, upper class, and etcetera, and prefer an English medium school.

Because there are certain things, so in that case, probably gender is also not a very important variable. The moment the... So all I am trying to say is that if you do a gender based segmentation or if you do geographical segmentation, that might not be relevant.

So, what is relevant is the data that comes from the behaviour. You go and check that how people are searching about various schools in Google, if you have that data, if you can track that data. How people are searching when they are planning to... it is one time or two time probably they do that. After that, they make their kids go to a particular school, and that is why they continue.

So, how parents are searching, which kind of names are coming at the top or which kind of these things they are following, which kind of let's say Facebook pages and etcetera they are following, which school names are coming more commonly in the Facebook talkings. That is something that if you can track that, and you can then you can see that how that particular person, the Facebook profile guy, is behaving in other aspects, then you will know that how I can do behavioral segmentation.

Other, let's say you can also do let's say, I have my customers who are already there in my school, all my students in this case... you should not say customers, they are students. But if you... they are not for profit making, but let's say in other situations if it is hospital and etcetera, which is a profit making organization, or there can be some other services let's say airlines, which is a profit making organization, then you can actually track the customer's behaviour.

That okay, so what kind of tickets he is buying, whether he is actually reacting to my promotional efforts, whether he is buying in sudden routes, whether he prefers only non-stop airlines or he also prefers some one or two stoppages in between if the prices are low, how much is the price sensitivity, all of these things are behavioral data.

The price sensitivity, the preference towards one stop or two stops, or whether he only travels during the holiday seasons or the festival seasons and etcetera, all of this data is behavioral data.

So, we can track that and then I can see that okay, people who are travelling during the holidays, whether I can go back and check their geography, from where to where they are travelling, then I know that okay, these are the guys who are actually staying away from their home and they are travelling back to their home. And the to and fro will give me an idea that where their home is and where their house, where their job location is.

So that kind of thing can be done after you create the segmentation, that these guys are holiday travelers or probably festival travelers. And then there can be certain travelers who are let's say business travelers, who travel only for two days, three days and some distance and then they come back. So the travel durations are small but very frequent, so these guys are business travelers. Then business travelers can be of different type, they can travel in business class, they can travel in economy class, their preference will also be different.

So all of this segmentation that we are doing when the data is available in ample level, we are doing **behavioral segmentation**. We are not doing geographic, demographic, or psychographic segmentation as the primary basis.

But still, the other types are relevant for description and understanding. So what is:

*   **Demographic Segmentation:** We can do segmentation based on age, life stage, gender, income, generation, race and culture, and so on.
*   **Geographic Segmentation:** We also do based on country, region, country size, city or state, which kind of state etcetera.
*   **Behavioral Segmentation:** We do like type of purchase decision or decision making practice, decision making unit and etcetera, and what kind of decision making, whether you are an initiator or whether you are the final purchase maker and etcetera.
*   **Psychographic Segmentation:** (Also socio-psychological) is your attitude, your lifestyle, your preferences towards your life, what you want to be, what you want to do for people or for your family, for yourself etcetera. All these comes in the psychographics.

## Generations and Cohorts

For example, one of the key things that we talk about in a demographic profile when we actually do segmentation and we are defining the segments, one of the key things while defining the segments we use is the **generation**.

So generation is the life stage when you are born. For example, Baby Boomers born in 1946 to 1964, and then the Generation X, Generation Y, Generation Z. These are US data. In India, we actually lag these people around by 10 to 20 years, depending on which part of India it is. It will lag from 10 to 20 years from these Baby Boomers X, Y, Z.

So these are all generations that we have seen. They are for the better definitions, so how they are different? The Baby Boomers were later people, and then telephone came, and then some people have seen the www era, and then now people have seen the mobile Internet era. So things have changed over time.

And we also talk about **cohort**. What is cohort? Cohort is something, something where people have similar kind of life experiences. This is not about when they are born or what they are not born. It is about whether they have had similar kind of life experiences or not. For example, there is a depression cohort, there was a World War 2 cohort, and there is a World War 1.

So, the birth year was a little bit ahead of that, but when they came of age, when they become consumers, what they experienced is something that matters. So for example, let's say I and somebody else who have born in different time periods can be in the same cohort if we are in different, we are from different countries.

So for example, India is lagging as I told that India is lagging a little bit, so India's guy who is 20 years age and some other less developed country's guy who is a 40 years age might be experiencing this thing at the same time, some facilities at the same time, so they are in the same cohort.

Examples of cohorts: Depression cohort, post-war cohort, boomers, gen X, gen Y cohort.

**Gen Y cohort** are people who are **millennials**. So who are born in 1977 to 1997, and they came of age in around 90s or 2000. They have seen the Y2K problem, and they had, their share of population is very high, 30 percent of the overall population of these people.

They like:
*   Free content
*   Community oriented stuff
*   Telecommuting mode
*   Everything social
*   Things which are wireless (because wireless technology is something that they have seen)

And what they dislike?
*   Anonymous mass marketing (You cannot do mass marketing to these people. People who are of previous age still watch TV or radio etc. For them mass marketing works. But for these kind of people who are more in the Internet or probably have shifted from radio or TV to Internet, for them targeted marketing works more.)
*   Anonymous (They want security)
*   Beaten paths (what has been already done)
*   Restricted access (e.g., Internet neutrality issues - you will see that most people who raised their voice were Gen Y)

And then the millennial cohorts who are big shoppers who still now co-purchase with their family members, mostly parents, but they have a say.

There was a time when we were kids, probably around 20 or 30 years back, we did not have much say in purchasing. But now that information is available, and millennials are having that information in their hands. They are using that tab and etcetera, they are watching TV and whatever various other things. From a very little age, they have an opinion about many things, and people have understood that we should give importance to the opinions of the kids as well.

So, when they have opinions, they are co-purchasing with their parents, it's true, but they are putting their say in what they will buy and what they will not buy. So this kind of behavior is mattering; so whether they were born, whether they are male or female does not matter, but they have an opinion, this is something, this is the behavior that matters.

Other characteristics of Gen Y/Millennials:
*   All information is experienced electronically.
*   They love multi-tasking.
*   They are co-creators. (Co-creation is a marketing concept that is coming up. You will see that new tools are saying that okay, you design your own T-shirt, you design it and I will print it and give it to you. That is all my job. You design your own card or mug or whatever and I will just print it and give it to you. It is co-creation. So co-creation is something that is coming up. People are saying that okay, you come up and you say something and we will create a course together, or we will create a product offerings together. In Subway and etcetera, you will see that people are saying okay, you tell me what kind of food items I will give, what kind of sauce I will put, you tell me because I do not know and I want to give you those choices.)
*   Co-creation is related with consumer empowerment: you are inviting the customer to take the choice. He will say what he will like, now you will not dictate him. So that kind of things millennials like, and that is something that is being given.
*   Another thing that is becoming more and more important in marketing is green marketing. Because these millennials are socially responsible. They are saying that okay, I do not want to have an AC, or I do not want to actually consume something which creates any kind of environmental problem. So this social responsibility all of a sudden has become a major issue because millennials actually behave like that. They like, they do not like concrete jungle, they like something which is more environmental. So socially responsible consumption has become an important factor. These are the people who have experienced all of these things together. So global warming was an issue because global warming is actually affecting the atmosphere, actually affecting the outside world. It is not something that happened 20 years back when we were kids, we did not experience that much, but now kids are experiencing that and they are listening to that, and information is more and more available at the tip of their fingers. So that is why they are more aware, they are more responsible, and that is actually affecting their behavior also.

Now, behavior is something that we can track. Their internal things are very difficult to track in a large scale, it is further difficult to track. So that is why we do behavioral segmentation.

## Life Stage and Gender

Another important thing that we try to say is **life stage** or **gender**. So gender, and to be specific in gender, I would talk about female as a gender. Female as a gender is very lucrative, or I would say very attractive for a marketer. Why? Because female has multiple life stages, and those life stages are very different from male. And from that, from these changes of life stages, their preference, their thinking process, what they give more importance, what they give less importance changes heavily.

And more so for a woman who is in an urban environment. For example:
*   When she is a singleton (no spouse, no children, under 45 - under 45 is not the case for India, in India it is probably under 25)
*   Then she goes for a marriage (So pre-marriage and even probably when you are a kid versus when you are a teenager, male versus female, female face those teenager related issues, and they have a different life experience, different kind of views about life.) You will see that women become very matured, there is a difference between how a male child and a female child gets the maturity level in terms of cognitive ability, emotional quotient and etcetera. All of these things also impact their behavior.
*   Then when you marry, all of your decisions all of a sudden from 'me oriented' become 'we oriented' (composite relationship for spouse, at that point you do not have a kid). But you try to take decisions which are good for both rather than one single person, and it is seen more for female than male. It has been very well researched that females are more community oriented, males are more I would say independent in that way. So female decisions are always 'we' decisions, and that is something that marketers actually capture. You would see lots of ads like Saffola ad, they target the female, they say that okay, you are the woman of the family and you have to decide what kind of food should come, what kind of I would say even what kind of mosquito coil will actually work in your family, that is something that is women's decision. One thing is they are homemakers. Another thing is they are 'we' people, so they actually do not think about themselves, they think about many people.
*   And then you have a first child, all of a sudden it becomes very huge importance you start giving on hygiene, huge importance you start giving on let's say sanitation and etcetera etcetera.
*   Then children go to school, your time... so time crunch is high here (when kids are very young). The time crunch goes down here, probably your I would say evenings are more free... not evenings, at least the afternoons are more free, and when the afternoons are free, you have to do something. So you go and watch probably a TV serial, or go and shop, or you work. So you have to actually create a solution, or you have to think that this kind of, there is some open space that is available, and how I can capture that open space of that person and probably some, I would say, some amount that they are willing to pay for that open space also. (Open time space I am trying to say). That is something that the marketers identify.
*   Then kids become independent, sometimes the second kid comes up. If the second kid comes up, the level of I would say importance on sanitation and hygiene that you have given with the first kid is not so strong with the second kid because in the second kid's case, you know, so for example, I will give a personal example. When I had my first kid (probably, I have only one kid), when I had my kid, my wife when she was deciding whether we will go to travel in some place, then a major important factor for her to choose the hotel was nothing else but the food, whether she can go and cook in the kitchen or not. Now big hotels will not give you that option probably, but all these bed and breakfast kinds of things will give you that option. So this is something that big hotels can think about that if they have to capture this kind of people, they should give the option of if somebody wants to come into the kitchen properly and then want to make his own food, they should give that option also. That because otherwise, people like us will not go to a big hotel, we will go to a good bed and breakfast when we have a small kid because that is something that is most important for a mother who is a mom of a first kid. Then who is a mom of a second kid probably will not be that much focused on the kid's preferences because he knows that okay, it will manage, I have experienced it, it is not so scary anymore, I will manage it.

So all I am trying to say is woman's life stage is a very important thing.

And one point of the Thomas Cook (if it is not there anymore), they actually created a holiday package targeted towards women: cooking classes, cycle trails, spas, and then this women group. The group of women who will actually avail these services depending on the group, depending on the age and preference and etcetera of the, the behavior of the women, the offerings are also different.

So cooking classes are not something that a young woman will want to have. Very young or very college-going women group, they will want to go for a spa probably, or they probably want to go for some pubs and etcetera, some liquor tasting and etcetera. On the other hand, a woman who is more of a family person, some 4-5 people or very elderly persons, elderly persons might want to go to historical places or places which are more religious, and middle-aged people might want to go for cooking classes and cycle trials and etcetera.

So, depending on which kind of woman you are, which age group, which life stage and etcetera, so all of these things I am talking about, that these are all geographical segmentation but it is actually geographical segments, the behavior is similar and you are creating segments like this based on geography, based on demography and etcetera.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 14: Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Week 3, Session 2, continuing our discussion on Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (STP). This lecture is presented by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur.

In the previous session, we covered some initial aspects of STP. We will continue from where we left off, discussing different bases for segmentation.

## Bases for Segmentation

### Geographic Segmentation

Geographic segmentation involves dividing the market based on geographical units like nations, states, regions, cities, or neighborhoods. Sometimes, geographical parameters can influence behavior.

*   **Example: Food Preferences in India**
    *   Observing a map showing food preferences (vegetarian vs. non-vegetarian) across India.
    *   Coastal areas often show higher non-vegetarian consumption (likely due to fish availability).
    *   Hilly regions (Northeast, Kashmir/Ladakh) also show higher non-vegetarian consumption (possibly due to agriculture challenges and ease of non-veg food sources).
    *   Areas with strong agriculture (Punjab, Haryana, Rajasthan, Gujarat, parts of UP) tend to have higher vegetarian food habits.
    *   This example illustrates how geographical location can explain behavioral patterns (like food choices).

### Demographic Segmentation

Demographic segmentation divides the market based on variables such as age, gender, income, occupation, education, religion, race, generation, nationality, social class, and family life cycle.

*   **Example: Indian Wedding Market**
    *   Matrimony websites often use demographic filters for segmentation.
    *   Key demographic factors used in this market include:
        *   **Language:** Bengali, Punjabi, etc. (often correlated with states).
        *   **State/Region:** Linked to language and cultural practices.
        *   **Caste and Religion:** Still significant factors in India for marriage decisions.
        *   **Life Stage:** Age group (20s, 30s, 40s) impacts preferences.
        *   **Past History:** Divorcees, widows/widowers may prefer partners with similar histories.
        *   **Educational Background:** Specific platforms exist for alumni of premier institutes (e.g., IIT IIM Shaadi.com).
        *   **Profession:** Doctor, Engineer, etc.
        *   **Geographic Location:** Where someone lives or is willing to relocate.

### Psychographic Segmentation

Psychographic segmentation divides buyers into different groups based on psychological/personality traits, lifestyle, or values. It looks at consumers' activities, interests, and opinions (AIOs).

*   **Example: Indian Music Customers**
    *   Segments based on engagement and preference depth:
        *   **Fanatics:** Very focused on specific genres/artists, high depth, low breadth.
        *   **Enthusiasts:** Less depth, more breadth (listen to various kinds of music but not deeply focused).
        *   **Casuals:** Less engaged.
        *   **Indifferent:** Least engaged.
    *   Data sources for identifying these segments: Tracking listening habits on music apps (Gaana, etc.), ticket purchases for concerts (MeraEvents, etc.).
*   **Example: Western Music Listeners (Bandsintown Data)**
    *   Segments based on engagement and socialness:
        *   **Super Fans:** High engagement, high socialness.
        *   **Plugged Indies:** High engagement, lower socialness.
        *   **Soloists:** Low socialness (prefer enjoying music alone), varying engagement.
        *   **Dedicated Diehards (Old School):** Passionate but may be stuck in past time periods, attend fewer shows but willing to pay more.
        *   **Tag Alongs:** Low engagement, high socialness (attend concerts to socialize, others' preferences matter more).

### Behavioral Segmentation

Behavioral segmentation divides buyers into groups based on their knowledge of, attitude toward, use of, or response to a product. This is often considered the most relevant segmentation as it's closest to the actual purchase behavior.

*   Marketers divide buyers based on:
    *   Knowledge about a product.
    *   Attitude towards a product.
    *   Use of a product.
    *   Response towards a product.
*   Key behavioral variables:
    *   **Needs and Benefits:** What customers seek from the product.
    *   **Decision Roles:** Identifying the initiator, influencer, decider, buyer, and user in the purchase process. Understanding these roles helps target the right person at the right stage.
*   **Example: Pregnancy Test Kits**
    *   The same product serves two distinct behavioral segments based on the occasion of use and motivation:
        *   **Planned Pregnancy:** Hopeful situation, anticipation, willing to pay more. Products marketed with emotional imagery (babies, flowers), higher price (e.g., $19.5).
        *   **Unplanned Pregnancy:** Fearful situation, seeking factual information quickly. Products marketed with stark, informative packaging ("Fact Plus"), lower price (e.g., $3.5).
    *   The packaging, placement (near ovulation kits vs. contraceptives), and pricing are tailored to the specific behavior and emotional state of each segment.
*   **Example: Earphones**
    *   An earphone accessory can be used with a mobile phone or a laptop.
    *   The same product might be packaged and priced differently depending on where it is sold (e.g., in a mobile store vs. a laptop store accessory section).
    *   Mobile phone owners might have different price sensitivity or design preferences than laptop owners. Selling the identical product with different packaging and price points targets these different behavioral usage occasions.
*   Other behavioral factors:
    *   **Occasion of Use:** When the product is used (e.g., holidays, daily commute).
    *   **User Status:** Non-users, ex-users, potential users, first-time users, regular users.
    *   **Usage Rate:** Light, medium, and heavy product users.
    *   **Buyer Readiness Stage:** Awareness, knowledge, liking, preference, conviction, purchase (especially for technology adoption).
    *   **Loyalty Status:** Hard-core loyals, split loyals, shifting loyals, switchers.
    *   **Attitude:** Enthusiastic, positive, indifferent, negative, hostile towards the product/brand.

## Criteria for Effective Segmentation

For a segment to be useful, it should possess certain characteristics:

*   **Identifiable:** Can we identify distinct groups? Segments should be defined by tangible attributes (demographic, geographic, sometimes behavioral/psychographic) that allow marketers to recognize customers belonging to each segment.
*   **Accessible:** Can marketers reach the segment effectively and economically?
*   **Substantial/Appropriately Sized:** Is the segment large and profitable enough to serve?
*   **Differentiated:** Do segments respond differently to different marketing mix elements? Members within a segment should be similar, and members across segments should be different. This difference should be significant enough to warrant different marketing approaches. (Visualized as non-overlapping distributions).
*   **Stable:** Will the segment remain relatively constant over time?
*   **Measurable:** Can the size, purchasing power, and characteristics of the segments be measured?

## Targeting

Once segments are identified, the next step is to evaluate and select one or more segments to enter. Targeting is not just about the potential revenue from a segment but also about the company's capability and the competitive landscape.

Factors to consider when evaluating segments:

*   **Segment Size, Growth, and Value:** Potential revenue and profitability.
*   **Company Position and Ease of Entry:** Do we have the resources and capabilities to serve this segment? How easy is it for us to enter?
*   **Competitive Landscape:** Ease of competitive entry, number, and strength of existing competitors.

This evaluation often results in a matrix where segments are plotted based on **Segment Attractiveness** and **Competitive Strength (Company Capability)**.

*   **Segment Attractiveness:** How profitable/large is the segment? (High to Low)
*   **Competitive Strength:** How capable is our company of capturing this segment? (High to Low)

Segments in the high-high quadrant are ideal targets ("Perfect"). Segments in the low-low quadrant should be avoided ("Stay Away"). Companies often target high-medium or medium-high segments based on strategic priorities.

*   **Example: Best Buy Customer Segmentation (Barry, Jill, Buzz, Ray, BB4B)**
    *   A classic B2B example (search "Best Buy customer centricity" for case study).
    *   Segments are defined (Barry, Jill, Buzz, Ray, BB4B) with data like yearly purchase, percentage of customers, margin, number of competitors, and capability to serve.
    *   Calculate potential money earned per segment (e.g., Yearly Purchase * % Customers * Margin / (Number of Competitors + 1)).
    *   Evaluate capability to serve (High/Medium/Low).
    *   Map segments onto the Segment Attractiveness vs. Competitive Strength matrix to decide which segments to target. (e.g., Barry might be high attractiveness/medium capability, Jill might be medium attractiveness/high capability).

## Positioning

After selecting the target segment(s), the company must decide how to position its offering in the minds of target customers. Positioning is about creating a distinct and valued place for the brand in the competitive landscape.

*   **Brand Recall Exercise:**
    *   Think of the first brand that comes to mind for categories like:
        *   Soft drinks
        *   Coffee
        *   Bottled water
        *   Beer
    *   The brands that come to mind first are those with strong top-of-mind awareness, a key goal of successful positioning.
    *   Being top-of-mind increases the chance of being included in the customer's consideration set and, subsequently, the chance of being chosen.

*   **Positioning Goals:** Companies aim for:
    *   **Share of Market:** Actual sales percentage.
    *   **Share of Mind:** Top-of-mind awareness.
    *   **Share of Heart:** Emotional connection and preference.

*   **Competitive Analysis for Positioning:**
    *   Gather customer perceptions and ratings of your brand and competitors on relevant attributes (e.g., taste, price, packaging).
    *   **Example Table Structure:**
        | Attribute         | Competitor A | Competitor B | Competitor C | (Your Brand) |
        | :---------------- | :----------- | :----------- | :----------- | :----------- |
        | Taste             | E            | G            | P            | (Need Data)  |
        | Price             | G            | E            | F            | (Need Data)  |
        | Packaging         | G            | F            | E            | (Need Data)  |
        | ... (Other factors) | ...          | ...          | ...          | ...          |
        *(E=Excellent, G=Good, F=Fair, P=Poor)*
    *   Based on this, identify opportunities to differentiate or match competitors.

*   **Key Positioning Concepts:**
    *   **Point of Difference (POD):** Attributes or benefits that consumers strongly associate with a brand, positively evaluate, and believe they could not find to the same extent with a competitive brand.
        *   Must be **desirable**, **deliverable**, and **differentiating**.
        *   *Examples:* Apple/Nike partnership (music changing with running speed), FedEx (overnight express shipping).
    *   **Point of Parity (POP):** Attributes or benefits that are not necessarily unique to the brand but may in fact be shared with other brands. These are necessary to be considered a legitimate player in a product category.
        *   Can be **category specific** (necessary to be in the category), **correlational** (potential negative associations from PODs), or **competitive** (matching competitors' PODs).
        *   *Example:* For YiPPee! noodles to be considered a legitimate alternative to Maggi, it must have certain characteristics expected of instant noodles.

*   **Using POD and POP in Positioning:**
    *   Point of Parity is required to be relevant for a target segment (to belong to the category).
    *   Point of Difference is required to position yourself uniquely *within* the segment, different from competitors.
    *   *Example: Tanishq Jewelry Ad (Alia Bhatt)*
        *   **Point of Parity:** Portraying the brand as "young," "energetic," and "contemporary" (matching positioning of other brands aiming for a younger audience).
        *   **Point of Difference:** Offering the service of trying jewelry at home for free, allowing the customer to feel comfortable and express their individuality in their own space (differentiating from traditional jewelry store experience).

## Next Steps

The next step is to visualize the market structure and brand positioning using **Perceptual Maps**. These maps help understand how consumers perceive different brands based on key attributes and identify potential positioning opportunities or crowded spaces. We will delve into the creation and interpretation of perceptual maps in the upcoming sessions.

## Conclusion

We have covered the various bases for segmenting a market, the process of evaluating and selecting target segments, and the fundamentals of positioning a brand within those segments using concepts like Point of Difference and Point of Parity.

Thank you for your attention. We will continue our discussion on STP with quantitative techniques in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 15: Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (Contd.)

## Introduction

Hello everybody! Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from Vinod Gupta School of Management, IIT Kharagpur, taking this course for you. We are in module three now, discussing segmentation, targeting, and positioning.

In the last video, we discussed at length about what segmentation is and how to do it, what targeting is and its utility, and how to position your product when you target a market.

In this presentation, we will go ahead from where we left off in the last class.

## Steps of Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning

There are generally five steps involved in the segmentation, targeting, and positioning process:

1.  **Analyze Consumer Behavior (using Factor Analysis):**
    *   Consumer behavior involves many dimensions.
    *   Sometimes, these behaviors need to be grouped together to identify underlying patterns or characteristics.
    *   Factor analysis is used to combine multiple observed behaviors into fewer, underlying, meaningful characteristics.
    *   *Example:* Behaviors like buying only during sales, using coupons, or avoiding delivery charges might all relate to a single characteristic: price sensitivity. Another example is brand loyalty, observed through purchasing only major brands or being loyal even during competitor promotions.
    *   Factor analysis helps in creating these meaningful characteristics from observed behaviors. (We will discuss factor analysis in a different part of the course).

2.  **Cluster Analysis (based on behavior):**
    *   Based on the meaningful behavioral characteristics derived from Factor Analysis, customers are grouped into segments.
    *   The goal is to identify customers who exhibit similar behavioral patterns (are "close" to each other) and differentiate them from customers with different patterns (are "away" from each other).
    *   Classic methods for clustering include:
        *   Hierarchical Clustering (e.g., Ward's method)
        *   K-Mean Algorithm
        *   Model-based Algorithms

3.  **Defining the Segments (using Demographics):**
    *   Once behavioral segments are created (without using demographics so far), we need to name and profile these segments.
    *   This step involves using demographic information (age, gender, income, location, etc.) to describe the characteristics of the customers within each segment.
    *   The purpose is to predict which segment a new customer belongs to based on their easily observable demographics.
    *   Methods used for predicting segment membership based on demographics include:
        *   Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA)
        *   Multinomial Regression (or Binomial Logistic Regression if only two segments)
    *   Here, demographics act as independent variables (`x`) and the segment membership (a categorical variable) is the dependent variable (`y`).
    *   This profiling helps in understanding "who" the customers in each segment are, allowing for targeted marketing efforts.

4.  **Targeting:**
    *   Once segments are defined and profiled, marketers choose which segments to target based on attractiveness, profitability, and strategic fit.

5.  **Positioning:**
    *   Develop and communicate a unique value proposition for the targeted segments.

Today, we will focus primarily on the clustering part (Step 2) and touch upon defining segments (Step 3) using multinomial regression. We will skip the factor analysis part for now and assume we have the behavioral data points ready for clustering.

## Cluster Analysis Methods

We will focus on three main clustering methods: Hierarchical Clustering, Ward's method (a type of Hierarchical Clustering), and K-Means.

### Hierarchical Clustering

Hierarchical clustering builds a hierarchy of clusters. It can be done in two ways:

*   **Agglomerative Clustering:** Starts with each data point as a separate cluster and progressively merges the closest clusters until only one cluster remains. (Starts from the bottom and goes up).
*   **Divisive Clustering:** Starts with all data points in one cluster and recursively splits the cluster until each data point is in its own cluster. (Starts from the top and goes down).

Both methods create an hierarchy.

#### Calculating Distance

To merge or split clusters, we need to calculate the distance between data points or clusters. A common measure is **Euclidean Distance**.

Let's say we have `k` characteristics for each person. For person `i`, the characteristics are `x1i, x2i, ..., xki`. For person `j`, the characteristics are `x1j, x2j, ..., xkj`.

The Euclidean distance between person `i` and person `j` is calculated as:

```
Distance(i, j) = sqrt( Σ (xmi - xmj)^2 ) for m = 1 to k
```

*   *Example:* If person A has scores (5, 7) on two characteristics (Brand Awareness, Price Sensitivity) and person B has scores (3, 4), the Euclidean distance is `sqrt((5-3)^2 + (7-4)^2) = sqrt(2^2 + 3^2) = sqrt(4 + 9) = sqrt(13)`.

We calculate the pairwise distances between all data points. This can be represented in a distance matrix. The diagonal elements are zero (distance of a point from itself), and the matrix is symmetric.

In agglomerative hierarchical clustering, we start by merging the two closest points (those with the minimum distance in the matrix). Then, we need a method to calculate the distance between the newly formed cluster (e.g., AB) and other points or clusters (C, D, E, etc.). Different linkage methods determine how this distance is calculated:

*   Single Linkage (minimum distance between points in the clusters)
*   Complete Linkage (maximum distance between points in the clusters)
*   Average Linkage (average distance between all pairs of points across the clusters)
*   Centroid Linkage (distance between the centroids of the clusters)
*   Ward's Method (minimizes variance - discussed below)

#### Determining the Optimal Number of Clusters

Hierarchical clustering produces a dendrogram (a tree-like diagram) showing the merges or splits. To decide where to stop (how many segments to have), we look for a significant "jump" or "kink" in the distance at which clusters are merged.

This is often visualized with a "Scree Plot" or "Elbow Plot," where the y-axis shows the distance (or within sum of squares for Ward's method) at which merges/splits occur, and the x-axis shows the number of clusters. An "elbow" point suggests a good number of clusters, as further merging/splitting beyond this point provides diminishing returns in terms of distance reduction or variance explanation.

*   *Example:* If distances covered when going from 7 to 6 clusters is small, from 6 to 5 is small, but from 5 to 4 there is a large jump, it might suggest 5 segments is a good choice.

### Ward's Method

Ward's method is a specific type of agglomerative hierarchical clustering. Instead of directly using distance, it focuses on minimizing the total within-cluster variance.

*   It starts with each point in its own cluster.
*   At each step, it merges the pair of clusters that results in the minimum increase in the total within-cluster variance (or sum of squared errors - ESS).
*   It essentially tries to maximize the `r-squared` value, where `r-squared = (TSS - ESS) / TSS`.
    *   `TSS` (Total Sum of Squares): Variance when all points are in one cluster.
    *   `ESS` (Error Sum of Squares): Sum of squared distances of points from their respective cluster centroids.
    *   `TSS - ESS`: The variance explained by the clustering.
    *   Maximizing `r-squared` means maximizing the variance explained by the segments.
*   Ward's method is primarily for quantitative variables and tends to produce clusters of roughly equal size if there are no outliers.
*   Like other hierarchical methods, a Scree plot (using within sum of squares on the y-axis) helps determine the optimal number of clusters.

### K-Means Method

K-Means is an iterative clustering algorithm that partitions data into a pre-defined number of `K` clusters.

*   **Step 1:** Decide on the number of clusters, `K`. This is often informed by hierarchical methods like Ward's or the Scree plot.
*   **Step 2:** Randomly select `K` points from the data as initial cluster centroids (or means).
*   **Step 3:** Assign each data point to the cluster whose centroid is the closest (e.g., using Euclidean distance).
*   **Step 4:** Recalculate the centroid (mean) for each cluster based on the data points assigned to it.
*   **Step 5:** Repeat steps 3 and 4 until the cluster assignments no longer change or the change in centroids is below a certain threshold (i.e., the clusters become stable).

K-Means can sometimes provide a better refinement of clusters compared to hierarchical methods once the number of clusters is decided.

## Segment Stability

A good segmentation solution should ideally be relatively stable across different clustering methods (Hierarchical, Ward's, K-Means) and different starting points (especially for K-Means, which is sensitive to initial centroid placement).

## Next Steps

In the next video, we will perform the coding part using a dataset in R. We will apply these three clustering methods (Hierarchical, Ward's, K-Means) to create segments. Subsequently, we will try to define and target these segments using methods like Multinomial Regression.

Thank you very much. I will see you in the next video.
