# Marketing Analytics: Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (Contd.)
## Lecture 16: Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (Contd.)

This is Lecture 16 of the Marketing Analytics course, part of Week 3, Session 4. This lecture continues the discussion on Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (STP), focusing on applying clustering methods and predictive modeling in R. The instructor is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur.

### Introduction

We have previously discussed how to perform segmentation, targeting, and positioning using clustering methods conceptually. In this session, we will work through a practical problem using a provided dataset.

### Data Description: `customer.csv`

The dataset used is `customer.csv`. It contains data about customers, likely collected through a loyalty card program. The variables are:

*   **Serial Number:** Unique identifier for each customer.
*   **Age:** Age of the customer.
*   **Male:** Dummy variable (1 = Male, 0 = Female).
*   **Income:** Income of the customer.
*   **Distance:** Distance of the retail store from the customer's address (obtained using tools like Google Maps).
*   **Shopping Expenditure (Exp):** Monthly shopping expenditure (in lakhs or thousands).
*   **Number of Visits (Nov):** Number of visits to the store in a given period (e.g., per month).
*   **Percentage Grocery (Pgro):** Percentage of total purchase in grocery.
*   **Percentage Food and Beverage (PFNB):** Percentage of total purchase in food and beverage.
*   **Percentage FMCG (PFMCG):** Percentage of total purchase in FMCG (Fast-Moving Consumer Goods).
*   **Percentage Apparel (Papparel):** Percentage of total purchase in apparel.

Variables like Age, Male, Income, and Distance are **demographic** data. Variables like Exp, Nov, Pgro, PFNB, PFMCG, and Papparel are **behavioral** data. We will primarily use the behavioral data for segmentation first.

#### Data Loading and Initial Check

The first step is to load the data into R and check its structure.

```r
# Set working directory to source file location (assuming the script is in the same folder as the data)
# setwd("path/to/your/files") # Uncomment and set your path if needed

# Load the data
data <- read.csv("customer.csv")

# Check the structure of the data
str(data)
```

The `str(data)` output shows 537 observations and 11 variables, all appearing as integer or numeric, which is suitable for analysis without immediate transformation.

#### Creating a Subset for Behavioral Variables

We create a subset of the data containing only the behavioral variables for clustering.

```r
# Create subset with behavioral variables (columns 6 to 11)
data_subset <- data[, 6:11]

# Check the dimensions of the subset
dim(data_subset)
```

This results in a data frame with 537 observations of 6 variables.

### Segmentation using Clustering

We will use clustering techniques to group customers based on their behavior.

#### Hierarchical Clustering

Hierarchical clustering helps visualize potential clusters using a dendrogram.

1.  **Calculate Distance Matrix:** Compute the distance between every pair of customers using the behavioral data. Euclidean distance is a common method.

    ```r
    # Calculate the distance matrix
    d <- dist(data_subset, method = "euclidean")

    # You can check the size, but printing is not recommended for large datasets
    # dim(as.matrix(d))
    ```

2.  **Apply Hierarchical Clustering:** Use the `hclust` function with the distance matrix. The 'complete' linkage method is used in the code shown (though 'ward' is mentioned initially as an option).

    ```r
    # Perform hierarchical clustering
    hfit <- hclust(d, method = "complete")
    ```

3.  **Plot the Dendrogram:** Visualize the hierarchical structure.

    ```r
    # Plot the dendrogram
    plot(hfit)
    ```

    The dendrogram will show how observations are grouped. For a large dataset (537 observations), the bottom part is very dense. We look for large gaps or branches at higher levels to identify potential clusters. The instructor visually identifies potential breaks suggesting 2, then 3, and finally 4 segments based on prominent branches.

4.  **Cutting the Tree (Choosing Number of Clusters Visually):** Based on the dendrogram, we can visually decide how many clusters to cut the tree into. Cutting into 3 or 4 segments seems reasonable based on the branches.

    ```r
    # Cut the tree into 3 segments
    # groups_3 <- cutree(hfit, k=3)
    # rect.hclust(hfit, k=3, border="red")

    # Cut the tree into 4 segments (visually chosen)
    groups_4 <- cutree(hfit, k=4)
    rect.hclust(hfit, k=4, border="red")
    ```

    The `rect.hclust` function draws rectangles around the chosen clusters on the dendrogram, making them visually distinct. Based on this, we decide to explore 4 clusters further.

#### Determining the Optimal Number of Clusters (Elbow Method)

While visual inspection of the dendrogram is helpful, a more quantitative method like the Elbow Method using Within-Sum-of-Squares (WSS) is often used, especially for K-Means.

1.  **Calculate WSS for different numbers of clusters:**
    *   **WSS (Within-Sum-of-Squares):** This metric measures the compactness of clusters. For each cluster, it's the sum of squared distances of each point from the cluster's centroid. The total WSS is the sum of WSS for all clusters. Lower WSS means more compact clusters.
    *   **Formula for WSS for k=1 (No Clusters):** When there's only one cluster (all data points), the centroid is the overall mean of the dataset. The total WSS in this case is the sum of squared distances of each point from the overall mean, summed across all variables. This is related to the total variance of the data.
        *   For a single variable `j`, the variance is $Var_j = \frac{1}{n-1} \sum_{i=1}^n (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j)^2$.
        *   The sum of squared distances from the mean for variable `j` is $\sum_{i=1}^n (x_{ij} - \bar{x}_j)^2 = (n-1) \times Var_j$.
        *   The total WSS for k=1 is the sum of these across all `k` variables: $\sum_{m=1}^k (n-1) \times Var_m$.

    ```r
    # Calculate WSS for k=1 (no clusters)
    # Sum of (n-1) * variance for each column
    wss <- (nrow(data_subset)-1) * sum(apply(data_subset, 2, var))

    # Loop through 2 to 15 clusters, running K-Means and storing total WSS
    for (i in 2:15) wss[i] <- sum(kmeans(data_subset, centers=i)$withinss)

    # Check the calculated WSS values for k=1 to 15
    # wss
    ```

2.  **Plot the Elbow Curve:** Plot WSS values against the number of clusters (`k`).

    ```r
    # Plot WSS vs. number of clusters
    plot(1:15, wss, type="b", xlab="Number of Clusters", ylab="Within groups sum of squares")
    ```

    The plot shows WSS decreasing as the number of clusters increases. The "elbow" is the point where the rate of decrease slows down significantly. Visually, the elbow appears around k=4 or k=5. Based on this and the dendrogram, we decide to use 4 clusters.

#### K-Means Clustering (Applying the chosen number of clusters)

Now we run K-Means clustering with the chosen number of segments (k=4) to get the cluster assignments for each observation.

```r
# Perform K-Means clustering with 4 centers
k1 <- kmeans(data_subset, centers=4)

# The cluster assignment for each observation is stored in k1$cluster
# head(k1$cluster)
```

### Segment Identification and Interpretation

We need to understand the characteristics of each of the 4 clusters. This is done by calculating the mean of each variable (both behavioral and demographic) for the observations belonging to each cluster.

1.  **Add Cluster Assignment to Original Data:** Add the cluster membership derived from K-Means (`k1$cluster`) back to the original data frame.

    ```r
    # Add cluster assignments to the original data frame
    mydata1 <- data
    mydata1$fit.cluster <- k1$cluster
    ```

2.  **Aggregate Data by Cluster:** Calculate the means of all variables, grouped by the assigned cluster ID.

    ```r
    # Aggregate data by cluster to find mean characteristics for each segment
    aggregate(mydata1, by=list(mydata1$fit.cluster), FUN=mean)
    ```

    The output is a table showing the average values for each variable within each cluster. We analyze this table to describe the segments.

    Let's interpret the segments based on the aggregate means (the exact numbers will depend on the K-Means run, but the relative differences should be similar):

    *   **Segment 1:** Very high Shopping Expenditure (e.g., 49). Low Number of Visits (e.g., 2). High percentage spent on Grocery and Apparel. Higher Age (e.g., 45). Predominantly Male (e.g., 0.875). Slightly higher Income. **Interpretation:** These are likely **B2B customers** (resellers, small stores) who visit infrequently but buy in bulk, focusing on core goods (grocery) and possibly other high-value items (apparel) to resell.
    *   **Segment 2:** Moderate Shopping Expenditure (e.g., 6.6). Average Number of Visits (e.g., 5). Significant expenditure on FNB and FMCG. Younger Age (e.g., 33). More Male than Female. Average Income. Average Distance. **Interpretation:** These could be regular **FMCG/FNB focused buyers** who visit weekly.
    *   **Segment 3:** Moderate Shopping Expenditure (e.g., 6.8). Average Number of Visits (e.g., 5). Highest percentage spent on Apparel (e.g., 35). Youngest Age (e.g., 32). Slightly more Female than Male. Average Income. Shortest Distance (e.g., 3.78 km). **Interpretation:** These are likely **Apparel buyers**, possibly younger females living closer to the store, making weekly visits.
    *   **Segment 4:** Higher Moderate Shopping Expenditure (e.g., 10). Average Number of Visits (e.g., 5). Highest percentage spent on FMCG (e.g., 40), also significant Grocery. Older Age than 2 & 3 (e.g., 36). Slightly more Male than Female. Higher Income than 2 & 3. Slightly longer Distance than 2 & 3. **Interpretation:** These might be **FMCG/Grocery focused family buyers**, with higher income, potentially traveling slightly further, and making weekly larger purchases for household needs.

    These interpretations help marketers understand *who* these customer groups are and *what* their needs and behaviors are.

### Targeting: Predicting Segment Membership

Once segments are identified, the next step in STP is targeting. We need a way to assign new customers to a segment when they register (using their demographic information), even before they start exhibiting purchase behavior. We can use demographic variables (Age, Male, Income, Distance) to predict which segment a new customer is most likely to belong to. Since the segment is a categorical variable with more than two levels (1, 2, 3, 4), we use Multinomial Logistic Regression.

1.  **Check Segment Sizes:** It's useful to see the proportion of customers in each segment.

    ```r
    # Calculate the percentage of customers in each segment
    table(mydata1$fit.cluster) / nrow(mydata1) * 100
    ```

    Segment 1 (B2B) is small (~3%), while the others are larger (~30%, ~25%, ~40%). This confirms the B2B interpretation for segment 1.

2.  **Prepare Data for Regression:** The cluster assignment needs to be treated as a categorical variable (factor) for multinomial regression.

    ```r
    # Convert cluster assignment to a factor
    mydata1$fit.cluster <- as.factor(mydata1$fit.cluster)
    ```

3.  **Run Multinomial Logistic Regression:** Use the `multinom` function from the `nnet` library. The formula specifies `fit.cluster` as the dependent variable and demographic variables as independent variables.

    ```r
    # Load the nnet library for multinomial regression
    library(nnet)

    # Run the multinomial logistic regression model
    # The formula predicts fit.cluster based on demographic variables
    model_multinom <- multinom(fit.cluster ~ age + male + income + distance, data = mydata1)

    # View the model summary
    summary(model_multinom)
    ```

    The summary output shows coefficients, standard errors, and Z-values for predicting segments 2, 3, and 4 relative to the base segment (Segment 1, which is set as the reference category by default).

    *   **Interpretation of Summary:**
        *   The coefficients show the change in the log-odds of being in a specific segment (relative to segment 1) for a one-unit increase in the independent variable.
        *   The Z-values (coefficient / standard error) and corresponding P-values indicate the significance of each variable in predicting segment membership. Z-values with absolute magnitude greater than 1.96 are typically considered significant at the 5% level.
        *   Looking at the p-values (not shown in the raw `summary` output, but can be calculated or inferred from Z-values), the instructor notes that `distance` is not significant for predicting any segment relative to segment 1. Some other coefficients might also be non-significant.

    *   *(Instructor mentions dropping non-significant variables like distance and rerunning, but doesn't show the new summary in the video. The code below shows how you would rerun the model without distance.)*

    ```r
    # (Optional) Rerun model without non-significant variables, e.g., distance
    # model_multinom_reduced <- multinom(fit.cluster ~ age + male + income, data = mydata1)
    # summary(model_multinom_reduced)
    ```

4.  **Using the Model for Prediction:** For a new customer with known demographic variables, we can use the model to predict their probability of belonging to each segment.

    *   The model calculates a score (often called a "linear predictor" or "logit") for each segment `j` (relative to the base segment 1):
        `Score_j = Intercept_j + Coeff_Age_j * Age + Coeff_Male_j * Male + Coeff_Income_j * Income + ...`
    *   The score for the base segment (Segment 1) is `Score_1 = 0`.
    *   The probability of belonging to segment `j` is calculated using the softmax function:
        `P(Segment = j) = exp(Score_j) / (exp(Score_1) + exp(Score_2) + exp(Score_3) + exp(Score_4))`
        Since `Score_1 = 0`, this simplifies to:
        `P(Segment = j) = exp(Score_j) / (1 + exp(Score_2) + exp(Score_3) + exp(Score_4))`
    *   A new customer is assigned to the segment with the highest predicted probability.

    ```r
    # Example prediction calculation for a new customer (Illustrative based on coefficients)
    # Let's say a new customer is 30 years old, Male (1), Income 6 lakhs
    # Scores relative to Segment 1 (based on the full model with distance):
    # Score_2 = Intercept_2 + Coeff_Age_2*30 + Coeff_Male_2*1 + Coeff_Income_2*6 + Coeff_Distance_2*AvgDistance
    # Score_3 = Intercept_3 + Coeff_Age_3*30 + Coeff_Male_3*1 + Coeff_Income_3*6 + Coeff_Distance_3*AvgDistance
    # Score_4 = Intercept_4 + Coeff_Age_4*30 + Coeff_Male_4*1 + Coeff_Income_4*6 + Coeff_Distance_4*AvgDistance
    # Score_1 = 0

    # Probabilities:
    # P(Seg=1) = exp(0) / (exp(0) + exp(Score_2) + exp(Score_3) + exp(Score_4))
    # P(Seg=2) = exp(Score_2) / (exp(0) + exp(Score_2) + exp(Score_3) + exp(Score_4))
    # etc.
    ```

### Model Evaluation (Predictive Performance)

How well does this model predict segment membership? We can evaluate its accuracy using a train-test split.

1.  **Split Data into Training and Testing Sets:** Divide the data into a set used to train the model and a set used to evaluate its performance on unseen data.

    ```r
    # Ensure factor levels are consistent if splitting
    # mydata1$fit.cluster <- factor(mydata1$fit.cluster)

    # Create a data partition (e.g., 70% train, 30% test) - package 'caret' is useful but not shown here
    # A simple split by row index for demonstration
    set.seed(123) # for reproducibility
    train_indices <- sample(1:nrow(mydata1), size = floor(0.7 * nrow(mydata1)))
    train_data <- mydata1[train_indices, ]
    test_data <- mydata1[-train_indices, ]

    # Check dimensions
    # dim(train_data)
    # dim(test_data)
    ```

2.  **Train Model on Training Data:**

    ```r
    # Train the multinomial model on the training data
    model_trained <- multinom(fit.cluster ~ age + male + income + distance, data = train_data)
    ```

3.  **Predict on Testing Data:** Use the trained model to predict segment membership for the testing data.

    ```r
    # Predict cluster membership on the testing data
    predictions <- predict(model_trained, newdata = test_data, type = "class") # or type = "prob"
    ```

4.  **Create Confusion Matrix:** Compare the predicted segments to the actual segments in the testing data.

    ```r
    # Create a confusion matrix
    conf_matrix <- table(Actual = test_data$fit.cluster, Predicted = predictions)
    conf_matrix
    ```

    The confusion matrix shows how many observations from each actual segment were predicted into each segment. The diagonal elements are correct predictions.

5.  **Calculate Accuracy:** The overall accuracy is the sum of the correctly predicted observations (diagonal elements) divided by the total number of observations in the test set.

    ```r
    # Calculate accuracy
    accuracy <- sum(diag(conf_matrix)) / sum(conf_matrix)
    accuracy
    ```

    Based on the example matrix shown by the instructor (50+53+17 on the diagonal out of ~150 total test points), the accuracy is calculated as (50+53+17)/150 which is roughly 120/150 or 0.8. *Correction:* Looking closer at the matrix shown in the transcript, the diagonal seems to be around 50, 53, 17 (for segments 1, 2, 3, but segment 4 is missed in that reading). Let's re-read the transcript's diagonal: 50 (Seg1 predicted as 1), 53 (Seg2 as 2), 17 (Seg3 as 3). The fourth diagonal element (Seg4 as 4) is low, e.g., 0 or small. Total on the diagonal is 50+53+17 = 120. Total observations is ~150. Accuracy = 120/150 = 0.8. *Self-correction again:* The instructor says "50 plus 353 and another 17", which is clearly a misreading of the matrix shown (which has numbers like 50, 53, 17, 0 or similar on the diagonal). They then say "around 70... so 53 plus 17, 70 out of how many? Out of 150". This implies they might be *only* considering predicting segments 2 and 3 correctly out of the test set (maybe focusing on B2C segments). Their conclusion is "less than 50 percent which is bad". Let's trust the instructor's final verbal assessment of "less than 50 percent". This indicates that predicting segment membership based *only* on these basic demographics is not very accurate.

    *   **Interpretation of Accuracy:** A low accuracy (e.g., < 50%) means the demographic variables used are not strong predictors of behavioral segment membership. This suggests a need to include more relevant demographic variables or use other methods for prediction if behavioral data isn't available initially.

### Conclusion

We have demonstrated how to perform customer segmentation using hierarchical and K-Means clustering based on behavioral data. We then identified the characteristics of each segment by aggregating the data. Finally, we used multinomial logistic regression with demographic data to predict segment membership, which is crucial for targeting new customers. The accuracy of this predictive model using only basic demographics was low, highlighting the importance of selecting relevant variables for targeting or acknowledging the limitations of predicting behavior from simple demographics.

We can also explore other classification methods like Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) for predicting segment membership.

Thank you for being with me. We will discuss LDA briefly and then move on to the next module in the upcoming videos.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 17 : segmentation targeting and positioning (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 17: Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (Contd.)

This lecture continues the discussion on Segmentation, Targeting, and Positioning (STP), focusing on how to use consumer choice and behavioral data to segment customers based on their underlying preferences. The lecture demonstrates a method combining regression (or conjoint analysis) with clustering techniques in R.

## Recap: Segmentation Based on Behavior

In previous lectures, we discussed segmenting customers based on their visible behavior. This involves collecting data about customer actions (like purchases, website visits, etc.), bringing together those with similar behaviors using clustering techniques (like hierarchical or K-means clustering), and separating those with different behaviors into different segments.

## Inferring Preferences from Behavior (Choice Data)

Sometimes, direct behavioral data beyond just choices is limited. However, the choices a consumer makes can reveal their underlying preferences.

For example, if a consumer consistently chooses Brand A over Brand B, there's likely something specific about Brand A that they value highly compared to Brand B. By analyzing *what is different* between Brand A and Brand B (e.g., fragrance, color, performance quality) and observing choices across many different product variations, we can infer the weight or importance the consumer places on these different attributes. This is the core idea behind techniques like Conjoint Analysis.

Instead of directly asking consumers about their preferences on a scale (which can sometimes be unreliable or difficult to collect for large populations), we can infer these preferences from their observed choices or ratings of product profiles.

## Marrying Conjoint Analysis (Regression) and Clustering

This lecture demonstrates a powerful approach:
1.  **Infer Preferences:** Use a technique like regression (similar to the underlying method in Conjoint Analysis) for *each individual consumer* to estimate their personal preferences or 'part-worths' for different product attributes and their levels.
2.  **Segment Based on Preferences:** Cluster consumers based on these *inferred preference coefficients*. Consumers with similar preference structures will group together.

This approach combines two techniques sequentially to solve the segmentation problem when direct preference data isn't available but choice/rating data is.

## R Demonstration: Segmentation based on Inferred Preferences

We will use a dataset previously used for conjoint analysis, which contains ratings for different car profiles based on attributes like fuel type, capacity, and price.

First, we load the data and prepare it.

```R
# Set working directory (replace with your path if necessary)
# setwd("path/to/your/directory") 

# Read the data
data <- read.csv("your_data_file.csv") # Replace with your actual file name

# Check structure
str(data)
```

The `str(data)` output shows that 'Fuel', 'Capacity', and 'Price' are treated as integers. However, these represent categories (e.g., Fuel 1, 2, 3 for Diesel, Petrol, CNG), not numerical values. We need to convert them to factor variables for regression.

```R
# Convert categorical variables to factors
for (i in 2:4) { # Assuming columns 2, 3, and 4 are Fuel, Capacity, Price
  data[, i] <- as.factor(data[, i])
}

# Check structure again
str(data) 
```

Now, 'Fuel', 'Capacity', and 'Price' should appear as factors.

Next, we need to run a separate linear regression for *each customer*. In this dataset, each customer provided ratings for 9 different car profiles. The 'SerialNo' column acts as the customer ID. There are 60 customers in total.

The regression model for each customer will be:
`Rating ~ Fuel + Capacity + Price`

We need to store the preference coefficients (the regression coefficients) for each customer. Since Fuel, Capacity, and Price are factors with 3 levels each, R will automatically create dummy variables and drop one level for each factor to avoid multicollinearity. By default, R drops the first level alphabetically (Fuel 1, Capacity 1, Price 1). So, the model will estimate coefficients for:
*   Fuel 2 (vs Fuel 1)
*   Fuel 3 (vs Fuel 1)
*   Capacity 2 (vs Capacity 1)
*   Capacity 3 (vs Capacity 1)
*   Price 2 (vs Price 1)
*   Price 3 (vs Price 1)

We will create a matrix to store these 6 coefficients for each of the 60 customers. We will also implement a step to set coefficients to 0 if they are not statistically significant (e.g., P-value > 0.05) for that individual customer.

```R
# Create an empty matrix to store coefficients (60 customers, 6 coefficients)
matrix1 <- matrix(0, nrow = 60, ncol = 6)

# Assign column names based on the dummy variables expected from regression
colnames(matrix1) <- c("Fuel2", "Fuel3", "Capacity2", "Capacity3", "Price2", "Price3")

# Loop through each customer (SerialNo 1 to 60)
for (i in 1:60) {
  # Define the rows corresponding to the current customer's data
  start_row <- (i - 1) * 9 + 1
  end_row <- i * 9

  # Subset the data for the current customer
  customer_data <- data[start_row:end_row, ]

  # Run the linear regression model for the customer
  fit1 <- lm(Rating ~ Fuel + Capacity + Price, data = customer_data)

  # Extract summary of coefficients
  coef_summary <- summary(fit1)$coefficients

  # Extract coefficients for the dummy variables (excluding intercept)
  # Check which ones are significant (P-value < 0.05)
  # Set non-significant coefficients to 0
  
  # Coefficients are in column 1, P-values are in column 4 of the summary table
  # We need coefficients for rows 2 to 7 (Intercept + 6 dummy variables)
  
  significant_coefs <- ifelse(coef_summary[2:7, 4] < 0.05, coef_summary[2:7, 1], 0)
  
  # Store the significant coefficients in the matrix
  matrix1[i, ] <- significant_coefs
}

# Display the resulting matrix of coefficients
head(matrix1)
```

The `matrix1` now contains the inferred, statistically significant preferences (coefficients) for each customer towards the different attribute levels (relative to their respective base levels: Fuel 1, Capacity 1, Price 1). A 0 in the matrix means that attribute level was not found to be significantly preferred (or disliked) relative to the base for that customer.

Now, we can use this matrix of preferences (`matrix1`) for clustering. We convert it to a data frame first.

```R
# Convert the matrix to a data frame for clustering
DF <- as.data.frame(matrix1)
```

We use the elbow method (scree plot) to determine a reasonable number of clusters (k).

```R
# Determine optimal number of clusters using WSS (Within-cluster sum of squares)
wss <- (nrow(DF) - 1) * sum(apply(DF, 2, var))
for (i in 2:15) {
  wss[i] <- sum(kmeans(DF, centers = i)$withinss)
}

# Plot WSS to find the elbow point
plot(1:15, wss, type = "b", xlab = "Number of Clusters", ylab = "Within groups sum of squares", main = "Scree Plot for K-Means Clustering")
```

The plot shows a kink at k=2, suggesting that 2 clusters might be a good starting point. Let's try clustering with k=2 and k=3.

```R
# Perform K-means clustering with k=2
kmeans_result_2 <- kmeans(DF, centers = 2, nstart = 25) # nstart runs the algorithm multiple times with different centroids

# View cluster centers (average preferences for each segment)
kmeans_result_2$centers

# View cluster sizes
kmeans_result_2$size
```

Interpretation for k=2 (from `kmeans_result_2$centers`):

*   **Cluster 1:** Lower (close to 0) coefficients for Fuel 2, Fuel 3, Capacity 2, Capacity 3, Price 2, Price 3. This suggests this segment is *not very sensitive* to the tested variations in fuel type (vs Fuel 1), capacity (vs Capacity 1), or price (vs Price 1). Their preferences are not strongly driven by these attribute differences compared to the base levels.
*   **Cluster 2:** Higher (more negative/positive) coefficients. E.g., a significantly negative coefficient for Fuel 3 (CNG vs Diesel), significantly negative coefficients for lower capacities (Capacity 2, Capacity 3 vs Capacity 1), and significantly negative coefficients for higher prices (Price 2, Price 3 vs Price 1). This suggests this segment is *highly sensitive* to these attributes, strongly disliking CNG compared to Diesel, smaller capacities compared to 8-seater, and higher prices compared to the base price.

Let's try with k=3 clusters:

```R
# Perform K-means clustering with k=3
kmeans_result_3 <- kmeans(DF, centers = 3, nstart = 25)

# View cluster centers (average preferences for each segment)
kmeans_result_3$centers

# View cluster sizes
kmeans_result_3$size
```

Interpretation for k=3 (from `kmeans_result_3$centers`):

*   **Cluster 1:** Coefficients near 0 for fuel types. Strongly negative coefficients for Capacity 2 and Capacity 3. Strongly negative coefficients for Price 2 and Price 3. This segment is *not sensitive to fuel type* but *highly sensitive to capacity and price*, strongly preferring larger capacities and lower prices.
*   **Cluster 2:** Significant coefficients across most attributes (Fuel 3 negative, Capacity 2 & 3 negative, Price 2 & 3 negative). This segment is *sensitive to everything*, disliking CNG, smaller capacities, and higher prices compared to the base options. (Similar to Cluster 2 in the k=2 solution).
*   **Cluster 3:** Moderate negative coefficient for Fuel 3. Coefficients near 0 for capacities and prices. This segment is primarily *sensitive to fuel type* (specifically dislikes CNG compared to Diesel) but not strongly sensitive to capacity or price variations.

By analyzing the cluster centers, we can describe the distinct preference profiles of each customer segment.

## Targeting and Positioning Based on Preference Segments

Once segments are identified based on preferences, the next steps of STP can be applied:

1.  **Targeting:** Choose which segments to focus on based on their size, potential profitability, and alignment with the company's capabilities.
2.  **Positioning:** Develop a marketing mix strategy (Product, Price, Place, Promotion) tailored to the specific preferences and needs of the target segment(s). For instance, for a segment that is highly price-sensitive and prefers larger capacity, one might position a basic, 8-seater model competitively on price. For a segment sensitive to fuel type but less so to price, one might highlight the benefits of diesel over CNG, perhaps even for a slightly higher-priced model.

**Further Analysis (Optional):** To understand *who* belongs to which segment, one could add demographic data (age, income, location, etc.) to the customer data frame and use techniques like multinomial logistic regression or Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA) to predict segment membership based on demographics. This helps in identifying and reaching the target segments more effectively.

## Conclusion

This lecture demonstrated how marketing analytics techniques can be combined sequentially to solve complex problems. We married regression analysis (used here to infer individual preferences from choice data, similar to Conjoint Analysis) with cluster analysis to segment customers based on these hidden preferences. This provides a powerful approach for STP when direct, observable behavioral data for segmentation is limited, but choice or rating data is available.
'''

Lecture Name: Lecture 18 : demand forecasting and pricing

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 18: Demand Forecasting and Pricing

**Course:** Marketing Analytics
**Instructor:** Professor Swagato Chatterjee, Vinod Gupta School of Management, Indian Institute of Technology, Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Week 4, Module 4, and we will be focusing on **Demand Forecasting and Pricing**.

In the previous modules, we discussed understanding customer needs and segmenting customers. Now, we move towards understanding how much demand we can expect for our offering, given what customers want. This involves **Demand Forecasting** or **Sales Forecasting**.

Another major factor influencing sales is **Pricing**. This module will cover the basics of pricing and demand forecasting. In the next module (Module 5), we will delve into more advanced pricing strategies.

This first session will primarily focus on pricing, assuming we already know the demand function and how price impacts demand. We will explore how to find the optimal price to maximize demand or, more importantly, maximize profit.

We will be using Excel for demonstrations.

## Pricing with a Linear Demand Function

We know from basic economics that demand is often a function of price. A simple linear relationship can be represented as:

**Demand = a - b * p**

where:
*   `p` is the price
*   `a` and `b` are positive constants. This means as price increases, demand decreases.

If we assume the unit cost is 0, the profit is simply:

**Profit = Price * Demand**
**Profit = p * (a - b * p)**

To find the price that maximizes profit, we would typically take the derivative of the profit function with respect to price (`del Profit / del p`), set it to 0, and solve for `p`. To confirm it's a maximum, the second derivative (`del^2 Profit / del p^2`) must be negative.

However, real-world demand functions might not always be simple linear forms, making analytical solutions difficult. We can use analytical tools like Excel Solver to find the optimal pricing numerically.

### Example: Linear Demand (Unit Cost = 0)

Let's demonstrate this in Excel. Suppose we have historical data points relating price and demand.

| Price | Demand |
| :---- | :----- |
| 100   | 1000   |
| 110   | 900    |

We can plot these points on an X-Y scatter plot in Excel and add a linear trend line. The trend line equation will give us the relationship between demand and price.

*   **Steps in Excel:**
    1.  Enter Price and Demand data.
    2.  Insert an X-Y Scatter plot.
    3.  Right-click on the data points and select "Add Trendline...".
    4.  Choose "Linear".
    5.  Check "Display Equation on chart".

For the example data, the equation might look something like:
Demand = -10 * Price + 2000

Let's assume a target price, say 50.
*   **Price Cell:** Enter a hypothetical price (e.g., 50).
*   **Demand Cell:** Calculate demand using the trend line equation: `= 2000 - 10 * [Price Cell Reference]`
*   **Unit Cost:** Assume 0.
*   **Profit Cell:** Calculate Profit (Revenue in this case, since cost is 0): `= [Demand Cell Reference] * [Price Cell Reference]`

Now, we use Excel Solver to maximize the Profit cell by changing the Price cell.

*   **Steps in Excel Solver:**
    1.  Go to `Data` -> `Solver`. If Solver is not visible, go to `File` -> `Options` -> `Add-ins` -> `Excel Add-ins` -> `Go...` and check `Solver Add-in`.
    2.  **Set Objective:** Select the Profit cell.
    3.  **To:** Choose `Max`.
    4.  **By Changing Variable Cells:** Select the Price cell.
    5.  **Constraints:** You might add constraints like Price >= 0. For now, check "Make Unconstrained Variables Non-Negative".
    6.  **Solving Method:** Select `GRG Nonlinear` (suitable for non-linear problems, but works for linear too).
    7.  Click `Solve`.

Solver will find the optimal price that maximizes profit. In this linear example with zero unit cost, the optimal price should be 100, leading to a demand of 1000 and a profit of 100,000.

## Pricing with a Non-Linear Demand Function

Not all demand functions are linear. Sometimes, a polynomial or other non-linear function better represents the relationship between price and demand.

### Example: Non-Linear Demand (Fixed Unit Cost)

Let's use different hypothetical data for price and demand:

| Price | Demand |
| :---- | :----- |
| 1.5   | 60     |
| 2.0   | 51     |
| 2.5   | 20     |

Assume a fixed unit cost, say 0.6.

*   **Steps in Excel:**
    1.  Enter Price and Demand data.
    2.  Insert an X-Y Scatter plot.
    3.  Add a trend line. Try different types (Linear, Logarithmic, Polynomial, etc.) and choose the one that best fits the data (e.g., Polynomial of degree 2 often works well).
    4.  Check "Display Equation on chart".

For the example data, a polynomial (degree 2) might give an equation like:
Demand = -44 * Price^2 + 136 * Price - 45

Let's assume a target price, say 2.2.
*   **Price Cell:** Enter a hypothetical price (e.g., 2.2).
*   **Demand Cell:** Calculate demand using the polynomial equation: `= -44 * [Price Cell]^2 + 136 * [Price Cell] - 45`
*   **Unit Cost Cell:** Enter the fixed unit cost (e.g., 0.6).
*   **Profit Cell:** Calculate Profit: `= [Demand Cell] * ([Price Cell] - [Unit Cost Cell])`

Now, use Excel Solver to maximize the Profit cell by changing the Price cell.

*   **Steps in Excel Solver:** (Same as before)
    1.  `Set Objective`: Profit cell.
    2.  `To`: `Max`.
    3.  `By Changing Variable Cells`: Price cell.
    4.  `Constraints`: Make non-negative.
    5.  `Solving Method`: `GRG Nonlinear`.
    6.  Click `Solve`.

Solver will find the optimal price for this non-linear demand scenario with a fixed unit cost. For this example, the optimal price might be around 1.97.

## Two-Part Pricing Strategy

Sometimes, the profit comes from a combination of products, often a "capital item" sold at a lower margin (or even a loss) and "consumables" sold at a high margin. Classic examples include razors and blades, or printers and ink cartridges. The goal is to maximize the *total* profit from the bundle.

### Example: Razors and Blades

Assume we are selling razors and blades. The demand for razors depends on the price of the razor. For each razor sold, a certain number of blades are sold, generating additional profit.

Historical data for razor price and demand:

| Razor Price | Razor Demand |
| :---------- | :----------- |
| 10          | 12           |
| 11          | 10           |
| 12          | 8            |

Assume:
*   Unit cost of a razor is 8.
*   For each razor sold, 15 blades are sold.
*   Profit per blade is 1.

*   **Steps in Excel:**
    1.  Enter Razor Price and Razor Demand data.
    2.  Insert an X-Y Scatter plot for Price vs Demand.
    3.  Add a linear trend line (or appropriate non-linear one).
    4.  Display Equation on chart.

For the example data, the linear equation might be:
Razor Demand = 32 - 2 * Razor Price

Let's assume a target razor price, say 8.
*   **Razor Price Cell:** Enter a hypothetical price (e.g., 8).
*   **Razor Demand Cell:** Calculate demand using the trend line equation: `= 32 - 2 * [Razor Price Cell]`
*   **Razor Unit Cost Cell:** Enter the unit cost (e.g., 8).
*   **Blades per Razor Cell:** Enter the number of blades sold per razor (e.g., 15).
*   **Profit per Blade Cell:** Enter the profit per blade (e.g., 1).

Now, calculate the total profit:
*   **Profit from Razors:** `= [Razor Demand Cell] * ([Razor Price Cell] - [Razor Unit Cost Cell])`
*   **Profit from Blades:** `= [Razor Demand Cell] * [Blades per Razor Cell] * [Profit per Blade Cell]`
*   **Total Profit Cell:** `= [Profit from Razors Cell] + [Profit from Blades Cell]`

Use Excel Solver to maximize the Total Profit cell by changing the Razor Price cell.

*   **Steps in Excel Solver:** (Same as before)
    1.  `Set Objective`: Total Profit cell.
    2.  `To`: `Max`.
    3.  `By Changing Variable Cells`: Razor Price cell.
    4.  `Constraints`: Make non-negative.
    5.  `Solving Method`: `GRG Nonlinear`.
    6.  Click `Solve`.

Solver might suggest an optimal razor price like 4.5. This means you might charge less than the unit cost for the razor (making a loss per razor) but make a significant overall profit due to the high margin and volume from the blades. This highlights that profit maximization sometimes involves strategic pricing across a product portfolio, not just on individual items.

## Pricing with Variable Unit Cost

In some cases, the unit cost is not fixed but depends on the production volume, often decreasing due to economies of scale as demand increases (especially in make-to-order scenarios).

### Example: Variable Unit Cost

Historical data for price, demand, and unit cost:

| Price | Demand | Unit Cost |
| :---- | :----- | :-------- |
| 1.0   | 40     | 0.6       |
| 1.3   | 38     | 0.9       |
| 1.5   | 35     | 1.1       |
| 1.7   | 25     | 1.4       |
| 1.9   | 18     | 1.6       |

Notice that as demand decreases, the unit cost increases.

*   **Steps in Excel:**
    1.  Enter Price, Demand, and Unit Cost data.
    2.  Determine the relationship between Price and Demand (as in previous examples, using X-Y scatter and trend line). Let's assume a polynomial works well.
        *   Equation for Demand = f(Price).
    3.  Determine the relationship between Demand and Unit Cost (using X-Y scatter and trend line for Demand vs Unit Cost). As demand increases, unit cost should decrease. Let's assume a polynomial works well here too.
        *   Equation for Unit Cost = g(Demand).

Let's assume a target price, say 1.5.
*   **Price Cell:** Enter a hypothetical price (e.g., 1.5).
*   **Demand Cell:** Calculate demand using the Demand = f(Price) equation, referencing the Price Cell. (e.g., `-31.033 * [Price Cell]^2 + 64.678 * [Price Cell] + 6.479`)
*   **Unit Cost Cell:** Calculate unit cost using the Unit Cost = g(Demand) equation, referencing the Demand Cell. (e.g., `-0.0018 * [Demand Cell]^2 + 0.0639 * [Demand Cell] + 1.0003`)
*   **Profit Cell:** Calculate Profit: `= [Demand Cell] * ([Price Cell] - [Unit Cost Cell])`

Use Excel Solver to maximize the Profit cell by changing the Price cell.

*   **Steps in Excel Solver:** (Same as before)
    1.  `Set Objective`: Profit cell.
    2.  `To`: `Max`.
    3.  `By Changing Variable Cells`: Price cell.
    4.  `Constraints`: Make non-negative.
    5.  `Solving Method`: `GRG Nonlinear`.
    6.  Click `Solve`.

Solver will find the optimal price (e.g., around 1.22) considering that the unit cost changes based on the demand generated by that price.

## Next Steps

We have covered various pricing scenarios assuming we know the demand function. The next videos in this module will shift focus to **Demand Forecasting**: how to determine the demand curve or forecast demand when you don't have a predefined function, especially with large datasets. We will look at methods beyond simple curve fitting in Excel. We will also cover basics of bundling in the next video.

Thank you for being with me in this session. I will see you in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 19: Demand Forecasting and Pricing (Contd.)

**Course:** Marketing Analytics
**Instructor:** Professor Swagato Chatterjee
**Affiliation:** Vinod Gupta School of Management, Indian Institute of Technology, Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome to Lecture 19 of the Marketing Analytics course, part of Module 4 which focuses on Demand Forecasting and Pricing. In this video, we will discuss a classic case of pricing called **bundle pricing**.

## Bundling Basics

Bundling is particularly applicable in situations where multiple products are available and customers' preferences generally have a **negative correlation**. This means that a customer who highly values one product might place a lower value on another product, and vice versa.

Let's look at some examples to understand this concept. We will use an Excel file structure as described in the transcript.

### Example 1: Two Customers and Two Products (CPU and Monitor)

Consider two customers, C1 and C2, and two products, a CPU and a Monitor. Their willingness to pay (WTP) for each product is as follows:

| Customer | CPU WTP | Monitor WTP |
| :------- | :------ | :---------- |
| C1       | 900     | 400         |
| C2       | 400     | 900         |

*(Note: The transcript initially mentions dollars, then rupees, then back to dollars. We will stick to dollars for consistency based on the latter part of the example.)*

Willingness to Pay (WTP) represents the maximum amount a customer is willing to pay for a product. If the price is below their WTP, they buy. If the price is equal to their WTP, they are indifferent. If the price is above their WTP, they do not buy.

**Scenario: Individual Pricing**

Let's consider pricing the CPU and Monitor separately.

*   **CPU Pricing:**
    *   If price = $10: Both C1 (WTP 900) and C2 (WTP 400) buy. Revenue = $10 * 2 = $20.
    *   If price = $400: Both C1 and C2 buy. Revenue = $400 * 2 = $800.
    *   If price = $401: Only C1 buys (WTP 900). C2 does not buy (WTP 400). Revenue = $401 * 1 = $401.
    *   If price = $900: Only C1 buys (WTP 900). C2 does not buy. Revenue = $900 * 1 = $900.
    *   If price > $900: Neither buys. Revenue = $0.

    The maximum revenue for the CPU is **$900**.

*   **Monitor Pricing:**
    *   Following a similar analysis, if price = $400: Both C1 (WTP 400) and C2 (WTP 900) buy. Revenue = $400 * 2 = $800.
    *   If price = $401: Only C2 buys (WTP 900). C1 does not buy (WTP 400). Revenue = $401 * 1 = $401.
    *   If price = $900: Only C2 buys. Revenue = $900 * 1 = $900.

    The maximum revenue for the Monitor is **$900**.

**Total Maximum Revenue with Individual Pricing:** $900 (CPU) + $900 (Monitor) = **$1800**.

**Scenario: Bundle Pricing**

Now, let's consider selling the CPU and Monitor together as a bundle. The willingness to pay for the bundle for each customer is the sum of their individual WTPs.

*   C1 Bundle WTP = 900 (CPU) + 400 (Monitor) = 1300
*   C2 Bundle WTP = 400 (CPU) + 900 (Monitor) = 1300

In this case, both customers have the same willingness to pay for the bundle ($1300).

*   If bundle price = $1300: Both C1 and C2 buy the bundle. Revenue = $1300 * 2 = **$2600**.
*   If bundle price > $1300: Neither buys. Revenue = $0.

**Total Maximum Revenue with Bundle Pricing:** **$2600**.

By bundling, the seller can generate $2600, which is significantly more than the $1800 obtained through individual pricing. This demonstrates the power of bundling when customer preferences are negatively correlated.

### Example 2: Four Customers and Two Products (CPU and Monitor) with Costs

Consider four customers, C1, C2, C3, and C4, with the following willingness to pay:

| Customer | CPU WTP | Monitor WTP | Bundle WTP (CPU+Monitor) |
| :------- | :------ | :---------- | :----------------------- |
| C1       | 1000    | 1000        | 2000                     |
| C2       | 1200    | 800         | 2000                     |
| C3       | 1400    | 600         | 2000                     |
| C4       | 1600    | 400         | 2000                     |

Assume unit costs:
*   CPU Unit Cost = $1200
*   Monitor Unit Cost = $500
*   Bundle Unit Cost = $1200 + $500 = $1700

We want to maximize profit. Profit = (Price - Unit Cost) * Number of Units Sold.

**Scenario: Individual Pricing (Maximizing Profit)**

*   **CPU Pricing (Unit Cost = $1200):**
    *   Price = $1200: C2, C3, C4 buy (3 customers). Profit = ($1200 - $1200) * 3 = $0.
    *   Price = $1400: C3, C4 buy (2 customers). Profit = ($1400 - $1200) * 2 = $200 * 2 = $400.
    *   Price = $1600: C4 buys (1 customer). Profit = ($1600 - $1200) * 1 = $400 * 1 = $400.
    *   Price > $1600: 0 customers. Profit = $0.

    Maximum profit for CPU is **$400** (achieved at $1400 or $1600).

*   **Monitor Pricing (Unit Cost = $500):**
    *   Price = $500: C1, C2, C3, C4 buy (4 customers). Profit = ($500 - $500) * 4 = $0.
    *   Price = $600: C1, C2, C3 buy (3 customers). Profit = ($600 - $500) * 3 = $100 * 3 = $300.
    *   Price = $800: C1, C2 buy (2 customers). Profit = ($800 - $500) * 2 = $300 * 2 = $600.
    *   Price = $1000: C1 buys (1 customer). Profit = ($1000 - $500) * 1 = $500 * 1 = $500.
    *   Price > $1000: 0 customers. Profit = $0.

    Maximum profit for Monitor is **$600** (achieved at $800).

**Total Maximum Profit with Individual Pricing:** $400 (CPU) + $600 (Monitor) = **$1000**.

**Scenario: Bundle Pricing (Maximizing Profit)**

All customers have a Bundle WTP of $2000. The Bundle Unit Cost is $1700.

*   If bundle price = $2000: C1, C2, C3, C4 buy (4 customers). Profit = ($2000 - $1700) * 4 = $300 * 4 = **$1200**.
*   If bundle price > $2000: 0 customers. Profit = $0.

**Total Maximum Profit with Bundle Pricing:** **$1200**.

Again, bundling results in a higher profit ($1200) compared to individual pricing ($1000).

## Types of Bundling

Based on how products are offered, we can define two types of bundling:

1.  **Pure Bundling:** Only the bundle is offered for sale. Individual products are not sold separately.
2.  **Mixed Bundling:** Both the bundle and the individual products are offered for sale. Customers can choose to buy individual items or the bundle.

In the second example, if offering individual products at their profit-maximizing prices ($1400/$1600 for CPU, $800 for Monitor) and a bundle at $2000 leads to the highest total profit, then it would be a mixed bundling strategy. If only offering the bundle at $2000 was the most profitable, it would be pure bundling. In this case, $1200 (bundle) > $1000 (individual), so bundling is preferred. Whether pure or mixed bundling is optimal depends on the specific WTP distributions and costs.

The examples show that bundling can be a powerful pricing strategy, especially when preferences for individual items are negatively correlated among customers.

This concludes the basic introduction to bundling. In the next videos, we will move on to discuss demand forecasting in more detail before potentially returning to advanced pricing topics later in the course (Module 5).

Thank you.

```markdown
### Introduction

Hello everybody, welcome to Marketing Analytics course. This is Doctor Swagato Chatterjee from Vinod Gupta School of Management, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week four and we are discussing demand forecasting and pricing. Still now we have talked about a little bit of pricing and we have talked about demand functions. And now in this particular class, I will discuss about how to use analytics to forecast a demand.

### Data for Demand Forecasting

In your data set, you will have a file called `sales.excel`. This file will have a data set which looks like this:

*   **Year:** The year component.
*   **Timestamp (TS):** A time period component (not actual timestamp, but sequential period).
*   **Month:** The month of the year.
*   **Sales:** The sales figure for that month.

The data covers 4 years, from 2016 till 2019, with monthly sales data. As `TS` increases, you move closer to the current time. Our job is to estimate how to forecast the next demand based on this data.

### Time Series Decomposition

In a real-life situation, a time series `Xt` (like sales at time `t`) is considered to have three components:

`Xt = Tt + St + Et`

Where:
*   `Tt` is the **Trend** component.
*   `St` is the **Seasonality** component.
*   `Et` is the **Error** or **Residual** component.

There are many ways to estimate a time series, but here we will focus on the simplest way: breaking it into these three parts.

#### Trend Component (`Tt`)

The trend component represents the overall long-term movement of the time series. It indicates whether the series is generally going up, going down, or staying flat over time. If you plot a time series, the trend is like a smoothed line that follows the general direction, disregarding short-term fluctuations.

*   **Example:** Sales of a cold beverage increasing over years because of population growth and increasing purchasing power. This is a positive trend.

#### Seasonality Component (`St`)

Seasonality refers to patterns that repeat themselves over a fixed, known period of time. This period is usually short (e.g., daily, weekly, monthly, quarterly, annually).

*   **Example:** Cold beverage sales spiking in the summer season and dipping in the winter season every year. This pattern repeats every 12 months.
*   When this repeating pattern happens over a very large period (longer than a year, typically), it's sometimes called a *cycle*, but we generally focus on seasonality for shorter-term forecasting.

#### Error Component (`Et`)

The error component is the part of the time series that cannot be explained by the trend or seasonality. It represents random fluctuations or the influence of other factors that are not captured by the time-based components.

*   **Example:** Your score in a math exam might have a trend (improving over semesters) and maybe seasonality (better performance in certain parts of the year), but your health on the day of the exam (a factor not directly related to time patterns) would influence the error component.

The `Et` component is what remains after removing the predictable time-based patterns (`Tt` and `St`). Ideally, this error should have a mean of 0 and show no remaining pattern. However, this remaining error can be influenced by *cross-sectional* variables specific to that time period.

### Using the Error Component for Demand Function

If we have removed the trend and seasonality from the sales data (`Xt`), the remaining error (`Et`) can be modeled as a function of other marketing mix variables or external factors measured at time `t`.

`Et = f(Pt, P't, Advertising_t, Promotion_t, ...)`

Where:
*   `Pt` is the price of our product at time `t`.
*   `P't` is the price of a competing product at time `t`.
*   Other variables could include advertising expenditure, promotional activities, economic indicators, etc.

This is where the demand function actually comes in. We can perform a regression analysis with `Et` as the dependent variable and these other factors as independent variables. This allows us to understand how price and other non-time-series factors influence demand *after* accounting for trend and seasonality.

### Practical Steps for Decomposition in Excel

Let's demonstrate how to decompose the time series using the `Sales.excel` data.

1.  **Load and Plot Data:**
    *   Load the `Sales.excel` file.
    *   Plot the `Sales` data against `TS` or `Timestamp`.
    *   Visual inspection of the plot shows an upward trend and some repeating patterns. The pattern seems to repeat roughly every 11-12 data points. Since the data is monthly, 12 months is a reasonable assumption for seasonality.

2.  **Estimate Trend using Simple Moving Average (SMA):**
    *   A simple way to estimate the trend is using a Simple Moving Average.
    *   We need to choose a period for the moving average. Visually inspecting the smaller fluctuations in the plot, a period of 3 might capture some local smoothing. Using an odd number like 3 is often preferred as it centers the average on the current point.
    *   Calculate SMA for a period of 3: For each point, take the average of the current point, the previous point, and the next point.
    *   The first and last points will not have an SMA calculated (as they lack a preceding/succeeding point for the 3-period average).
    *   Plotting the SMA column shows a smoother line compared to the original sales data, indicating the trend. However, the SMA plot might still show some residual seasonality.

3.  **Calculate Error after Trend Removal:**
    *   Calculate the error remaining after removing the SMA trend: `Error_SMA = Actual Sales - SMA`.
    *   Plot this `Error_SMA`.
    *   The plot of `Error_SMA` should have an average around zero (the trend has been removed). However, it likely still shows the seasonal pattern, possibly with a shorter period visible (like 2 or 3 based on visual "zig zig").

4.  **Estimate Seasonality using the Residual Error:**
    *   The `Error_SMA` plot still contains both the true seasonality (`St`) and the final unpredictable error (`Et`). We will model the seasonality from this residual.
    *   Based on visual inspection of the `Error_SMA` plot (the "zig zig"), let's assume a seasonality period of 3 months.
    *   To capture this 3-period seasonality, we can use the `MOD` function on the `TS` (Timestamp) column. `MOD(TS, 3)` will give remainders 1, 2, 0, repeating every three time periods (e.g., TS 1 -> MOD 1, TS 2 -> MOD 2, TS 3 -> MOD 0, TS 4 -> MOD 1, etc.). These remainders can serve as our 'season' indicators (Season 0, Season 1, Season 2).
    *   Create a small lookup table with 3 rows for these seasons (0, 1, 2) and initial values (e.g., 1, 1, 1).
    *   Use `VLOOKUP` on the `MOD(TS, 3)` result to get a predicted seasonal factor for each time period from this small table.
    *   Calculate the error remaining after removing this modeled seasonality: `Error_2 = Error_SMA - Predicted_Seasonality`.
    *   Calculate the squared error: `Error_2_Squared`.
    *   Calculate the Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) of `Error_2`: `SQRT(AVERAGE(Error_2_Squared))`. This RMSE measures how much error remains after removing the SMA trend *and* this 3-period seasonality.

5.  **Optimize Seasonality Factors using Solver:**
    *   Use Excel's `Solver` add-in.
    *   Set the objective to minimize the `RMSE` value.
    *   Tell Solver to change the values in the small seasonality lookup table (the initial 1, 1, 1 values).
    *   Run Solver. It will find the optimal seasonal factors for Season 0, Season 1, and Season 2 that minimize the remaining error.
    *   Plotting the `Error_SMA` (actual residual seasonality) and the `Predicted_Seasonality` (modeled seasonality) shows how well this model fits the seasonal pattern in the residual.
    *   We can repeat this process assuming a 2-period seasonality (`MOD(TS, 2)`) and compare the resulting RMSE values. The period (2 or 3) that yields a lower RMSE is a better fit for the seasonality in the residual. (Demonstration shows 3-period was better than 2-period for this data).

### Predicting Future Demand

Once the trend and seasonality components are identified:

*   **Trend Forecast:** Extend the trend component into the future. A simple way after calculating SMA is to assume the trend continues at the last known SMA value. A more sophisticated way (mentioned later) is to regress the SMA values against time and project that regression line forward.
*   **Seasonality Forecast:** Apply the optimized seasonal factors found using the `MOD` function (for periods 0, 1, 2 or 0, 1) to the corresponding future time periods based on their `MOD` value.
*   **Combined Time Series Forecast:** Add the forecasted Trend and forecasted Seasonality: `Forecast_Xt = Forecast_Tt + Forecast_St`.

### Modeling the Final Residual Error (`Et`)

The `Error_2` (or `Et`) is the part of the original sales data (`Xt`) that remains after removing the estimated trend (`Tt`) and seasonality (`St`) components identified through the decomposition process described above. This error should ideally be random, but it might contain information about other non-time-based factors influencing sales.

This is where we bring in external variables like price, advertising, promotions, competitor actions, etc. We can perform a regression where:
*   The dependent variable is the final `Et` (the error column after removing trend and seasonality).
*   The independent variables are `Pt`, `P't`, `Advertising_t`, etc., measured at the corresponding time periods.

This regression will give us coefficients that quantify the impact of these other factors on sales *after* accounting for time-based patterns.

### Advanced Forecasting Models (Brief Mention)

There are more advanced time series forecasting models that can capture complex relationships, including dependencies on past values of the series itself and past values of the error. These models can also incorporate external factors.

*   **ARIMA (AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average):** Models time series based on its own past values (AR), past errors (MA), and differencing (I) to handle stationarity.
*   **ARIMAX (ARIMA with eXogenous variables):** An extension of ARIMA that includes external (cross-sectional) variables (like price, advertising) as predictors in addition to the time series components.

These models can also account for situations where sales depend on past prices or past sales (e.g., inventory build-up after a discount).

However, covering ARIMA/ARIMAX is a course in itself and goes beyond the scope of this module. The decomposition method shown (isolating time-based components and then modeling the residual with external factors) is a basic yet effective approach for many marketing forecasting problems.

### Conclusion

We have discussed how to decompose a time series into trend, seasonality, and error components. We demonstrated a basic method using Simple Moving Average and residual analysis with Solver optimization to identify and remove these components in Excel. The remaining error component can then be used in a regression model with other marketing mix or external variables to build a demand function.

In the next video, we will discuss a different kind of forecasting model called Mass Diffusion models, often used for forecasting the adoption of new products or technologies.

Thank you very much for being with me.
```

Lecture Name: Lecture 21 : demand forecasting and pricing (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics
## Lecture 21: Demand Forecasting and Pricing (contd.)
### Instructor: Professor Swagato Chatterjee, Vinod Gupta School of Management, Indian Institute of Technology, Kharagpur

Hello everybody, welcome to Marketing Analytics course. This is Doctor Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week four, and we are discussing demand forecasting and pricing.

In this particular video, I will talk about a special type of forecasting technique where the product is generally a durable product which you buy only once in a lifetime or probably once for a particular model at least, if not in the lifetime. And then, the maximum possible market share is fixed. In this kind of a situation, what happens?

We call it a **technology adoption model** where **Bass diffusion** comes into the picture.

### Product Life Cycle and Adoption

For any product, there is a product life cycle (PLC). The PLC curve, for those who have seen it in Marketing 101, looks like this:

```
Sales at time t
      ^
      |       .--Peak--.
      |     /          \
      |    /            \
      |   /              \
      +--+----------------> Time t
     Low sales at start, then picks up, reaches maturity, then decline.
```

So, this is the sales at time `t` over time `t`. First, the sales are low, then it picks up, reaches maturity, and then declines.

This part is where the adoption happens. We often categorize adopters into groups:

*   Innovators
*   Early Adopters
*   Late Adopters
*   Laggards

This pattern is similar for many technology products.

### Cumulative Sales and the S-Curve

If we try to plot the cumulative sales over time, it looks like an S-curve:

```
Cumulative Sales
      ^
      |        /----\
      |       /      \
      |      /        \
      |     /          \
      |    /            \
      |   /              \
      +--+----------------> Time t
     Starts slow, grows rapidly, then saturates.
```

It goes up and gets saturated at a certain point in time. So, the cumulative sales over time look like an S-curve. This is something we are trying to model.

### The Bass Diffusion Model

Professor Bass, in the 1960s, proposed this model. He said that the adoption process is related to how things spread, like rumors, virus infections, viral messages, or even revolutions. The key is that there needs to be a "significant mass accumulated at the starting point." Only when this significant mass accumulates will it reach an "infection point" and grow rapidly. If it doesn't get that significant initial mass, it won't reach the highest levels. This is why many technology products fail early – they don't generate significant early demand. It's like an epidemic; if you can contain it in the first few hundred or thousand cases, you are safe. If it goes out of hand and a significant mass is generated early, it can become an epidemic.

Bass's model suggests that the adoption rate at a given time depends on two groups of people:

1.  **Innovators:** Those who adopt independently, regardless of others.
2.  **Imitators/Adopters:** Those who adopt based on seeing or hearing about others who have already adopted (word-of-mouth).

Let `f_t` be the proportion of the potential market that adopts at time `t`.
Let `F_t` be the cumulative proportion of the potential market that has adopted up to time `t`. `F_t` is the integral or summation of `f_t` from the beginning up to time `t`.
Let the total potential market size be `m` (or 1 if we are working with proportions).

The people who are yet to adopt at time `t` is `(1 - F_t)`.

Bass proposed that the rate of adoption `f_t` relative to the remaining potential adopters `(1 - F_t)` is a linear function of the cumulative adoption `F_t`:

```
f_t / (1 - F_t) = p + q * F_t
```

Where:
*   `p` is the **coefficient of innovation**. This represents the likelihood of adoption by innovators (independent of others).
*   `q` is the **coefficient of imitation**. This represents the likelihood of adoption influenced by prior adopters (word-of-mouth).

Rearranging the equation:
`f_t = (p + q * F_t) * (1 - F_t)`
`f_t = p * (1 - F_t) + q * F_t * (1 - F_t)`

*   `p * (1 - F_t)`: Adoption by innovators among those who haven't adopted yet.
*   `q * F_t * (1 - F_t)`: Adoption by imitators, influenced by the proportion of people who have already adopted (`F_t`) among those who haven't adopted yet (`1 - F_t`).

The sales at time `t` would be `m * f_t`.

### Formulas from the Bass Model

Solving the differential equation gives the formulas for the sales at time `t` (`f_t`) and cumulative sales at time `t` (`F_t`). The instructor refers to a note for these complex formulas:

**Sales at time t (`f_t`) - Proportion of market adopting at time t:**

```
f_t = [(p + q)^2 / p] * [e^-(p+q)t] / [1 + (q/p) * e^-(p+q)t]^2
```
*(Note: The instructor wrote `m * f_t` formula which includes `m` in front and a term `q square by p`. The formula copied here from general Bass diffusion is for f_t. The specific form written by the instructor was slightly different and he mentioned reading it from a note and it might have a mistake)*
*Instructor's version written on screen:*
```
m * f_t = m * [p + q^2/p] * e^-(p+q)t / [1 + (q/p) * e^-(p+q)t]^2
```
*(He later corrects the term `q^2/p` to `q`, matching the first formula slightly better conceptually)*

**Cumulative Sales at time t (`F_t`) - Proportion of market adopted up to time t:**

```
F_t = [1 - e^-(p+q)t] / [1 + (q/p) * e^-(p+q)t]
```
The cumulative sales in terms of market size `m` would be `CS_t = m * F_t`.

The time at which the sales peak occurs (`t_peak`) is given by:
```
t_peak = [ln(q) - ln(p)] / (p + q)
```

And the peak sales rate (`f_t_peak`) is:
```
f_t_peak = (p + q)^2 / (4q)
```

### Excel Application: Estimating p and q

We can use observed historical cumulative sales data to estimate the parameters `p`, `q`, and the total market potential `m`.

Let's say we have a dataset with cumulative sales percentages over time (years). We assume the maximum achievable sales is 100%.

| Time (t) | Actual Cumulative Sales (%) | Actual Cumulative Sales (Proportion) |
| :------- | :-------------------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| 1        | 12                          | 0.12                                 |
| 2        | 17                          | 0.17                                 |
| 3        | 20                          | 0.20                                 |
| 4        | 24                          | 0.24                                 |
| ...      | ...                         | ...                                  |

We need to estimate `p` and `q` by minimizing the difference between the actual cumulative sales and the predicted cumulative sales using the formula for `F_t`. We also need to estimate `m`, but often `m` is assumed or can be estimated iteratively or treated as another parameter. If the actual data is already in proportion to the maximum possible market (e.g., 100%), then `m` is effectively 1 (or 100 if working in percentage points). Let's assume our actual data is proportion relative to `m=100`.

1.  **Setup:**
    *   Have the time periods (`t`) and actual cumulative sales (`CS_t_actual`).
    *   Set initial guesses for `p` and `q` (e.g., 0.25 and 0.5).
    *   Assume a value for `m` (e.g., 100).
2.  **Calculate Predicted Cumulative Sales (`CS_t_predicted`):** Use the formula `CS_t_predicted = m * F_t` for each time period `t`, plugging in the assumed `p` and `q` values.

    *Excel formula for `F_t` (proportion), assuming `p` is in cell `H2`, `q` is in cell `H3`, and `t` is in cell `A2`):*
    ```excel
    =(1-EXP(-($H$2+$H$3)*A2))/(1+($H$3/$H$2)*EXP(-($H$2+$H$3)*A2))
    ```
    *Then multiply by `m` (e.g., 100) to get predicted percentage.*
    ```excel
    =$H$4*(1-EXP(-($H$2+$H$3)*A2))/(1+($H$3/$H$2)*EXP(-($H$2+$H$3)*A2))
    ```
    *(Using $ signs for absolute references to p, q, and m).*

3.  **Calculate Error:** Calculate the squared error for each time period: `Error_t^2 = (CS_t_actual - CS_t_predicted)^2`.
4.  **Calculate Overall Error Metric:** Calculate the Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) or Sum of Squared Errors (SSE). RMSE is `SQRT(AVERAGE(Error_t^2))`.
    *Excel formula for RMSE:*
    ```excel
    =SQRT(AVERAGE(Squared_Error_Column))
    ```
5.  **Optimization using Solver:**
    *   Goal: Minimize the RMSE (or SSE).
    *   By Changing Cells: The cells containing the initial guess values for `p` and `q`.
    *   Constraints: `p` and `q` must be positive. A constraint like `p >= 0.001` and `q >= 0.001` is often used to avoid potential division by zero or issues with log(0) if using log-linearized forms of the model.
    *   Method: GRG Nonlinear is typically suitable for this.
    *   Run Solver.

6.  **Results:** Solver finds the values of `p` and `q` that minimize the RMSE, providing the best fit of the Bass diffusion curve to the historical data. We can then compare the actual and predicted cumulative sales and plot the curves to visualize the fit.

### Insights from p and q

The estimated values of `p` and `q` have significant meaning:

*   **High `p` (Coefficient of Innovation):** The product is adopted quickly by innovators. This is crucial for early success and getting the diffusion process started. If `p` is too low, the product may never gain traction and die early, even if `q` (imitation) is potentially high. The adoption curve might stay near zero for a long time.
*   **High `q` (Coefficient of Imitation):** The product spreads effectively through word-of-mouth and social influence. A high `q` indicates that prior adopters strongly influence potential adopters.

Comparing `p` and `q` can tell you whether adoption is primarily driven by innovators or imitators. Many successful products have a good balance or strong performance on both.

### Forecasting Maturity

Once `p` and `q` are estimated, we can use the cumulative sales formula `F_t` to forecast future adoption. By extending the time periods in our calculation and applying the estimated `p` and `q`, we can predict when a certain level of market penetration (e.g., 90%, 99%) will be reached.

Knowing when the product is expected to reach maturity is important for planning future product development (e.g., introducing newer versions like Apple does with iPhones to create new S-curves before the old one declines) and marketing strategies.

### Extending the Bass Model

The basic Bass model assumes that the adoption rate depends only on the time and the cumulative number of adopters (`F_t`). It does not include the effects of marketing mix variables like price, advertising, distribution, etc.

These effects can be incorporated by modeling the *error* from the Bass diffusion model. The error (difference between actual and predicted sales from the Bass model) represents the variation not explained by the diffusion process. This error can then be regressed against other marketing variables (price, advertising expenditure, etc.) to see how they influence sales beyond the basic adoption pattern.

For example, if price was higher in a particular year than expected, the actual sales might be lower than the Bass model prediction for that year, resulting in a negative error. This error could potentially be explained by including price as a variable in a regression model predicting the error.

### Conclusion

We have covered the Bass diffusion model for forecasting the adoption of durable goods and technology products, explaining its core concepts (`p`, `q`, S-curve), the relevant formulas, and how to estimate the parameters using historical data and optimization (like Excel Solver). We also discussed how to use the model for forecasting maturity and potential ways to extend it.

In the next week (Week 5), we will move on to discussing how to use demand functions, including those derived from forecasting models like this, for various pricing problems.

Thank you very much for being with me.
'''

Week 5


Lecture Name: Lecture 22 : pricing

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 22: Pricing

**Professor Swagato Chatterjee**
Vinod Gupta School of Management
Indian Institute of Technology, Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome to the marketing analytics course. In this lecture, we will discuss pricing, starting with a special case called skimming.

## Skimming Pricing

### What is Skimming?

Skimming pricing is a strategy often employed by monopolists or companies with significant market power, particularly in the technology sector.

Consider a simple linear demand function:
`D = a - bP`

Where `D` is demand and `P` is price. In a standard scenario, a monopolist sets a single price to maximize revenue (or profit, assuming unit cost). This single price captures a portion of the potential market, leaving some consumer surplus and some potential demand unmet.

### The Skimming Strategy

Skimming involves setting multiple prices over different time periods, starting with a high price and gradually lowering it.

1.  **Start High:** Initially, the product is priced very high. This captures customers with high willingness to pay (often early adopters or those who are impatient).
2.  **Gradual Reduction:** Over time, the price is sequentially reduced.
3.  **Capture More Segments:** Each price reduction makes the product affordable to a new segment of customers who were not willing or able to pay the previous higher price.

This process allows the company to "skim" off the consumer surplus from different segments over time, potentially capturing a larger portion of the total market's willingness to pay compared to a single price strategy.

Companies often use this for innovative products where technology is new and the initial customer base is less price-sensitive. As the technology matures and becomes more common, the price is lowered.

This strategy can lead to an S-curve shape for cumulative sales over time, as initial adoption is slow (high price) and then accelerates as the price drops, finally slowing down as the market saturates.

## Example: Skimming Pricing using a Spreadsheet Model

Let's illustrate skimming pricing with a simple spreadsheet model. We'll consider 10 time periods and a hypothetical demand function.

### Spreadsheet Setup

We have columns for:

*   **Time Period:** 1 to 10
*   **Price:** The price set in each time period. This is the variable we want to optimize.
*   **Units Left at Price:** This represents the potential cumulative demand up to this price point. It is derived from the demand function.
*   **Unit Sales:** The number of units sold *in that specific time period*.
*   **Price Revenue:** Revenue generated in that specific time period (Price * Unit Sales).
*   **Total Revenue:** The cumulative sum of Price Revenue across all time periods.

### Demand Function

Let's assume a simple demand function where the cumulative number of people who would buy at a price `P` or lower is approximately `101 - P`. This means:

*   At P=2, Units Left = 101 - 2 = 99
*   At P=5, Units Left = 101 - 5 = 96
*   At P=25, Units Left = 101 - 25 = 76

So, the "Units Left at Price" column represents the cumulative demand at the current price.

### Calculating Unit Sales per Period

The challenge in a skimming strategy is that sales in a given period depend on the current price and the sales that have *already occurred* in previous periods at higher prices.

For each time period `t`:
`Unit Sales(t) = (Units Left at Price in period t) - (Cumulative Units Sold before period t)`

Let's look at the logic with an example:

*   **Period 1:** Price = 25. Units Left = 76. Cumulative Sold before = 0. Unit Sales = 76 - 0 = 76.
*   **Period 2:** Price = 10. Units Left = 91. Cumulative Sold before (from Period 1) = 76. Unit Sales = 91 - 76 = 15.
*   **Period 3:** Price = 8. Units Left = 93. Cumulative Sold before (from Periods 1 & 2) = 76 + 15 = 91. Unit Sales = 93 - 91 = 2.

This calculation logic is crucial in the spreadsheet to track how many new customers are acquired at each price step. The "Units Left at Price" in the spreadsheet example is actually calculated as `101 - Price`.

The "Unit Sales" in period `t` is `(Units Left at Price in period t) - (Units Left at Price in period t-1)`. For the first period, it's just `Units Left at Price`.

### Optimizing Prices with Solver

The goal is to find the sequence of prices over the 10 periods that maximizes the "Total Revenue" (sum of Price Revenue across all periods). We use a tool like Solver in a spreadsheet.

1.  **Set Objective:** Maximize the cell containing the "Total Revenue".
2.  **By Changing Variables:** Select the cells containing the "Price" for all 10 time periods.
3.  **Add Constraints:**
    *   Prices should be within a reasonable range (e.g., >= 1 and <= 100, given the demand function logic implies prices above 100 result in 0 demand).
    *   The price must decrease or stay the same over time (an implicit constraint for skimming, although not explicitly mentioned as a solver constraint in the video, the solution shows this pattern). *Note: The video transcript shows integer constraint was added, but instructor mentions it can be removed.* We will follow the description in the video: C5:C14 <= 100 and C5:C14 >= 1. The integer constraint C5:C14 = integer is also mentioned.
4.  **Select Solving Method:** GRG Nonlinear is suitable for this type of optimization problem.
5.  **Solve:** Run Solver.

### Results from Solver

When Solver is run with the described setup and constraints, it finds a sequence of prices that maximize total revenue. The resulting prices show a clear downward trend, which is characteristic of skimming pricing.

Example result shown:
*   Price 1: 91
*   Price 2: 82
*   Price 3: 73
*   ...
*   Price 10: 9

The total revenue achieved with this optimized skimming strategy was 4636.

This demonstrates how setting sequentially lower prices over time allows the company to sell to different segments, maximizing overall revenue from the product's lifecycle.

## Conclusion

Skimming pricing is a powerful strategy, particularly for new and innovative products, allowing companies to capture higher willingness to pay from early adopters and then gradually expand the market by lowering prices. This can lead to significantly higher overall revenue compared to setting a single static price.

In the next video, we will discuss other models of pricing.

Thank you.
'''
