# Marketing Mix Models and Advertising Models (Contd.)

Hello, everybody. Welcome to Marketing Analytics Course. This is Doctor Swagato Chatterjee from VGSoM, IIT Kharagpur, who is taking this course for you.

And today, we are in week six, and we are discussing about Marketing Mix Model and Advertising Models.

## Recap and Today's Focus

In the last video, we have discussed how the various items of marketing mix actually interplay with each other. In that particular video, we have taken the Simple Linear Model and we tried to find out how the effects are different.

But in this particular video, we will try to do a little bit more. We will try to bring in a little bit of complexity, non-linearity in the model, and the effects of the past behavior also.

## Data Set

So, if you see the data set that we have, in week six, season 2, the data set looks like this. (Instructor shows data in Excel).

It's quarterly data:
- Quarter of the year (1, 2, 3, 4)
- Quarter number (sequential)
- Advertisements spend
- Price
- Actual Sales

These are the five data points that I have currently (Columns C, D, E, G, and I).

## Adstock: The Effect of Past Advertising

Advertisement is oftentimes assumed to have a carry-over effect or memory.
(Instructor draws a graph showing effect shooting up after an ad and then decaying over time until the next ad).

If this is time period, and this is the effect of an ad, and let's say, you give advertisement here, here, here and here, the corresponding sales or the effect of the advertisement will first shoot up. And then, in this period, when you have not given any advertisement, people still remember about you, but slowly that memory comes down. So memory decays, the decay of memory happens. It happens like this. And then again, you give some ad, again it goes up, again it happens like this, and again, it goes up and again it happens like this and so on.

How the decay will happen, whether it is a linear decay or non-linear decay or curve-related decay is something that is still an area of research, but this kind of things happen.

At any given point, the effect of the ad is dependent on how much ad you have given previously and how much decay has happened. Based on that assumption, there are lots of equations that have been created.

One of them involves something called *Adstock*. We are saying that the ad effect gets in my memory as a stock, it is like, it behaves like an inventory.

The *Adstock* formula for time period `t` is:
```
Adstock(t) = lambda * Adstock(t-1) + Current_Ad_Spend(t)
```
So, whatever previous stock was there in the last time period (`Adstock(t-1)`) that will decay a little bit (multiplied by `lambda`). Lambda is basically between 0 and 1. Plus the current ad spend at time `t`. If you give certain ad at this point, it will shoot up.

## Sales Forecast Model Equation

The sales has different components in it. We want to model how sales is dependent on price and advertisement (represented by Adstock).

The general form of the model being used is multiplicative:

```
Sales = Constant * Price^(-Elasticity) * Seasonal_Index * (Trend * Quarter_Number + Ad_Effect * Adstock)
```

Let's break down the components:

### Price Elasticity Component

The price effect on demand is basically `Price^(-Elasticity)`.
This comes from the definition of price elasticity of demand:
```
Elasticity = -(delta D / D) / (delta P / P)
```
Where D is demand and P is price.
Rearranging and integrating gives:
```
ln(D) = -Elasticity * ln(P) + C
D = exp(C) * P^(-Elasticity)
```
So, Demand is some constant (`exp(C)`) into `Price^(-Elasticity)`. This explains the `Price^(-Elasticity)` term in the model.

(Instructor mentions competitor price cross-elasticity, which would be a positive exponent for competitor price, but skips it for this model).

### Other Components

- **Seasonal Component:** `Seasonal_Index`. This is a multiplier based on the quarter of the year.
- **Trend Component:** `Trend * Quarter_Number`. This represents a linear trend over time. (Instructor initially mentions `Trend^Quarter_Number` but the formula in the Excel shows multiplication).
- **Adstock Effect Component:** `Ad_Effect * Adstock`. This is the impact of the calculated Adstock on sales. `Ad_Effect` is a parameter to be estimated.
- **Constant:** An overall baseline constant multiplier.

All of these parameters are unknown and need to be estimated:
- Lambda (decay rate for Adstock)
- Initial Stock (Adstock value before the first data period)
- Constant
- Trend
- Ad_Effect
- Elasticity
- Seasonal Indices (one for each of the four quarters)

## Implementing the Model in Excel

(Instructor switches to showing the Excel sheet and demonstrates the calculations).

We need to calculate the Adstock and the Sales Forecast based on initial guesses for the parameters.

### Initial Parameters

We start with initial guesses for the parameters (e.g., Initial Stock = 10, Lambda = 0.5, Constant = 1, Trend = 1, Ad Effect = 1, Elasticity = 1, Seasonal Indices = 1 for all quarters). These are placed in specific cells (e.g., H4 for Initial Stock, M1:M5 for Constant, Trend, Ad Effect, Lambda, Elasticity, and L6:M9 for the Seasonal Indices table).

### Adstock Calculation

We set up a column for Adstock (Column I).
- We have an `Initial_Stock` parameter (e.g., in cell H4).
- We have a `Lambda` parameter (e.g., in cell M4).
- Current Ad Spend is in Column E.

- For the first data row (e.g., I6, Quarter Number 1): `Initial_Stock * Lambda + Current_Ad_Spend`
- For subsequent periods (e.g., I7, Quarter Number 2): `Previous_Period_Adstock * Lambda + Current_Ad_Spend`

We need to fix the `Lambda` cell reference with F4 ($M$4) so it doesn't change when dragging the formula.

```excel
// Assuming Initial Stock in H4, Lambda in M4, Current Ad Spend in E
// Adstock for the first data row (e.g., I6)
=H4*$M$4+E6

// Adstock for the second data row (e.g., I7)
=I6*$M$4+E7

// Drag formula down Column I for all data points
```

### Sales Forecast Calculation

This is calculated using the full model equation (Column J). The parameters are in cells M1 (Constant), M2 (Trend), M3 (Ad Effect), M5 (Elasticity), and L6:M9 (Seasonal Indices table). Quarter number is in Column D, Quarter of Year is in Column C, Price is in Column F, and Adstock is in Column I.

We use VLOOKUP to get the correct Seasonal Index based on the Quarter of Year. We use the POWER function for the price elasticity term.

```excel
// Assuming parameters: Constant (M1), Trend (M2), Ad Effect (M3), Elasticity (M5), Lambda (M4), Initial Stock (H4)
// Seasonal Indices in L6:M9 (lookup table)
// Data columns: Quarter of Year (C), Quarter Number (D), Price (F), Actual Sales (G), Ad Spend (E), Adstock (I)

// Sales Forecast formula for a row (e.g., J6)
= $M$1  *  // Constant (fixed with F4)
  POWER(F6, -$M$5) *  // Price to the power of -Elasticity (Elasticity fixed with F4)
  VLOOKUP(C6, $L$6:$M$9, 2, FALSE) * // Seasonal Index (Table fixed with F4)
  ($M$2 * D6 + $M$3 * I6) // (Trend * Quarter Number + Ad Effect * Adstock). Trend and Ad Effect fixed with F4. Quarter Number (D6) and Adstock (I6) change per row.
```
(Instructor demonstrates building this formula step-by-step in cell J6 and dragging it down).

### Error Calculation

To evaluate the model fit, we calculate the Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE).

First, calculate the percentage error for each data point (Column K).
```excel
// Assuming Forecasted Sales in J, Actual Sales in G
// Percentage Error for a row (e.g., K6)
= (J6 - G6) / G6
```
Then, calculate the Absolute Percentage Error (Column L).
```excel
// Absolute Percentage Error (e.g., L6)
= ABS(K6)
```
Finally, calculate the Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) by taking the average of the absolute percentage errors (e.g., in cell J4).
```excel
// Assuming Absolute Percentage Errors are in column L (from row 6 down to the end of data)
// MAPE (e.g., J4)
= AVERAGE(L6:L...) // Range covers all data points
```

### Using Solver

The objective is to minimize the MAPE (cell J4) by changing the parameter cells.

Go to Data -> Solver.
- Set Objective: Select the cell containing the MAPE (J4).
- To: Minimize.
- By Changing Variable Cells: Select the cells containing all the parameters you want to estimate. This includes the Initial Stock (H4), the Constant, Trend, Ad Effect, Lambda, Elasticity (M1:M5), and the Seasonal Indices (M6:M9).
- Add Constraints:
    - Initial Stock (H4): Must be >= 0 and <= 100 (based on the range of ad expenditure).
    - Lambda (M4): Must be >= 0 and <= 1 (decay rate is between 0 and 1).
    - Constant (M1), Trend (M2), Ad Effect (M3), Elasticity (M5), Seasonal Indices (M6:M9): These should probably be >= 0 as sales and effects are typically non-negative. (Instructor sets M1:M9 >= 0).
    - (Instructor initially tried setting an upper bound for all M1:M9 but focuses on the >=0 constraint).
- Select a Solving Method: Evolutionary is suitable for non-linear, complex problems like this.
- Click Solve.

(Instructor runs the solver and stops it manually after it has run for some time).

The solver finds values for the parameters that minimize the MAPE based on the constraints and the selected method.
(Instructor shows the results from Solver).

The values obtained are the estimated parameters. For example, the elasticity might be around 1.18, Ad Effect around 51, Trend around 0.38. The Lambda value resulting from this run was 1, which suggests no decay. This might happen if the solver didn't run long enough or due to characteristics of the data. In a real-life situation, Lambda is typically less than 1, indicating decay. Running the solver for longer might yield a better lambda value.

## Adding Non-Linearity to Ad Effect (Advanced)

Another common refinement is to add non-linearity to the *effect* of the Ad Spend itself, capturing diminishing marginal returns. As ad expenditure goes up, the *marginal* increase in ad effect might decrease, suggesting a saturation point. This effect often looks like an S-curve.

(Instructor draws/describes a curve showing diminishing returns for higher ad spend).

To model this, you can modify how the `Current_Ad_Spend` contributes to the Adstock, perhaps by putting it through a transformation function like a logistic function before adding it. A common approach is to use:
```
Transformed_Ad_Spend(t) = Max_Effect * (1 / (1 + exp(-alpha * Current_Ad_Spend(t))))
```
Where `Max_Effect` is the maximum possible ad effect and `alpha` is a parameter controlling the steepness of the S-curve. You would then estimate `Max_Effect` and `alpha` along with the other parameters. Alpha must be positive for the effect to increase with ad spend and then saturate.

(Instructor mentions this as something the user can try implementing by adding an 'alpha' parameter and optimizing it in Solver, with alpha > 0).

## Using the Fitted Model

Once the model parameters are estimated, you can use them to forecast sales for future periods based on planned ad spend and price.

(Instructor demonstrates entering values for a future quarter, e.g., Quarter number 25).

For example, for a future period (Quarter number 25):
- Enter the Quarter of Year (e.g., 1).
- Enter planned Ad Spend (e.g., 32).
- Enter planned Price (e.g., 41).

Using the fitted parameters (Constant, Trend, Ad Effect, Elasticity, Lambda, Seasonal Indices), calculate the Adstock for this future period (using the last Adstock value from the historical data as `Adstock(t-1)` for the first forecast period). Then calculate the forecasted sales using the model equation.

This fitted model allows you to perform optimization. You can use Solver again, but this time to maximize the forecasted sales (or potentially forecasted revenue or profit, if cost data is included) by changing the future Price and Ad Spend values for the next period, while keeping the other model parameters fixed at their estimated values from the historical data.

## Comparison and Future Steps

In comparison to the previous video, this model is more complex as it includes non-linearity (the price term and potentially the ad effect itself) and the carry-over effect of advertising through Adstock.

The current model treats advertising as a single collective expenditure. However, the effect, saturation, and decay (`lambda`) of advertising might be different for different channels (TV, Radio, Digital, etc.). This model doesn't differentiate that yet.

In future videos, we will discuss in greater depth:
- How various types of ads in various channels actually affect sales.
- Bringing in other marketing mix components:
    - **Product:** R&D expenditure, product development/management costs.
    - **Place:** Supply chain costs, margins given to retailers/transporters (often these are investment areas where sales managers, branding managers, and supply chain managers might compete for budget).

These are all place-related costs/investments. We will explore how these investments interplay with:
- Advertisement expenditure in multiple channels.
- Product development expenditure.

This is how we gradually move towards more complex, realistic models. Here, we've brought in a little bit of non-linearity; further complexity can be brought into our data set and models.

We will do all of these things in the next videos, in this week six only.

## Conclusion

Thank you for being with me. I will meet you in the next video.

Lecture Name: Lecture 31 : marketing mix models and advertising models (contd.)

'''
Okay, here is the transcript converted into a structured markdown format.

# Marketing Analytics
## Lecture 31: Marketing Mix Models and Advertising Models (Contd.)

### Introduction and Recap of Adstock Model

Hello, everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 6 and discussing various kinds of advertising models.

In the last class, we discussed the adstock model. We explained that advertising has a long-term impact. Any advertisement you do will have a long-term impact that slowly wears out over time. When you run another advertisement, the impact will jump up again.

If you remember from the last class, we depicted this as:

*   **Y-axis:** Memory/Impact
*   **X-axis:** Time period

At various points in time, when you run an ad (represented by spikes), the memory/impact goes up. Then it slowly decays over time until the next ad causes it to jump up again. This pattern of decay is the core idea of the adstock model.

The decay effect happens over time, and we introduced the parameter lambda (λ) to represent this decay rate. Lambda is typically between 0 and 1 (0 < λ < 1), which signifies the gradual wearing out of the advertisement's impact.

### Integrated Marketing Communication and Multiple Channels

Today, we will discuss situations where you are not giving just one single ad, but multiple ads across multiple channels. This is part of **Integrated Marketing Communication (IMC)**, where marketing expenditure is done in various channels. Each channel will have its own effect. There might be interaction effects between channels, or they might act independently.

The dataset we are using for this discussion contains sales data over multiple time periods (Year, Quarter) and the expenditure in four specific channels:

*   Digital
*   Print
*   TV
*   Outdoor

Let's briefly discuss these channels:

*   **Digital:** This includes online media like Google Ads, Facebook, Instagram, etc. You can pay these platforms to show your ads.
    *   **Payment Models:** Digital advertising often uses different payment models:
        *   **Pay Per Click (PPC):** You pay when a customer clicks on your ad. This is typically used when the objective is direct action or engagement, often for established brands.
        *   **Pay Per Impression (PPI):** You pay for each time your ad is displayed to a user. This is often used for awareness generation, especially for new products or brands, where repeated exposure is important.
    *   Regardless of the model, expenditure is incurred in the digital channel.

*   **Print:** This includes newspapers and magazines.
    *   **Business Model:** Newspapers often make losses on subscriptions (cost of production higher than the selling price) but make significant money from advertising space. This is similar to the razor/blades pricing model we discussed earlier, where profit is made on a complementary product (blades/ads) rather than the core product (razor/newspaper). For example, a newspaper might cost 10-12 rupees to produce but sell for only 3-5 rupees, incurring a loss per copy. This loss is offset by the high revenue generated from selling ad space, justified by the large circulation (reach).

*   **TV:** Traditional television advertisements, usually bought in time slots (e.g., 10, 20 seconds) within programs. Prices often decrease for multiple rounds.

*   **Outdoor:** Includes large banners (billboards), ads on cars, buses, auto-rickshaws, etc. These incur costs for printing, production, and renting the space.

### Adstock for Each Channel & Interaction Effects

Each of these channels will create its own **adstock**. Multiple impressions or exposures in a channel build up a "stock" of memory or impact, which then decays over time if no further expenditure is made in that channel. Since the nature and target audience of each channel can be different, the adstock created by digital advertising might have a different effect on sales compared to the adstock created by TV advertising. Therefore, it's often necessary to model the effect of each channel's adstock separately, rather than simply summing up all advertising expenditure.

Furthermore, there can be **interaction effects** between channels. Different combinations of channels might be more effective for targeting specific customer segments. For instance:

*   A working professional might be reached effectively through digital ads (online platforms, streaming services) and radio ads (during commute).
*   An older demographic might be better reached through print (newspapers) and TV (news channels, specific programs).

If certain channel combinations are particularly effective for reaching and influencing a segment, their combined effect might be greater than the sum of their individual effects. This synergy can be modeled using interaction terms (e.g., Digital Stock * TV Stock).

### Other Marketing Mix Elements: R&D, Price, and Place

Beyond promotion (advertising), marketing mix models also incorporate other elements like Product, Price, and Place.

*   **R&D (Research and Development):** Expenditure on R&D aims to improve the product. Like advertising, the impact of R&D investment is often not immediate. It takes time for R&D to yield results (new features, better quality), for the product to be updated, and for customers to become aware of and appreciate the improvements. Thus, R&D expenditure can also be modeled with an adstock-like effect, creating a stock of "product quality" or "innovation" that decays if further investment isn't made.

*   **Price:** We previously discussed price elasticity.
    *   **Own Price Elasticity:** The change in demand due to a change in *our* product's price. This is typically negative (higher price -> lower demand). The relationship can be modeled multiplicatively: `Sales ~ Price ^ (-Elasticity_Own)`.
    *   **Cross-Elasticity:** The change in demand due to a change in a *competitor's* price. For substitute products, this is typically positive (competitor's higher price -> higher demand for our product). The relationship can be modeled multiplicatively: `Sales ~ Competitor Price ^ (+Elasticity_Competitor)`.

*   **Place (Retailer Margin):** "Place" is about distribution and where the product is sold. A key aspect, especially for FMCG, is getting shelf space or prominent placement in retail stores. Retailers are often incentivized with **margin** (the difference between the price they sell at and the price they buy at from the manufacturer).
    *   **Own Margin Elasticity:** Higher margin offered to retailers generally makes them more willing to stock and promote your product, leading to higher sales. This elasticity is typically positive. Modeled as: `Sales ~ Own Margin ^ (+Elasticity_Own_Margin)`.
    *   **Competitor Margin Elasticity:** If a competitor offers a higher margin to retailers, retailers might prioritize the competitor's product, negatively impacting your sales. This elasticity is typically negative. Modeled as: `Sales ~ Competitor Margin ^ (-Elasticity_Competitor_Margin)`.

### Formulating the Combined Marketing Mix Model

Combining these factors, a complex marketing mix model equation can be formulated. Based on the discussion, it often takes a mixed form, with some effects being multiplicative (like price, margin, seasonality) and others additive (like trend, adstock from promotional activities):

```
Sales = A * (Price ^ -E1) * (Comp_Price ^ E2) * (Own_Margin ^ E3) * (Comp_Margin ^ -E4) * Seasonal_Index
      + Trend * Time
      + (Digital_Stock * β_Digital)
      + (TV_Stock * β_TV)
      + (Print_Stock * β_Print)
      + (Outdoor_Stock * β_Outdoor)
      + (R&D_Stock * β_RnD)
      + (Interaction_Term1 * β_Int1)  // e.g., Digital_Stock * TV_Stock
      + ...other adstocks and interaction terms
      + ...other baseline effects
```

Where:
*   `A` is a constant baseline sales.
*   `E1, E2, E3, E4` are elasticities (E1, E4 expected negative; E2, E3 expected positive).
*   `Seasonal_Index` captures quarterly seasonality (e.g., different coefficients for Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4).
*   `Trend` captures overall growth or decline over time.
*   `Digital_Stock`, `TV_Stock`, etc., are the calculated adstocks for each channel (including R&D).
*   `β_Digital`, `β_TV`, etc., are the coefficients representing the impact of each stock on sales (expected positive).
*   `Interaction_Term1`, etc., represent combined effects of different stocks.
*   `β_Int1`, etc., are coefficients for interaction terms.

All coefficients related to stocks (β) are expected to be positive (more adstock/R&D stock leads to more sales), except for potential negative interactions. Elasticities have expected signs as discussed. The seasonal component must be positive, and the constant `A` must be positive as sales cannot be negative.

More complexity can be added, such as using S-curve functions instead of linear relationships for the adstock terms to capture saturation effects (impact plateaus at high expenditure levels).

### Next Steps

The main agenda is to be able to estimate this complex objective function formula based on the historical sales and marketing expenditure data. While we have done simpler optimizations in Excel, estimating a model with this many variables and complexities is difficult in Excel.

Therefore, we will use **R programming** in the next video to estimate the coefficients (A, E1, E2, E3, E4, βs, etc.) of this marketing mix model. Once we have this formula with estimated coefficients, we can use it for optimization, such as determining the optimal allocation of a marketing budget across different channels and R&D to maximize sales.

Thank you for being with me. I will meet you in the next video where we will solve this problem using R.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 32 : marketing mix models and advertising models (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 32: Marketing Mix Models and Advertising Models (Contd.)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee, VGSOM IIT Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is the 32nd lecture, and we are continuing our discussion on Marketing Mix Models. In the previous class, we discussed the fundamental equations for modeling sales response when considering multiple channels for advertising, as well as other factors like price and retailer margin, and how these factors impact sales. The goal was to define an objective function (minimizing error) that we could optimize.

Today, we will work through a practical example using R to implement a marketing mix model based on a real dataset.

## Data Setup and Initial Exploration

We will be using a quarterly dataset spanning from 2009 to 2016.

1.  **Clean Environment:**
    First, let's clean the R environment to ensure a fresh start.

    ```R
    # Clean console
    cat("\014")

    # Clean environment
    rm(list = ls())
    ```

2.  **Set Working Directory and Load Data:**
    Set the working directory to the location of the source file and read the data.

    ```R
    # Set working directory
    setwd(dirname(rstudioapi::getActiveDocumentContext()$path))

    # Read data
    data <- read.csv("your_data_file.csv") # Replace with actual file name
    ```

3.  **Inspect Data Structure:**
    Let's check the structure of the loaded data. We expect all relevant columns to be numeric (integer or numeric) for calculations.

    ```R
    # Check data structure
    str(data)
    ```

    The data includes columns like `Year`, `Quarter`, advertising spends in different channels (`Digital`, `Print`, `TV`, `Outdoor`), product-related factors (`RnD`, `Price`, `Comp_Price`, `Margin`, `Comp_Margin`), and `Sales`.

4.  **Create Time Variable (`qtrn`):**
    We create a sequential time variable `qtrn` (quarter number) from 1 to 32 (since there are 32 quarters in the data). This will be used to model the overall trend.

    ```R
    # Create quarter number variable
    data$qtrn <- 1:nrow(data)
    ```

5.  **Plot Sales Data:**
    Let's visualize the sales data over time to observe any trends or seasonality.

    ```R
    # Plot sales over time
    plot(data$qtrn, data$Sales, type = "l", xlab = "Quarter", ylab = "Sales", main = "Sales Over Time")
    ```

    The plot shows a generally increasing trend with some cyclical (seasonal) components. We need a model that can capture these effects.

## Ad Stock Calculation

Recall the concept of ad stock, where the impact of an advertisement persists over time but decays. The formula for ad stock at time `t` (`St`) is:
`St = λ * St-1 + Et` (for t > 1)
`S1 = λ * initial_stock + E1` (for t = 1)

Here, `λ` is the decay rate (between 0 and 1), and `Et` is the expenditure at time `t`. We need to calculate this for each advertising channel and for R&D, as R&D investments also have a lasting effect.

1.  **Define Initial Values for Ad Stocks and Lambdas:**
    We start with some initial guesses for the initial stock values and the decay rates (lambdas) for each of the five components (Digital, Print, TV, Outdoor, R&D).

    ```R
    # Initial stock values (arbitrary starting points)
    digital_in <- 80
    print_in <- 50
    tv_in <- 30
    outdoor_in <- 44
    rnd_in <- 20

    # Initial lambda (decay) values (arbitrary starting points, must be between 0 and 1)
    lambda <- rep(0.5, 5) # Assuming same lambda for all 5 initially
    ```

2.  **Initialize Ad Stock Vectors:**
    Create empty vectors to store the calculated ad stock values for each time period.

    ```R
    # Initialize stock vectors
    digital_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
    print_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
    tv_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
    outdoor_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
    rnd_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
    ```

3.  **Calculate Ad Stocks using a Loop:**
    We loop through each time period (quarter) from 1 to 32, applying the ad stock formula. The calculation is different for the very first period (i=1) compared to subsequent periods (i>1).

    ```R
    # Calculate ad stocks over time
    for (i in 1:nrow(data)) {
      if (i == 1) {
        # Calculation for the first period
        digital_stock[i] <- lambda[1] * digital_in + data$Digital[i]
        print_stock[i] <- lambda[2] * print_in + data$Print[i]
        tv_stock[i] <- lambda[3] * tv_in + data$TV[i]
        outdoor_stock[i] <- lambda[4] * outdoor_in + data$Outdoor[i]
        rnd_stock[i] <- lambda[5] * rnd_in + data$RnD[i]
      } else {
        # Calculation for subsequent periods
        digital_stock[i] <- lambda[1] * digital_stock[i-1] + data$Digital[i]
        print_stock[i] <- lambda[2] * print_stock[i-1] + data$Print[i]
        tv_stock[i] <- lambda[3] * tv_stock[i-1] + data$TV[i]
        outdoor_stock[i] <- lambda[4] * outdoor_stock[i-1] + data$Outdoor[i]
        rnd_stock[i] <- lambda[5] * rnd_stock[i-1] + data$RnD[i]
      }
    }
    ```

4.  **Verify Calculation:**
    We can check the first few calculated values for a stock like `digital_stock`.

    ```R
    # Check digital stock values
    digital_stock
    ```

    The first value should be `0.5 * 80 + 90 = 130`. The second should be `0.5 * 130 + 98 = 163`, and so on.

## Sales Prediction Model

The sales model we discussed was a multiplicative model incorporating trend, ad stocks, price/margin elasticities, and seasonality.
`Sales = A * (Trend Component) * (Ad Stock Component) * (Price Component) * (Margin Component) * (Seasonal Component)`

Expanding this based on our variables:
`Sales_t = A * (t + trend_slope) * (β_digital*DS_t + β_print*PS_t + β_tv*TV_t + β_outdoor*OS_t + β_rnd*RnD_t) * Price_t^β_price * Comp_Price_t^β_comp_price * Margin_t^β_margin * Comp_Margin_t^β_comp_margin * Seasonal_t`

We need initial guesses for the remaining parameters: A (constant), trend slope, ad effect betas, elasticities, and seasonal factors.

1.  **Define Initial Values for Remaining Parameters:**

    ```R
    # Initial values for A and trend slope
    A <- 1 # Constant
    trend_slope <- 0.5 # Trend component slope

    # Initial values for ad effects (betas)
    ad_effects <- rep(0.5, 5) # 5 betas for Digital, Print, TV, Outdoor, RnD

    # Initial values for elasticities
    # Order: Price, Comp_Price, Margin, Comp_Margin
    elasticity <- c(-0.5, 0.5, 0.5, -0.5) # Price and Comp_Margin expected negative, Comp_Price and Margin positive effect

    # Initial values for seasonality (assuming no seasonality initially)
    seasonality <- rep(1, 4) # 4 seasonal factors for Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4
    ```

    *Note: The elasticity values are typically negative for own price and competitor margin, and positive for competitor price and own margin.*

2.  **Calculate Predicted Sales:**
    Now, calculate the predicted sales using the formula and the current parameter values and calculated ad stocks.

    ```R
    # Calculate predicted sales based on current parameters and stocks
    predicted_sales <- A *
      (data$qtrn + trend_slope) * # Trend component
      (ad_effects[1]*digital_stock +
         ad_effects[2]*print_stock +
         ad_effects[3]*tv_stock +
         ad_effects[4]*outdoor_stock +
         ad_effects[5]*rnd_stock) * # Ad stock component (additive in this formulation)
      (data$Price^elasticity[1]) * # Price elasticity
      (data$Comp_Price^elasticity[2]) * # Competitor price elasticity
      (data$Margin^elasticity[3]) * # Margin elasticity
      (data$Comp_Margin^elasticity[4]) * # Competitor margin elasticity
      (seasonality[data$Quarter]) # Seasonal component (indexing by quarter number)
    ```

## Error Calculation (RMSE)

Our objective is to minimize the error between the actual sales and the predicted sales. We use Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE) as the metric.

```R
# Calculate the error (RMSE)
sales_error_value <- sqrt(mean((data$Sales - predicted_sales)^2))

# Print the initial error
print(sales_error_value)
```

With our initial, arbitrary parameter values, the RMSE is around 53,466. Our goal is to find parameter values that minimize this error.

## Optimization Setup

We use R's `optim()` function to find the parameter values that minimize the `sales_error_value`. The `optim()` function requires a function that takes a single vector of parameters and returns the value to be minimized (the error).

1.  **Create a Parameter Vector:**
    We need a single vector containing all the parameters we want to optimize. The order is crucial.

    ```R
    # Combine all parameters into a single starting vector
    # Order: initial_stocks (5), lambdas (5), A (1), trend_slope (1), ad_effects (5), elasticity (4), seasonality (4)
    starting <- c(
      digital_in, print_in, tv_in, outdoor_in, rnd_in, # 1-5: Initial stocks
      lambda,                                         # 6-10: Lambdas
      A,                                              # 11: Constant A
      trend_slope,                                    # 12: Trend slope
      ad_effects,                                     # 13-17: Ad effects (betas)
      elasticity,                                     # 18-21: Elasticities
      seasonality                                     # 22-25: Seasonality factors
    )

    # Check the length of the starting vector
    length(starting) # Should be 25
    ```

2.  **Define Parameter Bounds (Optional but Recommended):**
    Some parameters have natural constraints (e.g., lambdas must be between 0 and 1, initial stocks and seasonal factors should be non-negative). Defining lower and upper bounds can guide the optimization and ensure realistic results.

    ```R
    # Define lower bounds for parameters
    lower <- c(
      rep(0, 5),  # Initial stocks >= 0
      rep(0, 5),  # Lambdas >= 0
      1,          # A >= 1 (positive constant)
      0,          # Trend slope >= 0
      rep(0, 5),  # Ad effects (betas) >= 0
      rep(-Inf, 4), # Elasticities can be negative or positive (no bounds)
      rep(0, 4)   # Seasonality factors >= 0
    )

    # Define upper bounds for parameters
    upper <- c(
      rep(100, 5), # Initial stocks <= 100 (example max)
      rep(1, 5),   # Lambdas <= 1
      1000,        # A <= 1000 (example max)
      100,         # Trend slope <= 100 (example max)
      rep(100, 5), # Ad effects (betas) <= 100 (example max)
      rep(Inf, 4),  # Elasticities can be negative or positive (no bounds)
      rep(100, 4)  # Seasonality factors <= 100 (example max)
    )
    ```

## Optimization Function Implementation

We create a function `sales_error_function` that takes the parameter vector `x`, unpacks it, performs the ad stock and sales calculations, and returns the RMSE.

```R
# Optimization function that takes a parameter vector 'x' and returns the RMSE
sales_error_function <- function(x) {

  # Unpack parameters from the vector 'x' based on the defined order
  digital_in <- x[1]
  print_in <- x[2]
  tv_in <- x[3]
  outdoor_in <- x[4]
  rnd_in <- x[5]

  lambda <- x[6:10]

  A <- x[11]
  trend_slope <- x[12]
  ad_effects <- x[13:17]
  elasticity <- x[18:21]
  seasonality <- x[22:25]

  # --- Recalculate Ad Stocks ---
  digital_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
  print_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
  tv_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
  outdoor_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))
  rnd_stock <- numeric(nrow(data))

  for (i in 1:nrow(data)) {
    if (i == 1) {
      digital_stock[i] <- lambda[1] * digital_in + data$Digital[i]
      print_stock[i] <- lambda[2] * print_in + data$Print[i]
      tv_stock[i] <- lambda[3] * tv_in + data$TV[i]
      outdoor_stock[i] <- lambda[4] * outdoor_in + data$Outdoor[i]
      rnd_stock[i] <- lambda[5] * rnd_in + data$RnD[i]
    } else {
      digital_stock[i] <- lambda[1] * digital_stock[i-1] + data$Digital[i]
      print_stock[i] <- lambda[2] * print_stock[i-1] + data$Print[i]
      tv_stock[i] <- lambda[3] * tv_stock[i-1] + data$TV[i]
      outdoor_stock[i] <- lambda[4] * outdoor_stock[i-1] + data$Outdoor[i]
      rnd_stock[i] <- lambda[5] * rnd_stock[i-1] + data$RnD[i]
    }
  }

  # --- Calculate Predicted Sales ---
  predicted_sales <- A *
    (data$qtrn + trend_slope) * # Trend component
    (ad_effects[1]*digital_stock +
       ad_effects[2]*print_stock +
       ad_effects[3]*tv_stock +
       ad_effects[4]*outdoor_stock +
       ad_effects[5]*rnd_stock) * # Ad stock component
    (data$Price^elasticity[1]) * # Price elasticity
    (data$Comp_Price^elasticity[2]) * # Competitor price elasticity
    (data$Margin^elasticity[3]) * # Margin elasticity
    (data$Comp_Margin^elasticity[4]) * # Competitor margin elasticity
    (seasonality[data$Quarter]) # Seasonal component

  # --- Calculate RMSE ---
  error <- sqrt(mean((data$Sales - predicted_sales)^2))

  # Return the error
  return(error)
}
```

## Running the Optimization

Now we can call the `optim()` function with our starting values and the `sales_error_function`.

1.  **Basic Optimization (Unbounded):**
    First, let's run a basic optimization without specifying bounds.

    ```R
    # Run optimization without bounds
    optimization_result_unbounded <- optim(par = starting, fn = sales_error_function)

    # Print the result
    print("Unbounded Optimization Result:")
    print(optimization_result_unbounded$par) # Optimized parameters
    print(optimization_result_unbounded$value) # Minimum RMSE
    ```

    The output shows the optimized parameters and the minimized RMSE. Note that some parameters, like ad effects, might turn out negative, which might not be theoretically desired (advertising is expected to increase sales, implying a positive beta).

2.  **Bounded Optimization:**
    Let's run the optimization again, this time using the "L-BFGS-B" method and providing the lower and upper bounds we defined. This method respects the bounds during optimization.

    ```R
    # Run optimization with bounds
    optimization_result_bounded <- optim(
      par = starting,
      fn = sales_error_function,
      method = "L-BFGS-B",
      lower = lower,
      upper = upper
    )

    # Print the result
    print("Bounded Optimization Result:")
    print(optimization_result_bounded$par) # Optimized parameters
    print(optimization_result_bounded$value) # Minimum RMSE
    ```

    The bounded optimization typically results in a lower RMSE (better fit) and parameters that respect the defined bounds (e.g., positive ad effects). The output vector `optimization_result_bounded$par` now contains the estimated values for all 25 parameters in the order they were defined in the `starting` vector.

## Conclusion

This process allows us to estimate the impact of various marketing mix elements (including the lasting effects of advertising through ad stock) on sales. By minimizing the error between the actual sales and the sales predicted by our model, the `optim()` function finds the parameter values that best describe the historical relationship between our marketing efforts and sales.

Once we have the optimized parameters, we can:
*   Understand the relative effectiveness of different marketing channels (comparing the ad effects/betas).
*   Understand price and margin sensitivity (from elasticity values).
*   Quantify trend and seasonality.
*   Use the fitted model to predict sales for different levels of investment or price changes.
*   Potentially use this model within a further optimization framework to maximize sales or profit under budget constraints.

Implementing this kind of model requires careful structuring of the code and understanding how the optimization function works. It might be challenging initially, but practicing with data is key.

We have covered the core mechanics of fitting a marketing mix model with ad stock and other variables. In the next lecture, we will talk about different types of media data.

Thank you for your time. I will meet you in the next video.

```markdown
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 33: Marketing Mix Models and Advertising Models (Contd.)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee, VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics Course. We are in the final phase of week 6, and in this video, we will focus on **Media Data**, specifically how to decide ad placement in TV programs.

We've previously discussed marketing mix models to determine budget allocation across channels (like digital, TV). Now, let's say we've allocated a specific amount (`x`) for TV advertising. The problem is how to distribute this budget among various TV programs.

TV advertising involves placing 30-second or 20-second ads. Each program has specific rates, which typically increase with its popularity, demographics of its audience, and other factors.

## The Problem: Maximizing Impressions While Minimizing Cost

Given a TV budget, the goal is twofold:
1.  **Maximize Impression:** Reach the largest possible audience across target demographic segments.
2.  **Minimize Cost:** Spend the allocated budget efficiently.

This is an optimization problem. We need to decide *how many ads* to place in each program to achieve our impression goals at the minimum cost.

## Data Description

Let's look at the dataset provided (presumably an Excel sheet).

The dataset includes:
*   **Source (A1 to A9):** Different TV programs or shows.
*   **Number of Ads:** The number of ads you decide to place in each show. This is our **decision variable**.
*   **Per 30 Second Cost:** The cost of placing one 30-second ad in each program.

## Calculating Total Cost

The total cost of the advertising campaign is the sum of the cost incurred for ads in each program.
The total cost is calculated using the `SUMPRODUCT` function in Excel:

```excel
=SUMPRODUCT(B6:B14, C6:C14)
```
Where:
*   `B6:B14` contains the "Number of Ads" for programs A1 to A9.
*   `C6:C14` contains the "Per 30 Second Cost" for programs A1 to A9.

This is the value we will try to minimize.

## Demographic Profiles and Exposure

The dataset also provides demographic information about the audience for each program.
*   **Demographic Segments:**
    *   Women: 18-30, 31-40, 41-50, >50 years old
    *   Men: 18-30, 31-40, 41-50, >50 years old
*   **Population Size:** The total number of people (in millions) within each demographic segment in the target market (e.g., USA).
    *   Example: 20 million in Women 18-30 segment, 23 million in Women 31-40, etc.
*   **Percentage Watching:** The percentage of each demographic segment that watches a specific TV program.

This percentage data helps understand the audience composition of each program. For example, a program with high percentages for women might be female-dominated, while another might be male-dominated or broadly popular across all segments (which often correlates with higher ad costs).

## Calculating Total Exposure per Segment

The total exposure for a specific demographic segment is calculated by considering the number of ads placed in *each* program and the percentage of that segment watching that program.

For a given segment (e.g., Women 18-30) with a population of `Population_Segment`, the total exposure is:

```excel
=Population_Segment * SUMPRODUCT(Number_of_Ads_Program_A1:A9, %_Watching_Segment_Program_A1:A9)
```

In the Excel sheet, if:
*   `D4` is the Population for Women 18-30
*   `B6:B14` is the "Number of Ads" column
*   `D6:D14` is the "% Watching" for Women 18-30 for programs A1 to A9

The exposure for Women 18-30 would be:

```excel
=D4 * SUMPRODUCT($B$6:$B$14, D6:D14)
```
Note the use of `$` for absolute referencing (`$B$6:$B$14`) so that when this formula is dragged across columns (for other demographic segments), the reference to the "Number of Ads" column remains fixed, while the "% Watching" column reference updates automatically.

This calculation is repeated for all demographic segments.

## Simple Model: Optimization with Hard Constraints

In the simple model, we have hard constraints on minimum required exposure levels for each demographic segment.

**Objective:** Minimize Total Cost (`B19`).

**Decision Variables:** Number of Ads for each program (B6:B14).

**Constraints:**
1.  **Integer:** The number of ads must be integers (whole numbers).
2.  **Bounds:** The number of ads must be within a certain range (e.g., 0 to 20). An upper bound is needed for integer programming in Solver.
3.  **Minimum Exposure:** The calculated exposure for each demographic segment must be greater than or equal to the defined exposure goal for that segment.
    *   In Excel terms: `D15:K15 >= D17:K17` (where D15:K15 are calculated exposures and D17:K17 are the goals).

**Solver Setup (in Excel):**
*   Set Objective: `B19` to Minimize.
*   By Changing Variable Cells: `B6:B14`.
*   Subject to the Constraints:
    *   `B6:B14 = integer`
    *   `B6:B14 <= 20`
    *   `B6:B14 >= 0`
    *   `D15:K15 >= D17:K17`
*   Select a Solving Method: `Evolutionary` is often suitable for non-linear or integer problems, though Simplex might work for the initial simple case if constraints are linear. Evolutionary was used in the demo.

**Execution:** Running Solver finds the combination of ads (B6:B14) that minimizes B19 while satisfying all constraints. The example run resulted in a cost of around 3216. The output shows which programs receive ads (e.g., program A4 received 3 ads, A6 received 2, A7 received 2, etc.) to meet the goals.

**Complexity:** If the demographic profiles differed significantly across channels or regions, the problem would become more complex, potentially requiring a more powerful tool like R for modeling. However, the core principle of defining variables, calculating outputs (cost, exposure), setting objectives and constraints, and optimizing remains the same.

## Complex Model: Non-linear Cost and Penalties

We can introduce more realistic complexities:
1.  **Non-linear Ad Cost:** The cost per ad might decrease as you buy more ads for a single program (economies of scale). Instead of a fixed "Per 30 Second Cost," we have a lookup table showing the *total* cost for buying 1, 2, ..., up to 10 ads for each program.
2.  **Penalties for Missing Goals:** The exposure goals might not be strict, hard constraints. Instead, there might be a penalty cost for every unit of exposure a segment falls short of its goal.

**Non-linear Cost Calculation:**

We need to look up the cost based on the *number of ads* placed in each program.
A lookup table is provided, where the first row indicates the number of ads (1, 2, ..., 10) and subsequent rows provide the total cost for that number of ads for each program (A1, A2, ...).

The `HLOOKUP` function can be used:
```excel
=HLOOKUP(Number_of_Ads, Cost_Lookup_Table, Row_Index_for_Program, FALSE)
```

*   `Number_of_Ads`: The value from the B column (e.g., B6 for A1).
*   `Cost_Lookup_Table`: The range containing the numbers of ads (row 1) and the corresponding costs (e.g., C2:L14). This range should be absolute (`$C$2:$L$14`).
*   `Row_Index_for_Program`: This indicates which row in the `Cost_Lookup_Table` corresponds to the current program. This can be derived from the row number of the program entry in the main data table (e.g., L6 for A1, which is 1, plus 1 because HLOOKUP rows are 1-indexed).

**Correction for 0 Ads:** The `HLOOKUP` table starts from 1 ad. If 0 ads are placed, the cost is 0. The `HLOOKUP` needs to be wrapped in an `IF` statement:

```excel
=IF(Number_of_Ads > 0, HLOOKUP(Number_of_Ads, Cost_Lookup_Table, Row_Index_for_Program, FALSE), 0)
```

This formula is applied for each program to get its non-linear cost. The **Total Non-linear Ad Cost** is the sum of these costs.

**Penalty Calculation:**

For each demographic segment, calculate the deficit if exposure is below the goal:
```excel
=IF(Calculated_Exposure < Exposure_Goal, Exposure_Goal - Calculated_Exposure, 0)
```
Where:
*   `Calculated_Exposure` is the value calculated earlier for each segment.
*   `Exposure_Goal` is the target value for the segment.

The **Total Penalty** is the sum of these deficits across all segments, multiplied by a per-unit penalty rate (e.g., 1000 per missing thousand exposure).

**New Objective Function:**

The total cost to minimize is now:
`Total Cost = Total Non-linear Ad Cost + Total Penalty`

**Solver Setup (in Excel):**
*   Set Objective: Minimize the cell containing the `Total Cost` (e.g., B21).
*   By Changing Variable Cells: `B6:B14` (Number of Ads).
*   Subject to the Constraints:
    *   `B6:B14 = integer`
    *   `B6:B14 <= 10` (based on the lookup table limit)
    *   `B6:B14 >= 0`
*   Note: The hard exposure constraint `D15:K15 >= D17:K17` is REMOVED as the penalty now handles deviations below the goal.

**Execution:** Running Solver with these settings finds the number of ads (B6:B14) that minimizes the *total* cost, including potential penalties. The example run resulted in a minimum total cost of around 3087. In this specific optimal solution, the penalty happened to be zero, meaning the goals were met within the 0-10 ad limit at this minimum total cost. The ad allocation was different from the simple model (e.g., more ads in A1, A2, A3, zero in others).

## Further Model Extensions

The model can be extended further:
*   **Sales Optimization:** Instead of just impression goals, you could incorporate estimated conversion rates from impressions to sales for each segment. The objective could then be to maximize total sales or maximize sales per dollar spent, subject to budget constraints.
*   **Other Metrics:** Incorporate engagement metrics, brand lift, etc., depending on available data and objectives.

The general approach remains:
1.  Identify **decision variables** (e.g., number of ads).
2.  Calculate relevant **output metrics** (cost, exposure, sales, penalty).
3.  Define **objectives** (minimize cost, maximize sales) and **constraints** (budget, minimums/maximums).
4.  Use optimization tools (like Excel Solver, or programming in R/Python) to find the optimal values for the decision variables.

## Conclusion

This concludes our discussion on marketing mix models and advertising models for this week. We have seen how to allocate advertising budgets across media vehicles using optimization techniques in Excel, considering demographic data, varying costs, and different types of constraints (hard goals vs. penalties).

Next week, we will move into **Detail Level Analytics**, starting with exciting topics like:
*   **Recommendation Engines:** How systems suggest products (e.g., "Customers who bought this also bought...").
*   **Market Basket Analysis:** Identifying items that are frequently purchased together (e.g., "People who buy beer also buy diapers").

Thank you for being with me. I will see you in the next week.
```

Lecture Name: Lecture 34 : recommendation engine and retail analytics

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 34: Recommendation Engine and Retail Analytics

Hello everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are studying week 7, session 1, and in week 7, we will discuss about Recommendation Engine and Retail Analytics.

## Introduction to E-commerce and Retail Analytics

In the next couple of weeks (Week 7 and Week 8), we will majorly focus on e-commerce. Why e-commerce? E-commerce is growing significantly worldwide. Internet access is becoming widespread and affordable, even in rural areas, thanks to initiatives and competitive pricing from service providers. This widespread internet availability has led to the rise of many e-commerce firms like Flipkart, Amazon in India, and B2B platforms like Alibaba and Blackbuck. There are also aggregator platforms that connect customers with service providers.

E-commerce is becoming a crucial aspect of retail business, operating in urban, semi-urban, and rural areas. Although the majority of retail in India (and probably globally) is still unorganized, and e-commerce is a smaller part of the organized sector, its growth rate is remarkably high. Given this background, we must focus on how Marketing Analytics can assist retail firms, specifically e-commerce firms, where application is high.

## Why Recommendation Engine? (The Long Tail)

The concept of "selling deep" is relevant here. Getting initial sales is often easy. However, monetizing the "long tail" through surprising recommendations is key.

*   **The Long Tail:** This refers to the large number of incremental sales that can be achieved by selling small amounts of many different niche products. While each individual sale might be small, collectively they can contribute significantly to revenue.
*   **Incremental Sales:** Recommendation engines drive these incremental sales. For example, if a customer intends to buy a mobile phone and the e-commerce site recommends a protective cover, the customer might decide to buy it as an extra item. This is cross-selling.
*   **Impact:** While the conversion rate for a specific recommendation might be low (e.g., 10%), across millions of transactions, this can lead to substantial additional revenue. If 10% of 10 million mobile phone buyers also buy a ₹500 cover, that's 1 million extra units sold, generating ₹500 million in additional revenue.
*   **The "Aaha" Feeling:** Recommendations can create a sense of surprise or discovery that is appealing to the customer.
*   **Age of Recommendations:** We are moving from an age of information overload to an age where recommendations from platforms or other sources are highly valued. People tend to trust recommenders, even if they don't know the source personally, assuming they wouldn't recommend something harmful.
*   **Nudge Effect:** A simple recommendation, or "nudge," can significantly increase the chances of a customer buying an item they weren't initially planning to purchase.

## Types of Recommendations

Recommendation engines in retail, especially e-commerce, manifest in various ways:

*   **Frequently Bought Together:** Suggesting items that are commonly purchased in the same basket. (Example: Book 1 and Book 2). This is applicable when the items are purchased simultaneously.
*   **Customers Who Bought This Item Also Bought These Items:** Suggesting items that previous buyers of the current item also purchased, potentially in different transactions over a period. (Example: If someone bought Book 1, they also often bought Book 2). This is applicable post-purchase or when viewing a product page.
*   **What Other Items Do Customers Buy After Viewing This Item?** Suggesting items that customers purchased after viewing a specific item, even if they didn't buy the viewed item itself. (Example: A customer views Book 1 but decides to buy Book 2 instead). This is applicable during the information search or evaluation stage of the customer journey.

The data required for these recommendations can differ:

*   **"Frequently Bought Together":** Requires analyzing items within the same purchase basket.
*   **"Customers Who Bought This Item Also Bought":** Requires analyzing purchase history across different baskets for the same customers.
*   **"Customers Buy After Viewing This Item":** Requires combining customer viewing data with purchase data.

While the data sources might differ initially, the underlying algorithms are often similar.

## Recommender Problem Definition

A good recommender system provides relevant suggestions:

*   **Fit:** Recommendations should be relevant to the user's needs, interests, and context.
    *   Show programming books to a software engineer.
    *   Show baby toys/books on baby upbringing to a new mother or parent.
    *   Avoid showing programming books to a mother or baby toys to someone without children.
*   **Incrementality:** Do not recommend items the user already knows about or would find/buy anyway. The goal is to drive *incremental* sales. Calculating recommendations has a cost (time, resources). This cost is only justified if it leads to a purchase that wouldn't have happened otherwise.
*   **Exploration vs. Offence:** Expand the user's taste by recommending items that are reasonably related to their interests, but avoid recommending things that are too far off or potentially offensive/annoying. The degree of "farness" is a critical decision.

## Challenges in Recommendation Engines

Building effective recommendation systems faces several challenges:

*   **Huge Amount of Data:** E-commerce platforms deal with millions of customers and millions of distinct products. Calculating similarities and predictions across such massive datasets is computationally intensive and time-consuming.
*   **Real-time Results:** Recommendations need to be presented to the user instantly while they are browsing the website or app, not hours later. This requires significant pre-computation or very efficient real-time processing.
*   **New Customers (Cold Start Problem):** New users have limited purchase or rating history, making it difficult to determine their preferences and find similar users or items.
*   **New Items (First Rater Problem):** New products have no purchase or rating history, making it impossible to recommend them using methods based on past interactions.
*   **Sparsity:** Even with many users, users typically only interact with a small fraction of the available items. This results in a highly sparse user-item matrix (lots of empty entries), making it hard to find users who have rated or purchased the *same* items.
*   **Old Customers (Glut of Information):** For long-term customers, there might be too much historical data, making it challenging to determine which interactions are most relevant to their current preferences.
*   **Volatile Customer Data:** User preferences and life stages change over time (e.g., before/after marriage, before/after having children). Recommendations need to adapt to these changes.
*   **Popularity Bias:** Collaborative filtering methods tend to recommend popular items, making it difficult to recommend niche products to users with unique tastes.

## Collaborative Filtering

This is a common and widely used approach, notably by Amazon.

*   **Concept:** Collaborative filtering leverages the collective behavior of users to make recommendations. The core idea is that if user A and user B have similar tastes (based on their past interactions like purchases or ratings), then user A might be interested in an item that user B liked but A hasn't interacted with yet.
*   **Process:**
    1.  Maintain a dataset of user interactions (ratings, purchases) for various items.
    2.  Find users who are *similar* to the current active user based on their overlapping interaction history.
    3.  Identify items that these similar users liked (rated highly or purchased) but the active user has not interacted with.
    4.  Recommend these items to the active user.
    *   **User-Based Collaborative Filtering:** Focuses on finding similar *users* first. (Amazon initially used this, but shifted).
    *   **Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering:** Focuses on finding similar *items* first (as Amazon currently does).
*   **Similarity Measurement:** Similarity between users (or items) can be calculated using various measures:
    *   Pearson Correlation
    *   Cosine Similarity
    *   Covariance, Standard Deviations
    *   Weighted combinations of these measures. The weight of similarity between two users (a and u, denoted as W_a,u) can be a function of their correlation (C_a,u) and the number of co-rated/co-purchased items (m). A common weighting scheme might give more importance if the number of shared items is high.
*   **Prediction:** The predicted rating or likelihood of purchase for an item for the active user (a) can be calculated as a weighted sum of the ratings (or behaviors) of similar users (u) for that item (i), adjusted by the average rating of the active user (ra).
    *   Formula: `Prediction(a, i) = ra + SUM [W_a,u * (r_u,i - r_u)] / SUM [|W_a,u|]` (Where r_u,i is user u's rating for item i, r_u is user u's average rating, and W_a,u is the similarity weight between user a and user u).
*   **Selecting Neighbors:** Recommendations are typically based on the ratings/purchases of:
    *   The *n* most similar users.
    *   All users whose similarity weight exceeds a certain threshold.

## Content-Based Filtering

This approach focuses on the characteristics of the items themselves.

*   **Concept:** Recommendations are based on the properties or features of the items the user has liked or interacted with in the past. If a user liked item X, which has feature F, they might be interested in other items that also have feature F.
*   **Process:**
    1.  Extract meaningful features from the content of each item (e.g., movie genre, actors, director; book topic, author, keywords; product specifications).
    2.  Create a profile of the user's preferences based on the features of the items they have interacted with positively (rated highly, purchased).
    3.  Recommend items whose features match the user's profile.
    *   Examples: Recommending newspaper articles based on topics the user has read, recommending movies based on genre and actors liked.
*   **Advantages:**
    *   **No Cold Start for Items:** Can recommend new items as long as their content features are defined, even if no one has interacted with them yet.
    *   **No need for other users' data:** Recommendations are based solely on the active user's past interactions and item content, avoiding the cold start problem for *new users* (though initial interactions are still needed to build a profile) and sparsity.
    *   **Handles Unique Tastes:** Can recommend niche items if their features match the user's specific profile.
    *   **Provides Explanations:** It is easier to explain *why* an item was recommended (e.g., "You are seeing this because you liked items with [feature X]").
*   **Disadvantages:**
    *   **Content Representation:** Requires item content to be structured and convertible into meaningful features for mathematical calculation. Not all content is easily encoded this way.
    *   **User Profile Learning:** Requires learning a function that maps user preferences to item content features, which might not capture all nuances of user taste.
    *   **Limited Exploration:** Tends to recommend items similar to what the user already likes, potentially limiting exposure to diverse items or genres.
    *   **Ignores Quality Judgments:** Does not explicitly consider the ratings or opinions of other users, which can be a valuable signal of item quality or popularity.

## Conclusion

We have discussed the importance of recommendation engines in retail analytics, particularly in e-commerce, focusing on driving incremental sales in the "long tail." We introduced the challenges and two major types of recommendation systems: Collaborative Filtering and Content-Based Filtering. Collaborative filtering relies on the preferences of similar users, while content-based filtering relies on the features of the items themselves. Both have their strengths and weaknesses.

In the next video, we will delve deeper into User-Based Collaborative Filtering.

Thank you.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 35 : recommendation engine and retail analytics (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 35: Recommendation Engine and Retail Analytics (Contd.)

Hello everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 7, session 2, and we are discussing recommendation engines and retail analytics. We were specifically discussing collaborative filtering, and I have discussed quite a lot in the last video, but I will continue from there.

So, here we will talk about user-based and then item-based collaborative filtering. These are the two things we will discuss in detail.

## Collaborative Filtering: User-Based vs. Item-Based

The approach is similar. You have to find out a similarity matrix between the customers.

### Rating-Based Collaborative Filtering

Previously, I talked about the rating-based approach. In the rating-based approach, we create the similarity based on the correlation of the ratings. Because ratings are often given in a 1 to 5 point scale, we can find out correlations of that.

### Purchase Decision-Based Collaborative Filtering

On the other hand, if it is a purchased decision-based approach, the approach is to represent a customer as an N-dimensional vector of items.

*   **Dimension of the vector:** N, where N is the total number of items.
*   **Vector values:**
    *   Positive (e.g., 1) for purchased or positively rated items.
    *   Negative (e.g., -1) for negatively purchased or negatively rated items.
    *   Zero (e.g., 0) for items not purchased.
*   **Similarity matrix:** Cosine matrix between the customers.

So, the similarity matrix is cosine, where I find out a cosine matrix between the customers and then generate recommendations based on a few customers who are most similar.

**Cosine Similarity:**
Cos theta represents the angle between two vectors. The smaller the angle, the more similar the vectors. Cos 0 is 1. Therefore, the higher the cosine value, the higher is the similarity, because the theta (the angle) is smaller. This is something that we will try to deal with.

### User-Based Collaborative Filtering Details

*   **Approach:** Find customers most similar to the target user based on their purchase history/ratings vector (using cosine similarity).
*   **Recommendation:** Rank each item according to how many similar customers have purchased it.
*   **Computational Cost:** Computationally expensive. If there are M number of customers and N number of items, you have to create a matrix of size M x N.
*   **Challenges:**
    *   Severe performance and scaling issues for very large datasets (e.g., 10 million customers and 1 million items).
    *   Requires batch processing beforehand; cannot always be done in real-time.
    *   Dimensionality reduction (techniques like SVD, PCA) can increase performance but can also reduce the quality of the recommendation.

## Cluster Models for Recommendation

Cluster models offer a different kind of approach.

*   **Approach:** Divide the customer base into many segments (clusters) and treat the recommendation task as a classification problem.
    *   First step: Break customers into various segments (clustering).
    *   You deal with a smaller number of clusters (small m) instead of the large number of customers (capital M).
*   **Assignment:** Assign the new user to the segment containing the most similar customers (similar to K-Nearest Neighbor or K-Means Clustering).
*   **Recommendation:** Use the purchase and ratings of *only the customers within that specific segment* to generate recommendations.
*   **Advantages:**
    *   Better online scalability and performance than user-based collaborative filtering because they compare the user to a controlled number of segments.
*   **Disadvantages (Quality Issue):**
    *   Reduces the quality of the recommendation (a type of dimensionality reduction, but on users, not items).
    *   Recommendations are less relevant because the "similar customers" found by the clustering method might not be the *most* similar customers in reality, especially if the clustering is not based directly on purchase data or the similarity measure is different.
    *   To improve quality, it needs online segmentation, which is almost as expensive as finding similar customers using collaborative filtering.

Frankly speaking, collaborative filtering (user-based or item-based) is more common than cluster models for recommendation engines.

## Item-Based vs. User-Based Collaborative Filtering

There are two types of collaborative filtering: User-to-User and Item-to-Item.

Consider a matrix where rows represent users (a, b, c...) and columns represent items (1, 2, 3...). The values in the matrix could be ratings or purchase counts.

| User | Item 1 | Item 2 | Item 3 | ... | Item N |
| :--- | :----- | :----- | :----- | :-- | :----- |
| a    | R_{a1} | R_{a2} | R_{a3} | ... | R_{aN} |
| b    | R_{b1} | R_{b2} | R_{b3} | ... | R_{bN} |
| c    | R_{c1} | R_{c2} | R_{c3} | ... | R_{cN} |
| ...  | ...    | ...    | ...    | ... | ...    |
| M    | R_{M1} | R_{M2} | R_{M3} | ... | R_{MN} |

Where R represents rating or purchase count.

*   **Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering:** If you calculate similarity (like cosine) between the *columns* of this matrix, you find the similarity between items. Item X is similar to Item Y if people who bought Item X also tended to buy Item Y.
*   **User-to-User Collaborative Filtering:** If you calculate similarity (like cosine) between the *rows* of this matrix, you find the similarity between users. User A is similar to User B if they tend to purchase/rate items similarly.

In both cases, you are recommending products to users based on these similarity matchings.

### Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering Details

*   **How it works:** Matches each of the user’s purchased and rated items to similar items. Combines those similar items into a recommendation list.
*   **Algorithm:** An iterative algorithm. Builds a similar items table offline.
*   **Similarity:** Calculates the similarity between a single product and all related products using the cosine measure. The vector corresponds to an *item* and is M dimensions (corresponding to the M customers who have purchased that item).
*   **Advantage:** The number of items in an e-commerce firm is generally less than the number of customers (especially in established firms). Because the number of products is lower, it's often better to do item-to-item collaborative filtering as it takes less time computationally (M dimensions in the vector, where M is user count).
*   **Computation:**
    *   Offline computation: Builds a similar item table. This calculation is time-intensive and done beforehand (batch processing).
    *   Online recommendation: Given a similar item table, the algorithm finds items similar to each of the user's purchased and rated items, aggregates them, and recommends the most popular/correlated ones.
*   **Scalability:** Scales independently of the catalog size or the number of customers. It only depends on the catalog size (which is usually smaller than the number of customers).
*   **Quality:** Recommendation quality is often excellent since it recommends highly correlated similar items.

## Comparison of Models (Scalability and Quality)

| Method                             | Scalability                                                                 | Quality                                                 |
| :--------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **User-Based Collaborative Filtering** | Impractical for large datasets; computationally expensive.                  | Good, but dimensionality reduction reduces quality.     |
| **Cluster Models**                 | Better online scalability due to comparison with controlled number of segments. | Relatively poor; recommendations can be less relevant. |
| **Item-Based Collaborative Filtering** | Excellent; expensive table built offline; online lookup is fast and scales independently of user count. | Excellent; recommends highly correlated similar items. |

Generally, Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering is considered better for large-scale systems due to its favorable balance of scalability and quality.

## Empirical Evidence (MovieLens Dataset)

Studies have been conducted using the MovieLens dataset (1 million ratings, 6040 users, 3090 movies - number of movies is lower than users).

*   **Finding:** The best overall results (considering both computation time and recommendation accuracy) are often reached by Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering.
*   **Example Results (Illustrative):**
    *   Item-to-Item CF: Computation time ~170 seconds, Mean Absolute Error (MAE) ~0.6382, Prediction time ~3 seconds.
    *   Other methods might have slightly lower MAE (higher accuracy, e.g., 0.66, 0.67) but take significantly longer computation time.

Item-to-Item gives similar results but with much less time.

## Related Applications

Some real-world applications using these concepts:

1.  **Pandora (Music Recommendation):**
    *   Based on the Music Genome Project data.
    *   Assigns ~400 attributes (done by musicians) for each song (takes about half an hour per song).
    *   Uses this method (content-based, but can be viewed as item-based on attributes) to find songs similar to user's favorites.
    *   **Benefit:** Accurate, doesn't need lots of user information initially.
    *   **Drawback:** Doesn't scale perfectly well for discovery across a vast, rapidly changing library; library can feel limited at times.

2.  **Netflix (Movie Recommendation):**
    *   Based on ratings, watching, and searching habits.
    *   Recommends based on similar users AND movies sharing characteristics with rated films.
    *   **Hybrid Model:** Combines collaborative filtering (user/item), content-based, knowledge-based, and demographic techniques.
    *   Uses an ensemble method of many different algorithm approaches to leverage strengths and overcome shortcomings of individual techniques.

3.  **Google YouTube (Video Recommendation):**
    *   Focuses on bringing videos to users that they believe users will be interested in to increase video views, length of time watched, and maximize enjoyment (ultimately increasing ad revenue).
    *   Shifted its recommendation system around 2010 from a random walk approach to one based on Amazon's Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering.
    *   Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering appears to be effective for video recommendation.

## Summary of Recommendation Approaches Covered

We have learned about:

*   User-to-User Collaborative Filtering
*   Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering
*   Cluster-Based Recommendation Engines
*   Content-Based Methods (mentioned in applications)
*   Hybrid/Ensemble Methods (combining multiple approaches)

These are the core concepts we will try to use in smaller hands-on problems in the next videos.

Thank you for being with me in this video. I will come back with the next video in terms of collaborative filtering. First, we will do Item-to-Item.

Thank you very much.
