# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 36: Recommendation Engine and Retail Analytics (contd.)

This lecture focuses on a hands-on session for building an Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering recommendation engine using R.

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This session, part of Week 7, continues the discussion on recommendation engines, specifically focusing on a practical implementation of item-to-item collaborative filtering.

## Data and Setup

The implementation will use an R script named `easy2.r`. This script is a simplified version of a more complex online example, made easier to understand for beginners.

The data used is a user-item matrix.
*   Rows represent users.
*   Columns represent items (in this case, FM radio channels or songs).
*   The cell values are binary (0 or 1), indicating whether a user has listened to (or interacted with) a specific item (1 if yes, 0 if no).

This structure is crucial for building the recommendation system.

Initially, a small sample file (`sample.csv`) is used for demonstration due to time constraints during the video. A larger file (`lastFM_matrix_germany.csv`) is also mentioned as available for applying the same code, although it will take longer to process.

```r
# Set working directory (assuming data file is in the source file location)
# This step is usually done via RStudio or a similar IDE.
# setwd("your_data_directory") 

# Read the smaller sample data
data.germany <- read.csv("sample.csv")

# Display dimensions of the loaded data
dim(data.germany) 
# In the small sample, this is 19 observations (users) of 13 variables (1 user ID + 12 items)

# Example of using the larger dataset (commented out for speed)
# data.germany <- read.csv("lastFM_matrix_germany.csv")
# dim(data.germany)
# The larger dataset has 1257 observations (users) of 286 variables (items)
```

## Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering Concept

In item-to-item collaborative filtering, the core idea is to find items that are similar to each other based on user interactions. If a user likes item A, and item A is similar to item B, then the system might recommend item B to that user (provided they haven't already interacted with item B).

Imagine a matrix with users as rows and products as columns (like the `data.germany` dataframe).

| User | Product 1 | Product 2 | ... | Product N |
| :--- | :-------- | :-------- | :-- | :-------- |
| 1    | 1         | 0         | ... | 1         |
| 2    | 0         | 1         | ... | 0         |
| ...  | ...       | ...       | ... | ...       |
| M    | 1         | 1         | ... | 0         |

To find similarity between two items (say, Product 3 and Product 10), we look at the columns corresponding to these items across all users. If users who interacted with Product 3 also tended to interact with Product 10, they are considered similar.

Using an analogy: If students who took Professor X's course also frequently took Professor Y's course, then Professor X and Professor Y's courses are similar in terms of student preference patterns.

The goal is to create an **Item-Item Similarity Matrix**. This matrix will have items as both rows and columns (an N x N matrix, where N is the number of items). Each cell `(i, j)` in this matrix will represent the similarity between Item `i` and Item `j`.

| Item    | Item 1   | Item 2   | ... | Item N   |
| :------ | :------- | :------- | :-- | :------- |
| Item 1  | Sim(1,1) | Sim(1,2) | ... | Sim(1,N) |
| Item 2  | Sim(2,1) | Sim(2,2) | ... | Sim(2,N) |
| ...     | ...      | ...      | ... | ...      |
| Item N  | Sim(N,1) | Sim(N,2) | ... | Sim(N,N) |

Properties of this matrix:
*   The diagonal elements (Item `i` with Item `i`) will have the highest similarity (typically 1).
*   The matrix will be symmetric (Sim(i, j) = Sim(j, i)). This means we only need to calculate one half of the matrix.

## Calculating Item Similarity

The lecture uses correlation as the measure of similarity between items. Correlation between two items is calculated across all users. If two items have a high positive correlation in their interaction patterns across users, they are considered highly similar.

First, an empty matrix `A` is created to store the item-item similarities. Its dimensions are (Number of Items) x (Number of Items). The items in the `data.germany` dataset are columns 2 through 13 (as the first column is the user ID).

```r
# Create an empty matrix to store similarities
num_items <- ncol(data.germany) - 1 # Number of items
A <- matrix(0, num_items, num_items)

# Assign column names (item names) to the matrix
colnames(A) <- colnames(data.germany)[-1]
rownames(A) <- colnames(data.germany)[-1]

# Display the empty matrix structure
A
```

Next, nested loops are used to calculate the correlation between each pair of items and populate the matrix `A`.

```r
# Calculate correlation for each pair of items
# Note: This calculates the full symmetric matrix, though only half is needed due to symmetry.
for (i in 1:num_items) {
  for (j in 1:num_items) {
    # Calculate correlation between column i+1 and column j+1 of the original data
    A[i, j] <- cor(data.germany[, (i + 1)], data.germany[, (j + 1)])
  }
}

# Display the filled similarity matrix
A
```

The resulting matrix `A` shows the pairwise correlations between all items. Diagonal elements are 1, and the matrix is symmetric. Correlation values range from -1 to +1.

## Normalizing Similarity Scores

To ensure all similarity scores are positive and scaled consistently (e.g., between 0 and 1), min-max normalization is applied to the similarity matrix `A`.

The formula used is: `Normalized Value = (Original Value - Minimum Value) / (Maximum Value - Minimum Value)`

```r
# Normalize the similarity matrix A
min.val <- min(A)
max.val <- max(A)
A <- (A - min.val) / (max.val - min.val)

# Display the normalized similarity matrix
A
# Now values range between 0 and 1.
```

## Creating the Recommendation Matrix

An empty matrix `r` is created to store the recommendation scores for each user and each item. This matrix has the same dimensions as the original user-item data (Number of Users x Number of Items).

```r
# Create an empty recommendation matrix with the same dimensions as original data
r <- data.germany

# Set all item columns to 0 initially
r[, 2:(num_items + 1)] <- 0

# Display the empty recommendation matrix structure
r
```

## Calculating Recommendation Scores

For each user and each item they *have not* interacted with, a recommendation score is calculated.

The logic is:
1.  Identify an item `j` that user `i` has *not* seen/interacted with.
2.  Find items that are most similar to item `j` (using the similarity matrix `A`).
3.  Check if user `i` has interacted with any of these similar items (using the original data `data.germany`).
4.  The recommendation score for item `j` for user `i` is based on the similarity scores of item `j` with the similar items that user `i` *has* interacted with. A higher score suggests a stronger recommendation.

The formula implicitly used (or concept explained) is related to weighted sum: `Score(i, j) = Sum (Similarity(j, k) * Interaction(i, k)) / Sum (Similarity(j, k))` for items `k` similar to `j`. The `Interaction(i, k)` is 1 if user `i` interacted with item `k`, and 0 otherwise. The denominator `Sum(Similarity(j, k))` could be the sum of similarities to *all* similar items, or just the ones the user interacted with, depending on the exact implementation detail. The R code uses `sum(h * k) / sum(k)` where `h` is the interaction vector for similar items and `k` is the similarity vector for similar items.

```r
# Calculate recommendation scores for each user and item
for (i in 1:nrow(data.germany)) { # Loop through users
  for (j in 1:num_items) {       # Loop through items (columns 2 to num_items+1)

    # Check if the user has already interacted with the item (data.germany[, j+1] is 1)
    # If the user HAS interacted, the recommendation score remains 0 (as initialized)
    if (data.germany[i, (j + 1)] != 1) { # Only calculate if the user HAS NOT interacted

      # Get the column of similarities for item j from the normalized similarity matrix A
      # k represents the similarities of item j with all other items
      k <- A[, j]

      # Get the original interaction history for user i across all items (excluding user ID)
      # h represents whether user i interacted with each item
      # We add 1 because A is indexed from 1 to num_items, but data.germany items are columns 2 to num_items+1
      h <- data.germany[i, 2:(num_items + 1)]

      # Calculate the weighted sum of similarities based on user history
      # The score is the sum of (similarity * interaction) for items the user has seen,
      # divided by the sum of similarities of all items (or similar items, depending on logic)
      # The code implementation shown in the video uses sum(h * k) / sum(k)
      # where k is the *full* similarity column vector, not just top similar items.
      # A more typical approach might use only the similarities of top N similar items
      # and only sum those where h is 1.
      # Let's stick to the code as presented:
      
      # Calculate the numerator: sum of similarities * interactions
      numerator <- sum(h * k)

      # Calculate the denominator: sum of all similarities for item j
      denominator <- sum(k) # This sum is over all similarities of item j with other items

      # Calculate the recommendation score
      if (denominator > 0) { # Avoid division by zero
        r[i, (j + 1)] <- numerator / denominator
      } else {
        r[i, (j + 1)] <- 0 # Should not happen if A is normalized correctly and not all zeros
      }
    }
  }
}

# Display the recommendation score matrix
r
# Scores are 0 if the user already saw the item or if numerator is 0 (user saw no similar items)
# Otherwise, scores are between 0 and 1, indicating recommendation strength.
```

*(Self-correction based on transcript review): The code snippet `sum(h * k) / sum(k)` calculates the weighted average similarity. `h` is the user's history (0s and 1s for all items), `k` is the similarity of the target item `j` with *all* other items. The numerator `sum(h * k)` sums the similarities of item `j` *only* with the items the user `i` has seen (`h` is 1). The denominator `sum(k)` is the sum of similarities of item `j` with *all* other items. This gives a score relative to the total possible similarity, weighted by seen items.*

## Generating Top N Recommendations

Finally, for each user, the recommendation matrix `r` is sorted to find the items with the highest recommendation scores. The top N items (here, N=5) that the user hasn't seen are selected as recommendations.

An empty matrix `recommendation` is created to store the final list of recommended item names for each user.

```r
# Create a matrix to hold the top 5 recommendations for each user
num_recommendations <- 5
recommendation <- matrix("", nrow(data.germany), num_recommendations)

# Assign user names as row names
rownames(recommendation) <- data.germany$user

# Assign generic column names for recommendations
colnames(recommendation) <- paste0("Reco", 1:num_recommendations)

# Populate the recommendation matrix
for (i in 1:nrow(data.germany)) { # Loop through users

  # Get the recommendation scores for user i (excluding the user ID column)
  user_scores <- r[i, 2:(num_items + 1)]

  # Order the items based on their recommendation scores in decreasing order
  # The result is the *indices* of the items in decreasing order of score
  ordered_indices <- order(user_scores, decreasing = TRUE)

  # Select the indices of the top N recommendations
  top_indices <- ordered_indices[1:num_recommendations]

  # Get the actual names of the items corresponding to the top indices
  # Remember to add 1 because the original data's item columns are 2 to num_items+1
  top_item_names <- colnames(data.germany)[top_indices + 1]

  # Store the recommended item names in the recommendation matrix
  recommendation[i, ] <- top_item_names
}

# Display the final recommendations
recommendation
```

The output matrix `recommendation` shows the top 5 recommended items for each user based on the item-to-item collaborative filtering logic implemented. Items with a score of 0 (either already seen or user saw no similar items) would appear lower in the ranked list, and the top 5 are chosen from the highest scoring items the user hasn't seen.

## Summary of Steps

The process for Item-to-Item Collaborative Filtering involved:
1.  Starting with a User-Item interaction matrix (M users, N items).
2.  Calculating an Item-Item Similarity matrix (N x N) based on user interaction patterns (using correlation here).
3.  Normalizing the similarity scores (e.g., to a 0-1 range).
4.  Creating an empty Recommendation Score matrix (M users, N items).
5.  For each user and for each item they have *not* seen, calculating a recommendation score based on the similarity of that item to other items the user *has* seen.
6.  For each user, sorting the recommendation scores and selecting the top N items (that they haven't seen) as the final recommendations.

This completes the hands-on session for item-to-item collaborative filtering. The same process can be applied to larger datasets like `lastFM_matrix_germany.csv`. The next lecture will cover User-to-User (User-Based) Collaborative Filtering.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 37 : recommendation engine and retail analytics (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 37: Recommendation Engine and Retail Analytics (Contd.)

This lecture, presented by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur, continues the discussion on Recommendation Engines, focusing specifically on **User-Based Collaborative Filtering (CF)**. Building upon the previous session's item-to-item collaborative filtering, this video explains how to find similarity between users and use that to generate recommendations.

## Introduction to User-Based Collaborative Filtering

In User-Based CF, the core idea is to find users who are similar to the target user and recommend items that these similar users liked or interacted with, but the target user has not yet experienced. This contrasts with Item-Based CF, which finds items similar to those the target user liked.

The process typically involves:
1.  Finding users similar to the target user.
2.  Identifying items that similar users interacted with.
3.  Excluding items the target user has already interacted with.
4.  Generating recommendations based on the interactions of similar users with the remaining items.

We will use the same dataset as the previous video, `sample.csv`, to demonstrate this concept using R.

## Setting Up the Environment and Loading Data

First, we need to set the working directory and load the data.

```r
# Set session working directory to source file location
# Session -> Set Working Directory -> To Source File Location

# Read the dataset
data.Germany <- read.csv("sample.csv")

# Display the structure and dimensions of the dataset
str(data.Germany)
dim(data.Germany)
```

The dataset `sample.csv` contains 19 observations (users) and 13 variables (columns), including a user ID column and columns representing interaction (0 or 1) with 12 different songs/movies.

## Data Preparation: Handling Cold-Start Users

In User-Based CF, users who have not interacted with *any* item in the dataset cannot be recommended anything based on their interaction history or similarity to other users' interactions. These are considered **cold-start users**. We need to remove them from the analysis.

We can identify these users by checking the sum of their interactions across all items. If the sum is 0, they have not interacted with anything.

```r
# Check row sums for interaction columns (excluding the user ID column)
# This returns a logical vector: TRUE if row sum > 0, FALSE otherwise
rowSums(data.Germany[, 2:13]) > 0

# Subset the data to keep only users with at least one interaction
data.Germany <- data.Germany[rowSums(data.Germany[, 2:13]) > 0, ]

# Check the dimensions again - the number of observations should decrease
dim(data.Germany)
```

After this filtering, the dataset should have fewer observations (users) because those with no interactions have been removed.

## Calculating User-to-User Similarity

Now we need to calculate the similarity between each pair of users. Since our interaction data is binary (0 or 1), we can use metrics like **correlation**, **cosine similarity**, or **Euclidean distance** (standardized).

The instructor uses **correlation** here as it's generally well-understood. To calculate user-to-user correlation, we need to treat each row (user) as a vector of item interactions and find the correlation *between* these row vectors. The `cor()` function in R calculates column-wise correlation by default. To calculate row-wise correlation, we need to **transpose** the data matrix so that users become columns and items become rows.

We also drop the first column (User ID) before calculating correlation, as it's not interaction data.

```r
# Calculate the correlation matrix of the transposed interaction data
# Exclude the first column (User ID) before transposing
a <- cor(t(data.Germany[, -1]))

# Convert the resulting matrix to a data frame for easier manipulation
a <- as.data.frame(a)

# Display the structure and dimensions of the correlation matrix
str(a)
dim(a)
```

The resulting data frame `a` is a user-by-user similarity matrix. The diagonal elements will be 1 (correlation of a user with themselves), and off-diagonal elements represent the correlation between different users based on their interaction patterns.

## Naming Rows and Columns

To make the similarity matrix more readable, we assign the actual user IDs as row and column names.

```r
# Assign column names using the User IDs from the original data
colnames(a) <- data.Germany$user

# Assign row names using the same User IDs (since it's a square matrix)
rownames(a) <- data.Germany$user

# Display the first few rows of the named similarity matrix
head(a)
```

Now the rows and columns are labeled with the user IDs, making it clear which users are being compared.

## Normalizing Similarity Scores

The instructor demonstrates normalizing the similarity scores to a range between 0 and 1. While not strictly necessary for generating recommendations (as relative scores matter), it can be useful for interpreting or comparing scores across different datasets.

The normalization formula used is:
`Normalized X = (X - Minimum X) / (Maximum X - Minimum X)`

```r
# Apply the normalization formula to the entire similarity data frame 'a'
# min(a) finds the minimum value across the whole data frame
# max(a) finds the maximum value across the whole data frame
a <- (a - min(a)) / (max(a) - min(a))

# Display the first few rows of the normalized similarity matrix
head(a)
```

All values in `a` should now be between 0 and 1.

## Generating Recommendations

Now we generate the recommendation matrix. This matrix will store a "recommendation score" for each item for each user, indicating how strongly that item should be recommended.

First, create a placeholder data frame `r` with the same dimensions and user IDs as the filtered data, but initialized with zeros.

```r
# Create a new data frame 'r' with the same structure as data.Germany
# Initialize all interaction columns with 0
r <- data.Germany
r[, -1] <- 0 # Set all columns except the first (User ID) to 0

# Display the structure and first few rows of the recommendation matrix
str(r)
head(r)
```

Now, iterate through each user (`i`) and each item (`j`). For each user-item pair, if the user has *not* already interacted with the item, calculate a recommendation score.

The recommendation score for user `i` and item `j` is calculated as a **weighted average** of the interactions of users *similar to user i* with item `j`, where the weights are the similarity scores between user `i` and their neighbors.

The steps inside the loops are:
1.  Check if user `i` has seen movie `j`. The interaction data is in `data.Germany[i, j + 1]` (j+1 because the first column is user ID).
2.  If user `i` has *not* seen movie `j` (value is 0):
    *   Get the similarity scores of user `i` with all *other* users from matrix `a`. These scores are in `a[, i]`.
    *   Sort these similarity scores in decreasing order and get the *indices* (positions) of the top N similar users (excluding user `i` themselves). In this example, the instructor looks at the top 5 most similar users (neighbors). The `order()` function with `decreasing = TRUE` gives the indices after sorting. We take indices 2 to 6 (excluding the first index which is the user themselves). Let's call these neighbors `l`.
    *   Get the interaction data of these neighbor users `l` with item `j`. This data is in `data.Germany[l, j + 1]`. Let's call these interactions `h`.
    *   Get the similarity scores between user `i` and these neighbor users `l`. These scores are from `a` at the column index `i` and row indices `l`. Let's call these scores `k`.
    *   Calculate the recommendation score for user `i` and item `j` as `sum(h * k) / sum(k)`. This is the weighted average: sum of (neighbor interaction * neighbor similarity) divided by sum of neighbor similarities.
3.  If user `i` *has* seen movie `j` (value is 1), the recommendation score remains 0 (as initialized), meaning no recommendation is needed.

```r
# Get the column names for the items
item_names <- colnames(data.Germany)[-1]

# Loop through each user (rows of data.Germany, excluding the header row)
for (i in 1:nrow(data.Germany)) {

  # Loop through each item (columns of data.Germany, excluding User ID)
  for (j in 1:ncol(data.Germany[, -1])) {

    # Check if the user has NOT seen the movie (interaction value is 0)
    if (data.Germany[i, j + 1] == 0) { # j+1 because data.Germany includes UserID

      # Get similarity scores for the current user (i) with all other users
      user_similarity_scores <- a[, i]

      # Order the similarity scores descendingly and get indices of the top N neighbors (e.g., top 5)
      # Exclude the user themselves (index 1 after sorting)
      top_n_neighbors_indices <- order(user_similarity_scores, decreasing = TRUE)[2:6]

      # Get the row indices of the top N neighbors in the original data.Germany frame
      neighbor_rows_in_data <- top_n_neighbors_indices

      # Get the historical interaction data of these neighbors for item j
      # data.Germany has UserID column, so item j is at j+1
      neighbor_interactions <- as.numeric(data.Germany[neighbor_rows_in_data, j + 1])

      # Get the similarity scores between current user (i) and these top N neighbors
      # 'a' matrix is user x user similarity
      neighbor_similarity_scores <- as.numeric(a[neighbor_rows_in_data, i])

      # Calculate the weighted recommendation score
      # Sum of (neighbor_interaction * neighbor_similarity) / Sum of neighbor_similarity
      # Add a small epsilon to the denominator to avoid division by zero if sum(neighbor_similarity_scores) is 0
      denominator <- sum(neighbor_similarity_scores)
      if (denominator > 0) {
        r[i, j + 1] <- sum(neighbor_interactions * neighbor_similarity_scores) / denominator
      } else {
        r[i, j + 1] <- 0 # Or handle appropriately, if neighbors had 0 similarity
      }
    }
    # If the user has seen the movie (data.Germany[i, j + 1] == 1), r[i, j + 1] remains 0 as initialized
  }
}

# Display the first few rows of the populated recommendation matrix
head(r)
```

The matrix `r` now contains calculated recommendation scores for items that users have not yet interacted with. Higher scores indicate stronger recommendations.

## Finalizing Recommendations: Top N Items

Finally, for each user, we sort the items based on their recommendation scores in descending order and select the top N items (e.g., top 5) to recommend.

```r
# Create a data frame to store the top recommendations
# It will have user IDs and columns for Reco 1, Reco 2, ..., Reco 5
reco_table <- data.frame(user = data.Germany$user,
                         Reco_1 = character(nrow(data.Germany)),
                         Reco_2 = character(nrow(data.Germany)),
                         Reco_3 = character(nrow(data.Germany)),
                         Reco_4 = character(nrow(data.Germany)),
                         Reco_5 = character(nrow(data.Germany)),
                         stringsAsFactors = FALSE)

# Loop through each user
for (i in 1:nrow(data.Germany)) {

  # Get the recommendation scores for the current user across all items (excluding User ID column in r)
  user_scores <- r[i, -1]

  # Order the item scores in descending order and get the column indices of the sorted items
  # We only care about the top 5
  top_5_indices <- order(user_scores, decreasing = TRUE)[1:5]

  # Get the actual item names corresponding to these top 5 indices
  # The column names of r[,-1] are the item names
  recommended_items <- colnames(r[, -1])[top_5_indices]

  # Populate the reco_table for the current user
  reco_table[i, 2:6] <- recommended_items
}

# Display the final recommendation table
head(reco_table)
```

The `reco_table` now shows the top 5 recommended items for each user based on the User-Based Collaborative Filtering approach.

## Conclusion

This video demonstrated the process of implementing User-Based Collaborative Filtering in R using a sample dataset. Key steps included filtering cold-start users, calculating user similarity (correlation in this case), and generating recommendations based on the weighted interactions of similar users. The difference from Item-Based CF lies in calculating user-to-user similarity rather than item-to-item similarity.

The instructor encourages practicing this code with a larger dataset to observe nuances and potentially encounter challenges, which can then be discussed for further learning.

## Creating Binary Ratings and User Profiles

To simplify the rating system for genre preference calculation, we convert the ratings (1-5) into a binary system (liked/disliked). A common approach is to consider ratings 4 and 5 as 'liked' and lower ratings (1, 2, 3) as 'disliked'. Due to the potential positive skew in ratings, using 4 and 5 as the threshold for 'liked' is often practical.

1.  Create a new column `binary_rating` in the ratings data.
    *   If `rating >= 4`, set `binary_rating = 1` (liked).
    *   If `rating < 4`, set `binary_rating = -1` (disliked).

    ```R
    # This code takes time for 105k ratings
    # binary_ratings <- ratings
    # binary_ratings$binary_rating <- -1 # Default to disliked
    # binary_ratings$binary_rating[binary_ratings$rating >= 4] <- 1 # Set to liked if >= 4

    # A potentially faster way using ifelse or direct indexing:
    ratings$binary_rating <- ifelse(ratings$rating >= 4, 1, -1)
    binary_ratings <- ratings # Use this for subsequent steps
    ```
    This creates a dataset with `user ID`, `movie ID`, original `rating`, `timestamp`, and `binary_rating` (1 or -1).

2.  Convert this data into a matrix format where rows are movies, columns are users, and values are the binary ratings (1, -1, or 0/NA if not rated). This is a user-item matrix. The `reshape` library can be used for this.

    ```R
    # library(reshape) # Or reshape2

    # Convert to wide format: Movies x Users
    # Assuming the movie IDs are the row identifiers
    # And user IDs are column identifiers
    # And binary_rating is the value

    # This is a conceptual representation of the reshape operation
    # The actual recommenderlab format is a sparse matrix

    # Before creating the recommenderlab sparse matrix,
    # we need a matrix where rows are movies and columns are users
    # Let's call this binary_matrix_wide
    # It will have ~10325 rows and 668 columns

    # Fill NA values (movies not rated by a user) with 0.
    # binary_matrix_wide[is.na(binary_matrix_wide)] <- 0 # Convert NA to 0

    # Remove movies that haven't been rated by ANY user.
    # Identify rows where all values are 0 (or NA before filling)
    # Keep only rows where at least one rating exists.
    # Apply the same filtering to the genre matrix to keep consistency.

    # Example: filter binary_matrix_wide
    # rated_movie_indices <- which(rowSums(abs(binary_matrix_wide)) > 0) # Find movies with at least one non-zero rating
    # binary_matrix_wide_filtered <- binary_matrix_wide[rated_movie_indices, ]

    # Update movie list if needed
    # movies_filtered <- movies[rated_movie_indices, ] # Filter movie details
    ```
    After filtering, the matrices have consistent rows (rated movies) and columns (users or genres).

## Calculating User Genre Profiles

Now we can calculate a user's overall preference for each genre. This is done by taking the dot product (matrix multiplication) of the genre matrix (movies x genres) and the filtered binary rating matrix (movies x users).

The genre matrix (`genre_matrix_filtered`) tells us for each movie, which genres it belongs to (1 or 0). The binary rating matrix (`binary_matrix_wide_filtered`) tells us for each movie, whether each user liked (1), disliked (-1), or didn't rate (0) it.

Multiplying these matrices:
*   `Result Matrix` (genres x users) = `Transpose(genre_matrix_filtered)` (genres x movies) * `binary_matrix_wide_filtered` (movies x users)

The `Result Matrix` will have dimensions 18 x 668 (genres x users). Each cell `Result Matrix[i, j]` will contain the sum of binary ratings given by user `j` for all movies belonging to genre `i`. A positive sum indicates a net preference for that genre, a negative sum indicates a net dislike, and zero means either no movies of that genre were rated or the likes/dislikes balanced out.

```R
# Example using matrix multiplication in R:
# Transpose genre_matrix_filtered (movies x genres) to genres x movies
# t(genre_matrix_filtered) # Dimensions: genres x movies

# Now multiply
# user_genre_profile <- t(genre_matrix_filtered) %*% binary_matrix_wide_filtered
# Dimensions: (genres x movies) %*% (movies x users) = genres x users

# Check the dimensions of user_genre_profile: should be 18 x 668
# This matrix represents each user's aggregated sentiment towards each genre.
```

The instructor then shows this `user_genre_profile` matrix (or a similar result) and discusses how users might have different scales of values depending on how many movies they rated. A movie buff might have high positive or negative values, while someone who rated fewer movies might have values closer to zero, even if they strongly liked/disliked the few movies they rated in a genre. This highlights the need for normalization if comparing profiles directly.

Normalization is applied to the user genre profile matrix to make scores comparable across users. The transcript mentions converting values greater than 0 to 1 and values less than or equal to 0 to 0, creating a binary preference profile (prefers genre / doesn't prefer genre).

```R
# Normalize user_genre_profile (example based on transcript's 0/1 logic)
# user_genre_profile_normalized <- ifelse(user_genre_profile > 0, 1, 0)
# This creates a binary preference profile per user per genre.
```

## Model Evaluation

Evaluating a recommendation engine is crucial to understand its performance. `recommenderlab` provides functionalities for evaluation.

1.  Set up an evaluation scheme. This involves splitting the data into training and test sets. For recommendation systems, this often means hiding some of the user's ratings (test set) and training the model on the remaining ratings (training set), then checking how well the model predicts the hidden ratings.
    *   Specify the data (`rating_matrix_recommenderlab`).
    *   Specify the method (`cross-validation`, `split`, etc.).
    *   Specify parameters for the method (e.g., `k` folds for cross-validation, `given` number of items to use for testing).
    *   Specify the minimum rating to consider as "relevant" for evaluation (e.g., `goodRating = 5` or `goodRating = 4`).

    ```R
    # Create evaluation scheme: Split data, use 10 items from each user for testing
    # goodRating = 5 means ratings of 5 are considered relevant/good recommendations
    # method = "split" means a simple train/test split
    # given = 10 means for each user in the test set, 10 random ratings are kept for training, the rest are for testing
    evaluation_scheme <- evaluationScheme(rating_matrix_recommenderlab, method = "split", given = 10, goodRating = 5)

    # You can also use cross-validation
    # evaluation_scheme_cv <- evaluationScheme(rating_matrix_recommenderlab, method = "cross-validation", k = 5, given = 10, goodRating = 5)
    ```

2.  Run the evaluation using different model configurations (e.g., varying the number of nearest neighbors `k`).

    ```R
    # Define the recommender algorithm(s) to evaluate
    # Evaluate UBCF with k=5, 10, 20 nearest neighbors
    algorithms <- list(
      "UBCF_k5" = list(name = "UBCF", param = list(method = "cosine", k = 5)),
      "UBCF_k10" = list(name = "UBCF", param = list(method = "cosine", k = 10)),
      "UBCF_k20" = list(name = "UBCF", param = list(method = "cosine", k = 20))
    )

    # Evaluate the algorithms using the evaluation scheme
    evaluation_results <- evaluate(evaluation_scheme, algorithms, n = c(1, 3, 5, 10)) # Evaluate performance for top N recommendations (1, 3, 5, 10)

    # The 'evaluation_results' object contains performance metrics (precision, recall, etc.) for each algorithm
    # Summarize the results
    # plot(evaluation_results, legend = "topright") # Visualize ROC curve or Precision-Recall curve
    ```
    The output of `evaluate` and plotting it shows how different models (e.g., with different `k` values) perform in terms of metrics like precision and recall at different recommendation list lengths (N).

## Setup and Data Loading

```r
# Set working directory to source file location (assumed step)

# Load the recommenderlab library
library(recommenderlab)

data = MovieLense
```

The MovieLense dataset contains information about movies, their years, genres, and ratings.

## Data Preparation: Creating a Sparse Matrix

The core of recommendation engines often involves working with a user-item rating matrix. Since most users only rate a small fraction of items, this matrix is typically very large and contains many empty or zero values, making it "sparse". The `recommenderlab` package is optimized to handle such sparse matrices efficiently.

The `MovieLense` object loaded initially is a `sparseDataSet`. We will convert this into a `dgTMatrix` which is a common sparse matrix format, then into a `realRatingMatrix` which is used by `recommenderlab`.

```r
# Convert sparseDataSet to dgTMatrix format
# sparse_ratings = new("dgTMatrix", i = as.integer(movie$user) - 1, j = as.integer(movie$item) - 1, x = movie$rating, Dim = c(length(unique(movie$user)), length(unique(movie$item))))
# Note: The exact code snippet from the video creating the dgTMatrix 'sparse_ratings' from the 'movie' dataset object is slightly different in variable names but the concept is the same. The MovieLense dataset is already in a format easily convertible.

# A simpler way with recommenderlab built-in data:
real_ratings = as(MovieLense, "realRatingMatrix")

# Let's check the structure and dimensions
real_ratings
```

The `realRatingMatrix` object efficiently stores the user-item ratings. It tells us the number of users (rows) and the number of items (columns), and the total number of ratings present. For the MovieLense dataset, we have 943 users and 1664 movies, with 99,392 ratings. The full matrix would have 943 * 1664 = 1,569,152 potential ratings, highlighting its sparsity.

We can add dimension names (user and movie IDs) for better readability.

```r
# Add dimension names (optional but helpful)
# Assuming the MovieLense dataset structure, the realRatingMatrix might already have these or can be added from source data if needed.
# For example, from the source data 'movie' if available after loading:
# colnames(real_ratings) = paste("m", 1:ncol(real_ratings), sep = "")
# rownames(real_ratings) = paste("u", 1:nrow(real_ratings), sep = "")
```

## Calculating Similarity

Recommendation engines often rely on similarity measures. We can calculate similarity between users (User-Based Collaborative Filtering) or between items (Item-Based Collaborative Filtering). Here, we demonstrate user-user similarity using the cosine method.

```r
# Calculate user-user similarity for the first 25 users using cosine similarity
similarity_ratings_25 = similarity(real_ratings[1:25, ], method = "cosine", which = "users")
similarity_ratings_25

# To visualize the similarity matrix (e.g., for the first 10 users for better visualization)
similarity_ratings_10 = similarity(real_ratings[1:10, ], method = "cosine", which = "users")
image(similarity_ratings_10)
```

The image plot shows the similarity matrix. Redder colors indicate higher similarity between users.

## Building and Evaluating Recommendation Models

We will build and evaluate two types of collaborative filtering models: User-Based (UBCF) and Item-Based (IBCF). We'll use a split evaluation scheme to divide the data into training (80%) and testing (20%) sets. The `given = -5` parameter means that for each user in the test set, 5 of their ratings are used as "known" items to make predictions for the remaining "unknown" items in the test set.

### User-Based Collaborative Filtering (UBCF)

UBCF finds users similar to the active user and recommends items that those similar users liked.

```r
# Define evaluation scheme
e = evaluationScheme(real_ratings, method = "split", train = 0.8, given = -5)
e

# Build UBCF model using the training data
# Parameters: normalize="center" (mean centering), method="cosine", nn=25 (nearest neighbors)
ubcf_model = Recommender(getData(e, "train"), method = "UBCF", parameter = list(normalize = "center", method = "cosine", nn = 25))
ubcf_model

# Make predictions on the test set (known items)
ubcf_predict = predict(ubcf_model, getData(e, "known"), type = "ratings")

# Calculate prediction accuracy (RMSE) on the unknown items in the test set
rmse_ubcf = calcPredictionAccuracy(ubcf_predict, getData(e, "unknown"))
rmse_ubcf
```

The RMSE for UBCF is calculated (e.g., 1.02 in the video). This indicates the average error in predicting ratings.

### Item-Based Collaborative Filtering (IBCF)

IBCF finds items similar to the items the active user liked and recommends those similar items.

```r
# Build IBCF model using the training data
# Parameters: normalize="center", method="cosine", k=350 (number of similar items)
# Note: The video demonstrated with k=35 first due to time, but k=350 is the intended larger number.
ibcf_model = Recommender(getData(e, "train"), method = "IBCF", parameter = list(normalize = "center", method = "cosine", k = 35)) # Using k=35 for quicker demo as in video
# Use k=350 for potentially better results but longer computation:
# ibcf_model = Recommender(getData(e, "train"), method = "IBCF", parameter = list(normalize = "center", method = "cosine", k = 350))

ibcf_model

# Make predictions on the test set (known items)
ibcf_predict = predict(ibcf_model, getData(e, "known"), type = "ratings")

# Calculate prediction accuracy (RMSE) on the unknown items in the test set
rmse_ibcf = calcPredictionAccuracy(ibcf_predict, getData(e, "unknown"))
rmse_ibcf
```

The RMSE for IBCF is calculated (e.g., 1.35 with k=35 in the video, which was higher than UBCF's 1.02). Comparing the RMSE values helps determine which method performed better on this dataset split. This is often a trial-and-error process to find the best algorithm and parameters.

## Making Specific Recommendations

We can use the trained model (e.g., UBCF) to recommend the top N items for a specific user. Let's recommend the top 5 movies for user 610.

```r
# Make top 5 recommendations for user 610 using the UBCF model
# We use type="topNList" to get ranked list of items, not predicted ratings
recommendations = predict(ubcf_model, real_ratings[610, ], type = "topNList", n = 5)

# View the recommendations
as(recommendations, "list")
```

The output provides a list of the top recommended movies for user 610 based on the UBCF model.

## Conclusion

We have covered loading and preparing data, calculating similarity, building User-Based and Item-Based Collaborative Filtering models using the `recommenderlab` package, evaluating their performance using RMSE, and making specific recommendations for a user.

Recommendation engines are a powerful tool in marketing analytics. The R `recommenderlab` package provides efficient implementations.

**Practice Suggestion:**
It is highly recommended to practice these steps with different datasets. Try sourcing data from platforms like Kaggle or even creating a small dummy dataset. Replicating the code on new data will help you understand the nuances and challenges involved in data preparation and model tuning, as the provided examples are often clean and ready-to-use.

We have now discussed recommendation engines extensively over the past few videos. We will move on to new topics in the next week. Thank you for watching!

# Marketing Analytics: Lecture 40 - RFM and Market Basket Analysis

## Introduction

Hello everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 8, and in this week, we will be discussing Recency Frequency Monetary (RFM) and Market Basket Analysis.

We are continuing our discussion on retail analytics, where these two techniques are mostly used. These are two different, perhaps slightly older, concepts, but still heavily used today, especially when we do simple analytics, not focusing heavily on predictive models, keeping things simple but still meaningful or fast. In these situations, we generally use RFM and Market Basket Analysis.

Initially, this week, we will discuss RFM (Recency Frequency Monetary Analysis), and in the later part of the week (in the later videos), we will talk about Market Basket Analysis.

## Recency Frequency Monetary (RFM) Analysis

First, the Recency Frequency Monetary Analysis. This is an analysis technique used generally as a tool to segment customers.

Before discussing this, in a previous video, I discussed that there are various types of segmentation you can do for a customer: demographic, geographic, attitudinal or psychographic, and then behaviour. Behavioural segmentation is majorly focused on a person's behaviour, how they express themselves, the actions they take.

In marketing analytics, specifically in the context of retail and many other service businesses, we will focus on behavioural analytics and segmentation. We will focus on a specific part of behavioural segmentation where people's **purchase behaviour** is the variable used for further analysis and segmentation.

### Why is Purchase Behaviour Needed for Segmentation?

Why do we need to segment customers based on their purchase behaviour? Because purchase behaviour is the most, I would say, a behaviour that consumers cannot specifically hide. That is the most crude, raw version of their behaviour that you can see.

You might not be able to see from your data whether the consumer has seen your advertisement, what their preference pattern is, which kind of movies they watch, which kind of websites they visit, or where traffic is coming from. You might get a vague idea from the data you generally collect. But a very concrete, strong behaviour available to you is from **scanner data**.

Scanner data means the data collected based on customer purchases. For example, in a retail store, when you check out after buying several products, the billing counter scans your products. The data created from that scanning process is called scanner data. That's basically an individual's purchase data; you might not have much else about that individual. Perhaps when they registered for a loyalty card, you might have basic details like name, email, phone number, address, family members' names or numbers, at most.

But that purchase data is the data you can use to create an idea or a 360-degree view about the customer **from the purchase point of view**. That is why purchase behaviour becomes a very important factor.

### What Aspects of Purchase Behaviour Should We Focus On?

The next decision point is, what in this purchase behaviour will I focus on? If I have a customer's purchase history, what exactly will I focus on?

*   How much groceries he bought?
*   How much apparels he bought?
*   How much FMCG products he bought?
*   Or whether I will focus on how much is the total monetary value of their basket?
*   Or will I focus on how many units, how many different types of SKUs he bought?

Researchers have done a lot of work and found that while many characteristics can be created from purchase behaviour, certain aspects better predict the profitability of the customer than others. These aspects are primarily **Recency (R), Frequency (F), and Monetary (M)**.

### Presentation Outline

My presentation will cover:

1.  Introduction: What is RFM?
2.  RFM Analysis: How it is done.
3.  Procedure.
4.  RFM for measuring performance of salespeople.
5.  Insights of RFM: Why it is important in the industry.
6.  Alternative RFM measures.

### What is RFM?

Let's start with an introduction using an analogy. Suppose you are a math teacher of Class 10, and you want to evaluate the performance of your students in a recent semester test to see if they are ready for the final exam.

You want to know:
*   Are they weak in certain areas? Strong in others?
*   Who is weak, who is strong?
*   How can you improve their performance?

To select good students (e.g., for an Olympiad) and identify those needing more focus, some things that come to mind are:

*   **Recency:** Have students attended classes recently? The more recently they attended, the more accustomed they are to your teaching and likely more receptive.
*   **Frequency:** Have students solved homework problems? How often? How many class tests, quizzes, or problems you gave them have they worked on?
*   **Monetary:** Have students put enough effort (time/resources) to learn the subject? (In the student analogy, 'monetary' is metaphorical, representing effort/investment).

These are the three aspects: Recency, Frequency, and Monetary. We will extend this idea to the context of customer purchase behaviour.

To evaluate this problem, you will segment students and use data and intuition to identify good and bad students. This is similar to what a marketing manager does for customers.

In today's customer-driven economy, proper customer insights are necessary. By improving user experience and establishing long-term relationships between customers and the company, survival against tough competition is possible. This requires innovative marketing plans tailored to customer satisfaction. RFM analysis helps achieve this.

RFM is a simple and intuitive technique for customer segmentation and has been used by marketing managers for decades.

### What is RFM? (Customer Context)

RFM is a marketing technique used to analyze customer value.

*   **Recency:** When did customers make their last purchase? (How recently)
*   **Frequency:** How often customers make their purchase? (How many purchases in a period)
*   **Monetary:** How much money does the customer spend? (Total value of purchases)

A customer who buys frequently, even in small amounts, is important.

**Example (Ola/Uber):** You get push notifications or offers. When do they come?
1.  If you are a new customer and just used the service: They send offers to encourage further usage (**Recency**).
2.  Even if you used the service a long time ago (e.g., one year back), they might still send an offer, especially if your past **Monetary** component or **Frequency** component was high.

If, for example, you spent a lot on Ola last year (high monetary, possibly high frequency) but stopped, they might identify you based on your past high Frequency and Monetary value, even if your Recency is low now. They might send offers to reactivate you.

The "sweet spot" every company aims for is customers who are most **Recent**, most **Frequent**, and have the highest **Monetary** spending. These customers have the highest value.

### What is RFM Analysis?

RFM analysis helps companies make decisions on promotions and offers for selected customer bases. You send targeted emails or communications, not mass ones, because targeted marketing has a much higher ROI (Return on Investment) than mass communication.

Targeted marketing requires data and analysis techniques to gain insights. RFM analysis is one such insight generation technique.

*   It is a method that helps companies find ways to improve customer spending (increase ROI).
*   It is useful to track lost customer bases (those with low RFM scores) and incentivize them to purchase again.
*   It helps track the customer base and build relationships that can increase sales and productivity.
*   It is also useful to identify and track minimum losses.

These are the basic needs that RFM addresses.

### Procedure for RFM Analysis

There are multiple procedures, but the most common one, which we will discuss and use in our R code in the next video, involves dividing customers into groups based on their R, F, and M values.

The easiest method is to divide the customer base into equal groups, often 10 (deciles), based on each of the RFM metrics.

1.  **Sort by Recency:** Take your customer data and sort it based on the date of the last purchase. Calculate the time distance from today. The smaller the distance, the more recent the purchase.
2.  **Assign Recency Scores:** Divide the sorted customers into 10 equal groups (deciles). Assign scores from 0 to 9 (or 1 to 10).
    *   Conventionally, higher scores indicate better performance.
    *   For recency (time distance), lower distance is better. So, the group with the *smallest* time distance (most recent purchase) gets the highest score (e.g., 9 or 10). The group with the largest distance gets the lowest score (e.g., 0 or 1).
    *   *Correction:* My slide shows 0-9 scale, where 9 is most favoured and 0 is least favoured. For Recency *distance*, smaller is better, so the group with the smallest distance gets score 9, second smallest gets 8, and so on, down to the group with the largest distance getting score 0.
3.  **Sort by Frequency:** Sort the customer data based on the number of purchases in a given period. Higher frequency is better.
4.  **Assign Frequency Scores:** Divide the sorted customers into 10 deciles. The group with the *highest* frequency gets score 9, the next highest gets 8, down to the lowest frequency group getting score 0.
5.  **Sort by Monetary:** Sort the customer data based on the total monetary value spent in a given period. Higher monetary value is better.
6.  **Assign Monetary Scores:** Divide the sorted customers into 10 deciles. The group with the *highest* monetary value gets score 9, the next highest gets 8, down to the lowest monetary group getting score 0.

So, score 0 means least favoured (e.g., high recency distance, low frequency, low monetary value), whereas 9 means most favoured (e.g., low recency distance, high frequency, high monetary value).

### Combining RFM Scores

Once you have assigned an R score, an F score, and an M score to each customer (each on a scale, e.g., 0-9), how do you get an ultimate RFM score? There are different ways.

One simple way is to combine the scores with weights. For example, you can combine them by giving weightages like 100 to Recency, 10 to Frequency, and 1 to Monetary.

Let's look at an example table:

| Customer ID | Recency Score (R) | Frequency Score (F) | Monetary Score (M) | Combined Score (100R + 10F + 1M) | Rank |
| :---------- | :---------------- | :------------------ | :----------------- | :------------------------------- | :--- |
| 1           | 9                 | 7                   | 9                  | (9*100) + (7*10) + (9*1) = 979   | 1    |
| 2           | 8                 | 9                   | 9                  | (8*100) + (9*10) + (9*1) = 899   | 2    |
| 3           | 2                 | 9                   | 9                  | (2*100) + (9*10) + (9*1) = 299   | 3    |
| 5           | 2                 | 2                   | 1                  | (2*100) + (2*10) + (1*1) = 221   | 4    |
| 4           | 1                 | 9                   | 4                  | (1*100) + (9*10) + (4*1) = 194   | 5    |
| 6           | 0                 | 1                   | 0                  | (0*100) + (1*10) + (0*1) = 10    | 6    |

*Note: The table in the transcript shows Customer ID 4 & 5 in a different order relative to their scores, but the ranking based on the combined score is consistent.*

By giving weights like 100, 10, and 1, the score directly encodes the individual R, F, and M scores. For instance, 979 tells you the R score is 9, F is 7, and M is 9. The highest combined score gets the highest rank.

In this simple weighting scheme (100, 10, 1), Recency has the highest impact on the final score. A difference of 1 in the Recency score changes the combined score by 100, whereas a difference of 1 in Frequency changes it by 10, and Monetary by 1. This means the customer's most recent behaviour is prioritized. For example, Customer 5 (score 221, R=2, F=2, M=1) is ranked higher than Customer 4 (score 194, R=1, F=9, M=4), even though Customer 4 has much higher F and M scores, because Customer 5's Recency score is higher.

This implies that Recency is considered the most important factor in this specific simple method. In real life, the relative importance might be debated, and we will discuss alternative methods later.

### RFM for Measuring Salesperson Performance

RFM can also be applied to evaluate the performance of salespeople.

*   RFM analysis of salespersons gives a clear idea to managers about how well they are performing.
*   Analysis by comparing total generated revenue (monetary), number of orders (frequency), and time since last order (recency) is possible.
*   By identifying weaknesses, decision-making regarding training, promotion, or employment termination is possible.

You can rank salespeople based on how recently they got an order, how many orders they got, and the total volume of their orders.

**Limitation:** RFM is generally not useful for companies who provide unique products which are not purchased frequently or in large quantities (e.g., high-value infrequent purchases like cars or houses). It's best suited for businesses with repeat customer purchases.

### RFM vs. Predictive Models

Let's compare RFM with more complex predictive models.

| Feature                 | RFM                                                | Predictive Models (e.g., Regression, ML)           |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- |
| **Ease of Use**         | Easy for managers (intuitive)                      | Black box for managers (requires specialists)      |
| **Implementation**      | Can build it yourself                              | Requires trained data miner, analysis validation   |
| **Portability**         | Portable across industries (same technique applies) | Contextual (model specific to industry/context)    |
| **Seasonality**         | Somewhat effective at mitigating confounding effect | Seasonality is typically an issue unless handled   |
| **Stability**           | Definition is stable, doesn't need re-definition   | Typically needs to be rebuilt/recalibrated often |
| **Applicability**       | Applies to all customers, supports full database sort | Doesn't always apply to all segments; may differ |
| **Cross-organization**  | Can use for reactivation, cross-selling directly   | Additional models needed for specific programs     |

RFM has advantages in ease of use, implementation effort, generalizability, and stability compared to predictive models, making it a practical tool for quick and broad customer understanding.

### Insights from Recency

Recency, specifically the time elapsed since the last purchase, is a particularly powerful variable and can provide numerous insights when combined with other data.

*   **Last Purchase Date + Frequency + Monetary:** The core RFM analysis for overall customer value.
*   **Last Web Date + Last Store Visit:** Combining recency across channels helps understand channel-specific behaviour and segment customers based on their channel preferences.
*   **Last Web Date + Corporate Recency (Purchase Date):** If these dates are close, the web visit likely led to a purchase. If the web date is recent but no purchase was made, or a purchase was made elsewhere, it might indicate a lost sale needing reactivation efforts.
*   **Division 1 Recency + Division 2 Recency (for companies with multiple product divisions):** If a customer has recent purchases in one division but not another, it indicates a potential for cross-selling into the second division.
*   **Individual Recency + Household Recency:** If these are close, the individual is likely the primary decision-maker for the household's purchases from your business. You might focus communication efforts on this individual rather than mass mailing the household.
*   **Your Company's Recency + Competitor/Co-corporate Recency:** Understanding when customers last bought from you versus a competitor can help identify churn risk or reactivation opportunities.
*   **Recency + Products Purchased:** Analyzing recent purchases in combination with specific product categories can identify patterns useful for recommendation engines (e.g., "customers who recently bought X also bought Y").

Empirical data shows a strong relationship between Recency and customer value.

*   Customers with more recent purchases (lower time distance) generally have higher purchase revenue, higher sales per piece, and higher overall margin compared to those whose last purchase was long ago.
*   Looking at customers segmented by RFM scores, the "best" RFM groups (highest R, F, M) tend to have sales per piece and margins significantly higher than the "worst" groups.

This empirical evidence supports the focus on Recency as a key indicator of potential future value and profitability.

### Alternative RFM Measures

While the 100-10-1 weighting scheme for decile scores is simple, alternative methods exist for calculating the ultimate RFM score, often aiming for more meaningful weightages or calculation methods.

One example assigns weighted points based on specific criteria:

| Recency Criteria        | Points Assigned | Relative Weightage | Weighted Points (Points * Weightage) |
| :---------------------- | :-------------- | :----------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| Within past 2 months    | 20              | 5                  | 100                                  |
| Within 4 months         | 10              | 5                  | 50                                   |
| Within 6 months         | 5               | 5                  | 25                                   |
| Within 9 months         | 3               | 5                  | 15                                   |
| Within 12 months        | 1               | 5                  | 5                                    |

| Frequency Criteria      | Points Assigned         | Relative Weightage | Weighted Points (Points * Weightage) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------- | :----------------------------------- |
| For each purchase (max 15 points) | 3 per purchase (max 15) | 2                  | Varies (Points * 2)                  |

| Monetary Criteria         | Calculation                                  | Relative Weightage | Weighted Points (Calculation * Weightage) |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------------------------- | :----------------- | :---------------------------------------- |
| Monetary Value (max 25) | 10% of dollar volume within 12 months (max 25) | 3                  | Varies (10% of $ * 3, max 75)             |

The total RFM score for a customer is the sum of their weighted points for Recency, Frequency, and Monetary value. Customers with higher cumulative points are considered more preferred.

**Example:** John has high cumulative points, making him a good prospect for targeted marketing. Smith has very low total weighted and cumulative points, suggesting marketing efforts towards him might be a misdirected effort.

Another alternative method mentioned is using regression. You can model customer profitability (or a similar metric) as the dependent variable and R, F, and M as independent variables. The coefficients from the regression model can serve as empirical weights for R, F, and M when calculating the RFM score.

So, this is how we do RFM analysis using different procedures.

## References

*   Connie L. Bauer, "A Direct Mail Customer Purchase Model," Journal of Direct Marketing, 1988.

## Conclusion

In this video, we introduced RFM analysis, its purpose, procedure, applications, comparison with predictive models, and some alternative approaches. In the next video, we will demonstrate how to perform RFM analysis in R using a real dataset.

Thank you very much. See you in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 41: RFM and Market Basket Analysis (contd.)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee, IIT Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. We are in week 8, session 2. In this session, we continue our discussion on **RFM analysis (Recency, Frequency, Monetary)**.

As discussed in the previous video, RFM analysis is a technique used for **behavioral segmentation** of customers based on their purchase data.
*   **R (Recency):** How recently a customer has purchased.
*   **F (Frequency):** How many times a customer has purchased within a certain period.
*   **M (Monetary):** How much money a customer has spent within a certain period.

A high RFM score typically indicates a profitable customer, while a low score suggests a customer who might need reactivation efforts.

## RFM Implementation in R

We will be working with the `week 8s 2.R` file. This file uses a data set available directly from the internet. You need to ensure your internet connection is active.

### Loading the Data

The data is available at a public URL. We will use the `read.table` function to load it directly.

```r
# Load data from the internet
df <- read.table(text = "
    customerID date_of_purchase number_of_purchases dollar_value
    19703 19970101 1 117.50
    19704 19970101 1 19.95
    8348 19970101 2 128.90
    8349 19970101 1 11.50
    2029 19970101 1 11.75
    2030 19970101 1 20.95
    2031 19970101 1 20.95
    8350 19970101 2 128.90
    8351 19970101 1 11.50
    2032 19970101 1 11.75
    ... # (truncated for brevity, the actual URL is longer)
    ", header = TRUE) # Assuming the URL contains similar data with headers

# In the provided R file, the actual URL is used:
# df <- read.table("http://chen.kq.free.fr/docs/cust_data.txt", header = TRUE)
```

The data contains `customerID`, `date_of_purchase`, `number_of_purchases`, and `dollar_value`.

### Data Preparation: Date Conversion

The `date_of_purchase` is currently stored as an integer (e.g., `19970101`). We need to convert it to a proper date format to calculate recency.

First, let's check the structure:

```r
str(df)
```
The output shows that `date_of_purchase` is an integer.

To convert to date, we first convert it to character and then use the `as.Date` function, specifying the input format.

*   `%Y`: Full year (e.g., 1997)
*   `%y`: Two-digit year (e.g., 97)
*   `%m`: Month (e.g., 01)
*   `%d`: Day (e.g., 01)
*   `%B`: Full month name (e.g., January)
*   `%b`: Abbreviated month name (e.g., Jan)

Our date format is `YYYYMMDD`, which corresponds to `%Y%m%d`.

```r
# Convert the date column to character first
df$date_of_purchase_char <- as.character(df$date_of_purchase)

# Convert the character date to Date format
df$date_of_purchase <- as.Date(df$date_of_purchase_char, format = "%Y%m%d")

# Remove the temporary character column if desired
# df$date_of_purchase_char <- NULL

# Or, combine into one step as in the provided code:
df[,2] <- as.Date(as.character(df[,2]), format = "%Y%m%d")
```

Let's check the structure again:

```r
str(df)
```
Now, `date_of_purchase` should be of type `Date`.

### Data Cleaning: Removing Duplicates

It's possible the data contains duplicate entries. We should remove them.

```r
# Check initial dimension
dim(df)

# Remove duplicate rows
df <- unique(df)

# Check dimension after removing duplicates
dim(df)
```
The dimension will likely decrease significantly, indicating duplicate records were present.

### Calculating Time Distance for Recency

Recency is calculated based on the difference between a reference date (often today's date or the last date in the dataset) and the purchase date. The smaller the difference, the more recent the purchase.

We'll use `Sys.Date()` to get the current system date as our reference point.

```r
# Get today's date
sys_date <- Sys.Date()
print(sys_date) # Note: this will vary based on when you run the code

# Calculate the time distance in days
df$timedist <- sys_date - df$date_of_purchase

# View the calculated time distances
head(df)
```
The `timedist` column now contains the number of days between the system date and each purchase date. Higher `timedist` means less recent.

### Defining RFM Scoring Philosophy

Remember the desired scoring:
*   **Recency:** Lower time distance -> Higher Recency score (more recent = better).
*   **Frequency:** Higher number of purchases -> Higher Frequency score (more frequent = better).
*   **Monetary:** Higher dollar value -> Higher Monetary score (spends more = better).

We will convert these raw values into scores, typically on a scale like 0-9 or 1-10.

### Scoring using `ntile` and `tidyverse`

The `tidyverse` package offers convenient functions like `mutate` (to add/change columns) and `ntile` (to divide data into quantiles or 'tiles').

First, install and load `tidyverse` if you haven't already.

```r
# install.packages("tidyverse") # Uncomment and run if not installed
library(tidyverse)
```

Now, we can use `mutate` and `ntile` to create the RFM scores. `ntile(column, n)` divides the `column` into `n` roughly equal groups and assigns a group number (1 to n). We want 10 groups (deciles) and scores from 0-9.

*   For Frequency and Monetary (where higher values are better), `ntile(column, 10)` gives 1-10. Subtracting 1 (`ntile - 1`) gives 0-9, where higher raw values get higher scores.
*   For Recency (where *lower* time distance is better), `ntile(timedist, 10)` gives 1-10. To flip the scale so that lower time distance (score 1 from `ntile`) gets a high recency score (9) and higher time distance (score 10 from `ntile`) gets a low recency score (0), we use `10 - ntile(timedist, 10)`. This results in scores from `10-1=9` down to `10-10=0`.

```r
# Calculate RFM scores using ntile
df <- df %>%
  mutate(
    recency_score = 10 - ntile(timedist, 10),
    frequency_score = ntile(number_of_purchases, 10) - 1,
    monetary_score = ntile(dollar_value, 10) - 1
  )
```

After running this, check the summary of the new score columns:

```r
summary(df[, c("recency_score", "frequency_score", "monetary_score")])
```
The minimum and maximum values should be 0 and 9, respectively.

### Visualizing Score Distributions

Let's look at the distribution of the calculated scores using histograms. `ntile` forces the scores into bins with roughly equal numbers of customers.

```r
hist(df$recency_score, main = "Recency Score Distribution")
hist(df$frequency_score, main = "Frequency Score Distribution")
hist(df$monetary_score, main = "Monetary Score Distribution")
```

Now, compare these to the histograms of the *original* variables:

```r
# Convert timedist to numeric for histogram if needed
# df$timedist_numeric <- as.numeric(df$timedist) # Not needed if already numeric difference

hist(as.numeric(df$timedist), main = "Original Time Distance Distribution")
hist(df$number_of_purchases, main = "Original Frequency Distribution")
hist(df$dollar_value, main = "Original Monetary Distribution")
```
You'll likely notice that the original `dollar_value` and `number_of_purchases` distributions are highly skewed, while `timedist` might be more evenly spread. Using `ntile` creates uniform bins in the *scores*, but this might mask the underlying skewness in the original data.

### Alternative Scoring: Manual Binning

When original variables are heavily skewed, manually defining score intervals based on the data's distribution might be preferable to `ntile`. You would look at the histogram of the raw variable and decide where to place the bins to create a more meaningful distribution of scores across the customer base.

For example, manually binning the `dollar_value`:

```r
# Manual Monetary Scoring based on observed distribution
df <- df %>%
  mutate(
    monetary_score_manual = case_when(
      dollar_value < 10 ~ 1,
      dollar_value >= 10 & dollar_value < 30 ~ 2,
      dollar_value >= 30 & dollar_value < 50 ~ 3,
      dollar_value >= 50 & dollar_value < 100 ~ 4,
      dollar_value >= 100 & dollar_value < 125 ~ 5,
      dollar_value >= 125 & dollar_value < 150 ~ 6,
      dollar_value >= 150 & dollar_value < 200 ~ 7,
      dollar_value >= 200 & dollar_value < 300 ~ 8,
      dollar_value >= 300 ~ 9,
      TRUE ~ 0 # Assign 0 for any case not covered (e.g., NAs or errors)
    )
  )

# Note: The bins and scores (1-9 vs 0-9) can be adjusted based on preference and data
# Let's adjust to 0-9 to match previous scoring
df <- df %>%
    mutate(
        monetary_score_manual = case_when(
            dollar_value < 10 ~ 0,
            dollar_value >= 10 & dollar_value < 30 ~ 1,
            dollar_value >= 30 & dollar_value < 50 ~ 2,
            dollar_value >= 50 & dollar_value < 100 ~ 3,
            dollar_value >= 100 & dollar_value < 125 ~ 4,
            dollar_value >= 125 & dollar_value < 150 ~ 5,
            dollar_value >= 150 & dollar_value < 200 ~ 6,
            dollar_value >= 200 & dollar_value < 300 ~ 7,
            dollar_value >= 300 ~ 8,
            TRUE ~ 9 # Or assign a higher score for very high values, depends on definition
        )
    )

# Let's follow the instructor's verbal description of 0-10, 10-30, etc. and the histogram.
# Instructor mentions mapping 0-10 to 1, 10-30 to 2, 30-50 to 3 etc.
# He also says scores 1, 2, 3 for the big lump up to 50.
# Let's replicate the logic shown in the code block provided in the original transcript:
# The code snippet in the transcript starts mapping from dollar_value < 10 to score 1.
# It seems to define bins and map to scores 1 through 5+ initially based on visual inspection.
# Let's create a score from 1 to 9/10 based on manual bins.
df <- df %>%
  mutate(
    monetary_score_manual = case_when(
      dollar_value < 10 ~ 1,
      dollar_value >= 10 & dollar_value < 30 ~ 2,
      dollar_value >= 30 & dollar_value < 50 ~ 3,
      dollar_value >= 50 & dollar_value < 100 ~ 4,
      dollar_value >= 100 & dollar_value < 125 ~ 5,
      dollar_value >= 125 & dollar_value < 150 ~ 6,
      dollar_value >= 150 & dollar_value < 200 ~ 7,
      dollar_value >= 200 & dollar_value < 300 ~ 8,
      dollar_value >= 300 ~ 9 # Assuming 9 is the top score
    )
  )
```
Let's visualize the manually binned monetary score distribution:

```r
hist(df$monetary_score_manual, main = "Manual Monetary Score Distribution")
```
This distribution might look more spread out compared to the `ntile` version for skewed data. You would apply similar manual binning to Frequency if it's heavily skewed. Recency might be fine with `ntile` if its distribution is more even.

### Combining Scores into RFM

Finally, combine the Recency, Frequency, and Monetary scores into a single RFM score. The common practice is to weight them: R * 100 + F * 10 + M * 1. This gives higher importance to Recency, then Frequency, then Monetary, which is often relevant as recent customers are generally considered more valuable.

Using the `ntile` scores:
```r
df$RFM_score_ntile <- df$recency_score * 100 + df$frequency_score * 10 + df$monetary_score
```
Using manual Monetary score (and `ntile` for R/F):
```r
df$RFM_score_manual_M <- df$recency_score * 100 + df$frequency_score * 10 + df$monetary_score_manual
```

You can examine the top customers based on the RFM score:

```r
# Sort by RFM score descending to see top customers
df_sorted <- df[order(-df$RFM_score_ntile), ]
head(df_sorted)
```
Customers with RFM scores like 999 are the most valuable (most recent, most frequent, highest spending). Scores like 054 indicate very low recency (last purchase long ago), but decent frequency and monetary value.

Visualize the RFM score distribution:

```r
hist(df$RFM_score_ntile, main = "RFM Score Distribution (Ntile)")
hist(df$RFM_score_manual_M, main = "RFM Score Distribution (Manual M)")
```

## Conclusion on RFM Analysis

RFM analysis is a simple yet powerful technique for customer segmentation based on purchase behavior.

*   **Ease of Implementation:** It's straightforward and can even be done using tools like Excel or SQL, especially as dedicated macros and procedures exist for it in databases.
*   **Behavioral Insight:** It provides a quick way to understand customer value based on their past actions.
*   **Flexibility:** Scoring can be done using automated methods like `ntile` or manual binning based on data distribution and business logic.

While simple, it is a very common and widely used method in marketing for segmentation and targeting.

That concludes the RFM analysis section. In the next video, we will continue with Market Basket Analysis.

Thank you very much. I will come back with the next video.
