# Marketing Analytics
## Lecture 42: RFM and Market Basket Analysis (Contd.)

### Introduction

Hello everybody, welcome to the marketing analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 8, session 3 (video lecture number 3), and we will be discussing Market Basket Analysis in this session.

In the earlier videos of this week, we discussed RFM analysis, which is one part of retail analytics used mainly in retail but also in other contexts. Market Basket Analysis, on the other hand, is primarily used in retail, majorly in e-commerce but also in brick-and-mortar retail stores.

The data used in Market Basket Analysis is generated from scanner data. Scanner data is the information generated when you scan items at the billing counter.

### What is Basket Data?

A very common type of data is basket data, often also called transaction data. This data comes directly from transactions.

In a supermarket's transaction database, each record (row) typically represents a transaction between a customer and a shop. Each record corresponds to a basket containing specific items bought in a single visit to a retail store.

**Why do people buy lots of products together?**

*   **Brick-and-Mortar Retail:** If it's a store far from your home (supermarket, hypermarket), there's a travel cost involved. You plan your visit and purchase multiple items to make the trip worthwhile. Smaller Kirana stores nearby might see multiple visits with fewer items per basket.
*   **E-commerce:**
    *   Historically, delivery fees encouraged buying multiple items in one transaction to amortize the cost.
    *   This is still seen in food delivery platforms (like Zomato, Swiggy) where a delivery fee per transaction encourages ordering multiple items from one restaurant.
    *   However, for platforms like Amazon (especially with Prime) or Flipkart, where delivery fees are minimal, people often buy just one or two items at a time, reducing the concept of a large "basket."

Therefore, Market Basket Analysis is most effective when transactions involve multiple items, which might be less common now for certain e-commerce scenarios compared to physical supermarkets or specific e-commerce models like food delivery. Despite this, it remains an important topic.

### Transaction Database Example

Here's an example of how a transaction database might look:

| Transaction ID | Apple | Beer | Cheese | Dates | Eggs | Fish | Glue | Honey | Ice Cream |
| :------------- | :---- | :--- | :----- | :---- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :---- | :-------- |
| 1              | 1     | 1    |        | 1     |      |      | 1    | 1     |           |
| 2              |       |      | 1      | 1     | 1    |      |      |       |           |
| 3              |       | 1    | 1      |       |      |      |      |       |           |
| ...            |       |      |        |       |      |      |      |       |           |

*   Each row is a `Transaction ID`.
*   Columns represent products.
*   `1` indicates the product was purchased in that transaction.
*   Blank indicates it was not purchased.

In the first transaction (ID 1), Apple, Beer, Dates, Glue, and Honey were bought.
In the second transaction (ID 2), Cheese, Dates, and Eggs were bought.
In the third transaction (ID 3), Beer and Cheese were bought.

**Challenge:** In a real retail scenario with thousands of products and millions of customers/transactions, this matrix becomes huge. It's often necessary to analyze subsets of the data, perhaps by:
*   Product category (dairy, apparel, etc.).
*   Time period (this month, this week), as transaction patterns can change based on offers, seasons, etc.

### What is Market Basket Analysis?

Any analysis done with market basket data is called Market Basket Analysis.
*   **Input:** A list of purchases by purchaser (identified by transaction ID, not necessarily customer name).
*   **Output:** Identify purchase patterns.

MBA tries to answer questions like:
*   What items tend to be purchased together (e.g., steak and potatoes, beer and pretzels, bread and butter)? Some combinations are obvious, others are not. Finding the non-obvious ones is key.
*   What items are purchased sequentially (e.g., buying a house then buying furniture; buying a car then buying tires or lubricants later; buying a computer then buying accessories or internet connections)?
*   What items tend to be purchased by season?

We primarily focus on the first question (items purchased together).

MBA helps us categorize customer purchase behavior and identify actionable information.
*   Create purchase profiles (segmenting transactions based on the items bought together).
*   Find the profitability of each purchase profile.
*   Use the information for marketing:
    *   **Layout or Catalogs:** How to arrange products in a physical store or an online catalog/website. Example: Placing high-end dishwashing gels near crockery and cake-based dishwashing bars near detergents, based on who buys what with high-end vs. metallic utensils. Example: Amazon showing "customers who bought this also bought..." bundles.
    *   Select products for promotion.
    *   Product placement and space allocation in stores.

Steve Schmidt, former President of ACNielsen US, highlighted MBA benefits for:
*   Selection of promotions.
*   Merchandising strategy.
*   Understanding price sensitivity for certain item groups.
*   Uncovering customer spending patterns.
*   Identifying promotional opportunities (bundling).

**Why is it used?**
*   Mainly in retail outlets.
*   Telecommunications (bundling services).
*   Banks (bundling products like loans and insurance).
*   TV Bundles (like Tata Sky creating sports, regional, or movie bundles based on viewership patterns).
*   Insurance, Medical, etc.

A key principle mentioned is that "customer shop on personal needs, not on product grouping." While we analyze categories, customers' decisions are driven by their needs, which is important to keep in mind.

### Basic Data Analysis and Association Rules

Let's look at a simplified example:

| Transaction | Beer | Chips | Milk | Soda |
| :---------- | :--- | :---- | :--- | :--- |
| 1           | 1    | 1     |      |      |
| 2           |      |       | 1    | 1    |
| 3           | 1    |       |      |      |
| 4           |      |       | 1    | 1    |
| 5           | 1    | 1     |      |      |
| 6           |      |       | 1    |      |

*   Beer and Chips together: 2 transactions
*   Beer and Milk together: 0 transactions
*   Soda and Milk together: 2 transactions
*   Most common individual product: Milk (4 transactions)

While Beer and Chips together make sense, Milk and Soda together might seem less obvious. Identifying these unexpected co-occurrences is part of MBA.

### Purchase Profiles Examples

MBA can reveal various purchase profiles based on co-occurring items:
*   Beauty conscious (cotton balls, hair dye, cologne, nail polish)
*   Health conscious
*   Sport conscious
*   Likely smokers
*   Casual drinkers
*   New family
*   Illness over-the-counter (digestive aids, pain relievers, fever medicine bought together for travel)
*   Home handyman
*   Sentimental products
*   Kids/Pets focused
*   Gardening, Automotive, TV/Stereo enthusiasts (hobbies)
*   Seasonal/Traditional
*   Homemakers

Marketers can prioritize these profiles based on profitability. For example, "Kids Fashion" might have a high average margin per transaction ($15.24), making it a profile to push, while a "Smoker" profile might have a lower margin ($2.88). Profiles like "Student" or "Home Office" might be price-sensitive and less attractive for aggressive pushing.

### Affinity Positioning and Cross-selling

MBA findings can inform:
*   **Affinity Positioning:** Placing related items close together (e.g., coffee and coffee makers).
*   **Cross-selling:** Recommending related items. If someone buys cold medicine, suggest digestive medicine or orange juice.

### Limitations of Market Basket Analysis

While useful, MBA has limitations:
*   Some limitations can be addressed by more complex techniques like predictive analytics or recommendation engines.
*   It can take significant time (e.g., over 18 months was cited in one context) to gather enough data and implement findings fully. Data availability is key.
*   MBA primarily **identifies hypotheses** (e.g., bread and washing powder are related) which then need further testing or validation through other predictive methods before full implementation.
*   Measurement of impact (ROI of MBA-driven strategies) can be difficult.
*   It can be difficult to identify meaningful product groupings manually.
*   Complexity grows exponentially with the number of products and transactions because the analysis involves relationships between all possible item combinations.

### Benefits of Market Basket Analysis

Despite limitations, MBA remains valuable because:
*   **Simple Computation:** The core calculations (support, confidence) are straightforward.
*   **Undirected:** You don't need prior hypotheses about which items should go together; the analysis discovers patterns.
*   Different data forms (transaction logs) can be easily analyzed.

### Scale of Real-World Data

The simple examples used in class (like 20 transactions of 9 items) are small.
A large supermarket chain with multiple stores might generate 20 million baskets per month from a pool of around 1000 products.

To find association rules like "If a basket contains X and Y, then it also contains Z," you would have:
*   Millions of transactions to scan.
*   Potentially billions of possible rules (e.g., 500 million distinct possible rules from 20M transactions and 1000 products).
*   Calculating accuracy and coverage for each rule by trawling through the massive database is computationally expensive.

This highlights the need for more efficient algorithms.

### Discovering Rules: Confidence and Coverage (Support)

An association rule looks like this:
**IF** {Item A, Item B, ...} **THEN** {Item C, Item D, ...}

This structure is often simplified to a single item in the "THEN" part:
**IF** {Antecedent} **THEN** {Consequent}

Key metrics for evaluating rules:

1.  **Confidence (Accuracy):**
    *   Answers: Given that the IF part is true, how often is the THEN part true?
    *   Calculation: P(Consequent | Antecedent) = P(Antecedent AND Consequent) / P(Antecedent)
    *   This is the same as accuracy.

2.  **Coverage (Support):**
    *   Answers: How much of the database contains the IF part? (Sometimes refers to the *entire* rule A->B, meaning P(A and B))
    *   Calculation (of the IF part): P(Antecedent) = (Number of transactions containing Antecedent) / (Total number of transactions)
    *   Calculation (of the whole rule): P(Antecedent AND Consequent) = (Number of transactions containing both Antecedent AND Consequent) / (Total number of transactions). This is often referred to as the **support of the itemset {Antecedent, Consequent}**.

Let's use the earlier 6-transaction example data.
Consider the rule: **IF** {Beer, Cheese} **THEN** {Honey}

*   How many transactions contain {Beer, Cheese}? Transaction 3 contains Beer and Cheese. There are 6 transactions total.
    *   *Correction*: Looking at the *first* data example (20 transactions), Beer and Cheese appear together in Transaction 3 and Transaction 19.
    *   Number of transactions with {Beer, Cheese}: 2
    *   Total transactions: 20
    *   Coverage (Support of {Beer, Cheese}): 2/20 = 10%

*   Now, out of the transactions that contain {Beer, Cheese}, how many also contain {Honey}?
    *   Transaction 3 has Beer and Cheese, but no Honey.
    *   Transaction 19 has Beer and Cheese, and also Honey.
    *   Number of transactions with {Beer, Cheese, Honey}: 1
    *   Number of transactions with {Beer, Cheese}: 2
    *   Confidence (of the rule {Beer, Cheese} -> {Honey}): P(Honey | Beer, Cheese) = P(Beer, Cheese, Honey) / P(Beer, Cheese) = 1 / 2 = 50%.

### Finding Interesting Rules (Surprise)

An interesting rule is one that happens significantly more than expected by chance.

Consider the general probabilities of buying individual items:
*   P(Bread) = 10% (i.e., 10% of baskets contain bread)
*   P(Washing Powder) = 4% (i.e., 4% of baskets contain washing powder)

If buying bread and buying washing powder were independent events, the probability of a basket containing *both* would be:
P(Bread AND Washing Powder) = P(Bread) * P(Washing Powder) = 0.10 * 0.04 = 0.004 or 0.4%.
This means we would expect about 4 out of 1000 baskets to contain both items by chance.

Now, suppose we analyze the data and find that 20 out of 1000 baskets actually contain both bread and washing powder. This is 5 times higher than expected by chance (20 vs 4). This difference is surprising and suggests a connection between the purchase of these two items that isn't immediately obvious. It creates a hypothesis that consumers connect these two items in some way.

This "surprise element" (how much the observed frequency differs from the expected frequency under independence) is a key factor in identifying interesting rules.

Such a surprising discovery (like bread and washing powder being bought together more often than expected) can be exploited:
*   Place bread and washing powder at opposite ends of the supermarket. This forces customers who want both to walk through more aisles, increasing the chance of impulse buys and boosting revenue.

### Efficiently Finding Rules: The Apriori Algorithm

Given the massive number of potential rules in real-world datasets (hundreds of millions), checking every single combination for coverage and confidence is computationally infeasible. We need more efficient methods.

The **Apriori algorithm** is a data mining technique used to find frequent itemsets and generate association rules.
*   It is not overly complex but is simple, fast, and effective for finding interesting rules in basket/transaction data.
*   It uses operations efficient in standard database systems.
*   It's widely used in retail and industry R&D.

**Note on Terminology:** In the context of the Apriori algorithm, we often shift focus from "rules" directly to **itemsets** (combinations of items, like {Beer, Cheese, Honey}). The **coverage** of a rule is often the same as the **support** of the corresponding itemset (e.g., the coverage of the rule {Beer, Cheese} -> {Honey} is the support of the itemset {Beer, Cheese, Honey}).

We will delve into the details of the Apriori algorithm in the next video.

### Conclusion

We have discussed the basics of Market Basket Analysis, its data requirements (transaction data), its applications in marketing (layout, promotions, profiling), the key metrics (confidence, coverage/support), how to identify "interesting" rules by looking for surprising co-occurrences, and the need for efficient algorithms like Apriori to handle large datasets.

Thank you for being with me. I will come back in the next video to discuss the Apriori algorithm in detail.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 43 : rfm and market basket analysis (contd.)

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 43: RFM and Market Basket Analysis (Contd.)

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. We are in Week 8, Session 4, discussing Market Basket Analysis. In previous sessions, we discussed the requirement of Market Basket Analysis and the basic version of the algorithm. Now, let's talk about the Apriori algorithm, which is an advanced version suitable for larger datasets.

## Apriori Algorithm

The Apriori algorithm finds association rules in two stages:

1.  **Find all itemsets with a specified minimal support:** Instead of analyzing all possible combinations, we first identify only those itemsets (combinations of items) that occur with at least a minimum specified support percentage in the dataset. An itemset is simply a specific set of items. For example, "apples and cheese" is a two-item set.
2.  **Use these large itemsets to help generating the rules:** Once the potential large itemsets are identified, we use them to generate candidate rules efficiently. This step significantly narrows down the possibilities, allowing for faster processing on large itemsets to generate rules.

### Terminology in Apriori Algorithm

*   **k-itemset:** A set containing *k* items.
    *   A three-item set contains three items.
    *   A one-item set contains one item.
    *   A two-item set contains two items.
*   **Support (of an itemset):** An itemset has *s* percentage support if it occurs in *s* percentage of the total transactions in the database.
*   **Minimum Support:** The Apriori algorithm starts by specifying a minimum support threshold. We only consider itemsets that meet or exceed this threshold.
    *   For example, if the database has 20 million transactions and the minimum support is 1%, an itemset must occur at least 200,000 times. If the minimum support is 0.01%, it must occur at least 2,000 times.
*   **Large Itemset:** An itemset whose support is at least the minimum support threshold. It does *not* mean an itemset with many items.
*   **L k:** The set of all large *k*-item sets in the database.
*   **C k:** A set of candidate large *k*-item sets. The algorithm generates this set, which contains all *k*-item sets that *might* be large (i.e., they meet certain conditions derived from smaller large itemsets). Lk is a subset of Ck.

## How Apriori Algorithm Works

The core idea is based on the property that **any subset of a large itemset must also be a large itemset.** Conversely, if an itemset has a subset that is *not* large (i.e., its support is below the minimum threshold), then the itemset itself cannot be large.

Let's illustrate with an example:
Suppose we have items 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and their supports are:
*   Item 1: 30%
*   Item 2: 5%
*   Item 3: 25%
*   Item 4: 9%
*   Item 5: 12%

Let the minimum support threshold be 10%.

1.  **Find Large 1-itemsets (L1):**
    *   Items 1, 3, 5 meet the 10% threshold. So, L1 = { {1}, {3}, {5} }.
    *   Items 2 and 4 do not meet the threshold.

2.  **Find Large 2-itemsets (L2):**
    *   We only need to consider combinations of items from L1. Possible candidate 2-itemsets (C2) are {1, 3}, {1, 5}, {3, 5}.
    *   We check the support for these candidates:
        *   Support({1, 3}): Let's say it's 20%. (Large)
        *   Support({1, 5}): Let's say it's 9%. (Not Large)
        *   Support({3, 5}): Let's say it's 11%. (Large)
    *   So, L2 = { {1, 3}, {3, 5} }.

3.  **Find Large 3-itemsets (L3):**
    *   We look for candidates by combining itemsets from L2. The only possible combination is {1, 3, 5}.
    *   Now, check the subsets of {1, 3, 5}: {1, 3}, {1, 5}, {3, 5}.
    *   From L2, we know that {1, 5} is *not* a large itemset (support is 9%).
    *   Since a subset ({1, 5}) of {1, 3, 5} is not large, the itemset {1, 3, 5} cannot be large. We don't even need to calculate its support.
    *   Therefore, there are no large 3-itemsets. L3 is empty.

The algorithm stops when Lk is empty. In this example, L3 is empty, so we stop. The large itemsets found are L1 and L2.

### Apriori Algorithm Steps

1.  Find all large 1-itemsets (L1).
2.  For k = 2, while L k-1 is non-empty:
    *   Generate candidate k-itemsets (C k) by joining L k-1 with itself (combinations of large (k-1)-itemsets that share k-2 items).
    *   Prune C k: Remove any candidate k-itemset that has a (k-1)-itemset subset which is not in L k-1.
    *   Scan the database to count the support of the remaining candidates in C k.
    *   Determine L k from C k by selecting candidates that meet the minimum support.
    *   Increment k.
3.  The set of all large itemsets is the union of all L k found.

This pruning step (removing candidates whose subsets are not large) is crucial for efficiency, especially with large databases and many items. It avoids calculating the support for a vast number of combinations that cannot possibly meet the minimum support threshold.

To further optimize, candidate generation can be done by arranging items lexicographically within itemsets and only joining itemsets that differ in the last item. This trick guarantees capturing all candidates that might have enough support while still requiring a pruning step. This method is particularly convenient for implementation with relational transactional data.

After finding all large itemsets, the second stage involves generating the association rules from these large itemsets and checking their confidence against a minimum confidence threshold.

## Conclusion

We have discussed the core concepts and the algorithm for Apriori, which is a key technique for efficiently finding large itemsets in Market Basket Analysis. In the next video, we will move on to a hands-on demonstration of how to implement this.

For further details, you can refer to these links (mentioned in the original presentation):

*   [http://rakesh.agrawal-family.com/papers/vldb94apriori.pdf](http://rakesh.agrawal-family.com/papers/vldb94apriori.pdf) (Original Apriori paper)
*   [http://infolab.stanford.edu/~ullman/mining/book.html](http://infolab.stanford.edu/~ullman/mining/book.html) (Textbook on Data Mining)

An example was shown briefly in the presentation slides, which you can check out and try on your own.

Thank you for watching this video. We covered the Apriori algorithm and its usage in Market Basket Analysis. In the next video, we will implement it hands-on.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 44 : rfm and market basket analysis (contd.)

```markdown
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 44: RFM and Market Basket Analysis (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics class. This session is part of Week 8 and focuses on performing **Market Basket Analysis** in a hands-on manner using R. We will use the `arules` library and a publicly available grocery store transaction dataset.

## Setting up the Environment and Data

To begin, we need to load the necessary R libraries. The primary library for association rule mining is `arules`. The dataset we will use is an inbuilt dataset within R, possibly related to the `datasets` library, but the `arules` package often includes or can directly handle the necessary data format.

```r
# Install the necessary libraries if you haven't already
# install.packages("arules")
# install.packages("datasets") # Datasets might be a base package already installed

# Load the libraries
library(arules)
# library(datasets) # Load if needed, but arules often works directly with its data
```

The instructor notes that `datasets` might be a base package and already installed. The focus will be on `arules`.

The data we will use is the `groceries` dataset, which is an inbuilt dataset available after loading the `arules` library. Each row in this dataset represents a transaction, listing the items purchased together.

```r
# Load the groceries dataset
data(groceries)

# Inspect the data (optional, but good practice)
# The instructor briefly shows the raw data in a separate file, but here we work with the loaded dataset
# summary(groceries)
# inspect(groceries[1:5]) # Look at the first few transactions
```

The dataset consists of approximately 10,000 transactions.

## Initial Data Exploration: Item Frequency

Before generating rules, it's useful to understand the frequency of individual items. We can create an item frequency plot to visualize the most popular items.

```r
# Create an item frequency plot for the top 20 items
itemFrequencyPlot(groceries, topN = 20, type = "relative")
```

This plot shows the relative frequency (support) of the top 20 most frequently purchased items. As observed in the plot, **whole milk** is the most common product, followed by other vegetables, rolls/buns, soda, yoghurt, bottled water, etc. Whole milk appears in about 25% of the transactions (or around 2500 out of 10,000).

## Key Concepts: Support, Confidence, and Lift

Market Basket Analysis relies on three key metrics to evaluate the strength and interestingness of association rules:

1.  **Support:**
    *   Definition: The fraction of transactions that contain a particular item set.
    *   Formula: Support({A, B}) = (Number of transactions containing A and B) / (Total number of transactions)
    *   In the context of a rule A -> B, Support(A -> B) = Support({A, B}). It indicates how frequently the items in the rule (both antecedent and consequent) appear together relative to all transactions.

2.  **Confidence:**
    *   Definition: The probability that the consequent item(s) are purchased given that the antecedent item(s) are purchased.
    *   Formula: Confidence(A -> B) = Support({A, B}) / Support({A}) = P(B | A)
    *   It measures the reliability of the rule. A high confidence suggests that B is likely to be bought when A is bought.

3.  **Lift:**
    *   Definition: The ratio by which the confidence of a rule exceeds the expected confidence assuming the antecedent and consequent are independent.
    *   Formula: Lift(A -> B) = Confidence(A -> B) / Support({B}) = P(B | A) / P(B)
    *   It indicates how much more likely the consequent is to be purchased when the antecedent is purchased, compared to its baseline purchase probability.
    *   Lift > 1: A and B are positively associated (items are more likely to be bought together than separately).
    *   Lift = 1: A and B are independent (no association).
    *   Lift < 1: A and B are negatively associated (items are less likely to be bought together than separately).
    *   A higher lift value indicates a stronger association and a more useful rule for marketing action.

These three metrics help judge a rule's effectiveness:
*   **Support:** Ensures the rule is not a statistical anomaly and occurs frequently enough.
*   **Confidence:** Measures the accuracy of the prediction (if A, then B).
*   **Lift:** Measures the usefulness/actionability of the rule by showing the strength of the association compared to chance.

## Generating Association Rules using Apriori

We use the Apriori algorithm to find association rules that meet minimum support and confidence thresholds.

```r
# Run the Apriori algorithm
# parameters: specify minimum support and confidence
rules <- apriori(groceries, parameter = list(support = 0.001, confidence = 0.8))
```

This code generates rules with a minimum support of 0.001 (i.e., occurring in at least 0.1% of transactions) and a minimum confidence of 0.8 (i.e., the rule is correct at least 80% of the time when the antecedent occurs).

## Inspecting and Interpreting Rules

We can get a summary of the generated rules and inspect individual rules.

```r
# Get a summary of the generated rules
summary(rules)
```

The summary provides information about the number of rules generated, their size distribution (number of items in the rule), and the range (minimum, mean, maximum) of support, confidence, and lift values. In this case, using the specified parameters, 410 rules were generated, with sizes ranging from 3 to 6 items.

```r
# Inspect the first few rules
inspect(rules[1:5])
```

Looking at the first 5 rules generated by default (which may not be the "best" rules based on metrics):

```
    lhs                                 rhs             support     confidence  lift
[1] {liquor, red blush wine}          => {bottled beer} 0.001935701 0.9047619   11.20020
[2] {curd, cereals}                   => {whole milk}   0.001013585 0.9166667    3.618777
[3] {spread cheese, fruit/vegetable}  => {whole milk}   0.001013585 0.8333333    3.288547
[4] {root vegetables, whipped sour cream, flour} => {whole milk} 0.001731145 0.8500000    3.353439
[5] {butter, soft cheese, domestic eggs} => {whole milk} 0.001013585 0.8333333    3.288547
```

Let's interpret the first rule: `{liquor, red blush wine} => {bottled beer}`.
*   **Support (0.00193):** This combination (buying liquor, red blush wine, AND bottled beer) occurs in about 0.19% of all transactions. This is low, but meets our minimum threshold.
*   **Confidence (0.90476):** When a customer buys both liquor and red blush wine, they also buy bottled beer in approximately 90.5% of those transactions. This is very high.
*   **Lift (11.200):** When a customer buys liquor and red blush wine, they are 11.2 times more likely to also buy bottled beer compared to the probability of buying bottled beer by itself (its base support). This indicates a strong, non-random association.

The second rule: `{curd, cereals} => {whole milk}`.
*   **Support (0.00101):** This combination occurs in about 0.1% of transactions.
*   **Confidence (0.916667):** When a customer buys both curd and cereals, they also buy whole milk in approximately 91.7% of those transactions. High confidence.
*   **Lift (3.618):** The lift is lower compared to the first rule. The instructor explains this is because whole milk is bought very frequently anyway (high base support). A lift of 3.6 means that buying curd and cereals makes buying whole milk 3.6 times *more* likely than its already high base probability. While the confidence is high, the *relative* increase in probability isn't as dramatic as in the first rule with lower base probabilities.

## Filtering and Sorting Rules

We can sort the rules by metrics like confidence, support, or lift to find the most "interesting" ones based on our criteria.

```r
# Sort rules by confidence in decreasing order
rules_conf <- sort(rules, by = "confidence", decreasing = TRUE)

# Inspect the top 5 rules by confidence
inspect(rules_conf[1:5])
```

Sorting by confidence reveals rules with very high confidence (close to 1.0), meaning the consequent almost always occurs when the antecedent does. However, these rules often have low support, indicating the antecedent combination itself is rare.

We can also filter rules based on the number of items involved or specific items.

**Filtering by Rule Length (`maxlen`)**:
To find simpler rules (e.g., 2 or 3 items total in the rule), we can specify a maximum length parameter during rule generation.

```r
# Generate rules with a maximum length of 3 items
rules_maxlen3 <- apriori(groceries, parameter = list(support = 0.001, confidence = 0.8, maxlen = 3))

# Sort these rules by confidence
rules_maxlen3_conf <- sort(rules_maxlen3, by = "confidence", decreasing = TRUE)

# Inspect the top rules with max length 3
inspect(rules_maxlen3_conf[1:5])
```

This helps identify shorter, potentially easier-to-action rules.

**Filtering by Item Inclusion (`appearances`)**:
We can find rules where a specific item appears in the antecedent (LHS) or the consequent (RHS).

To find rules where 'whole milk' is the *consequent* (i.e., rules predicting the purchase of whole milk):

```r
# Generate rules where the RHS must be 'whole milk'
rules_whole_milk_rhs <- apriori(groceries, parameter = list(support = 0.001, confidence = 0.8),
                               appearance = list(rhs = "whole milk", default = "lhs"))

# Sort these rules by confidence
rules_whole_milk_rhs_conf <- sort(rules_whole_milk_rhs, by = "confidence", decreasing = TRUE)

# Inspect the top rules predicting 'whole milk'
inspect(rules_whole_milk_rhs_conf[1:5])
```

To find rules where 'whole milk' is in the *antecedent* (i.e., rules starting with 'whole milk' and other items):

```r
# Generate rules where 'whole milk' is in the LHS and minimum rule length is 2
rules_whole_milk_lhs <- apriori(groceries, parameter = list(support = 0.001, confidence = 0.8, minlen = 2),
                               appearance = list(lhs = "whole milk", default = "rhs"))

# Sort these rules by confidence
rules_whole_milk_lhs_conf <- sort(rules_whole_milk_lhs, by = "confidence", decreasing = TRUE)

# Inspect the top rules starting with 'whole milk'
inspect(rules_whole_milk_lhs_conf[1:5])
```
Note the `minlen = 2` parameter here ensures that the rule is at least two items long (one on the LHS, one on the RHS). If `whole milk` is the only item on the LHS, the rule will be at least 2 items total (1 on LHS, 1+ on RHS).

## Marketing Implications

Generating and sorting rules is a data science task. The next crucial step is interpreting these rules and translating them into marketing actions.

For example, consider the rule `{liquor, red blush wine} => {bottled beer}` with a high lift. This suggests a strong association. A marketing manager could use this insight in several ways:
*   **Product Placement:** Place bottled beer near the liquor and red blush wine sections.
*   **Bundling/Promotions:** Offer a discount or bundle deal when a customer buys all three items.
*   **Cross-promotion:** Advertise bottled beer in areas displaying wine and liquor.

Similarly, if a rule like `{curd, cereals} => {whole milk}` is found, even with lower lift but high confidence, it might still be actionable. Since whole milk is a frequent purchase, placing curd and cereals nearby could encourage impulse buys or simplify shopping for customers who typically buy all three.

The decision of *which* rules to act upon depends on business objectives, feasibility, and further testing (e.g., A/B testing different store layouts or promotions). Marketing managers need to evaluate rules not just on statistical metrics (support, confidence, lift) but also on their potential business impact and strategic fit.

## Conclusion

Market Basket Analysis using algorithms like Apriori helps uncover hidden associations between products in transaction data. By defining and evaluating rules based on support, confidence, and lift, we can identify strong patterns. These patterns provide valuable insights for making data-driven marketing decisions related to product placement, promotions, bundling, and more.

This concludes the session on Market Basket Analysis. We have seen how to perform the analysis in R and interpret the results.

We will continue our discussion in Week 9. Thank you for being with me.

# Marketing Analytics: Customer Churn and Customer Lifetime Value (Contd.)

This lecture continues the discussion on customer churn prediction, focusing on how to predict churning behavior using a real-world dataset and applying different modeling techniques in R.

## Introduction

Hello everyone, welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. In the previous video, we briefly discussed the importance of customer churn. In this session, we will delve into predicting customer churning behavior using analytical models.

We will be using an R script file named `week 9 S2.r`.

## Data Description and Initial Exploration

The dataset used is a Telco customer churn data, downloaded from IBM Watson's website. It's freely available for academic use.

Let's look at the data structure. It contains around 7000 observations (customers). The variables include:

*   **Customer ID:** Unique identifier for each customer.
*   **Gender:** Male or Female.
*   **Senior Citizen:** Whether the customer is a senior citizen (Yes/No, represented as 1/0).
*   **Partner:** Whether the customer has a partner (Yes/No).
*   **Dependents:** Whether the customer has dependents (Yes/No).
*   **Tenure:** Number of months the customer has stayed with the company.
*   **PhoneService:** Whether the customer has phone service (Yes/No).
*   **MultipleLines:** Whether the customer has multiple lines (Yes/No, No phone service).
*   **InternetService:** Customer's internet service type (DSL, Fiber optic, No).
*   **OnlineSecurity:** Whether the customer has online security (Yes/No, No internet service).
*   **OnlineBackup:** Whether the customer has online backup (Yes/No, No internet service).
*   **DeviceProtection:** Whether the customer has device protection (Yes/No, No internet service).
*   **TechSupport:** Whether the customer has tech support (Yes/No, No internet service).
*   **StreamingTV:** Whether the customer has streaming TV (Yes/No, No internet service).
*   **StreamingMovies:** Whether the customer has streaming movies (Yes/No, No internet service).
*   **Contract:** The contract term (Month-to-month, One year, Two year).
*   **PaperlessBilling:** Whether the customer has paperless billing (Yes/No).
*   **PaymentMethod:** The payment method used (Electronic check, Mailed check, Bank transfer (automatic), Credit card (automatic)).
*   **MonthlyCharges:** The amount charged to the customer monthly.
*   **TotalCharges:** The total amount charged to the customer.
*   **Churn:** The target variable - whether the customer churned (Yes/No).

Before jumping into prediction, it's good practice to understand the data through initial exploration, like using pivot tables in Excel. This helps identify potential predictors.

*   **Gender vs. Churn:** Initial pivot table analysis shows no significant difference in churning rates between male and female customers.
*   **Senior Citizen vs. Churn:** Counter-intuitively, senior citizens show a much higher churn rate (almost 40%) compared to non-senior citizens (less than 25%).
*   **Partner/Dependents vs. Churn:** Customers with partners or dependents tend to be less prone to switching. This could be due to shared services or family plans, making switching more complicated or costly.
*   **Tenure vs. Churn:** Grouping tenure reveals that customers with shorter tenure have a higher probability of churning, which decreases significantly as tenure increases. This aligns with the expectation that long-term customers are generally more loyal.
*   **Payment Method vs. Churn:** Customers using electronic checks show a higher propensity to churn compared to those using mailed checks, bank transfers, or credit cards. This might indicate electronic check users are more tech-savvy and perhaps more willing to switch for better online deals.

## Data Preparation in R

Let's move to R for data preparation and modeling.

```R
# Check and install tidyverse if not present
if (!require("tidyverse")) {
  install.packages("tidyverse")
}

# Load tidyverse
library(tidyverse)

# Set working directory to source file location (assuming the data file is in the same location as the script)
setwd(dirname(rstudioapi::getActiveDocumentContext()$path))

# Read the data
db_churn <- read.csv("telco_customer_churn.csv")

# Check data structure
str(db_churn)
```

Initially, many variables, including categorical ones, are read as character types.

```R
# Load janitor for easy tabulation
if (!require("janitor")) {
  install.packages("janitor")
}
library(janitor)

# Check churn distribution
db_churn %>% tabyl(Churn)
```

The churn distribution shows about 26% churn rate and 73% non-churn rate.

```R
# Convert SeniorCitizen to factor
db_churn$SeniorCitizen <- as.factor(db_churn$SeniorCitizen)

# Convert other character columns to factors (except TotalCharges)
db_churn <- db_churn %>%
  mutate_if(is.character, as.factor)

# TotalCharges has some missing values read as " "
# Let's check how many missing values are there
sum(is.na(db_churn$TotalCharges)) # Check for NA first

# It seems missing values are not read as NA by default. Let's check for empty strings or spaces.
sum(db_churn$TotalCharges == " ")

# Convert TotalCharges to numeric, coercing errors to NA
db_churn$TotalCharges <- as.numeric(as.character(db_churn$TotalCharges))

# Check for NAs again
sum(is.na(db_churn$TotalCharges))

# Filter out rows with missing TotalCharges
db_churn <- db_churn %>%
  filter(!is.na(TotalCharges))

# Check dimensions after filtering
dim(db_churn) # Should be slightly less than 7043
```

11 rows with missing `TotalCharges` were removed.

Let's look at the distribution of continuous variables: `MonthlyCharges`, `TotalCharges`, and `tenure`.

```R
# Histograms of continuous variables
hist(db_churn$MonthlyCharges)
hist(db_churn$TotalCharges)
hist(db_churn$tenure)
```

The histograms show that these variables are not normally distributed; they are skewed. For some modeling techniques, especially distance-based ones, normalization is helpful. We'll define a normalization function that scales values between 0 and 1.

```R
# Normalization function
norm_x <- function(x){
  (x - min(x)) / (max(x) - min(x))
}

# Create a new dataframe for normalized data and factor conversions
db_churn2 <- db_churn

# Normalize continuous variables
db_churn2$TotalCharges <- norm_x(db_churn2$TotalCharges)
db_churn2$MonthlyCharges <- norm_x(db_churn2$MonthlyCharges)
db_churn2$tenure <- norm_x(db_churn2$tenure)

# Handle specific categories like "No internet service" or "No phone service"
# For columns 7 to 16 (excluding 11 and 12 which are numeric now)
# And columns 1 to 5
# Let's verify column indices and names first
names(db_churn2)

# Columns from InternetService to StreamingMovies (7 to 16 are mostly categorical, except tenure, MonthlyCharges, TotalCharges)
# The relevant columns are 7:11 and 13:16 (or better, select by name)
cols_to_process <- c("InternetService", "OnlineSecurity", "OnlineBackup", "DeviceProtection", "TechSupport", "StreamingTV", "StreamingMovies", "MultipleLines", "PhoneService")

for (col in cols_to_process) {
  if (is.factor(db_churn2[[col]])) {
    levels(db_churn2[[col]]) <- c(levels(db_churn2[[col]])[grep("No internet service|No phone service", levels(db_churn2[[col]]), invert = TRUE)], "No")
    db_churn2[[col]] <- as.factor(db_churn2[[col]])
  }
}

# Also check columns 1:5 - they are already factors like gender, partner, dependents, SeniorCitizen, CustomerID (CustomerID will be removed later)
# Let's re-convert them to factors explicitly if needed or confirm their types
# db_churn2 <- db_churn2 %>%
#   mutate_at(c("gender", "SeniorCitizen", "Partner", "Dependents", "PhoneService", "MultipleLines", "InternetService", "OnlineSecurity", "OnlineBackup", "DeviceProtection", "TechSupport", "StreamingTV", "StreamingMovies", "Contract", "PaperlessBilling", "PaymentMethod", "Churn"), as.factor)

# Remove CustomerID as it's not a predictor
db_churn2 <- db_churn2 %>% select(-customerID)

# Verify factor levels again if needed
# summary(db_churn2)
```

## Model Building

Now, we split the data into training and testing sets to build and evaluate our predictive models.

```R
# Load caret for splitting data and model training
if (!require("caret")) {
  install.packages("caret")
}
library(caret)

# Set seed for reproducibility
set.seed(7)

# Create stratified training index based on Churn
# Using createDataPartition from caret for stratified split
train_index <- createDataPartition(db_churn2$Churn, p = 0.7, list = FALSE)

# Create training and testing datasets
db_train <- db_churn2[train_index, ]
db_test <- db_churn2[-train_index, ]

# Check dimensions of train and test sets
dim(db_train) # Around 70% of 7032
dim(db_test)  # Around 30% of 7032
```

We will build three different models: Decision Tree, Random Forest, and Logistic Regression.

### Decision Tree

```R
# Load rpart and rpart.plot for Decision Tree
if (!require("rpart")) {
  install.packages("rpart")
}
if (!require("rpart.plot")) {
  install.packages("rpart.plot")
}
library(rpart)
library(rpart.plot)

# Build the Decision Tree model
# Using the formula approach: predict Churn based on all other variables
tree_churn <- rpart(Churn ~ ., data = db_train, method = "class") # Use method="class" for classification

# Plot the tree
rpart.plot(tree_churn, extra = 104, fallen.leaves = TRUE, main = "Decision Tree for Churn Prediction")

# Print the tree rules (more detailed)
print(tree_churn)
```

The decision tree visually represents the rules for predicting churn. The primary split is based on `Contract`. Customers with 'One year' or 'Two year' contracts are much less likely to churn (node with low 'yes' probability). For month-to-month customers, the next important factor is `InternetService` (Fiber optic is associated with higher churn). Further splits involve `tenure`. This tree gives actionable insights: encouraging longer contracts is key to reducing churn.

### Random Forest

Random Forest is an ensemble method that builds multiple decision trees and combines their predictions.

```R
# Random Forest model using caret's train function
# This might require the 'randomForest' package
if (!require("randomForest")) {
  install.packages("randomForest")
}
library(randomForest)


# Define training control (e.g., cross-validation) - simplified here for brevity
# train_control <- trainControl(method="cv", number=5, classProbs=TRUE, summaryFunction=twoClassSummary) # Example, but the example uses simpler train()

# Train the Random Forest model
# Using caret's train for tuning mtry (number of variables randomly sampled at each split)
# We need to make sure Churn is a factor with valid R levels for caret
# Let's check the levels first
levels(db_train$Churn) # Should be "No", "Yes"

# Rename levels to be R-friendly
levels(db_train$Churn) <- make.names(levels(db_train$Churn)) # Changes "No" to "No" and "Yes" to "Yes" - already R friendly, good.

# Train the model - using method="rf" (random forest)
# It will tune the 'mtry' parameter by default or based on tuneLength
model_rf <- train(Churn ~ ., data = db_train, method = "rf",
                  ntree = 75, # Number of trees
                  tuneLength = 5, # How many mtry values to try
                  metric = "ROC", # Metric to optimize
                  trControl = trainControl(classProbs=TRUE, summaryFunction=twoClassSummary)) # Required for ROC metric

print(model_rf)
plot(model_rf) # Plot performance vs mtry
```

The `train` function evaluates the Random Forest model with different `mtry` values and reports metrics like ROC. The output shows the performance for each `mtry` value tried.

### Logistic Regression

Logistic Regression is a traditional statistical method for binary classification.

```R
# Convert Churn to numeric (0 for No, 1 for Yes) for logistic regression
db_train_logistic <- db_train
db_test_logistic <- db_test

# Re-check levels
levels(db_train_logistic$Churn)

# Convert factor levels to numeric 0 and 1
db_train_logistic$Churn <- ifelse(db_train_logistic$Churn == "Yes", 1, 0)
db_test_logistic$Churn <- ifelse(db_test_logistic$Churn == "Yes", 1, 0)

# Build the initial logistic regression model with all predictors
model_logistic_full <- glm(Churn ~ ., data = db_train_logistic, family = "binomial")

# Use step function for backward variable selection (based on AIC)
# Note: stepAIC requires MASS package
if (!require("MASS")) {
  install.packages("MASS")
}
library(MASS)

model_logistic <- stepAIC(model_logistic_full, direction = "backward")

# Summary of the final logistic regression model
summary(model_logistic)
```

The summary shows the coefficients and their significance for each predictor in the final logistic regression model chosen by the `stepAIC` function. Many variables are statistically significant predictors of churn.

## Model Evaluation

We will evaluate the performance of the three models on the unseen test data, primarily using ROC curves.

```R
# Load ROSE for ROC plotting (or use pROC/ROCR)
if (!require("ROSE")) {
  install.packages("ROSE")
}
library(ROSE) # or pROC or ROCR

# Predict probabilities on the test set for each model

# Decision Tree Prediction (get probabilities)
pred_tree_prob <- predict(tree_churn, newdata = db_test, type = "prob")[, "Yes"] # Get probability of 'Yes' class

# Random Forest Prediction (get probabilities)
pred_rf_prob <- predict(model_rf, newdata = db_test, type = "prob")[, "Yes"] # Get probability of 'Yes' class

# Logistic Regression Prediction (get probabilities)
pred_logistic_prob <- predict(model_logistic, newdata = db_test_logistic, type = "response") # Get probability of churn (1)
```

Now, let's plot the ROC curves.

```R
# Plot ROC curves
roc_tree <- roc(db_test$Churn, pred_tree_prob) # Need factor Churn for ROSE/pROC
roc_rf <- roc(db_test$Churn, pred_rf_prob)
roc_logistic <- roc(db_test$Churn, pred_logistic_prob) # Using original test set Churn (factor)

plot(roc_tree, col = "blue", main = "ROC Curves")
lines(roc_rf, col = "red")
lines(roc_logistic, col = "darkgreen")
legend("bottomright", legend = c("Decision Tree", "Random Forest", "Logistic Regression"), col = c("blue", "red", "darkgreen"), lwd = 2)

# Print AUC values
auc(roc_tree)
auc(roc_rf)
auc(roc_logistic)
```

The ROC curves show the trade-off between the true positive rate and the false positive rate. A curve closer to the top-left corner indicates better performance. Visually and by AUC values, Random Forest and Logistic Regression perform significantly better than the single Decision Tree and are quite comparable to each other.

We can also evaluate performance using a confusion matrix for a specific cutoff probability. A common cutoff is the base churn rate in the training data (around 26.5%).

```R
# Get the base churn rate from the training data
base_churn_rate <- mean(db_train_logistic$Churn)
base_churn_rate # Approximately 0.265

# Create predicted classes for logistic regression using the base churn rate as cutoff
pred_logistic_class <- ifelse(pred_logistic_prob > base_churn_rate, "Yes", "No")

# Create the confusion matrix
# Need to convert actual Churn in test set back to "Yes"/"No" factor matching predicted levels
actual_churn_test_factor <- ifelse(db_test_logistic$Churn == 1, "Yes", "No")
actual_churn_test_factor <- factor(actual_churn_test_factor, levels = c("No", "Yes"))
pred_logistic_class_factor <- factor(pred_logistic_class, levels = c("No", "Yes"))


confusionMatrix(pred_logistic_class_factor, actual_churn_test_factor)
```

The confusion matrix shows how many customers were correctly predicted as churners (True Positives), correctly predicted as non-churners (True Negatives), incorrectly predicted as churners (False Positives), and incorrectly predicted as non-churners (False Negatives). Metrics like Sensitivity (True Positive Rate - ability to identify actual churners) and Specificity (True Negative Rate - ability to identify actual non-churners) are important.

Using the base churn rate (around 0.265) as the cutoff, the confusion matrix shows that the model correctly predicts a good percentage of actual churners (high sensitivity).

## Conclusion

We explored the Telco churn data, performed necessary data preparation steps including handling missing values, converting variable types, and normalization. We built three models: Decision Tree, Random Forest, and Logistic Regression. The ROC curves and confusion matrix evaluation indicate that both Random Forest and Logistic Regression perform well in predicting churn, significantly better than a single decision tree.

While machine learning models like Random Forest often offer higher predictive power, econometric models like Logistic Regression can provide valuable insights into *why* customers churn through their coefficients and significance levels, aiding marketing managers in developing targeted retention strategies. In this case, Logistic Regression performed comparably to Random Forest on the ROC curve.

The explanation part of analyzing which variables are driving churn is still crucial for marketing actions. We will continue exploring how to explain churning behavior and discuss customer lifetime value in the upcoming videos.

Thank you for being with me. We will continue this topic in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics: Customer Churn and Customer Lifetime Value (Contd.)

This lecture, part of the NPTEL Marketing Analytics course by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur, focuses on customer switching behavior in the context of products, contrasting it with service churn discussed previously.

## Introduction: Product Switching Behavior

Unlike durable goods, non-durable products, such as Fast-Moving Consumer Goods (FMCG) like soap, toothpaste, or shampoo, often see customers switching between brands. This switching is common due to factors like:

*   **Multiple Moments of Truth:** Customers interact with the product frequently.
*   **Variety Seeking Behavior:** Customers might try different brands out of curiosity or desire for change.
*   **Dissatisfaction/Return:** Customers might switch to a new brand and, if unsatisfied, return to their previous brand.

This dynamic switching is prevalent for brands like Coke and Pepsi, or Lux and Lifebuoy.

For marketers, the goal isn't necessarily to eliminate switching entirely (as it's inherent in these categories) but to:

*   **Minimize Outflow:** Reduce the number of customers leaving their brand.
*   **Maximize Inflow:** Attract customers from other brands.
*   **Maintain or Increase Stable Market Share:** Ensure that even with switching, the overall proportion of customers remains favorable or grows.

## Case Study: Retailer Private Label Strategy

This lecture presents a case study involving a retail shop. This retailer has its own private label brands (e.g., More's private label, Big Bazaar's private label) alongside national or major brands. The retailer's objective is to understand how to encourage customers to switch from these major brands to their private label.

Retailers can influence customer choice by:

*   **Pricing:** Offering price discounts on private labels.
*   **Placement:** Positioning private labels favorably in the store.
*   **Service/Promotions:** Providing additional benefits or promotions for buying private labels.

For low-involvement products like flour (`Aata`), the retailer acts as the 'pool maker'. Customers come to the *retailer* for groceries, not specifically for a single brand like Aashirvaad Aata. This gives the retailer significant negotiation power over manufacturers of national brands. This power is used either to introduce and promote private labels or to charge manufacturers for favorable shelf space/placement.

Analyzing customer switching behavior and customer lifetime value (CLV) is crucial for both retailers and manufacturers in this scenario to make informed decisions about pricing, promotion, and slotting fees.

## Understanding Brand Switching Data

The case study uses an Excel file (`brand switching.xlsx`) containing data on customer brand switching probabilities.

The data includes:

*   **7 Brands:** Brand 1 through Brand 7.
*   **Private Label:** Brand 4 is highlighted as the retailer's private label.
*   **Transition Matrices:** Two matrices showing the probability of a customer switching from one brand (row) to another (column) between two time periods.
    *   One matrix for a **non-promotion** scenario.
    *   One matrix for a **promotion** scenario (promotion is for Brand 4, the private label).
*   **Initial Market Share:** The starting distribution of customers across the 7 brands.

### Transition Matrix Explained

A transition matrix models the probability of moving from one state (brand bought in period 1) to another state (brand bought in period 2). It's a core concept in Markov chains.

*   Rows represent the brand bought in the previous period.
*   Columns represent the brand bought in the current period.
*   Each cell `P(i, j)` is the probability that a customer who bought brand `i` in the previous period will buy brand `j` in the current period.
*   The sum of probabilities in each row must equal 1.

For example, if the probability of a Brand 1 customer staying with Brand 1 is 0.42, and switching to Brand 3 is 0.13, switching to Brand 4 is 0.1, and so on, the sum of probabilities across that row is 1.

The data shows that these transition probabilities are not necessarily symmetric (e.g., switching from Brand 1 to Brand 2 might have a different probability than switching from Brand 2 to Brand 1). This data was collected from customer purchase history and surveys.

### Promotion Impact

The promotion scenario matrix shows how these switching probabilities change when Brand 4 (the private label) is promoted (e.g., via a price discount). The average price for Brand 4 is $0.045$. During promotion, it's discounted by 20%, making the price $0.045 * 0.8$.

## Calculating Market Share Over Time

We can use the transition matrix to predict market share in future periods. If we have the market share distribution in period T, multiplying this vector by the transition matrix gives the market share distribution in period T+1.

Initial Market Share (vector) * Transition Matrix = Market Share at T+1 (vector)

Mathematically, if the initial market share is a row vector `M0` (1x7) and the transition matrix is `T` (7x7), the market share at time `t` is `Mt = M0 * T^t` (matrix multiplication).

In Excel, this is done using the `MMULT` function, which performs matrix multiplication.

### Steps for Calculating Market Share in Excel

1.  Have the initial market share vector (e.g., a 1x7 range of cells).
2.  Have the transition matrix (a 7x7 range of cells).
3.  Select a range of cells for the output market share vector (a 1x7 range).
4.  In the function bar, type `=MMULT(array1, array2)`.
    *   `array1` is the market share vector from the previous period.
    *   `array2` is the transition matrix.
5.  **Important:** Press **Shift + Ctrl + Enter** simultaneously. This tells Excel to perform an array calculation and will fill the selected output range with the result.

Let's see the calculation for the first few periods with promotion:

*   **Initial Market Share (T=0):** Given in the data.
*   **Market Share after 1 Period (T=1):** `MMULT(Initial Market Share, Promotion Transition Matrix)`
*   **Market Share after 2 Periods (T=2):** `MMULT(Market Share at T=1, Promotion Transition Matrix)`
*   **Market Share after 3 Periods (T=3):** `MMULT(Market Share at T=2, Promotion Transition Matrix)`

The instructor then calculated the market share for periods T=4, T=5, T=6 using the *non-promotion* transition matrix, starting from the market share state at T=3.

*   **Market Share after 4 Periods (T=4):** `MMULT(Market Share at T=3, Non-Promotion Transition Matrix)`
*   **Market Share after 5 Periods (T=5):** `MMULT(Market Share at T=4, Non-Promotion Transition Matrix)`
*   **Market Share after 6 Periods (T=6):** `MMULT(Market Share at T=5, Non-Promotion Transition Matrix)`

By performing these calculations, we can see how the market share for each brand evolves over time under different promotion strategies. The calculations showed Brand 4's market share increasing over the first few periods with promotion.

## Calculating Long-Term Steady State Market Share

If the transition probabilities remain constant over a very long period, the market share distribution will eventually reach a **steady state**. In this state, the market share for each brand no longer changes from one period to the next.

Mathematically, the steady state market share vector `M_steady` satisfies the equation:

`M_steady * T = M_steady`

This can be rewritten as `M_steady * (T - I) = 0`, where `I` is the identity matrix and `0` is a zero vector. However, this system is linearly dependent (as the rows of T sum to 1), so the trivial solution `M_steady = 0` is the only solution to `M_steady * (T - I) = 0`.

To find the non-trivial steady state vector, we use the property that the sum of the probabilities in the market share vector must be 1. We modify the system of equations:

Let `M_steady = [p1, p2, ..., p7]`, where `pi` is the steady state market share for brand `i`.
The system of equations from `M_steady * T = M_steady` can be written as:
`p1*T(1,1) + p2*T(2,1) + ... + p7*T(7,1) = p1`
`p1*T(1,2) + p2*T(2,2) + ... + p7*T(7,2) = p2`
...
`p1*T(1,7) + p2*T(2,7) + ... + p7*T(7,7) = p7`

Rearranging these gives:
`p1*(T(1,1)-1) + p2*T(2,1) + ... + p7*T(7,1) = 0`
`p1*T(1,2) + p2*(T(2,2)-1) + ... + p7*T(7,2) = 0`
...
`p1*T(1,7) + p2*T(2,7) + ... + p7*(T(7,7)-1) = 0`

This is equivalent to `M_steady * (T - I_diag) = 0`, where `I_diag` is a matrix with -1 on the diagonal and 0 elsewhere (derived from `M_steady * T - M_steady * I = 0`).
To solve this uniquely such that the probabilities sum to 1, we replace one of the rows in the system with the equation `p1 + p2 + ... + p7 = 1`.

In Excel, this is often set up by creating a matrix `M` where the first `n-1` rows represent the system `M_steady * (T - I_diag) = 0` equations (excluding one redundant equation), and the last row represents `[1, 1, ..., 1]`. The target vector `y` will be `[0, 0, ..., 0, 1]`. The steady state vector `M_steady` is then found by solving `M_steady * M = y`, which means `M_steady = y * M_inverse`. *Correction:* The instructor's approach in Excel appears to set up a system `A * M_steady (transpose) = y (transpose)`, where A is derived from the transition matrix. Let's follow the steps shown.

### Steps for Calculating Steady State in Excel

1.  Create a matrix `M` from the **transpose** of the transition matrix.
2.  Subtract 1 from the diagonal elements of this transposed matrix. This captures the `(T(i,i) - 1)` terms.
3.  Replace one entire row of this matrix (e.g., the last row) with `[1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1]`. This incorporates the `sum(pi) = 1` constraint.
4.  Create a target vector `y` (a 7x1 column) with zeros for the first six rows and a 1 in the last row.
5.  The steady state market share probabilities (as a column vector) are found by calculating the inverse of matrix `M` and multiplying it by vector `y`.
    *   `Steady State Vector = MMULT(MINVERSE(M), y)`
6.  Select the output range (a 7x1 range).
7.  In the function bar, type `=MMULT(MINVERSE(M_range), y_range)`.
8.  Press **Shift + Ctrl + Enter**.

Using this method:

*   **Non-Promotion Steady State:** Calculate steady state using the Non-Promotion Transition Matrix. Brand 4's steady state market share is approximately 5.3%.
*   **Promotion Steady State:** Calculate steady state using the Promotion Transition Matrix. Brand 4's steady state market share is approximately 10%.

### Comparing Scenarios

The long-term steady state analysis reveals a significant difference:

*   **No Promotion:** Brand 4 saturates around 5.3% market share. Revenue per transaction for Brand 4 is $0.045$.
*   **With Promotion:** Brand 4 saturates around 10% market share. Revenue per transaction for Brand 4 is $0.045 * 0.8 = $0.036$.

Even though the revenue per transaction is lower with promotion, the long-term market share is almost double. This suggests that the increased volume due to the higher market share likely outweighs the lower per-unit revenue, making the promotion strategy potentially more profitable in the long run.

## Conclusion

Analyzing brand switching using transition matrices and calculating steady state market shares is a powerful tool for understanding the long-term impact of marketing strategies like promotions, especially in product categories with frequent repurchase. It helps marketers evaluate whether the gain in market share (and potential volume) compensates for changes in price or cost. Future sessions will delve deeper into calculating Customer Lifetime Value (CLV) based on this kind of switching behavior.
