Okay, here is the transcript converted into a structured markdown format.

# Marketing Analytics: Customer Churn and Customer Lifetime Value (Contd.)

## Introduction

Hello everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. In this particular week, we are discussing Customer Churn and Customer Lifetime Value (CLV). We are in session 4, and this is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT, Kharagpur. In this session, we will talk about customer lifetime value, or basically, the economics of CRM.

## Why CRM is Important

As I told at the earlier stage of this particular week, you have to maintain a relationship with customers. It's like any relationship – for example, a personal relationship. When my wife was my girlfriend, in the initial days when we were not married in our college days, we had to understand each other, create understanding, and spend time together. Lots of effort was needed to build that relationship. Now, we have been in this relationship for a long time; I'm married and have a kid. Once we've passed this long path, to sustain the relationship further, the effort required has come down significantly.

*   **Cost Effectiveness:** I understand her pretty well, she understands me pretty well. Decisions we take, shortcomings we have are generally well understood and settled. We are in a steady state, and being in a steady state is generally less costly, less effortful to create satisfaction or have a sustained relationship. Sustained relationships have multiple benefits, one being that the cost or effort comes down. The marginal cost to sustain a long-term relationship comes down. So, cost-effectiveness is a major outcome of customer relationship management.

*   **Loyalty and Forgiveness:** We also gain a little bit of loyalty. In a husband-wife relationship, with loyalty, you know that even if you are away from home or haven't given enough time for some time, your partner will try to help, support, or sometimes, up to a certain point, forgive you. You have leverage in doing something not exactly good for the partner. In the context of services, even if you have a service failure, if the customer relationship is strong, the customer will forgive your failures. Chances of service failure causing dissatisfaction will be lower. Customers won't switch for minute, petty reasons.

*   **Better Outcomes and Satisfaction:** Because I've been in a relationship with my wife for quite some time, I can get better outcomes from her very easily. She understands me very well. She knows exactly what she will cook or the kind of discussions we will have so that my intellectual or food-related taste simulation can happen. She knows me in and out. So, she knows exactly what has to be done to make me happy. The chances of a mutually beneficial outcome are much higher. This is also the case for CRM. The customer knows what the service provider can provide because the service provider knows what the customer is seeking. More knowledge means more satisfaction.

These are all reasons why CRM is important.

## The Economics of CRM: Measuring Value

The next question is: CRM is important, fair enough. How will I measure that? How will I monetize or measure whether keeping a relationship with person X versus person Y is more profitable or better? This is where the economics of CRM come in, and that is why we will be doing customer lifetime value calculations.

If you look at the evolution:
*   1960s: Product-centric marketing
*   Later: Segment-centric marketing
*   1990s onwards: Customer-centric marketing

As market share goes up, profitability also goes up. Focus has often been on market share, etc.

### Car Purchase Analogy: Value Beyond the Product

Think about a situation when you buy a car. Let's say you buy an Alto for around 3 lakh rupees. This 3 lakh rupees goes to Maruti. But is that the only thing you need? Are all your travel needs fulfilled? No.

*   **Driver:** Somebody has to drive the car. You might need a driver, or learn driving. Maruti has driving training services and certified drivers available.
*   **Fuel:** You need fuel. Automobile companies have associations with fuel companies (HPCL, IOCL, Reliance, etc.). When you buy the car, they might get a share if you use a specific fuel.
*   **Service & Maintenance:** If the car breaks down, you need service. Maruti has service centers. Let's say maintenance is 20-30,000 rupees per year. If you keep the car for 6 years, that's around 1.8 lakh rupees for service on a 3 lakh rupees car. This cost is incurred over time and captured by Maruti's service centers.
*   **Lubricants:** For oil changes, etc., they tie up with lubricant companies. Let's say 1000 rupees per year, totaling 5000 over 5 years. They take a share of that.
*   **Insurance:** You buy auto insurance. Over 5 years, you pay premiums, and the insurance companies (often tied up with the manufacturer) take a cut.
*   **Finance:** If you take a car loan, they get a certain amount from the bank because they are providing a customer lead.

All these things add up. The total cost for owning a car might come up to 7.2 lakhs for a car that initially cost 2.5 lakhs (in this specific example data). The remaining 5 lakhs comes *from services only*, provided over a longer period, over multiple moments of truth. This can only be obtained *if you have customer relationship management*. Strong CRM is the only way to capture that extra 5 lakhs.

In this analogy breakdown:
*   Customer expenses (fuel, service, spares, etc.): 35% + 14% = 49%
*   Financial (loan): 7%
*   Product: Only 34%

Most of the money is coming from outside the product itself. Frankly speaking, there is no "product company." You cannot be just a product company; you have to be a service company and have customer relationship management through multiple touch points.

## Customer Lifetime Value (CLV) Calculation

CLV is calculated based on the lifetime of the customer relationship. Certain variables are important:

1.  **Lifetime:** How long the customer stays.
2.  **Average Revenue per Year:** How much they spend annually.
3.  **Referrals Generated:** Do they bring in new customers?
4.  **Additional Purchases:** Do they buy other products/services?

These are the major sources.

The profitability journey often looks like this:
*   **Initial Year:** Always a loss (acquisition cost).
*   **Slowly goes up:**
    *   Revenue Growth (more purchases).
    *   Cost Savings (knowing the customer reduces service cost).
    *   Referrals.
    *   Price Premium (loyal customers might pay more).

This is the general sequence of making money in a customer lifetime value calculation.

### Example 1: B2B Case (Buford Electronics)

Let's calculate CLV with a small example for B2B. Buford Electronics in the USA is deciding whether to focus on small companies or large companies.

**Initial Data:**

| Feature                     | Small Companies | Large Companies |
| :-------------------------- | :-------------- | :-------------- |
| Average Revenue (per year)  | < $3,000        | $25,000 - $100,000 |
| Conversion Ratio            | 15%             | 5%              |
| Cost per Sales Visit        | $20             | $100            |
| Number of Sales Visits      | 2               | 9               |
| Turnover (Year 1)           | $1,200          | $44,000         |
| Margin (Year 1)             | $360            | $8,500          |
| Number of Prospects Approached | 40,000        | 8,000           |

**Initial Acquisition Analysis:**

*   **Sales Expense per Prospect:** Cost per Sales Visit * Number of Sales Visits
    *   Small: $20 * 2 = $40
    *   Large: $100 * 9 = $900
*   **Acquisition Cost per Customer (Net):** (Sales Expense per Prospect / Conversion Ratio) * 100
    *   Small: ($40 / 15%) * 100 = $267 (approx)
    *   Large: ($900 / 5%) * 100 = $18,000
*   **First Year Net Contribution:** Margin (Year 1) - Acquisition Cost per Customer
    *   Small: $360 - $267 = $93 (Profit)
    *   Large: $8,500 - $18,000 = -$9,500 (Loss)

Based on the first year alone, it looks like focusing on small companies is profitable ($93 per customer, or $93 * 6000 customers = $560,000 total segment profit), while large companies result in a significant loss (-$9500 per customer, or -$9500 * 400 customers = -$3.8 million total segment loss).

My question is: Should I *only* focus on small companies? Should I *not* focus on large companies? To answer that, we need to do the customer lifetime value calculation over several years.

**CLV Calculation Setup (Small Companies):**

Let's take a lifetime of 10 years (1998-2007).

| Year | Sales ($) | Margin % | Margin ($) | Marketing Expense ($) | Retention Rate (%) |
| :--- | :-------- | :------- | :--------- | :-------------------- | :----------------- |
| 1998 | 1200      | 30%      | 360        | 267 (Acquisition)     | 75%                |
| 1999 | 1500      | 32%      | 480        | 75 (Retention)        | 80%                |
| 2000 | 1800      | 34%      | 612        | 70                    | 82%                |
| 2001 | 2000      | 35%      | 700        | 65                    | 84%                |
| 2002 | 2200      | 37%      | 814        | 60                    | 85%                |
| 2003 | 2300      | 38%      | 874        | 58                    | 86%                |
| 2004 | 2400      | 39%      | 936        | 56                    | 87%                |
| 2005 | 2500      | 40%      | 1000       | 55                    | 87%                |
| 2006 | 2500      | 40%      | 1000       | 55                    | 87%                |
| 2007 | 2500      | 40%      | 1000       | 55                    | 87%                |

*(Note: Sales, Margin %, Marketing Expense, and Retention Rate values over the years come from data/forecasting assumptions.)*

**CLV Calculation Steps (Small Companies):**

Let's add more columns for calculation. Assume a discount rate of 10%.

| Year | Margin ($) | Marketing Expense ($) | Customer Value Contribution ($) (Margin - Expense) | Discounting Factor (1 / (1+r)^t) | Number of Accounts (Starting with 6000 in 1998) | Total Contribution ($) (Contrib. * # Accounts) |
| :--- | :--------- | :-------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| 1998 | 360        | 267                   | 93                                 | 1 / (1.1)^0 = 1                  | 6000                                            | 93 * 6000 = 558,000                            |
| 1999 | 480        | 75                    | 405                                | 1 / (1.1)^1 = 0.909              | 6000 * 0.75 = 4500                              | 405 * 4500 = 1,822,500                         |
| 2000 | 612        | 70                    | 542                                | 1 / (1.1)^2 = 0.826              | 4500 * 0.80 = 3600                              | 542 * 3600 = 1,951,200                         |
| ...  | ...        | ...                   | ...                                | ...                              | ...                                             | ...                                            |
| 2007 | 1000       | 55                    | 945                                | 1 / (1.1)^9 = 0.424              | ... (Number of accounts decreases over time)    | ...                                            |

*Customer Value Contribution = Margin ($) - Marketing Expense ($)*
*Discounting Factor = 1 / (1 + Discount Rate)^Year Index (starting from 0)*
*Number of Accounts = Initial Customers * Retention Rate (Year 1) * Retention Rate (Year 2) * ... * Retention Rate (Current Year - 1)*
*Total Contribution = Customer Value Contribution * Number of Accounts*

If we sum the "Total Contribution ($)" column for all 10 years, we get the **Total Segment Contribution**. For small companies, this was calculated as approximately $13,184,000.

**Average Customer Value (Small Companies):** Total Segment Contribution / Initial Number of Customers
$13,184,000 / 6000 = $2197 (approx)

**Comparing Small vs. Large Companies CLV:**

The same calculation is done for large companies using their specific data assumptions (higher initial turnover/margin, higher acquisition cost, potentially different margin growth, expense reduction, and retention rates).

For large companies, the average customer value was calculated as approximately $4014.

**Comparison Summary:**

*   **Average Customer Lifetime Value:** Large Companies ($4014) are significantly more valuable per customer than Small Companies ($2197).
*   **Total Segment Contribution (Initial Batch):** Small Companies ($13.18 million approx) have a much larger total segment contribution than Large Companies ($4014 * 400 initial customers = $1,605,600 approx).

**Strategic Implication:**

You have to take a call. Large customers have a bigger average CLV, but the total contribution from that segment (based on the number of prospects approached) is smaller than the total contribution from the small customer segment.

You cannot *only* focus on large customers; you have to mix and match. However, you *should* prefer large customers *provided* you can withstand the significant loss in the first year ($9500 per customer). If you do not have deep enough pockets to absorb that initial loss, it might be better to go for small customers initially, even though their individual CLV is lower.

That is the customer lifetime value for the B2B case.

### Example 2: B2C Case (Resort)

Let's consider a B2C example, like a resort.

**Initial Data & Assumptions:**

*   Acquisition Cost (Year 1): $2000 per customer
*   Initial Revenue (Room Stay - Year 1): $12,000 per customer
*   Room Revenue Growth Rate: 10% per year
*   Initial Cost (Room Stay - Year 1): $9600 per customer
*   Room Cost Growth Rate: 8% per year
*   Discounting Factor: 15% per year

**Calculation Steps:**

1.  **Revenue (Room Stay) per Year:**
    *   Year 1: 12000
    *   Year 2: 12000 * 1.10 = 13200
    *   Year 3: 13200 * 1.10 = 14520
    *   ...and so on.

2.  **Cost (Room Stay) per Year:**
    *   Year 1: 9600
    *   Year 2: 9600 * 1.08 = 10368
    *   Year 3: 10368 * 1.08 = 11197.44
    *   ...and so on.

3.  **Profit from Room Stay per Year:** Revenue - Cost
    *   Year 1: 12000 - 9600 = 2400
    *   Year 2: 13200 - 10368 = 2832
    *   ...and so on.

4.  **Additional Revenue/Profit Sources:** (These values/rates come from CRM data analysis)
    *   **Profit from Increased Purchase of Room Services (In-room orders, etc.):**
        *   Year 1: $0
        *   Year 2: $800 (let's say)
        *   Year 3 onwards: Increases at a rate of 1.2 (compounded)
    *   **Profit from Other Services (Beverage, Spa, etc.):**
        *   Year 1: $0
        *   Year 2: $500 (let's say)
        *   Year 3 onwards: Increases at a rate of 1.2 (compounded)
    *   **Profit from Reduced Overhead:** (Due to knowing the customer, fixing rooms, etc.)
        *   Calculated as 10% of the Room Stay Revenue for that year. This is a *reduction* in cost, hence a *profit*.
    *   **Profit from Referrals:**
        *   Year 1: $0 (Acquisition year)
        *   Year 2: $1980 (let's say)
        *   Year 3 onwards: Increases at a rate of 1.1 (compounded) - chain effect.

5.  **Total Profit per Year:** (Profit from Room Stay) + (Profit from Room Services) + (Profit from Other Services) + (Profit from Reduced Overhead) + (Profit from Referrals) - (Acquisition Cost * only in Year 1)

| Year | Acquisition Cost | Profit from Room Stay | In-room Services | Other Services | Reduced Overhead | Referrals | Total Profit (before discounting) |
| :--- | :--------------- | :-------------------- | :--------------- | :------------- | :--------------- | :-------- | :-------------------------------- |
| 1    | 2000             | 2400                  | 0                | 0              | (10% of 12000) 1200 | 0         | 2400 + 1200 - 2000 = 1600         |
| 2    | 0                | 2832                  | 800              | 500            | (10% of 13200) 1320 | 1980      | 2832 + 800 + 500 + 1320 + 1980 = 7432 |
| 3    | 0                | ...                   | 800 * 1.2 = 960  | 500 * 1.2 = 600 | (10% of 14520) 1452 | 1980 * 1.1 = 2178 | ...                           |
| ...  | ...              | ...                   | ...              | ...            | ...              | ...       | ...                               |

*Note: The calculated Total Profit column in the transcript example shows a loss in the first year (-$400), implying a slight difference in the assumed initial numbers or categories compared to my simplified table above, but the principle of summation holds.*

6.  **Discounting Factor (for 15% rate):** 1 / (1 + 0.15)^Year Index (starting from 0)
    *   Year 1 (Index 0): 1 / (1.15)^0 = 1
    *   Year 2 (Index 1): 1 / (1.15)^1 = 0.8696
    *   Year 3 (Index 2): 1 / (1.15)^2 = 0.7561
    *   Year 4 (Index 3): 0.6575
    *   Year 5 (Index 4): 0.5718
    *   Year 6 (Index 5): 0.4972

7.  **Net Present Value (NPV) per Year:** Total Profit (before discounting) * Discounting Factor

| Year | Total Profit | Discounting Factor | NPV ($) |
| :--- | :----------- | :----------------- | :------ |
| 1    | -400         | 1                  | -400    |
| 2    | 7432         | 0.8696             | 6463.6  |
| 3    | ...          | 0.7561             | ...     |
| 4    | ...          | 0.6575             | ...     |
| 5    | ...          | 0.5718             | ...     |
| 6    | ...          | 0.4972             | ...     |

8.  **Total CLV:** Sum of the NPV ($) column for all years considered (e.g., 5 years as per the example).

In the example shown, the total CLV for a single customer over 5 years was calculated as $24,420.

**Conclusion for B2C Example:**

If you acquire this customer at a cost of $2000 today, the net money you will gain over 5 years is over $24,000. This is a significant return. This kind of money can be made when you keep the relationship with the customer properly.

## Conclusion

Customer relationship management is a very important factor for a business because it significantly impacts long-term profitability, as quantified by Customer Lifetime Value. CLV helps understand the true worth of a customer relationship beyond the initial transaction.

I will see you in the next video. Thank you very much.

#### Comparison and Decision

The CLV without corporate branding (Net Present Value per customer) is $345.
The CLV with corporate branding (Net Present Value per customer) is $401.

The additional money generated per customer by implementing corporate branding is $401 - $345 = $56.

So, the increase in total profit across all customers is $56 * 115,000 (number of unique guests) = $6,440,000.

If the cost of doing the branding (the $1M additional marketing investment) is lower than this total increased profit ($6.44M), then Rosewood should go for corporate branding.

Based on the positive value ($56 per customer CLV increase), the decision was made to go for corporate branding.

# Marketing Analytics: Customer Churn and Customer Lifetime Value (Contd.) - Survival Analysis

Hello, everybody, welcome to the marketing analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur. In this particular class, which is in week 9, the last session, we will discuss about **survival analysis** in the case of Customer Churn.

## What is Survival Analysis?

Survival analysis is a statistical method for analyzing the expected duration of time until one or more events happen, such as death in biological organisms or failure in mechanical systems. In marketing, we apply it to understand how long customers 'survive' (remain customers) before 'dying' (churning).

You will see that this is a **survival probability curve**. It is actually talking about that for every time period, if you are alive today, what is the probability that you will be alive tomorrow? What is the probability that you will be alive day after tomorrow and so on – two days later, three days later, four days later?

So, how much is the probability that over a certain period of time you will be alive? And this is something which is important to know. Because certain times, it is in the context of, let us say, customer lifetime calculation. This part matters for calculating how much customer lifetime there is.

The survival probability curve shows the probability of surviving beyond a certain time *t*. For example, you might see that initially 80 percent of customers survive beyond seven years, and then at a certain point (like the green point in the example curve), 50 percent of customers are surviving after eight years. The end of the curve basically gives the expected survival time.

If I can give you information like this:

$$ P(Lifetime > t) $$

that is the probability that your lifetime will be greater than *t*. That is what this particular graph represents. If I am giving this probability, then the summation (or integration in the case of a continuous distribution) of *t* multiplied by this probability is basically your **expected lifetime**.

$$ Expected\ Lifetime = \sum t \cdot P(Lifetime > t) $$ (for discrete time)

or

$$ Expected\ Lifetime = \int t \cdot P(Lifetime > t)\ dt $$ (for continuous time)

If I can by chance get this particular probability, if I can find out what is the expected lifetime, then that is something that I can use for customer lifetime value calculation. Because whether I can do the calculation for 5 years or 6 years or 10 years, what is the value that I will consider sometimes comes from this kind of analysis and how much you are concentrating on expected lifetime.

There are various other cases where these particular things apply. For example, it can also be applied to understand how much will be the time taken before you die if you have a specific disease like COVID-19. In certain cases, survival time is much lower; in others, it is much higher.

Think about the probability of living up to 60, 70, or 80 years in general. But given that you have a specific disease, what is the probability that you will live, and how does that change over time?

Let us say there are two curves for survival probability vs. time for two groups, Group 1 and Group 2. As time increases, as you get older, your probability of living comes down, obviously. But for Group 1 (e.g., cancer patients), the probability of living might be much lower than for Group 2 (people who are not cancer patients).

Here, the concept of **hazard rate** (or **hazard ratio**) is important. The hazard rate represents the instantaneous rate of an event occurring at time *t*, given that it has not occurred before time *t*. It's essentially the risk of 'death' (churn) at a specific point in time. If one group has a higher hazard rate, their survival probability drops more steeply. For example, once you get aged, the impact of cancer on your probability of living might be much, much higher, which increases the hazard rate for that group compared to a healthy group.

In our case, it's not cancer; we are talking about customers. Given some service failure, older customers or loyal customers will have a higher chance to stay back, while non-loyal customers will have higher chances of going away. If I can find out which aspects impact a customer's decision of staying or not staying in a particular company, then that can be analyzed using survival analysis techniques.

## Key Issues and Applications

What are the key issues survival analysis addresses in a customer context?

*   How the likelihood of customer churn changes over time.
*   Determine potential intervention points (when should we act to prevent churn?).

Questions it can answer:

*   How many years/months on average do customers stay? (Expected lifetime)
*   If I do not do anything, how many customers will leave my service by a certain time?
*   How long do male customers stay compared to female customers? (Comparing survival between groups)
*   Is there any difference between nationalities, or any other customer group?
*   Is our understanding of the customer lifecycle accurate with reality?

**Survival regression** allows us to apply a model to survival analysis to predict when an event (churn) is likely to occur based on various factors. We can model the relationship between customer churn time and other customer characteristics.

This allows us to answer questions like:

*   What is the probability that this customer, who is a female non-senior citizen with dependents, will stay for two years?
*   What are the significant factors that drive churn?

There are lots of applications:

*   **Telecommunication:** Huge application in churn prediction.
*   **Insurance/Mortgages:** Predicting policy cancellations or mortgage defaults.
*   **Mail order/Retail:** Predicting customer inactivity or churn.
*   **Manufacturing:** Analyzing the lifetime of machine components.
*   **Public Sector:** Time intervals to critical events.
*   **Retail:** Time until a food customer starts purchasing non-food items, and so on.

## Estimation Techniques

We have two main estimation techniques:

1.  **Kaplan-Meier Estimator:** This is a non-parametric method. It estimates the survival function ($S(t)$), which is the probability of surviving past time *t*. The basic idea is that the probability of surviving up to time *t* is the product of the probabilities of surviving each smaller time interval up to *t*.

    The formula for the Kaplan-Meier estimator is:

    $$ S(t_j) = S(t_{j-1}) \times \left(1 - \frac{d_j}{n_j}\right) $$

    Where:
    *   $S(t_j)$ is the estimated survival probability at time $t_j$.
    *   $S(t_{j-1})$ is the estimated survival probability at the previous time point $t_{j-1}$.
    *   $d_j$ is the number of events (deaths/churns) at time $t_j$.
    *   $n_j$ is the number of individuals at risk (still 'alive'/customers) just before time $t_j$.

    This formula essentially says that the probability of remaining alive up to time *j* is the probability of remaining alive up to time *j-1* multiplied by the probability of remaining alive *at* time *j* (given you were alive at *j-1*). The probability of remaining alive at time *j* is $1 - P(\text{death at } j) = 1 - \frac{d_j}{n_j}$.

    The **hazard rate** ($h(t)$) can be derived from the survival function. It's the rate of change of the log of the survival rate:

    $$ h(t) = - \frac{d}{dt} \log(S(t)) $$

    Why log? Because the survival probability is often a very small number (e.g., 0.00000 something). Taking the log makes it more handleable (e.g., log(10^-6) = -6).

    Using Kaplan-Meier, we can calculate and plot survival curves for different groups and show whether one group has a higher hazard rate (steeper drop in survival) than another group. If the curves are significantly different (tested using methods like the log-rank test), we can say the groups have different survival experiences.

2.  **Cox Proportional Hazard Model:** This is a semi-parametric regression method that allows us to examine the relationship between survival time and other covariates (predictor variables). It models the hazard rate as:

    $$ h(t | x_1, x_2, \dots) = h_0(t) \times \exp(\beta_1 x_1 + \beta_2 x_2 + \dots) $$

    Where:
    *   $h(t | \dots)$ is the hazard rate at time *t* given the covariates $x_1, x_2, \dots$.
    *   $h_0(t)$ is the baseline hazard function (the hazard rate when all covariates are 0).
    *   $\beta_i$ are the coefficients for the covariates $x_i$.
    *   $x_i$ are the covariate values (e.g., age, gender, income group).

    This model assumes that the hazard ratio between any two individuals is constant over time (the 'proportional hazards' assumption). The $\exp(\beta_i)$ term gives the **hazard ratio** for the covariate $x_i$.

    *   If $\exp(\beta_i) > 1$, the covariate *increases* the hazard rate (increases the risk of the event, e.g., higher churn risk).
    *   If $\exp(\beta_i) < 1$, the covariate *decreases* the hazard rate (decreases the risk of the event, e.g., lower churn risk, higher survival probability).

    Instead of just comparing two groups (like in Kaplan-Meier for a single variable), the Cox model lets you include multiple variables simultaneously (like age, gender, and income together) and see how each one impacts the hazard rate, controlling for the others. For instance, it might show that people from a high-income group have a much lower hazard rate (higher survival probability) than a lower-income group, after accounting for age and gender. The model tells you how much lower (e.g., hazard ratio of 0.5 means the hazard rate is halved).

You can read more about survival analysis from various online resources.

## R Demonstration

Let's quickly show a study using R. We will use the `surv.r` file.

First, install and load the necessary libraries:

```R
# Install if you don't have them
# install.packages("survival")
# install.packages("survminer")
# install.packages("dplyr")

library(survival)
library(survminer)
library(dplyr)
```

We will use the `ovarian` dataset, which is already built-in and relates to ovarian cancer survival.

```R
# Load the data (it's already available)
data(ovarian)

# Glimpse of the data structure
str(ovarian)
```

The data has 26 observations of six variables: `futime` (follow-up time), `fustat` (event status: 1=dead, 0=alive), `age`, `resid.ds` (residual disease: 1=no, 2=yes), `rx` (treatment group: 1, 2), and `ecog.ps` (ECOG performance status: 1=good, 2=bad).

Let's prepare the data by converting relevant columns to factors with descriptive labels:

```R
# Convert rx and resid.ds to factors with labels
ovarian$rx <- factor(ovarian$rx, levels = c(1, 2), labels = c("A", "B"))
ovarian$resid.ds <- factor(ovarian$resid.ds, levels = c(1, 2), labels = c("no", "yes"))
ovarian$ecog.ps <- factor(ovarian$ecog.ps, levels = c(1, 2), labels = c("good", "bad"))
ovarian$fustat <- factor(ovarian$fustat, levels = c(0, 1), labels = c("alive", "dead")) # Also event status

# Plot age distribution
hist(ovarian$age)
```

The age distribution seems possibly bimodal.

Let's create a new categorical variable `age_group`:

```R
# Create age groups
ovarian <- mutate(ovarian, age_group = ifelse(age >= 50, "old", "young"))
ovarian$age_group <- factor(ovarian$age_group)
```

### Kaplan-Meier Estimation

First, create a `Surv` object which encapsulates the survival time and event status.

```R
# Create a survival object
surv_object <- Surv(time = ovarian$futime, event = ovarian$fustat == "dead") # Event is "dead"

# Look at the survival object (it's numeric with status indication)
print(surv_object)
```

The output shows time values, with a '+' indicating censoring (the event did not occur within the observation period).

Now, let's fit the Kaplan-Meier model based on the treatment group (`rx`) and summarize it.

```R
# Fit Kaplan-Meier model by treatment group (rx)
fit1 <- survfit(surv_object ~ rx, data = ovarian)

# Summarize the fit
summary(fit1)
```

The summary shows the number of individuals at risk (`n.risk`), number of events (`n.event`), survival probability (`Surv`), and standard error (`std.err`) and confidence interval (`lower 95% CI`, `upper 95% CI`) at various time points where events occurred. It does this separately for each group (rx=A and rx=B). For example, at `time=59` for `rx=A`, there were 13 people, 1 event occurred, leaving 12. The survival probability is 12/13 = 0.923. At `time=115`, there were 12 people, another event, leaving 11. The cumulative survival is (12/13) * (11/12) = 0.846.

Let's plot the Kaplan-Meier curves:

```R
# Plot Kaplan-Meier curves
ggsurvplot(fit1, data = ovarian, conf.int = TRUE, pval = TRUE, risk.table = TRUE,
           legend.labs = c("Group A", "Group B"), legend.title = "Treatment Group",
           title = "Kaplan-Meier Survival Curves by Treatment Group")
```

The plot shows the survival curves for Group A and Group B. Group B seems to have slightly higher survival probabilities over time. The p-value shown (around 0.30) tests if the two curves are significantly different using the log-rank test. Since the p-value (0.30) is greater than 0.05, we conclude that the survival experience between treatment groups A and B is not statistically significantly different based on this data at the 5% significance level.

Let's try with `resid.ds` (residual disease status):

```R
# Fit Kaplan-Meier model by residual disease status (resid.ds)
fit2 <- survfit(surv_object ~ resid.ds, data = ovarian)

# Plot Kaplan-Meier curves
ggsurvplot(fit2, data = ovarian, conf.int = TRUE, pval = TRUE, risk.table = TRUE,
           legend.labs = c("No Residual Disease", "Residual Disease"), legend.title = "Residual Disease",
           title = "Kaplan-Meier Survival Curves by Residual Disease")
```

The plot shows survival curves for patients with and without residual disease. The p-value is 0.057. At a 5% significance level, we'd say it's not significantly different, but it's very close to significant (at a 6% level it would be). The curve for "Residual Disease" (yes) drops much more steeply than "No Residual Disease" (no), indicating a lower survival probability and higher hazard rate for patients with residual disease.

### Cox Proportional Hazard Model

Now let's use the Cox model to assess the effect of multiple variables simultaneously.

```R
# Fit Cox Proportional Hazard model with multiple covariates
fit_coxph <- coxph(surv_object ~ rx + resid.ds + age_group + ecog.ps, data = ovarian)

# Summarize the Cox model fit
summary(fit_coxph)
```

The summary output provides coefficients, standard errors, Z-scores, and p-values for each predictor variable (or level of factor variables, taking one as the reference). It also provides the exponentiated coefficients, which are the **hazard ratios**.

Let's interpret the summary:

```
Call:
coxph(formula = surv_object ~ rx + resid.ds + age_group + ecog.ps,
    data = ovarian)

  n= 26, number of events= 12

            coef exp(coef) se(coef)     z Pr(>|z|)
rxB      -1.3643    0.2556   0.6579 -2.074   0.0381 *
residdsyes  1.4998    4.4809   0.6604  2.271   0.0231 *
age_groupold  2.1375    8.4797   0.8238  2.595   0.0095 **
ecog.psbad  0.5970    1.8166   0.7339  0.813   0.4161
---
Signif. codes:  0 '***' 0.001 '**' 0.01 '*' 0.05 '.' 0.1 ' ' 1

    exp(coef) exp(-coef) lower .95 upper .95
rxB        0.2556     3.9129    0.0704    0.9290
residdsyes 4.4809     0.2232    1.2281   16.3426
age_groupold 8.4797     0.1179    1.6680   43.1188
ecog.psbad 1.8166     0.5505    0.4305    7.6672

Concordance= 0.829  (se = 0.047 )
Likelihood ratio test= 17.86  on 4 df,   p=0.001339
Wald test         = 14.73  on 4 df,   p=0.005266
Score (logrank) test = 17.5  on 4 df,   p=0.001516
```

*   **`rxB`:** The coefficient is -1.3643, and the hazard ratio (`exp(coef)`) is 0.2556. The p-value is 0.0381, which is less than 0.05. This means treatment group B has a significantly *lower* hazard rate (about 0.26 times the hazard rate of group A, which is the reference). This implies a lower risk of death and higher survival probability compared to group A.
*   **`residdsyes`:** The coefficient is 1.4998, and the hazard ratio is 4.4809. The p-value is 0.0231, significant at the 5% level. This means having residual disease ('yes' vs 'no' reference) significantly *increases* the hazard rate by about 4.48 times.
*   **`age_groupold`:** The coefficient is 2.1375, and the hazard ratio is 8.4797. The p-value is 0.0095, highly significant. Being in the 'old' age group (vs 'young' reference) significantly *increases* the hazard rate by about 8.48 times.
*   **`ecog.psbad`:** The coefficient is 0.5970, and the hazard ratio is 1.8166. The p-value is 0.4161, which is much greater than 0.05. This means the ECOG performance status ('bad' vs 'good' reference) is *not* a significant predictor of the hazard rate in this model. We could potentially drop this variable.

Finally, we can visualize the results of the Cox model using a forest plot:

```R
# Plot Cox model results (forest plot)
ggforest(fit_coxph, data = ovarian)
```

The forest plot visually summarizes the hazard ratios and their confidence intervals for each variable. You can see that `rxB` (vs A), `residdsyes` (vs no), and `age_groupold` (vs young) have hazard ratios with confidence intervals that do not cross 1 and corresponding significant p-values. `ecog.psbad` (vs good) has a hazard ratio close to 1, a wide confidence interval crossing 1, and a non-significant p-value.

## Conclusion and Relevance to Marketing

This is how we can create and interpret survival analysis. This is highly applicable in checking how much time a customer will be staying with you or when you can do an intervention. Which kind of intervention reduces the hazard rate?

Like here, we are saying that whether `rx` (treatment), `resid.ds` (residual disease), or `age_group` is impacting the hazard rate. In a customer context, you could have seen that factors like customer segment, service plan, previous service issues, or marketing interventions impact the hazard rate (churn risk).

If customer lifetime is your value that you are checking, and every time period (e.g., after 2 months, 3 months, 5 months, 6 months), you record whether the customer is still with you (1 or 0), that constitutes your data for hazard rate calculation. Then you could find out what impacts your hazard rate.

For example, you did an intervention (e.g., a loyalty offer) in January, and the customer is still active in September. You did another intervention (e.g., a win-back campaign) in May, and another customer is also staying back in September. Which one had a significant impact on making the customer stay back? You might not know until you do this Cox analysis.

So, the Cox hazard model analysis helps find out exactly how to calculate customer lifetime based on covariates and, more importantly, how to increase customer lifetime by identifying which factors (including marketing actions) significantly impact the hazard rate. Churn management focuses on immediate risk, but survival analysis helps in understanding and potentially influencing the longer-term customer lifetime. Which actions will impact customer survival? Which won't? This can be done using the Cox proportional hazards model.

You can try this out for other customer data. You can get churn data from your customer databases and see whether survival analysis techniques are applicable there.

That is what we will cover for Week 9. In Week 10, we will start text mining, text analytics, and related topics.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 51: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics

## Introduction

Hello everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur. We are now in week 10, and over the next two weeks, we will be discussing text mining and its various applications, along with natural language processing, in the context of analytics, specifically Marketing Analytics.

## Text Mining in Marketing Analytics

Text mining is a major application area in Marketing Analytics due to the vast amount of information generated online. With the internet, digital technologies, and social media, people can express their feelings and information widely. This leads to the generation of unstructured information from various corners of the world.

### Structured vs. Unstructured Data

*   **Structured Data:** Data kept in a tabular form, well-organized, and readily available for statistical analysis.
*   **Unstructured Data:** Data that is not in a form suitable for direct data analytics. It requires transformation into a structured format before analysis.

### Unstructured Data in Marketing

A significant portion of marketing data is unstructured. Examples include:

*   **Text Data:**
    *   Customer reviews on platforms like Yelp, TripAdvisor, e-commerce websites. These are unstructured text.
    *   Blogs, travel logs, consumer-created content.
*   **Picture Data:** Influencer marketing involves posting pictures. Analyzing what is in the picture, prominent brands, and brand placement is important.
*   **Video and Audio Data:** Analyzing content in videos or audio recordings (e.g., identifying actions, products, context).

The world is filled with more unstructured data than structured data. Structured data is often created when we deliberately capture and organize data. Unstructured data, which constitutes a large percentage, often lies idle until captured and transformed.

## Applications of Text Mining in Marketing

Text mining and Natural Language Processing (NLP) are used to analyze unstructured text data in marketing contexts like customer reviews and blogs. Other classic applications include:

*   **Clickbait Detection:** Identifying posts designed solely to attract clicks for generating website traffic and ad revenue (e.g., "5 ways to kill mosquitoes without repellent"). Platforms like Facebook use text mining to detect and block these.
*   **Violence/Nudity Detection:** Analyzing content (pictures, text) for inappropriate material.
*   **Sentiment Analysis:** Determining the emotional tone (positive, negative, neutral) of text (reviews, social media posts).
*   **Spam Detection:** Identifying unsolicited or malicious text content (e.g., spam emails).

These techniques are crucial for understanding large amounts of natural language data generated by humans.

## Natural Language Processing (NLP) vs. Text Mining

*   **Text Mining:** A broad area involving mining any text, regardless of whether it was created by humans, machines, or website writers. It follows certain processes for extraction and analysis.
*   **Natural Language Processing (NLP):** A sub-field of computer science and linguistics. It involves text mining specifically by keeping in mind the linguistic characteristics of the text. NLP aims to programmatically understand human natural language (spoken or written) and analyze large amounts of such data. It considers nuances like grammar, sentence structure, sarcasm, and communication context.

The goal of NLP is to make computers understand human language at a human level, enabling analysis of large datasets that would be impossible or unreliable for humans to analyze qualitatively at scale.

## History of NLP

*   **17th Century:** Philosophers like Leibniz and Descartes proposed codes to relate different languages (theoretical stage).
*   **1930s:** Georges Artsrouni created the first bilingual dictionary using paper tape (early empirical work). Peter Troyanskii proposed a more detailed system including bilingual dictionaries and grammatical rules.
*   **1950:** Alan Turing's article "Computing Machinery and Intelligence" mentioned machine thinking capability, linking to AI concepts relevant to NLP.
*   **1980s:** First statistical machine translation systems were developed based on statistical models. This evolved into systems like Google Translate.

## Applications of NLP

NLP has numerous practical applications:

*   **Gmail's Auto Reply:** Generating automatic reply suggestions based on email content.
*   **Keyboard Next Word Prediction:** Suggesting the next word while typing (e.g., "Tata Consultancy Services").
*   **Google Translator:** Translating text from one language to another.
*   **IMDB Movie Review Analysis:** Analyzing reviews to understand genre, sentiment, etc.
*   **Article Recommendation:** Recommending articles based on the text being read (content-based filtering using text analysis).
*   **Spam Email Detection:** Identifying and filtering spam emails.
*   **Trend Analysis:** Identifying emerging topics or trends in text data.
*   **Call Center Chat/Text Transcript Analysis:** Analyzing conversations to assess agent performance, customer sentiment/satisfaction, and identify business problems.
*   **Customer Sentiment Analytics:** Understanding customer feelings about products, services, or brands.
*   **Movie Review Sentiment:** Classifying reviews as positive or negative and identifying reasons behind the sentiment.

These applications demonstrate the significant use of NLP in marketing.

## Steps in NLP/Text Mining: Pre-processing

Before analyzing text data, it needs to be pre-processed to make it usable. Key pre-processing steps include:

1.  **Clean the Data:**
    *   Ideally, start with clean ASCII text.
    *   Remove HTML tags, table mark-up, pictures, and advertisements.
    *   *Note:* Some HTML mark-ups (like `<b>` for bold, `<H1>` for heading) might carry useful structural information (e.g., title vs. paragraph) and might be preserved depending on the goal.
    *   Automate cleaning using wrapper induction.
2.  **Lemmatize or Stem:**
    *   Reduce words to their basic or root form (e.g., eating, eat, eaten -> eat; buyers, buyer, buying -> buy).
    *   This helps in aggregating similar words and understanding the core topic or action.
3.  **Remove Stopwords:**
    *   Remove commonly used words that do not contribute significant meaning (e.g., I, you, he, she, am, is, the, a, an).
    *   A stopwords dictionary is used. The decision to remove certain words (like "not") depends on the analysis goal (e.g., sentiment analysis often needs "not").
4.  **Find Named Entities:**
    *   Identify proper nouns like names of people, places, companies.
    *   These may be removed if not relevant to the analysis (e.g., a person's name in a hotel review) or kept if relevant (e.g., names in chat transcripts to identify who is talking).
5.  **Synonym Unification:**
    *   Group words with similar meanings together.
6.  **Part-of-Speech (POS) Tagging:**
    *   Identify the grammatical role of each word (noun, verb, adjective, adverb, etc.).
    *   This is crucial for sentiment analysis, where sentiment often describes a noun using an adjective (e.g., "book" (noun) is "good" (adjective)). Tagging helps link the sentiment (adjective) to the entity (noun) it describes.

### Important Considerations for Pre-processing

*   Most steps are optional and application-dependent. The choice of steps depends on the specific analytical goal and the nature of the data.
*   The steps are often language-dependent. The process (lemmatization, stopwords, POS tagging) changes significantly for non-English languages.
*   Free and open-source tools and Web APIs exist for most of these steps, often in the form of libraries in programming languages like R or Python. The goal in this course is to apply these existing tools, not to create new algorithms.

## Text Mining Process: Bag-of-Tokens

After pre-processing, text data needs to be converted into a structured format for analysis. One fundamental technique is creating a "Bag-of-Tokens".

*   **Bag-of-Tokens:** This method converts documents into sets of tokens (words).
*   Each unique word (after pre-processing like removing stopwords, punctuation, and potentially stemming/lemmatizing) becomes a token.
*   The resulting structured data often includes the frequency of each unique word in the document.
*   *Limitation:* This method loses the original order and context of words, limiting the analysis.

Despite this limitation, the Bag-of-Tokens model is one of the most widely used techniques in text mining.

## Conclusion and Next Steps

We have introduced the concepts of text mining and natural language processing, their importance in Marketing Analytics, and the necessary pre-processing steps.

In the next video, we will continue this discussion and delve into how tokenizing helps in text mining, specifically exploring concepts like Term Frequency (TF) and Inverse Document Frequency (IDF).

Thank you for being with me. I will see you in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 52: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (contd.)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee, VGSOM IIT, Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is the second session of Week 10, where we are discussing text mining, natural language processing, and specifically, tokenization.

In the previous video, we noted that **tokenization** severely limits the context. Tokenization involves converting text data into a set of tokens. Before this conversion, various preprocessing steps are performed, such as removing stop words, punctuation, and sometimes applying stemming or lemmatization. After these steps, each unique word is converted into a token. We can then analyze these tokens based on their frequency, finding which tokens are most and least frequent.

## Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF)

Tokens are often analyzed using a concept called **Term Frequency and Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF)**. This metric helps determine which token (or word) is more important within a collection of documents.

Let's consider an example: analyzing customer reviews for hotels. Suppose we have 10,000 reviews, with each row in our data representing one review. We have the serial number and the text of the review. After removing stop words, etc., what word do you think would be most frequent in these reviews?

If you analyze hotel reviews, the word "hotel" itself would likely be the most frequent. People constantly mention "this hotel," "the hotel," etc.

The question arises: is this most frequent word ("hotel") meaningful enough for our analysis? How can we differentiate words that are meaningful in creating information from those that are not? This is where the TF-IDF concept comes in.

### Understanding TF-IDF

Consider a collection of documents (e.g., review1, review2, ..., review6).

*   **Term Frequency (TF):** The more a word occurs *within a specific document*, the more important that word is *to that document*. This is the **Term Frequency**.
*   **Document Frequency (DF):** However, if a word occurs in *many different documents* across the collection (e.g., in review1, review2, review3, etc.), that particular word is generally *less important* in distinguishing one document from another. This is related to **Document Frequency**.

Think of a teacher who says "got it?" frequently while teaching history. In every single lecture (document), the word "got it" appears many times (high TF in each document). But because it appears in *all* his lectures (high DF), the word "got it" doesn't carry much specific meaning about the *topic* of any particular lecture. It's just a speech habit.

Conversely, if he uses "got it?" a lot only when teaching about *one specific historical event* and rarely otherwise, then "got it?" might be more important *in the context of that specific topic* (higher TF for that topic, lower DF across all topics).

Similarly, words that occur frequently in one document (high TF) but rarely across the *entire collection* of documents (low DF) are considered more important and specific to that document.

**TF-IDF** combines these two ideas:

*   **Term Frequency (TF):** Measures the importance of term `i` in document `j`.
*   **Inverse Document Frequency (IDF):** Measures the general importance of the term across the collection. It is *inverse* to the document frequency – the more documents a term appears in, the lower its IDF.

A high TF for a term like "apple" might mean "apple" is important *in this specific document*. But a high DF for "apple" (meaning it appears in many documents) suggests "apple" is not particularly distinctive across the collection; perhaps the whole collection is about apples.

### Calculating IDF

The formula for Inverse Document Frequency is typically:

`IDF(term) = log(Total number of documents / Number of documents containing the term)`

This is often calculated using base 2 logarithm, but the base doesn't fundamentally change the relative importance scores.

### Calculating TF-IDF

The TF-IDF score for a term `i` in document `j` is simply the product of its Term Frequency and Inverse Document Frequency:

`TF-IDF(term i, document j) = TF(term i, document j) * IDF(term i)`

This score indicates the importance of a term within a specific document relative to its frequency across the entire corpus. We can then use a cut-off score to filter out less important words.

### Example Calculation (Spreadsheet Walkthrough)

Let's walk through the example provided in the lecture:

**Documents:**
1.  I eat fish and vegetables.
2.  Fish are pets.
3.  My kitten eats fish.
4.  Kitten is a lovable pet.

**Preprocessing:**
*   Remove punctuation (periods).
*   Ideally, lemmatize words (e.g., pets -> pet), but this example keeps them separate to illustrate the difference.
*   Identify unique words (tokens) across all documents.

**Unique Word List (Tokens):**
`I, eat, fish, and, vegetables, are, pets, My, kitten, is, a, lovable, pet`

**Step 1: Calculate Term Frequency (TF)**
Count the frequency of each word *within* each document. Divide by the total number of words in that document.

*   **Document 1: "I eat fish and vegetables" (5 words)**
    *   I: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   eat: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   fish: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   and: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   vegetables: 1/5 = 0.2
*   **Document 2: "Fish are pets" (3 words)**
    *   Fish: 1/3 = 0.33
    *   are: 1/3 = 0.33
    *   pets: 1/3 = 0.33
*   **Document 3: "My kitten eats fish" (4 words)**
    *   My: 1/4 = 0.25
    *   kitten: 1/4 = 0.25
    *   eats: 1/4 = 0.25
    *   fish: 1/4 = 0.25
*   **Document 4: "Kitten is a lovable pet" (5 words)**
    *   Kitten: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   is: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   a: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   lovable: 1/5 = 0.2
    *   pet: 1/5 = 0.2

**Step 2: Calculate Inverse Document Frequency (IDF)**
For each unique word, count the number of documents it appears in. Then, calculate `log(Total Documents / Documents with word)`. Total documents = 4.

*   **I:** Appears in Doc 1 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **eat:** Appears in Doc 1 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **fish:** Appears in Doc 1, Doc 2, Doc 3 (3 documents). IDF = log(4/3)
*   **and:** Appears in Doc 1 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **vegetables:** Appears in Doc 1 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **are:** Appears in Doc 2 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **pets:** Appears in Doc 2 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **My:** Appears in Doc 3 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **kitten:** Appears in Doc 3, Doc 4 (2 documents). IDF = log(4/2) = log(2)
*   **is:** Appears in Doc 4 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **a:** Appears in Doc 4 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **lovable:** Appears in Doc 4 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)
*   **pet:** Appears in Doc 4 (1 document). IDF = log(4/1) = log(4)

*(Note: The log base 2 values shown in the video spreadsheet will give specific numerical results for these logs)*

**Step 3: Calculate TF-IDF**
Multiply the TF of a word in a specific document by the word's global IDF.

*   **Doc 1, word "I":** TF(Doc1, I) * IDF(I) = 0.2 * log(4)
*   **Doc 1, word "eat":** TF(Doc1, eat) * IDF(eat) = 0.2 * log(4)
*   **Doc 1, word "fish":** TF(Doc1, fish) * IDF(fish) = 0.2 * log(4/3)
*   ... and so on for all words in all documents.

Words like "fish," which appear in multiple documents, will have a lower IDF and thus a potentially lower TF-IDF score compared to words that appear frequently in one document but rarely elsewhere (even if the term frequency is the same). This indicates that "fish" is less distinctive to any *single* document in this small collection compared to words like "vegetables" or "pets".

**Another Example:** "Sachin is a cricketer. Roger plays tennis. Sachin meets Roger."

*   **Documents:** 3
*   **Doc 1:** "Sachin is a cricketer" (4 words - let's assume stop words 'is', 'a' are removed for TF-IDF calculation, leaving 3 terms: Sachin, cricketer)
*   **Doc 2:** "Roger plays tennis" (3 words: Roger, plays, tennis)
*   **Doc 3:** "Sachin meets Roger" (3 words: Sachin, meets, Roger)

**Unique Words (Tokens, ignoring assumed stop words):** Sachin, cricketer, Roger, plays, tennis, meets

*   **Sachin:** Appears in Doc 1, Doc 3 (2 documents)
*   **cricketer:** Appears in Doc 1 (1 document)
*   **Roger:** Appears in Doc 2, Doc 3 (2 documents)
*   **plays:** Appears in Doc 2 (1 document)
*   **tennis:** Appears in Doc 2 (1 document)
*   **meets:** Appears in Doc 3 (1 document)

**IDF Calculation (Log base 3 for simplicity):**
*   Sachin: log(3/2)
*   cricketer: log(3/1)
*   Roger: log(3/2)
*   plays: log(3/1)
*   tennis: log(3/1)
*   meets: log(3/1)

**TF Calculation (assuming equal weighting per word for simplicity in this manual example):**
*   In Doc 1: TF(Sachin)=1/2, TF(cricketer)=1/2
*   In Doc 2: TF(Roger)=1/3, TF(plays)=1/3, TF(tennis)=1/3
*   In Doc 3: TF(Sachin)=1/2, TF(meets)=1/2, TF(Roger)=1/2

**TF-IDF Calculation:**
*   Doc 1, Sachin: (1/2) * log(3/2)
*   Doc 1, cricketer: (1/2) * log(3/1)
*   Doc 2, Roger: (1/3) * log(3/2)
*   Doc 2, plays: (1/3) * log(3/1)
*   Doc 2, tennis: (1/3) * log(3/1)
*   Doc 3, Sachin: (1/2) * log(3/2)
*   Doc 3, meets: (1/2) * log(3/1)
*   Doc 3, Roger: (1/2) * log(3/2)

Comparing the TF-IDF scores: Words like "cricketer," "plays," "tennis," and "meets" which appear in only one document but are relatively frequent *within* that document will have higher TF-IDF scores than "Sachin" or "Roger" which appear in multiple documents, even if their TF in a specific document is similar. This suggests "cricketer," "plays," "tennis," and "meets" are more important keywords in distinguishing the content of these short documents.

## Word Cloud

Word clouds are a visual representation of word frequency. The size of a word in the cloud is proportional to its frequency in the text.

*   **Use Case:** Quickly understand the dominant themes or important keywords in a large body of text.
*   **Example:** A word cloud from a plot summary of a book might show prominent character names (Pierre, Natasha, Andrew), locations (Moscow), and key concepts (prince, princess).
*   **Tools:** Can be generated using software like R or Python, or online tools like wordclouds.com.

## Trending Topics

Trending topics are another form of summarization, often seen on platforms like Twitter. By analyzing keywords and their frequency across geographically tagged tweets, we can identify topics that are gaining prominence in specific regions. This can be useful for tracking the spread of information, rumors, or public opinion.

## Dependency Parsing

**Dependency Parsing** is a more advanced Natural Language Processing technique that analyzes the grammatical structure of a sentence to identify relationships between words. It determines which words depend on which other words.

*   **Purpose:** To understand the structural relationships (subject, object, verb, modifiers) within sentences, preserving some context lost in simple tokenization.
*   **Example:** "I am eating breakfast."
    *   Subject: I
    *   Verb: eating
    *   Object: breakfast
    *   Relationship: 'I' is the subject of 'eating', 'breakfast' is the object of 'eating'.

*   **How it helps:** By understanding these relationships, systems can process language more intelligently. For example, identifying the subject and object of an action allows a chatbot or a query system to correctly identify *who* is doing *what* to *whom/what*.

*   **Example Analysis:** "I want to upgrade my data package."
    *   Key Verb: 'want' (or 'want to upgrade' as a compound verb)
    *   Subject of 'want': 'I' (pronoun)
    *   Component Verb: 'upgrade' (depends on 'want')
    *   Object of 'upgrade': 'package' (noun)
    *   Modifier of 'package': 'data' (compound noun part)
    *   Modifier of 'package': 'my' (adjective/possessive, indicating ownership)

*   **Practical Application:** In a customer service bot:
    *   User: "I want to cancel my flight." -> Bot identifies 'I' as subject, 'cancel' as verb, 'flight' as object. It knows *your* flight.
    *   User: "My wife wants to upgrade her seat." -> Bot identifies 'wife' as subject, 'wants to upgrade' as verb, 'seat' as object, and notes possession ('her'). It understands someone *else's* request and will ask for clarification (e.g., "Can you provide the passenger's name?").

*   **Tools:** Libraries like `spaCy` in Python are commonly used for dependency parsing. Similar functionalities exist in R.

## Topic Modelling

**Topic Modelling** is a technique used to discover abstract "topics" that occur in a collection of documents. It helps in understanding the main themes discussed in a large dataset of text.

*   **Purpose:** Identify clusters of words that tend to appear together, suggesting they belong to a common topic.
*   **Outcome:** From a large text corpus, you can find out which parts are discussing which topics and which words are most representative of each topic.

We will discuss Topic Modelling and Dependency Parsing further in upcoming sessions.

## Conclusion

In this session, we discussed TF-IDF as a method to score the importance of words, word clouds for quick visual summarization, trending topics as a geographical or temporal form of summarization, and dependency parsing for understanding grammatical relationships within sentences. We also briefly touched upon topic modelling.

In the next video, we will have a hands-on session implementing term frequency, inverse document frequency, and creating word clouds using R. Later, we will explore spam detection, topic modelling, and dependency parsing.

Thank you for being with me. I will meet you in the next session.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 53: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

This lecture continues the discussion on Text Mining, focusing on practical implementation in R for marketing analytics contexts.

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 10, and this is the third session, where we are discussing Text Mining in the context of marketing.

We will be working with some text data using R. The data we'll use is freely available online and consists of Martin Luther King Jr.'s "I Have a Dream" speech.

## Text Mining Setup in R

### Required Libraries

To perform text mining in R, we need specific libraries. The primary library for this task is `tm` (Text Mining). Additionally, we will use:
*   `SnowballC`: For stemming (reducing words to their root form - though not explicitly used in this transcript's code, it's often used alongside `tm`).
*   `wordcloud`: For generating word clouds (demonstrated in the next video).
*   `RColorBrewer`: For color palettes, often used with word clouds.

### Installing and Loading Libraries

First, we need to ensure these libraries are installed. If they are not already installed, use the `install.packages()` function.

```r
install.packages("tm")
install.packages("SnowballC")
install.packages("wordcloud")
install.packages("RColorBrewer")
```

Once installed, load the libraries using the `library()` function.

```r
library(tm)
library(SnowballC)
library(wordcloud)
library(RColorBrewer)
```
*(Note: You might see warnings about packages being built under a different R version. As long as it's just a warning, it's usually okay to proceed.)*

### Loading the Data

The data is available from a public URL. We will set a file path variable to store this URL and then read the text line by line.

```r
# Set working directory (optional, but good practice)
# setwd("path/to/your/directory") # Replace with your actual path

# File path (URL of the text data)
filePath <- "https://www.gutenberg.org/files/24/24-0.txt"

# Read the text file line by line
text <- readLines(filePath)
```
You can inspect the loaded text to see its structure. It reads each line as a separate entry.

```r
# Inspect the first few lines of the text
# text # (This would print the entire text)
# text[1:10] # (This would print the first 10 lines)
```
As observed, the original source text may contain blank lines due to paragraph breaks.

### Saving and Reloading Data

To avoid re-downloading the data every time, especially if the internet connection is unstable, it's a good practice to save the downloaded data locally and then read from the local file.

First, convert the text vector into a data frame.

```r
text_df <- as.data.frame(text)
```
Save the data frame as a CSV file. We can set `row.names = FALSE` to prevent R from writing row numbers as an extra column.

```r
write.csv(text_df, "text_df.csv", row.names = FALSE)
```
Now, you can read the data from the local CSV file.

```r
text_one <- read.csv("text_df.csv")
```
The text content is now available in the `text_one$text` column. This is the data we will work with.

```r
# Inspect the text column
# text_one$text # (Prints the text content)
```

## Data Preprocessing and Cleaning

Before analyzing text, it's crucial to clean it. This involves several steps to standardize the text and remove irrelevant information.

### Converting to Corpus

Text analysis libraries like `tm` work efficiently with a special data structure called a **corpus**. A corpus is essentially a collection of text documents. We convert our text data frame column into a corpus. Since our data is in a single vector (column), we use `VectorSource`.

```r
docs <- Corpus(VectorSource(text_one$text))
```
Now, `docs` is a corpus object. You can inspect parts of it.

```r
# Inspect the first 10 documents (lines) in the corpus
inspect(docs[1:10])
```
The corpus metadata shows the number of documents (lines).

### Applying Transformations (`tm_map`)

The `tm` package provides the `tm_map()` function to apply transformations to the corpus. `content_transformer()` is used to create custom transformation functions if needed.

#### Removing Specific Characters

The raw text might contain characters that are not useful for analysis, like certain escape sequences or non-standard symbols. We can replace these with spaces. The `toSpace` function is a custom transformer defined for this purpose.

```r
# Define a custom function to replace patterns with spaces
toSpace <- content_transformer(function (x , pattern ) gsub(pattern, " ", x))

# Replace specific backslash characters with spaces
docs <- tm_map(docs, toSpace, "/")
docs <- tm_map(docs, toSpace, "@")
docs <- tm_map(docs, toSpace, "\\|") # Escape the pipe character
```
After applying transformations, you can inspect the corpus again to see the changes.

```r
# Inspect the corpus after removing characters
inspect(docs[1:10])
```

#### Converting to Lowercase

To treat "Word" and "word" as the same, we convert all text to lowercase.

```r
# Convert the text to lower case
docs <- tm_map(docs, content_transformer(tolower))
```
Inspect the corpus again.

```r
# Inspect the corpus after converting to lowercase
inspect(docs[1:10])
```
Now, all text should be in lowercase.

#### Removing Numbers

Numbers are often irrelevant in text analysis unless specifically required (e.g., analyzing prices or quantities). We remove them using `removeNumbers`.

```r
# Remove numbers
docs <- tm_map(docs, removeNumbers)
```
Inspect the corpus.

```r
# Inspect the corpus after removing numbers
inspect(docs[1:10])
```
While this specific speech might not have numbers, this is a standard cleaning step.

#### Removing Stopwords

Stopwords are common words like "the", "a", "is", "and" that appear very frequently but carry little meaning for distinguishing document content. `tm` provides a list of standard English stopwords.

```r
# View the default English stopwords
stopwords("english")

# Remove standard English stopwords
docs <- tm_map(docs, removeWords, stopwords("english"))
```
You might also want to remove specific words that are frequent in your dataset but irrelevant to your analysis (e.g., "hotel" in hotel reviews, or specific words that appear often but don't convey the main theme).

To remove custom words, provide a vector of words to the `removeWords` function.

```r
# Example: Removing custom words like "let", "will", "four"
# docs <- tm_map(docs, removeWords, c("let")) # To remove 'let'
# docs <- tm_map(docs, removeWords, c("will", "four")) # To remove 'will' and 'four'
```
Inspect the corpus after removing stopwords. You will see words like "the", "is", "and" are gone.

#### Removing Punctuation

Punctuation marks (`.`, `,`, `!`, `?`, etc.) should also be removed as they don't contribute to the meaning of words.

```r
# Remove punctuation
docs <- tm_map(docs, removePunctuation)
```
Inspect the corpus.

```r
# Inspect the corpus after removing punctuation
inspect(docs[1:10])
```
All punctuation should now be gone.

#### Removing Whitespace

Multiple spaces between words can occur after removing punctuation, stopwords, etc. We remove extra whitespace to ensure words are separated by single spaces.

```r
# Eliminate extra white spaces
docs <- tm_map(docs, stripWhitespace)
```
Inspect the corpus.

```r
# Inspect the corpus after removing whitespace
inspect(docs[1:10])
```
This completes the basic preprocessing steps.

## Creating a Document Term Matrix (DTM)

### What is a DTM?

A Document Term Matrix (DTM) is a matrix that represents the frequency of terms (words) in a collection of documents. It's structured with documents as rows and unique terms as columns. Each cell in the matrix indicates the frequency of a specific term in a specific document.

For large datasets, a standard matrix representation would be very large and contain mostly zeros (sparse matrix). The `tm` package handles this efficiently by storing the DTM in a sparse format, only storing the locations (document index `i`, term index `j`) and values (`v`) of non-zero entries.

### Creating DTM in R

We convert the cleaned corpus into a Document Term Matrix using the `DocumentTermMatrix()` function.

```r
# Create a Term Document Matrix (DTM)
dtm <- DocumentTermMatrix(docs)
```
You can inspect the DTM object. It shows the number of documents (rows) and terms (columns), and the sparsity (percentage of zeros).

```r
# Inspect the DTM object
inspect(dtm)
```
For this specific dataset, we have 45 documents (lines) and 163 unique terms (words) after cleaning.

### Understanding DTM Structure (Sparse Matrix)

While you *can* convert the DTM to a standard matrix, it's generally not recommended for large datasets due to memory constraints.

```r
# Convert DTM to a matrix (caution for large datasets)
m <- as.matrix(dtm)

# Inspect the matrix (will show frequencies)
# inspect(m) # This might print a large matrix
```
The sparse format (`i, j, v`) stores the document index (`i`), term index (`j`), and the frequency (`v`) for every term that appears at least once in a document.

## Analyzing Term Frequencies

Once the DTM is created, we can easily calculate term frequencies (total count of each unique word across all documents).

### Calculating Word Counts

Since the DTM has documents as rows and terms as columns, the total frequency of a word is the sum of its column in the matrix.

```r
# Calculate the frequency of each word (column sums of the matrix)
v <- colSums(m)
```

### Preparing Data for Plotting

To visualize the frequencies, we convert the frequencies vector into a data frame and sort it to easily identify the most frequent words.

```r
# Create a data frame of words and their frequencies
d <- data.frame(word = names(v), freq = v)

# Sort the data frame by frequency in descending order
d <- d[order(d$freq, decreasing = TRUE),]
```
Inspect the data frame to see the most frequent words at the top.

```r
# Inspect the frequency data frame
head(d)
```
The output will show words like "freedom", "dream", "ring", "nation", "will", etc., and their total counts.

### Visualizing Term Frequencies

We can create a bar plot to visualize the frequencies of the most common words. Let's plot the top 10 words.

```r
# Plot the top 10 most frequent words as a bar plot
barplot(d[1:10,]$freq, las = 2, names.arg = d[1:10,]$word,
        col ="lightblue", main ="Most frequent words",
        ylab = "Word frequencies")
```
The `las=2` argument rotates the x-axis labels vertically for better readability.

This bar plot visually represents the most prominent themes and words in the text, which in this case, is Martin Luther King Jr.'s speech.

## Conclusion

In this session, we covered the basic steps of text mining in R using the `tm` package:
1.  Loading text data.
2.  Preprocessing and cleaning the data (lowercase conversion, removing numbers, stopwords, punctuation, whitespace).
3.  Creating a Document Term Matrix (DTM) to represent word frequencies in a structured format.
4.  Calculating total term frequencies.
5.  Visualizing the most frequent terms using a bar plot.

This gives us a fundamental understanding of the vocabulary and prominent words in the text. In the next video, we will explore further concepts like creating word clouds and potentially delve into sentiment analysis. Thank you for being with me. I will see you in the next video.
