# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 54: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee
**Institution:** VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur
**Course:** Marketing Analytics
**Week:** 10
**Session:** 4

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 10, session 4, and today we will be discussing Text Mining, continuing from our previous session.

In the last class, we discussed text mining in the context of reading data, specifically Martin Luther King's data, and we performed some preprocessing steps. In this particular class, I will discuss a new topic: **Spam Detection**, and we will be using the **Naive Bayes algorithm** to achieve this.

## Review of Probability and Bayes Theorem

Before we start, we should discuss a little bit about **Bayes theorem**. We probably covered this in our statistics class in the early days, but I will just repeat it once more.

### What is Probability?

Probability of any event is basically the number of times that event is occurring divided by the total number of observations.
`P(Event) = Number of times Event occurs / Total number of observations`

Let's consider an example:
In a particular class, there are 8 males and 5 females.
*   Out of the 8 males, 4 wear spectacles and 4 do not.
*   Out of the 5 females, 3 wear spectacles and 2 do not.
Total number of people = 8 + 5 = 13.
Total number wearing spectacles = 4 + 3 = 7.
Total number not wearing spectacles = 4 + 2 = 6.

My question is, what is the probability that if I blindly choose someone from this class, that person will be a male? What is the probability of Male?
`P(Male) = Number of Males / Total Number of People = 8 / 13`

If I say, what is the probability if I blindly chose somebody, they will be wearing a spectacle?
`P(Spectacle) = Number of People wearing Spectacles / Total Number of People = 7 / 13`

### Conditional Probability

Now, listen to this question carefully: If I choose from men (or given that my choice is male), what is the probability that the randomly chosen person is wearing a spectacle?

This is a conditional probability question. We are looking for the probability of wearing a Spectacle **given** that the person is Male.

To answer this, if I choose from men, the total possible cases are the number of males. Out of these males, we want those who are also wearing spectacles. So, it's the number of males wearing spectacles divided by the total number of males.

In mathematical terms:
`P(Spectacle | Male) = Number of (Male AND Spectacle) / Number of (Male)`

Let's write this using probability notation. We can divide both the numerator and the denominator by the total number of people (13).
`P(Spectacle | Male) = (Number of (Male AND Spectacle) / Total) / (Number of (Male) / Total)`
`P(Spectacle | Male) = P(Male AND Spectacle) / P(Male)`

We denote "Male AND Spectacle" as `P(M and S)` or `P(M, S)`.
So, `P(S | M) = P(M and S) / P(M)`

### Bayes Theorem

Using a similar concept, Bayes Theorem relates conditional probabilities.
The basic form is:
`P(A | B) = P(A and B) / P(B)`

From this, we can rearrange to get:
`P(A and B) = P(A | B) * P(B)`

Now, the event "A and B" is the same as the event "B and A". If A and B are happening, it's the same as B and A happening.
So, `P(A and B) = P(B and A)`

Applying our rearranged formula to both sides:
`P(A | B) * P(B) = P(B | A) * P(A)`

And finally, the form of Bayes Theorem commonly used:
`P(A | B) = [P(B | A) * P(A)] / P(B)`

Remember these formulations, as we will require them for the Naive Bayes algorithm.

## Spam Detection and Naive Bayes Classification

We are doing spam detection. What exactly do you understand as spam?

The term "spam" originated from a historical context (related to a specific type of meat product, like a bad burger). Good emails/messages are often called **ham**, and undesirable ones are called **spam**.
*   **Spam:** Not useful messages.
*   **Ham:** Useful messages.

We have to find out whether a message is spam or ham. Google and other email providers now do a very good job of this. One of the basic ways they follow is the **Naive Bayes Classification**.

Naive Bayes is a **supervised learning** algorithm. This means you need pre-labeled data for training. You have past examples of messages that are already categorized as spam or ham.

Let's use an example to apply Bayes Theorem to spam detection.
Suppose you have a dataset of 100 emails:
*   20 were Spam (S)
*   80 were Ham (H)

You also observed the presence of the word "Viagra" (V), which is common in US-based spam mail.
*   Out of the 20 Spam emails, 4 contained the word "Viagra" (S and V) and 16 did not (S and not V).
*   Out of the 80 Ham emails, 1 contained the word "Viagra" (H and V) and 79 did not (H and not V).

Let's create a frequency table:

|             | Viagra (V) | Not Viagra (V') | Total |
| :---------- | :--------- | :-------------- | :---- |
| Spam (S)    | 4          | 16              | 20    |
| Ham (H)     | 1          | 79              | 80    |
| **Total**   | **5**      | **95**          | **100** |

Now, let's convert this to probabilities by dividing by the total (100) or the row/column totals where appropriate:

| P(Event)         | Viagra (V)    | Not Viagra (V') | Total |
| :--------------- | :------------ | :-------------- | :---- |
| P(S and V)       | 4/100 = 0.04  | 16/100 = 0.16   | 20/100 = 0.20 (P(S)) |
| P(H and V)       | 1/100 = 0.01  | 79/100 = 0.79   | 80/100 = 0.80 (P(H)) |
| **P(Word)**      | **5/100 = 0.05 (P(V))** | **95/100 = 0.95 (P(V'))** | **1.00** |

| P(Word | Class) | Viagra (V)  | Not Viagra (V') | Total |
| :------------- | :---------- | :-------------- | :---- |
| P(V | S)     | 4/20 = 0.20 | 16/20 = 0.80    | 1.00  |
| P(V | H)     | 1/80 = 0.0125 | 79/80 = 0.9875  | 1.00  |

Now, my question is: If a new message arrives that contains the word "Viagra" (V is observed), what is the probability that this message is Spam? We want to find `P(S | V)`.

Using Bayes Theorem:
`P(S | V) = [P(V | S) * P(S)] / P(V)`

Let's plug in the probabilities from our table:
*   `P(V | S)` (Probability of Viagra given it's Spam) = 4/20 = 0.20
*   `P(S)` (Probability of Spam) = 20/100 = 0.20
*   `P(V)` (Probability of Viagra) = 5/100 = 0.05

`P(S | V) = [0.20 * 0.20] / 0.05`
`P(S | V) = 0.04 / 0.05`
`P(S | V) = 0.8`

This means that if the word "Viagra" is present in the message, the probability of that message being spam is 0.8 (or 80%). If this value is higher than a cutoff threshold you set (e.g., 0.5), then that particular message will be classified as spam.

This is a very simplistic example of Naive Bayes, and it's called "Naive" because of the simplifying assumptions we will discuss later, and it's based on Bayes theorem.

## Strengths and Weaknesses of Naive Bayes

Based on the simple example, let's summarize the strengths and weaknesses:

**Strengths:**
*   **Simple:** The calculations are straightforward.
*   **Fast:** It's computationally efficient.
*   **Effective:** Often performs well in practice, especially for text classification.
*   **Handles noisy and missing data:** It can handle data where some information is missing or inaccurate.
*   **Requires relatively few samples for training:** It can learn effectively even with moderately sized training datasets.
*   **Works well with very large number of examples:** Scales well to large datasets.
*   **Easy to obtain the estimated probability for a prediction:** The output is a probability, which can be useful.

**Weaknesses:**
*   **Relies on an often faulty assumption of equally important and independent features:** This is the core "naive" assumption. We assume features (like words) are independent of each other, which is often not true in natural language.
*   **Not ideal for datasets with a large number of numeric features:** Naive Bayes performs better with categorical data (like the presence or absence of a word) than with continuous numeric data. This is why it's heavily used in text mining where features are often word counts or presence.
*   **Estimated probabilities are less reliable than predicted class:** While it gives probabilities, the absolute probability values might not be perfectly calibrated. It's better used for classification (Spam/Ham) rather than predicting precise probabilities, especially when dealing with complex, potentially dependent features. For example, in credit scoring (predicting probability of default), other algorithms might be preferred.

## Applying Naive Bayes with Multiple Features

We don't usually create spam detection techniques based on only one word. We use multiple words.

Let's extend our example. Suppose we consider 4 words: Viagra (V), Money (M), Groceries (G), and Unsubscribe (U).
And we have the following (hypothetical) conditional probabilities based on our training data (20 Spam, 80 Ham):

| Word          | P(Word=Yes | Spam) | P(Word=No | Spam) | P(Word=Yes | Ham) | P(Word=No | Ham) | P(Word=Yes) | P(Word=No) |
| :------------ | :------------ | :-------------- | :----------- | :------------- | :---------- | :---------- |
| Viagra (V)    | 4/20 = 0.20   | 16/20 = 0.80    | 1/80 = 0.0125| 79/80 = 0.9875 | 5/100 = 0.05| 95/100 = 0.95|
| Money (M)     | 10/20 = 0.50  | 10/20 = 0.50    | 70/80 = 0.875| 10/80 = 0.125  | 80/100 = 0.80| 20/100 = 0.20|
| Groceries (G) | 0/20 = 0.00   | 20/20 = 1.00    | 24/80 = 0.30 | 56/80 = 0.70   | 24/100 = 0.24| 76/100 = 0.76|
| Unsubscribe (U)| 12/20 = 0.60  | 8/20 = 0.40     | 23/80 = 0.2875| 57/80 = 0.7125 | 35/100 = 0.35| 65/100 = 0.65|
| **Class**     |               |                 |              |                | 20/100=P(S) | 80/100=P(H) |

*(Note: The exact probabilities from the table shown in the video screenshot were used for calculation, correcting the small discrepancies in spoken values where necessary).*

Now, suppose a new message arrives that has the words Viagra (V) and Unsubscribe (U), but *does not* have the words Money (M') and Groceries (G'). What is the probability of this message being spam? We want to find `P(S | V, U, M', G')`.

Using the Bayes formula:
`P(S | V, U, M', G') = [P(V, U, M', G' | S) * P(S)] / P(V, U, M', G')`

Here comes the **Naive** assumption: We assume the presence/absence of each word is independent *given the class (Spam or Ham)*.
So, `P(V, U, M', G' | S) = P(V | S) * P(U | S) * P(M' | S) * P(G' | S)`
And `P(V, U, M', G') = P(V) * P(U) * P(M') * P(G')` (Assuming words are independent generally, which is a stronger assumption, but simplified for the denominator calculation in basic Naive Bayes implementation).

Thus, the formula becomes:
`P(S | V, U, M', G') = [P(V | S) * P(U | S) * P(M' | S) * P(G' | S) * P(S)] / [P(V) * P(U) * P(M') * P(G')]`

Let's plug in the values from the table for V, U, M', G':
*   `P(V | S)` = 4/20 = 0.20
*   `P(U | S)` = 12/20 = 0.60
*   `P(M' | S)` (M is not occurring) = 10/20 = 0.50
*   `P(G' | S)` (G is not occurring) = 20/20 = 1.00
*   `P(S)` = 20/100 = 0.20

*   `P(V)` = 5/100 = 0.05
*   `P(U)` = 35/100 = 0.35
*   `P(M')` = 20/100 = 0.20
*   `P(G')` = 76/100 = 0.76

`P(S | V, U, M', G') = [0.20 * 0.60 * 0.50 * 1.00 * 0.20] / [0.05 * 0.35 * 0.20 * 0.76]`
`P(S | V, U, M', G') = [0.012] / [0.00266]`
`P(S | V, U, M', G') ≈ 4.51`

*(Self-correction: A probability cannot be greater than 1. There must be an error in the table values or calculation presented in the spoken text vs the image, or the denominator assumption. The standard Naive Bayes approach calculates the proportional score for each class (Spam and Ham) and then normalizes them to get probabilities. Let's re-calculate the numerator and denominator terms, focusing on the *proportional* score for Spam vs Ham.)*

Let's calculate the score for Spam and Ham for the message with words V, U, M', G':

Score for Spam ∝ `P(V | S) * P(U | S) * P(M' | S) * P(G' | S) * P(S)`
Score for Spam ∝ `(4/20) * (12/20) * (10/20) * (20/20) * (20/100)`
Score for Spam ∝ `(0.20) * (0.60) * (0.50) * (1.00) * (0.20)`
Score for Spam ∝ `0.012`

Now calculate the score for Ham:
`P(V | H)` = 1/80 = 0.0125
`P(U | H)` = 23/80 = 0.2875
`P(M' | H)` = 10/80 = 0.125
`P(G' | H)` = 56/80 = 0.70
`P(H)` = 80/100 = 0.80

Score for Ham ∝ `P(V | H) * P(U | H) * P(M' | H) * P(G' | H) * P(H)`
Score for Ham ∝ `(1/80) * (23/80) * (10/80) * (56/80) * (80/100)`
Score for Ham ∝ `(0.0125) * (0.2875) * (0.125) * (0.70) * (0.80)`
Score for Ham ∝ `(0.00359375) * (0.125) * (0.70) * (0.80)`
Score for Ham ∝ `0.0002515625`

The probability of being Spam is the Spam Score divided by the sum of Spam Score and Ham Score:
`P(Spam | V, U, M', G') = Score for Spam / (Score for Spam + Score for Ham)`
`P(Spam | V, U, M', G') = 0.012 / (0.012 + 0.0002515625)`
`P(Spam | V, U, M', G') = 0.012 / 0.0122515625`
`P(Spam | V, U, M', G') ≈ 0.9795`

This indicates a very high probability of being spam, given those words. If this probability is higher than your cutoff (e.g., 0.5), you classify it as spam.

## Addressing Limitations

### Zero Frequency Problem

What happens if a message has a word that never appeared in the training data *for a specific class*?
Look at the "Groceries (G)" row in the Spam column. `P(G=Yes | Spam) = 0/20 = 0`.
If a new message *does* contain the word "Groceries" (G=Yes), and we are calculating the spam probability, one of the terms in the numerator `P(G | S)` would be 0.
Score for Spam ∝ `P(V | S) * P(U | S) * P(M | S) * P(G | S) * P(S)`
If `P(G | S)` is 0, the entire numerator becomes 0, regardless of how high the other probabilities are.
This is a significant limitation: if *any* feature has zero probability given the class, the entire probability for that class becomes zero.

To solve this **Zero Frequency** limitation, we use **Laplace Estimator** (also known as Add-one smoothing).
Instead of using the raw counts `N(word and class) / N(class)`, we add 1 to the numerator and the number of unique words (vocabulary size) to the denominator. Or, more simply for presence/absence, add 1 to *all* counts in the table.
Using Laplace smoothing with `alpha=1`:
`P(Word=Yes | Class) = (Count(Word=Yes and Class) + 1) / (Count(Class) + 2)`
`P(Word=No | Class) = (Count(Word=No and Class) + 1) / (Count(Class) + 2)`
`P(Class) = (Count(Class) + 1) / (Total Count + Number of Classes)`

Let's recalculate `P(G=Yes | Spam)` using Laplace smoothing (alpha=1):
`P(G=Yes | Spam) = (0 + 1) / (20 + 2) = 1 / 22`
This is a small, non-zero value, preventing the total probability from becoming zero. Adding a small value (alpha) doesn't significantly change probabilities for features with many observations but prevents the zero issue for rare features. The denominator changes from `N(Class)` to `N(Class) + k*alpha` where k is the number of possible feature values (e.g., 2 for presence/absence). With alpha=1 and binary features, it's `N(Class) + 2`.

### Handling Continuous Data

As mentioned, Naive Bayes is not ideal for continuous numeric features. For example, if we used "number of words in the email" as a feature, it's a continuous variable.

To handle continuous data, we generally use **beaning** or **discretization**. This involves dividing the range of the continuous variable into bins or intervals and treating each bin as a categorical value.

Consider the example of the time of day an email arrives. In the early days of email, the time might have been important for spam detection (e.g., spam coming during US working hours). Time is continuous (0 to 24 hours).

We can divide the 24 hours into bins:
*   Bin 1: Night (e.g., 00:00 - 06:00)
*   Bin 2: Morning (e.g., 06:00 - 12:00)
*   Bin 3: Afternoon (e.g., 12:00 - 18:00)
*   Bin 4: Evening (e.g., 18:00 - 24:00)

Or more granularly, we could use 24 bins (one for each hour). Based on frequency plots (like the one shown, although not detailed), you might define bins based on where most spam occurs (e.g., high frequency between 6 PM and 6 AM might indicate a specific bin).

By doing this, we convert the continuous "time" variable into a categorical variable (e.g., "Morning", "Afternoon", "Evening", "Night"). This allows Naive Bayes to use this feature. The trade-off is that we lose some of the fine-grained variation within each bin.

## Conclusion

That's all on the theory of Naive Bayes for spam detection. In the next video, we will actually discuss how to use the Naive Bayes algorithm for spam detection using R as the programming language.

Thank you very much. I will see you in the next video.
'''

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 55: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

## Introduction

Hello everybody, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. I am Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT, Kharagpur, taking this course for you. We are in week 10, session 5. In this session, I will discuss how to use R programming to apply the Naive Bayes algorithm on a dataset for text classification.

## Dataset: SMS Spam Data

We will be using a dataset called SMS spam data. The dataset contains SMS messages and their classification as either 'spam' or 'ham' (not spam).

### Loading and Exploring the Data

First, we set the working directory and read the data.

```r
# Set working directory (replace with your path)
setwd("your_working_directory_path")

# Read the data
sms_raw <- read.csv("sms_spam.csv", stringsAsFactors = FALSE) # Assuming it's a CSV and strings should not be converted to factors immediately
```

The dataset structure typically looks like this:
*   A column indicating the `type` (spam or ham).
*   A column containing the SMS `text`.
*   Potentially a serial number column.

Example:
*   "Complementary 4-star Ibiza holiday..." - This is spam.
*   "Are you this much busy" or "Please ask mummy to call father" - These are ham.

We will analyze this data to build a model that can predict whether a new SMS message is spam or ham based on its text.

Let's look at the structure and a summary of the data.

```r
# Check the names and structure
names(sms_raw) <- c("serial", "type", "text") # Assuming column names based on description
str(sms_raw)
```

The `type` variable needs to be a factor for classification. It has two levels: 'spam' and 'ham'.

Let's check the distribution of spam and ham messages in the dataset.

```r
# Convert 'type' to factor
sms_raw$type <- factor(sms_raw$type)

# Check the distribution
table(sms_raw$type)
prop.table(table(sms_raw$type)) * 100
```

We see that approximately 86.5% are ham and 13.5% are spam. This is an imbalanced dataset, which is common in spam detection.

## Data Preparation for Text Mining

As done in previous sessions, we need to clean the text data and convert it into a suitable format for analysis, like a Document Term Matrix (DTM).

### Creating and Cleaning the Corpus

We use the `tm` library to create a corpus from the text data and perform cleaning steps.

```r
# Load the tm package
library(tm)

# Create a corpus from the text column
sms_corpus <- Corpus(VectorSource(sms_raw$text))

# Print the corpus (basic info)
print(sms_corpus)

# Inspect the first few documents
inspect(sms_corpus[1:10])

# Apply transformations (cleaning)
sms_corpus_clean <- tm_map(sms_corpus, content_transformer(tolower)) # Convert to lowercase
sms_corpus_clean <- tm_map(sms_corpus_clean, removeNumbers) # Remove numbers
sms_corpus_clean <- tm_map(sms_corpus_clean, removeWords, stopwords()) # Remove common stop words
sms_corpus_clean <- tm_map(sms_corpus_clean, removePunctuation) # Remove punctuation
sms_corpus_clean <- tm_map(sms_corpus_clean, stripWhitespace) # Remove extra whitespace

# Inspect cleaned corpus
inspect(sms_corpus_clean[1:10]) # Compare with original corpus
```

### Creating the Document Term Matrix (DTM)

Next, we create a Document Term Matrix from the cleaned corpus.

```r
# Create the DTM
sms_dtm <- DocumentTermMatrix(sms_corpus_clean)

# Check the DTM structure
sms_dtm
```

This creates a large matrix where rows represent documents (SMS messages) and columns represent terms (words), with cells containing the frequency of each term in each document.

## Splitting Data into Training and Testing Sets

It's crucial to split the data into training and testing sets *before* applying the model. We need to maintain consistency across the raw data, corpus, and DTM based on the row indices used for splitting.

```r
# Define split indices (e.g., 75% train, 25% test)
# Total observations: 5559
train_size <- 4169 # Approximately 75%
test_size <- 5559 - train_size # Remaining

# Split the raw data
sms_raw_train <- sms_raw[1:train_size, ]
sms_raw_test <- sms_raw[(train_size + 1):5559, ]

# Split the DTM (using the same indices)
sms_dtm_train <- sms_dtm[1:train_size, ]
sms_dtm_test <- sms_dtm[(train_size + 1):5559, ]

# Split the cleaned corpus (using the same indices)
sms_corpus_train <- sms_corpus_clean[1:train_size]
sms_corpus_test <- sms_corpus_clean[(train_size + 1):5559]
```

Let's check the distribution of spam and ham in the split datasets to ensure they are roughly similar to the original distribution.

```r
# Check distribution in training data
prop.table(table(sms_raw_train$type)) * 100

# Check distribution in testing data
prop.table(table(sms_raw_test$type)) * 100
```

The distributions are indeed similar (around 86.5% ham), which is good.

## Exploratory Analysis: Word Cloud

We can use word clouds to visualize the most frequent words in the training data. We can create separate word clouds for spam and ham messages to see if different words are prominent.

```r
# Load the wordcloud package
library(wordcloud)

# Create a word cloud for the entire training corpus
# Set random.order = FALSE to put most frequent words in the center
wordcloud(sms_corpus_train, min.freq = 50, random.order = FALSE) # min.freq threshold can be adjusted
```

To compare spam and ham, we need to create corpora or DTMs specifically for spam and ham messages. We can subset the *raw* data and then create separate corpora/DTMs from those subsets, or subset the existing DTM based on the `type` column from the raw data. Subsetting the DTM is generally more efficient if the DTM is already created.

Let's subset the raw data first to create separate corpora for spam and ham messages for word clouds.

```r
# Subset raw data for spam and ham
spam_raw <- sms_raw_train[sms_raw_train$type == "spam",]
ham_raw <- sms_raw_train[sms_raw_train$type == "ham",]

# Create separate corpora (and clean them similarly)
spam_corpus <- Corpus(VectorSource(spam_raw$text))
ham_corpus <- Corpus(VectorSource(ham_raw$text))

# Apply cleaning steps to the subsetted corpora (can be done with a function)
clean_corpus <- function(corpus){
  corpus <- tm_map(corpus, content_transformer(tolower))
  corpus <- tm_map(corpus, removeNumbers)
  corpus <- tm_map(corpus, removeWords, stopwords())
  corpus <- tm_map(corpus, removePunctuation)
  corpus <- tm_map(corpus, stripWhitespace)
  return(corpus)
}

spam_corpus_clean <- clean_corpus(spam_corpus)
ham_corpus_clean <- clean_corpus(ham_corpus)

# Create word clouds for spam and ham
wordcloud(spam_corpus_clean, min.freq = 5, random.order = FALSE, colors = brewer.pal(8, "Dark2"))
wordcloud(ham_corpus_clean, min.freq = 50, random.order = FALSE, colors = brewer.pal(8, "Dark2"))
```

By comparing the word clouds, we can visually identify words that appear frequently in spam messages versus ham messages. If the prominent words are different (e.g., 'call', 'free', 'now', 'mobile' in spam vs. 'just', 'will', 'can', 'do' in ham), it suggests that the word content is a good predictor for classification.

## Feature Selection: Filtering Terms

The DTM often contains many words that appear very infrequently. Including these rare words can increase noise and computational cost. We should filter the DTM to include only words that appear frequently enough to be potentially informative.

We can find frequent terms and create a dictionary of these terms.

```r
# Find terms appearing at least 'x' times
# Let's find terms appearing at least 5 times in the training DTM
sms_dict <- findFreqTerms(sms_dtm_train, lowfreq = 5)

# How many such words are there?
length(sms_dict)
```

In this case, there are 1216 words that appear at least 5 times in the training data. Using this dictionary, we create new DTMs for training and testing, containing only these frequent words.

```r
# Create DTMs using the dictionary of frequent words
sms_train_dtm <- DocumentTermMatrix(sms_corpus_train, control = list(dictionary = sms_dict))
sms_test_dtm <- DocumentTermMatrix(sms_corpus_test, control = list(dictionary = sms_dict))

# Check the dimensions of the new DTMs
sms_train_dtm
sms_test_dtm
```

## Transforming DTM for Naive Bayes

The standard Naive Bayes algorithm (specifically, the multinomial variant often used for text) typically works with presence/absence of features rather than raw counts. That is, it cares *if* a word appears in a document, not *how many times* it appears.

We need to transform the DTM from term *counts* to term *presence* (binary: 1 if word is present, 0 if absent). We'll also label these 0/1 values as "no"/"yes" which the `e1071` package's `naiveBayes` function often prefers.

```r
# Function to convert counts to binary (0/1) and label them
convert_counts <- function(x) {
  x <- ifelse(x > 0, 1, 0)
  x <- factor(x, levels = c(0, 1), labels = c("no", "yes"))
  return(x)
}

# Apply the function to the training and testing DTMs
# MARGIN = 2 applies the function to columns (terms)
sms_train <- apply(sms_train_dtm, MARGIN = 2, convert_counts)
sms_test <- apply(sms_test_dtm, MARGIN = 2, convert_counts)

# Check the structure of the converted data (now a matrix/data frame of factors)
str(sms_train)
# View a summary of the first few terms
summary(sms_train[, 1:5]) # Example for first 5 terms
```

This transforms the DTMs into matrices where each cell indicates whether a specific word (column) is present ("yes") or absent ("no") in a specific document (row).

## Applying the Naive Bayes Algorithm

Now we can train the Naive Bayes model using the `naiveBayes` function from the `e1071` package. The input variables (`x`) are the terms from the `sms_train` matrix, and the outcome variable (`y`) is the `type` column from the raw training data `sms_raw_train`.

```r
# Install and load the e1071 package if you haven't already
# install.packages("e1071")
library(e1071)

# Train the Naive Bayes model
# Formula: outcome ~ features (type ~ .) where '.' means all columns in sms_train
sms_classifier <- naiveBayes(sms_train, sms_raw_train$type)

# Check the trained model object (shows conditional probabilities)
sms_classifier
```

The model object contains tables showing the conditional probabilities P(Word = "yes" | Type = "ham") and P(Word = "yes" | Type = "spam") for each word in the dictionary. We can examine these for specific words.

Example: Look at the probabilities for the word "claim". First, find its index in the dictionary.

```r
# Find the index of the word 'claim' in the dictionary
claim_index <- grep("claim", sms_dict)
claim_index

# Look at the model's probability table for this index
# This requires understanding the structure of the model object,
# which often stores these in tables indexed by feature number.
# Assuming the model object's structure allows indexing the tables:
sms_classifier$tables[[claim_index]]
```

Looking at the output for 'claim':
*   P('claim' = "yes" | type = "ham") is very low (close to 0).
*   P('claim' = "yes" | type = "spam") is much higher (e.g., 0.15).
This large difference indicates that the word 'claim' is much more likely to appear in spam messages than in ham messages, making it a strong predictor.

## Evaluating the Model

After training, we use the model to predict the class labels (`spam` or `ham`) for the unseen testing data (`sms_test`).

```r
# Make predictions on the test data
sms_test_pred <- predict(sms_classifier, sms_test)
```

Finally, we evaluate the performance of the predictions by comparing them to the actual labels in the testing data (`sms_raw_test$type`). A confusion matrix is a standard tool for this.

```r
# Install and load the gmodels package for CrossTable
# install.packages("gmodels")
library(gmodels)

# Create a confusion matrix (CrossTable)
CrossTable(sms_test_pred, sms_raw_test$type,
           prop.chisq = FALSE, prop.t = FALSE, prop.r = FALSE,
           dnn = c('predicted', 'actual'))
```

The CrossTable output shows:
*   **True Negatives (TN):** Correctly predicted ham (e.g., 1203).
*   **True Positives (TP):** Correctly predicted spam (e.g., 151).
*   **False Positives (FP):** Incorrectly predicted spam (Type I error - saying it's spam when it's ham) (e.g., 47).
*   **False Negatives (FN):** Incorrectly predicted ham (Type II error - saying it's ham when it's spam) (e.g., 32).

From these numbers, we can calculate metrics:
*   **Overall Accuracy:** (TN + TP) / Total observations. This is the percentage of messages correctly classified. E.g., (1203 + 151) / 1390 ≈ 97%.
*   **Spam Detection Accuracy (Precision/Recall):**
    *   Precision (how many of the predicted spams were actually spam): TP / (TP + FP) = 151 / (151 + 47).
    *   Recall (how many of the actual spams were detected): TP / (TP + FN) = 151 / (151 + 32) ≈ 82%.

A high overall accuracy (97%) and a reasonably high recall for spam (82%) indicates the Naive Bayes model is performing quite well in detecting spam messages on this dataset.

## Conclusion

This session demonstrated how to apply the Naive Bayes algorithm using R for text classification, specifically for SMS spam detection. We covered the essential steps: data loading, cleaning, transformation, splitting data, feature selection (term filtering), converting DTM to binary format, training the model, and evaluating its performance using a confusion matrix.

The results show that a simple word-based approach using Naive Bayes can effectively distinguish between spam and ham messages.

That is all for this particular video. In the next week, we will use what we have learned in further advanced applications like sentiment mining and emotion mining to find newer insights. Thank you very much for being with me in this particular video, and I will see you in the next week. Thank you.
'''

'''
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 56: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (contd.)

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in Week 11, continuing our discussion on **Text Mining and Sentiment Analysis**. In the previous weeks, we covered basic text mining and sentiment mining, including some spam detection using the **Naive Bayes algorithm**. This session will focus on using Naive Bayes specifically for **sentiment mining**.

In the next session, we will explore pre-developed libraries for sentiment analysis, and later we will touch upon **Topic Modeling**.

## Using Naive Bayes for Sentiment Analysis

In this class, we will be using the dataset called `hotel_review`. This is the same dataset we used earlier in the course, but here we will focus on the text part.

### Dataset Description

The `hotel_review` dataset contains 4 columns:
1.  **Hotel Name**
2.  **Review Title**
3.  **Overall Rating**: A quantitative rating, typically on a 1 to 5 scale.
4.  **Review Content**: The actual text of the review.

Examples of review content are sentences like "This place is very nice to stay close to the railway station. The owners are the best and will help you with all your questions. We would definitely recommend this place."

In Week 1, we used the quantitative parts of this dataset (like overall rating and ratings for specific attributes like service, location, quality) to understand how different service attributes impact overall satisfaction, often using regression. We also looked at which aspects are more or less important, and where the hotel performs well or poorly.

Now, we are bringing in the **qualitative data** (the text reviews) to see what extra insights we can gain and how we can use this information in model building, particularly for predicting sentiment.

### Methodology

We will use **R programming** and the **Naive Bayes algorithm** for this task.

**Naive Bayes Recap:**

The core of the Naive Bayes algorithm relies on Bayes' theorem:

P(A | B) = [P(B | A) * P(A)] / P(B)

In the context of sentiment analysis, this translates to:

P(Sentiment | Word) = [P(Word | Sentiment) * P(Sentiment)] / P(Word)

For example, to find the probability of a review having a **positive sentiment** given that it contains the word "good":

P(Positive | "good") = [P("good" | Positive) * P(Positive)] / P("good")

We can estimate these probabilities from a training dataset:
*   P(Positive): Proportion of positive reviews in the training data.
*   P("good"): Proportion of reviews containing the word "good" in the training data.
*   P("good" | Positive): Proportion of positive reviews that contain the word "good".

Naive Bayes uses these probabilities to classify new reviews.

## R Implementation Steps

We will walk through the process in R.

1.  **Load the Data:**

    ```R
    # Set working directory (assuming script is in the data folder)
    # Session -> Set Working Directory -> To Source File Location
    
    # Read the data
    data = read.csv("hotel_review.csv")
    ```

    The data has 942 observations and 4 variables.

    ```R
    # View the data structure
    str(data)
    ```

    Output shows:
    *   `Hotel.Name`: Factor with 23 levels (23 different hotels).
    *   `Review.Title`: Factor with 923 levels (some titles are repeated).
    *   `Review.Overall.Rating`: Numeric (1 to 5 scale).
    *   `Review.Content`: Factor with 941 levels (most content is unique).

2.  **Classify Reviews as Positive or Negative:**

    We need to convert the 1-5 rating into a binary sentiment (e.g., positive/negative). A simple approach is using a **median split**.

    ```R
    # Find the median overall rating
    median(data$Review.Overall.Rating)
    ```

    The median is 4. Let's define reviews with rating >= 4 as positive (1) and < 4 as negative (0).

    ```R
    # Create a new variable for sentiment (1 for positive, 0 for negative)
    data$Review.Overall.Rating = ifelse(data$Review.Overall.Rating >= median(data$Review.Overall.Rating), 1, 0)
    
    # Check the distribution of sentiment classes
    table(data$Review.Overall.Rating)
    ```

    Output: 210 reviews classified as 0 (negative), 732 as 1 (positive).
    This is about 22% negative and 78% positive, which is somewhat biased but manageable.

    ```R
    # Convert the sentiment variable to a factor
    data$Review.Overall.Rating = as.factor(data$Review.Overall.Rating)
    
    # Check the table again (now as factors)
    table(data$Review.Overall.Rating)
    ```

3.  **Text Preprocessing:**

    We need to clean the `Review.Content` text. We will use the `tm` (text mining) library.

    ```R
    # Install and load the tm library
    # install.packages("tm") # Uncomment and run if not installed
    library(tm)
    ```

    Convert the review content into a **corpus** (a collection of text documents).

    ```R
    # Create a volatile corpus from the review content
    corpus = VCorpus(VectorSource(data$Review.Content))
    
    # Inspect the first few documents (optional)
    # inspect(corpus[1:10])
    ```

    Now, apply transformations to clean the corpus:
    *   Convert to lowercase.
    *   Remove numbers.
    *   Remove English stop words (common words like 'the', 'a', 'is' that don't carry much meaning).
    *   Remove punctuation.
    *   Trim excessive whitespace.

    ```R
    # Convert to lowercase
    corpus_clean = tm_map(corpus, content_transformer(tolower))
    
    # Remove numbers
    corpus_clean = tm_map(corpus_clean, removeNumbers)
    
    # Remove English stop words
    corpus_clean = tm_map(corpus_clean, removeWords, stopwords("english"))
    
    # Remove punctuation
    corpus_clean = tm_map(corpus_clean, removePunctuation)
    
    # Remove excessive whitespace
    corpus_clean = tm_map(corpus_clean, stripWhitespace)
    
    # Inspect the cleaned corpus (optional)
    # inspect(corpus_clean[1:10])
    ```

4.  **Create Document Term Matrix (DTM):**

    A DTM represents the text data as a matrix where rows are documents (reviews) and columns are terms (words). The cells typically contain the frequency of each term in each document.

    ```R
    # Create a Document Term Matrix
    dtm = DocumentTermMatrix(corpus_clean)
    
    # Check the DTM structure (optional)
    # dtm
    ```

    The DTM is often very large and sparse (most entries are zero).

5.  **Split Data into Training and Testing Sets:**

    It's important to split the data randomly, as review order might not be independent (earlier reviews could influence later ones).

    ```R
    # Set seed for reproducibility
    set.seed(10)
    
    # Create a random sample of indices for the training set (e.g., 660 out of 942)
    train_indices = sample(1:nrow(data), size = 660, replace = FALSE)
    
    # Create training and testing data for the original dataset
    data_train = data[train_indices, ]
    data_test = data[-train_indices, ]
    
    # Create training and testing sets for the cleaned corpus
    corpus_train = corpus_clean[train_indices]
    corpus_test = corpus_clean[-train_indices]
    
    # Create training and testing sets for the DTM
    dtm_train = dtm[train_indices, ]
    dtm_test = dtm[-train_indices, ]
    ```

    Check the class distribution in the training and testing sets to ensure they are similar.

    ```R
    # Check proportion of classes in training and testing sets
    prop.table(table(data_train$Review.Overall.Rating))
    prop.table(table(data_test$Review.Overall.Rating))
    ```

    The proportions are reasonably close (around 22-23% negative in train, 20-21% in test).

6.  **Exploratory Analysis: Word Clouds**

    Let's visualize the most frequent words in the positive and negative review subsets of the training data. This can give initial insights into discriminating words.

    ```R
    # Install and load the wordcloud library
    # install.packages("wordcloud") # Uncomment and run if not installed
    library(wordcloud)
    
    # Separate training data into high (positive) and low (negative) ratings
    high_rating_train = data_train[data_train$Review.Overall.Rating == 1, ]
    low_rating_train = data_train[data_train$Review.Overall.Rating == 0, ]
    
    # Create corpus for high and low rating reviews
    corpus_high = VCorpus(VectorSource(high_rating_train$Review.Content))
    corpus_low = VCorpus(VectorSource(low_rating_train$Review.Content))
    
    # Clean high rating corpus (applying the same cleaning steps as before)
    corpus_high = tm_map(corpus_high, content_transformer(tolower))
    corpus_high = tm_map(corpus_high, removeNumbers)
    corpus_high = tm_map(corpus_high, removeWords, stopwords("english"))
    corpus_high = tm_map(corpus_high, removePunctuation)
    corpus_high = tm_map(corpus_high, stripWhitespace)
    
    # Clean low rating corpus
    corpus_low = tm_map(corpus_low, content_transformer(tolower))
    corpus_low = tm_map(corpus_low, removeNumbers)
    corpus_low = tm_map(corpus_low, removeWords, stopwords("english"))
    corpus_low = tm_map(corpus_low, removePunctuation)
    corpus_low = tm_map(corpus_low, stripWhitespace)
    
    # Create DTMs for high and low rating corpora
    dtm_high = DocumentTermMatrix(corpus_high)
    dtm_low = DocumentTermMatrix(corpus_low)
    
    # Create word clouds
    # You might need to calculate frequencies first
    freq_high = colSums(as.matrix(dtm_high))
    freq_low = colSums(as.matrix(dtm_low))
    
    # Generate word cloud for high ratings
    wordcloud(words = names(freq_high), freq = freq_high, min.freq = 5, random.order = FALSE, colors = brewer.pal(8, "Dark2"))
    ```

    The word cloud for high ratings shows words like "hotel", "room", but also positive adjectives like "good", "great", "staff", "food", "stay", "service".

    ```R
    # Generate word cloud for low ratings
    wordcloud(words = names(freq_low), freq = freq_low, min.freq = 5, random.order = FALSE, colors = brewer.pal(8, "Dark2"))
    ```

    The word cloud for low ratings shows "hotel", "room", "rooms" prominently, similar to high ratings. However, words like "good" and "great" are less prominent or absent. This suggests that adjectives might be more informative for sentiment than nouns like "hotel" or "room".

7.  **Filtering DTM and Converting to Binary:**

    The DTM might contain many rare terms. We should filter terms that appear infrequently. We also need to convert the counts in the DTM to a binary indicator (1 if the word is present, 0 if not present) as Naive Bayes typically works better with binary features for text classification.

    ```R
    # Find terms that appear at least 5 times in the training DTM
    freq_terms = findFreqTerms(dtm_train, lowfreq = 5)
    
    # Filter the DTMs to include only these frequent terms
    dtm_train_filtered = dtm_train[, freq_terms]
    dtm_test_filtered = dtm_test[, freq_terms]
    
    # Function to convert term counts to binary (present/absent)
    convert_counts = function(x) {
        x = ifelse(x > 0, 1, 0)
    }
    
    # Apply the conversion function to the filtered DTMs
    dtm_train_binary = apply(dtm_train_filtered, MARGIN = 2, convert_counts)
    dtm_test_binary = apply(dtm_test_filtered, MARGIN = 2, convert_counts)
    
    # Convert the binary DTMs back to matrices (if they aren't already)
    dtm_train_binary = as.matrix(dtm_train_binary)
    dtm_test_binary = as.matrix(dtm_test_binary)
    
    # Check structure (optional)
    # str(dtm_train_binary)
    ```

8.  **Train Naive Bayes Model:**

    Now we can train the Naive Bayes model using the binary training DTM and the training sentiment labels.

    ```R
    # Install and load the E1071 library (contains Naive Bayes)
    # install.packages("E1071") # Uncomment and run if not installed
    library(E1071)
    
    # Train the Naive Bayes model
    # y variable is data_train$Review.Overall.Rating (factor 0 or 1)
    # x variables are the columns of dtm_train_binary
    classifier = naiveBayes(x = dtm_train_binary, y = data_train$Review.Overall.Rating)
    
    # View the classifier model (optional)
    # print(classifier)
    # You can see the probabilities P(word | class) for each word and class
    # Example: Looking at the probability for the word 'good'
    # You would look for the row corresponding to 'good' and see its probabilities for class 0 and class 1.
    # E.g., classifier$tables$good
    ```

    Examining the probabilities for a word like "good" shows a much higher probability of occurrence in class 1 (positive reviews) than in class 0 (negative reviews), indicating it's a strong predictor of positive sentiment.

9.  **Predict on Test Data:**

    Use the trained classifier to predict sentiment on the unseen test data.

    ```R
    # Predict sentiment on the test DTM
    predictions = predict(classifier, newdata = dtm_test_binary)
    ```

10. **Evaluate the Model:**

    Compare the predicted sentiment with the actual sentiment in the test set using a confusion matrix (cross-tabulation).

    ```R
    # Install and load the gmodels library (for cross-tabulation)
    # install.packages("gmodels") # Uncomment and run if not installed
    library(gmodels)
    
    # Create a cross-tabulation (confusion matrix)
    # Actual values are rows, Predicted values are columns
    CrossTable(x = data_test$Review.Overall.Rating, y = predictions, prop.chisq = FALSE)
    ```

    Output will look something like this (exact numbers may vary due to random split):

    ```
    # Sample Output Structure (numbers are illustrative)
    Total Observations in Table:  282
    
                 | predictions 
    data_test$Review.Overall.Rating |         0 |         1 | Row Total
    --------------------------------|-----------|-----------|----------
                  0               |        33 |        59 |        92
                  1               |        10 |       180 |       190
    --------------------------------|-----------|-----------|----------
         Column Total             |        43 |       239 |       282
    ```

    From the cross-tabulation:
    *   **Overall Accuracy:** (Correctly Classified / Total) = (33 + 180) / 282 = 213 / 282 ≈ 0.755 (75.5%)
    *   **Correctly predicted negatives (True Negatives):** 33
    *   **Incorrectly predicted negatives (False Negatives):** 59 (Actual negative, predicted positive)
    *   **Correctly predicted positives (True Positives):** 180
    *   **Incorrectly predicted positives (False Positives):** 10 (Actual positive, predicted negative)

    Evaluating performance per class:
    *   **Recall/Sensitivity for Class 0 (Negative):** (True Negatives / Actual Negatives) = 33 / (33 + 59) = 33 / 92 ≈ 0.359 (Only about 36% of actual negative reviews were correctly identified as negative).
    *   **Recall/Sensitivity for Class 1 (Positive):** (True Positives / Actual Positives) = 180 / (10 + 180) = 180 / 190 ≈ 0.947 (About 95% of actual positive reviews were correctly identified as positive).

    The model is quite good at identifying positive reviews but struggles more with negative reviews. This might be due to the class imbalance in the data (many more positive reviews than negative). We might need further techniques or different models to improve the classification of the minority class (negative reviews).

## Conclusion

This session demonstrated how to apply the **Naive Bayes algorithm** for **sentiment mining** on text data using R. We covered loading data, preprocessing text (cleaning, corpus creation, DTM), splitting data, building the Naive Bayes model, and evaluating its performance. We saw that while the overall accuracy might look decent, it's important to look at performance for each class, especially in unbalanced datasets. The Word Cloud exploration also hinted that adjectives play a more significant role in sentiment than common nouns.

In the next session, we will explore using specialized pre-built libraries that are designed specifically for sentiment analysis.

Thank you, and I will see you in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 57: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

**Course:** Marketing Analytics
**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee
**Institute:** VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. We are currently in Week 11, session 2, continuing our discussion on Text Mining and Sentiment Analysis. In the previous session, we explored how to perform sentiment analysis using the Naive Bayes algorithm, which is a relatively simple approach.

## Alternative Sentiment Analysis Algorithms

Beyond Naive Bayes, there are other, often more sophisticated algorithms that can be applied to the document-term matrix created from text data to predict a target variable (like sentiment). These include:

*   Random Forest
*   Support Vector Machines (SVM)
*   Decision Tree

Researchers have successfully used these algorithms in various contexts, such as analyzing hotel reviews, movie reviews, or other types of text data. They collect data, create a corpus, and often manually label the text as positive or negative. Based on this labeled data, they identify which words are associated with positive or negative reviews. This work has led to the development of pre-existing libraries or lexicons.

## Sentiment Lexicons (Libraries)

Sentiment lexicons are essentially dictionaries containing words and their associated sentiment scores or categories. Instead of defining the meaning of a word, they quantify its emotional or sentimental value based on how it has been used in large, pre-analyzed text datasets.

These lexicons can provide:

*   Binary classification (e.g., positive/negative)
*   Scores on a continuum (e.g., -5 to +5)
*   Association with specific emotions (e.g., anger, joy, sadness)

While ideally, one might create a custom lexicon for a specific domain or context, for quick analysis and decision-making in marketing analytics, using readymade libraries is often a practical approach. Marketing analytics professionals typically focus on applying existing tools and libraries to derive insights, rather than building new ones from scratch.

## Exploring Sentiment Lexicons using R (`tidytext`)

We will explore how to use these lexicons in R, specifically using the `tidytext` package. This demonstration will follow the concepts presented in the book "Text Mining with R" by Julia Silge and David Robinson, available online as a free web version.

The general methodology using `tidytext` and lexicons is as follows:

1.  **Load Text Data:** Start with your raw text data.
2.  **Tokenize:** Break the text into individual words or tokens using `unnest_tokens`. This converts the 'untidy' text data into a 'tidy' format where each word is in its own row.
3.  **Join with Lexicon:** Join the tidy text data with a chosen sentiment lexicon based on the 'word' column.
4.  **Summarize:** Aggregate the sentiment scores or categories to the document level or specific sections of the text.
5.  **Visualize:** Use libraries like `ggplot2` to visualize the sentiment trends or distributions.

### Available Sentiment Lexicons in `tidytext`

The `tidytext` package provides access to several popular sentiment lexicons:

1.  **Afinn:** Created by Finn Årup Nielsen.
    *   Assigns words scores between -5 and +5.
    *   Negative scores indicate negative sentiment, positive scores indicate positive sentiment.
    *   You can access it using `get_sentiments("afinn")`.
    *   Example output shows words like "abandon" (score -2), "abandoned" (-2), "abhor" (-3), "abhorrent" (-3). It contains around 2477 words.

2.  **Bing:** Created by Minqing Hu and Bing Liu.
    *   Categories words in a binary fashion: positive or negative.
    *   You can access it using `get_sentiments("bing")`.
    *   Contains a larger number of words (6786) compared to Afinn, but provides less granular information (only binary).

3.  **NRC:** Created by Saif Mohammad and Peter Turney.
    *   Categories words in a binary fashion (positive/negative).
    *   Also categories words into 8 basic emotions: anger, anticipation, disgust, fear, joy, sadness, surprise, and trust.
    *   You can access it using `get_sentiments("nrc")`.
    *   Contains the largest number of words (13901) among the three.
    *   A single word can be associated with multiple sentiments/emotions (e.g., "abandoned" associated with anger, fear, sadness, and negative sentiment).

### Validation of Sentiment Lexicons

These lexicons were constructed using methods like crowdsourcing or manual labeling by experts. They were validated against various text sources such as:

*   Restaurant and movie reviews
*   Twitter data

It's important to be mindful of the context. A lexicon validated on Western English texts (like movie reviews) might not be perfectly applicable to other contexts, such as Indian English or domain-specific texts (e.g., medical reports, legal documents) without caution. Domain-specific lexicons also exist.

### Demonstrating Sentiment Analysis on Jane Austen Novels

The `tidytext` book uses Jane Austen's novels as an example corpus.

The process involves:

1.  Loading the novels using the `janeaustenr` library.
2.  Using `dplyr` and `stringr` for data manipulation.
3.  Creating a "tidy" dataset of words, preserving book, line, and chapter information using `unnest_tokens`.
4.  Joining the tidy word list with a sentiment lexicon (e.g., NRC).

```r
# Example R code snippet (from tidytext book, not directly in video transcript, but implied)
# Need to install packages first: install.packages(c("janeaustenr", "tidytext", "dplyr", "stringr", "ggplot2"))

library(janeaustenr)
library(tidytext)
library(dplyr)
library(stringr)
library(ggplot2)

# Create tidy books dataset
tidy_books <- austen_books() %>%
  group_by(book) %>%
  mutate(
    linenumber = row_number(),
    chapter = cumsum(str_detect(text, 
                                regex("^chapter [\\divxlc]", 
                                      ignore_case = TRUE)))) %>%
  ungroup() %>%
  unnest_tokens(word, text)

# Example: Find words associated with 'joy' using NRC lexicon
nrc_joy <- get_sentiments("nrc") %>% 
  filter(sentiment == "joy")

tidy_books %>%
  filter(book == "Emma") %>%
  inner_join(nrc_joy) %>%
  count(word, sort = TRUE)

# Output would show words and their counts from "Emma" that are classified as 'joy' in the NRC lexicon
# e.g., good: 359, young: 192, friend: 166, etc.
```

This analysis helps identify the most frequent words associated with a specific emotion within a text.

### Sentiment Trajectory Across a Novel

One can analyze how sentiment changes throughout a text. This involves:

1.  Breaking the text into smaller sections (e.g., blocks of 80 lines).
2.  Calculating the sentiment score for each section (e.g., using Bing lexicon: count positive words - count negative words).
3.  Plotting the sentiment score against the section number or position in the text.

```r
# Example R code snippet (from tidytext book, not directly in video transcript, but implied)
# Calculate sentiment score for sections using Bing lexicon
bing_sentiment <- tidy_books %>%
  inner_join(get_sentiments("bing")) %>%
  count(book, index = linenumber %/% 80, sentiment) %>%
  pivot_wider(names_from = sentiment, values_from = n, values_fill = 0) %>%
  mutate(sentiment = positive - negative)

# Plot sentiment trajectory
ggplot(bing_sentiment, aes(index, sentiment, fill = book)) +
  geom_col(show.legend = FALSE) +
  facet_wrap(~book, ncol = 2, scales = "free_x")
```

Plots show sentiment trends for different novels (Sense & Sensibility, Pride & Prejudice, Mansfield Park, Emma, Northanger Abbey, Persuasion). Some show significant swings between positive and negative sentiment (like Pride & Prejudice, Sense & Sensibility), while others are more consistently positive (like Emma, Persuasion).

### Comparing Lexicons

Comparing the sentiment trajectories generated by different lexicons (Afinn, Bing, NRC) on the same text reveals that while the absolute sentiment scores might differ significantly, the relative patterns (dips and peaks) are often similar. For instance, NRC might yield much higher absolute positive scores compared to Bing or Afinn. This emphasizes that the absolute sentiment score from a lexicon is less meaningful than its relative value or the overall trend it shows.

### Identifying Most Common Sentiment Words

Analyzing the most frequent positive and negative words across a corpus provides insight into the language used. Using the Bing lexicon, one can identify common negative words like "miss", "poor", "doubt", "afraid", "impossible", "sorry" and common positive words like "well", "good", "great", "better", "enough", "happy" in the Jane Austen novels. Word clouds can visually represent the frequency of these words, often separated by sentiment.

```r
# Example R code snippet (from tidytext book, not directly in video transcript, but implied)
# Get word counts with sentiment from Bing lexicon
bing_word_counts <- tidy_books %>%
  inner_join(get_sentiments("bing")) %>%
  count(word, sentiment, sort = TRUE) %>%
  ungroup()

# Filter for top words per sentiment and visualize (e.g., using a bar plot or word cloud)
# ... plotting code ...
```

### Beyond Single Words

A limitation of simple lexicon-based methods is that they typically treat words in isolation. Phrases like "not good" (negative) would be incorrectly classified if only "good" (positive) is considered. More advanced techniques are needed to handle negation and multi-word phrases.

## Application to Hotel Review Data

We can apply these lexicon-based techniques to our hotel review dataset. For each review text, we can use lexicons like NRC and Afinn to calculate:

*   An overall sentiment score (e.g., using Afinn scores or Bing positive/negative counts).
*   Scores for the 8 different emotions (using NRC counts).

This process will create new features (e.g., `overall_sentiment`, `E1` to `E8`) for each review.

These new features, derived from the qualitative text data, can then be used alongside the existing quantitative aspect ratings (Location, Food, etc.) in a predictive model (like regression) to understand how qualitative aspects (sentiment and emotions) impact the overall hotel rating given by a customer. This provides valuable insights for managers on which emotions or sentiments expressed in reviews are most strongly associated with higher or lower overall ratings.

## Practice

It is strongly recommended to practice the code examples provided in Chapter 2 of the "Text Mining with R" book (the web version provided in the course materials). By running the code yourself, you will gain practical experience in using `tidytext` and applying sentiment lexicons.

Thank you for your attention in this session. We will continue to apply these concepts to the hotel review dataset in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 58: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome back to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are currently in Week 11, Session 3, discussing Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics. In the previous session, we discussed various lexicons (libraries) that can be used to extract sentiment from text data. In this session, we will put that knowledge into practice using R and analyze a real dataset.

The dataset we will be using is the same hotel review dataset that was used in Week 1. It contains hotel reviews with a review title, overall rating, and the review content.

## Data Loading and Preparation

The first step in any data analysis is to load the dataset. We will use R for this.

1.  **Set Working Directory:** Ensure your working directory is set to the location of the source file or the data file.

    ```R
    # Set working directory to source file location
    setwd(dirname(rstudioapi::getActiveDocumentContext()$path))
    ```

2.  **Read the Dataset:** We will read the `hotel_review.csv` file.

    ```R
    # Read the dataset
    data <- read.csv("hotel_review.csv", header = TRUE)
    ```

3.  **Check Data Structure:** Let's inspect the structure of the loaded data.

    ```R
    # Check the structure of the data
    str(data)
    ```

    The structure shows columns like `review_title`, `hotel_name`, `city`, `overall_rating`, and `review_content`. We will be primarily using the `review_content` column for text analysis and `overall_rating` as a potential dependent variable later.

## Text Preprocessing

Before we can analyze the text, we need to clean it and convert it into a suitable format. The general steps are:

1.  Get the raw data.
2.  Convert it to a corpus.
3.  Clean the corpus.
4.  Tokenize the clean corpus.
5.  Find sentiments and emotions for each token (word).
6.  Perform analysis.

We will use the `tm` (Text Mining) library for corpus creation and cleaning.

1.  **Load `tm` library:**

    ```R
    # Load the tm library
    library(tm)
    ```

2.  **Create a Corpus:** Convert the `review_content` column into a corpus object.

    ```R
    # Create a corpus from the review content
    data_corpus <- Corpus(VectorSource(data$review_content))
    ```

    This creates a corpus with 942 documents (one for each review).

3.  **Inspect the Corpus (Optional):** You can inspect the corpus or individual documents.

    ```R
    # Print the corpus summary
    data_corpus

    # Inspect the first few documents (e.g., first 10)
    inspect(data_corpus[1:10])
    ```

4.  **Clean the Corpus:** Apply standard cleaning steps to the corpus.

    *   Convert to lowercase: `tm_map` applies a transformation to the corpus. We use `content_transformer(tolower)` to convert text to lowercase. This is important so that words like "Hotel", "HOTEL", and "hotel" are treated as the same word.
    *   Remove numbers: Numbers often don't carry significant sentiment.
    *   Remove stopwords: Stopwords are common words like "the", "a", "is", "in" that don't usually add much meaning to the text's sentiment. The `tm` library provides a list of standard English stopwords.
    *   Remove punctuation: Punctuation marks can interfere with tokenization and analysis.
    *   Remove whitespace: Remove extra spaces left after removing punctuation or other characters.

    ```R
    # Apply cleaning steps
    corpus_clean <- tm_map(data_corpus, content_transformer(tolower)) # Convert to lowercase
    corpus_clean <- tm_map(corpus_clean, removeNumbers)              # Remove numbers
    corpus_clean <- tm_map(corpus_clean, removeWords, stopwords("english")) # Remove stopwords
    corpus_clean <- tm_map(corpus_clean, removePunctuation)          # Remove punctuation
    corpus_clean <- tm_map(corpus_clean, stripWhitespace)            # Remove extra whitespace
    ```

5.  **Convert Clean Corpus to Data Frame:** Many other text analysis libraries work best with character vectors or data frames. We convert the cleaned corpus back to a character data frame. `content_transformer(identity)` extracts the plain text content.

    ```R
    # Convert the clean corpus back to a data frame with character strings
    corpus_clean_df <- as.data.frame(text = content_transformer(identity)(corpus_clean), stringsAsFactors = FALSE)
    ```

    Now `corpus_clean_df` is a data frame with one column, where each row contains the cleaned text of a review.

    ```R
    # Inspect the cleaned data frame
    head(corpus_clean_df$text)
    ```

## Lexicons for Sentiment and Emotion Analysis

As discussed previously, lexicons are pre-labeled lists of words. We will focus on the NRC (Emolex) lexicon, which provides labels for positive/negative sentiment and 8 basic emotions:

*   **Positive:** Joy, Anticipation, Surprise, Trust
*   **Negative:** Sadness, Disgust, Anger, Fear

The NRC lexicon is available through the `tidytext` package in R.

## Extracting Sentiments and Emotions per Document

Our goal is to get a count of words associated with each sentiment and emotion for *each* review document. This will result in a matrix or data frame where rows are documents and columns are sentiment/emotion counts.

1.  **Load Required Libraries:** We will need `tidytext`, `dplyr` (for data manipulation), and potentially `ggplot2` (though not used for plotting in this specific section, it's often loaded with tidytext).

    ```R
    # Load required libraries
    library(dplyr)
    library(tidytext)
    library(ggplot2) # Often used with tidytext
    ```

    *Note:* If you don't have `tidytext` installed, you'll need to run `install.packages("tidytext")`. When you first try to use the NRC lexicon with `get_sentiments("nrc")`, you might be prompted to install the `textdata` package and accept licensing terms (usually by typing `1` and pressing Enter).

2.  **Initialize Matrix for Results:** Create a matrix `A` to store the sentiment and emotion counts for each document. It will have as many rows as documents and 11 columns (positive, negative, and 8 emotions + overall sentiment).

    ```R
    # Initialize a matrix to store sentiment/emotion counts
    A <- matrix(0, nrow = dim(corpus_clean_df)[1], ncol = 11)
    ```

    `dim(corpus_clean_df)[1]` gives the number of rows (documents).

3.  **Loop Through Documents:** Iterate through each row (document) in the `corpus_clean_df`.

    ```R
    # Loop through each document
    for (i in 1:dim(corpus_clean_df)[1]) {

      # Get the text of the current document
      text_data <- data.frame(text = corpus_clean_df$text[i], stringsAsFactors = FALSE)

      # 1. Tokenize the text (break into words)
      tokens <- text_data %>%
        unnest_tokens(word, text)

      # 2. Inner join with NRC lexicon to find words with sentiment/emotion
      sentiments <- tokens %>%
        inner_join(get_sentiments("nrc"), by = "word")

      # 3. Count occurrences of each sentiment/emotion
      sentiments_count <- sentiments %>%
        count(sentiment)

      # 4. Spread the counts into columns
      sentiments_spread <- sentiments_count %>%
        spread(sentiment, n, fill = 0) # Use fill=0 for sentiments not present

      # 5. Handle potential missing columns (if certain sentiments/emotions aren't in any document) and calculate sentiment score
      # This is crucial to ensure all 10 sentiment/emotion columns exist for every row
      b <- sentiments_spread %>%
        mutate(
          anticipation = ifelse(exists("anticipation", where = .), anticipation, 0),
          anger = ifelse(exists("anger", where = .), anger, 0),
          disgust = ifelse(exists("disgust", where = .), disgust, 0),
          fear = ifelse(exists("fear", where = .), fear, 0),
          joy = ifelse(exists("joy", where = .), joy, 0),
          negative = ifelse(exists("negative", where = .), negative, 0),
          positive = ifelse(exists("positive", where = .), positive, 0),
          sadness = ifelse(exists("sadness", where = .), sadness, 0),
          surprise = ifelse(exists("surprise", where = .), surprise, 0),
          trust = ifelse(exists("trust", where = .), trust, 0)
        ) %>%
        mutate(
          # Calculate overall sentiment as positive - negative
          sentiment = positive - negative
        )

      # 6. Extract the 11 relevant values and store in matrix A
      # The 'spread' and 'mutate' steps create columns for every possible sentiment/emotion
      # We take the relevant columns (anticipation, anger, etc. and the calculated sentiment)
      # Ensure column order is consistent or use column names for robustness
      # Based on the mutate order, the relevant columns are anticipation, anger, disgust, fear, joy, negative, positive, sadness, surprise, trust, sentiment
      # Let's re-select in a defined order for safety
      b_ordered <- b %>% select(positive, negative, anticipation, anger, disgust, fear, joy, sadness, surprise, trust, sentiment)

      # Populate the i-th row of matrix A
      A[i, ] <- as.numeric(b_ordered) # Convert to numeric vector

      # Optional: Print loop progress
      if (i %% 100 == 0) {
        print(paste("Processing document:", i))
      }
    }
    ```

    *Explanation of `ifelse(exists("sentiment_name", where = .), sentiment_name, 0)`:* The `spread` function only creates columns for sentiments/emotions *present* in that specific document. The `exists` function checks if a column with a specific name (e.g., "anticipation") exists within the current data frame (`.`). If it exists, we take its value; otherwise, we put 0. This ensures all 10 emotion/sentiment columns are present for every document before calculating the overall `sentiment` score and storing it in matrix `A`. The 11th column is the overall `sentiment` score (positive - negative).

4.  **Combine with Original Data:** Bind the matrix `A` containing the sentiment/emotion counts back to the original data frame.

    ```R
    # Convert matrix A to a data frame with appropriate column names
    sentiment_scores_df <- as.data.frame(A)
    colnames(sentiment_scores_df) <- c("positive_count", "negative_count", "anticipation_count", "anger_count", "disgust_count", "fear_count", "joy_count", "sadness_count", "surprise_count", "trust_count", "overall_sentiment_score")

    # Combine the original data with the sentiment/emotion counts
    data_bound <- cbind(data, sentiment_scores_df)
    ```

    Now `data_bound` is the original dataset with the sentiment and emotion counts added as new columns.

## Analyzing the Impact of Sentiment and Emotions on Rating

We can now use the extracted sentiment and emotion counts as predictors for the `overall_rating` using regression analysis.

1.  **Predict Rating using Positive and Negative Counts:**

    ```R
    # Linear regression model using only positive and negative counts
    fit_pos_neg <- lm(overall_rating ~ positive_count + negative_count, data = data_bound)

    # Summarize the model
    summary(fit_pos_neg)
    ```

    The summary shows the coefficients for positive and negative word counts. We might observe that positive counts are significantly associated with higher ratings, while negative counts might have an unexpected coefficient initially, which warrants further investigation or model refinement.

2.  **Predict Rating using Emotions and Sentiments:** Let's include all the emotion counts as well.

    ```R
    # Linear regression model using all sentiment and emotion counts
    fit_all <- lm(overall_rating ~ positive_count + negative_count + anticipation_count + anger_count + disgust_count + fear_count + joy_count + sadness_count + surprise_count + trust_count, data = data_bound)

    # Summarize the model
    summary(fit_all)
    ```

    This summary shows the effect of each emotion and the general positive/negative counts on the rating. However, there might be multicollinearity issues among these predictor variables (emotions might be correlated).

## Checking for Multicollinearity (VIF)

Multicollinearity occurs when predictor variables are highly correlated with each other, which can make interpreting individual coefficients unreliable. We can check this using the Variance Inflation Factor (VIF) from the `car` library. A common threshold for VIF is 4.

1.  **Install and Load `car` library:**

    ```R
    # Install the car package if you haven't already
    # install.packages("car")

    # Load the car library
    library(car)
    ```

2.  **Calculate VIFs for the Full Model:**

    ```R
    # Calculate VIFs for the model with all predictors
    vif(fit_all)
    ```

    Examine the VIF values. If any are significantly above 4 (e.g., 7.10 for anticipation, sadness also high), we should consider removing the problematic predictors one by one, starting with the one with the highest VIF, and re-running the model.

3.  **Address Multicollinearity and Re-run Model:** Based on the VIF results, let's remove `anticipation_count` first and check VIFs again.

    ```R
    # Remove anticipation_count and re-run the model
    fit_reduced1 <- lm(overall_rating ~ positive_count + negative_count + anger_count + disgust_count + fear_count + joy_count + sadness_count + surprise_count + trust_count, data = data_bound)

    # Check VIFs for the reduced model
    vif(fit_reduced1)
    ```

    If sadness is still high, remove it as well.

    ```R
    # Remove sadness_count and re-run the model
    fit_final <- lm(overall_rating ~ positive_count + negative_count + anger_count + disgust_count + fear_count + joy_count + surprise_count + trust_count, data = data_bound)

    # Check VIFs for the final model
    vif(fit_final)
    ```

    Ensure all VIFs are now below the threshold (e.g., 4).

4.  **Summarize and Interpret the Final Model:**

    ```R
    # Summarize the final model
    summary(fit_final)
    ```

    Now the coefficients can be interpreted more reliably. The summary shows which sentiment/emotion counts are statistically significant predictors of `overall_rating` and the direction of their impact.

    *   **Positive count:** Should have a significant positive coefficient.
    *   **Negative count:** May or may not be significant, or have an unexpected sign. *Correction*: After addressing multicollinearity, the negative coefficient might become significant and negative, which makes sense.
    *   **Trust count:** Might have a significant positive coefficient.
    *   **Surprise count:** Might have a significant negative coefficient, suggesting not all surprises are welcome in hotel reviews.
    *   **Fear and Disgust counts:** Often have significant negative coefficients, indicating these emotions are strong drivers of low ratings.
    *   Other emotions (anger, joy): Check their significance and sign.

## Conclusion

By extracting sentiment and emotion counts from text reviews using lexicons like NRC and combining this text-derived data with other quantitative data (like overall rating), we can gain deeper insights into what aspects of customer experience drive their satisfaction. This analysis allows us to understand the specific emotions associated with positive and negative reviews and prioritize improvements.

This demonstrates how to use text data, process it using standard techniques, extract meaningful quantitative features (sentiment/emotion counts), and then use these features in standard quantitative models like linear regression.

In the next video, we will discuss another text mining technique called Topic Modeling.

Thank you.
