# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 59: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

## Introduction

Hello everyone, welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are currently in Week 11, and this lecture continues our discussion on Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics.

Today, we will discuss a specific research paper that I have personally worked on. This paper applies qualitative analysis, text mining techniques, and quantitative analytics together to generate actionable insights. The purpose of discussing this paper is to show how these techniques can be applied in a real-world marketing context. It also provides insights into potential research questions in the field for academics.

Data science is an area where academia and industry are rapidly converging. Research conducted in academia, particularly in this area, is often directly applicable and relevant to industry practices.

## Discussed Paper

The paper we will be focusing on is titled: **"Explaining customer ratings and recommendations by combining qualitative and quantitative user generated content"**.

You can find this paper by searching on **scholar.google.com**.

```
explaining customer ratings and recommendations by combining qualitative and quantitative user generated content
```

The paper was published in 2019 in **Decision Support Systems**, which is a highly-rated journal (ABDC A* ranking).

**Accessing the Paper:**
*   Not all institutions may have direct access to the published version.
*   A shareable version is often available on platforms like **ResearchGate** or **Academia.edu**. Searching the title on Google should lead you to these platforms where you can potentially request or download the paper.

## Understanding User Generated Content (UGC)

**User Generated Content (UGC)** refers to any content created by the users of a website or platform. This includes:

*   Pages
*   Pictures
*   Videos
*   Blog posts
*   Reviews
*   Ratings

Often, UGC is **unstructured data**. Traditionally, analyzing this required manual content analysis in marketing research. However, with marketing analytics, we can process UGC in an automated, machine-generated way to derive quantitative insights. These insights can then be used in further analysis to create actionable strategies for organizations.

## Reviews vs. Ratings

It's important to differentiate between reviews and ratings:

*   **Ratings:** These are typically quantitative (e.g., a star rating out of 5, a score out of 10).
*   **Reviews:** These are typically qualitative text descriptions provided by the user.

Many platforms (like TripAdvisor, which was shown as an example) provide both:

*   **Qualitative Data:** Review text, review title, user country.
*   **Quantitative Data:** Star ratings (overall and for specific attributes like service, value, location, sleep quality), number of likes/helpful votes, user's total likes/followers.

Both quantitative and qualitative UGC contain valuable information that needs to be analyzed. Consumer ratings and recommendations are particularly important as they serve as key performance indicators, measure customer satisfaction, and provide information for potential new customers.

Happy customers tend to give positive feedback, while unhappy customers give negative feedback. The rise of online platforms has led to more customers sharing information. Motivations for sharing vary:

*   Moral obligation to help others.
*   Desire to be heard (egocentric).
*   Wanting to stand out or disagree with the majority.

Organizations must learn to handle both positive and negative reviews effectively. Analyzing reviews helps understand:

*   Important features or aspects customers focus on.
*   Emotions associated with positive and negative feedback.

Understanding these emotions can inform communication strategies and service improvements (e.g., an airline partnering with a nostalgic beverage brand to evoke positive emotions).

## Project Goal

In this research project, we combined both qualitative (text reviews) and quantitative (ratings and recommendations) UGC from online airline data to understand what explains customer ratings and recommendations.

## Project Outline

1.  **Text Mining:** Initial processing of the raw text data.
2.  **Text Analysis:**
    *   Identifying aspects of core services and augmented services from the text.
    *   Extracting sentiment and emotions related to these aspects.
3.  **Analysis of Context:** Analyzing how the relative importance of service attributes and emotions varies for different types of customers or service contexts (e.g., low-cost vs. full-service airlines).

## Research Questions

1.  **Impact of Core vs. Augmented Services:**
    *   What constitutes core service vs. augmented service?
    *   How do ratings and discussions about core and augmented service aspects explain customer satisfaction (overall ratings and recommendations)?
2.  **Role of Sentiment and Emotion:**
    *   Do overall sentiment and emotions expressed in textual reviews explain customer satisfaction?
    *   Does the sentiment related to *specific core and augmented service aspects* explain customer satisfaction?
3.  **Contextual Differences:**
    *   How does the explanatory power of augmented services change based on the model of service delivery (e.g., low-cost vs. full-service airlines)?

**Core Service vs. Augmented Service:**

*   **Core Service:** The basic benefit the service provides (e.g., transportation in airlines, food in a restaurant).
*   **Augmented Service (Add-ons):** Additional features or experiences beyond the core (e.g., in-flight entertainment, lounge access, valet parking, ambience in a restaurant).

Understanding the relative importance of core vs. augmented aspects, and which specific components within them matter most, is crucial for resource allocation and strategy.

**Sentiment and Emotion:**

*   **Overall Sentiment/Emotion:** The general feeling expressed in the entire review.
*   **Aspect-wise Sentiment/Emotion:** The feeling expressed specifically about a particular service aspect (e.g., positive sentiment about seat comfort, negative sentiment about punctuality). Emotions like anger, fear (high arousal) and trust, anticipation (low arousal) play different roles.

## Methodology and Text Mining Process

The methodology involved several steps to convert raw text into quantitative variables:

1.  **Data Collection:** Collected 41,397 customer reviews from 362 airlines across 158 countries.
    *   Overall customer rating: 1 to 10 scale.
    *   Recommendation: Binary (0/1).
    *   Quantitative Attribute Ratings: For specific attributes (e.g., value for money, seat comfort - 5-point scale).
2.  **Text Preprocessing:** Cleaned the raw review text to create a structured corpus. This typically involves:
    *   Removing punctuation, numbers, special characters.
    *   Converting text to lowercase.
    *   Removing stop words (common words like 'the', 'a', 'is').
    *   Stemming or lemmatization (reducing words to their root form).
3.  **Feature Generation & Aspect Identification:**
    *   Calculated Term Frequency (TF) and Inverse Document Frequency (IDF) to identify important words.
    *   Used Part-of-Speech (POS) tagging to identify nouns.
    *   Selected a list of top nouns based on frequency/TF-IDF.
    *   **Manual Clustering:** Grouped these top nouns into relevant service aspects based on human interpretation (e.g., "seat", "leg space", "cramp" related to 'Comfort'). Six aspects were identified:
        *   Punctuality
        *   Food and Beverage
        *   Comfort
        *   Staff Behavior
        *   In-flight Entertainment
        *   Check-in
    *   A "bag of words" (list of relevant nouns) was created for each aspect.
4.  **Calculating Aspect-wise Sentiment:**
    *   For each review, identify sentences that contain any of the nouns associated with a specific service aspect (e.g., find all sentences in a review that mention "food", "meal", "drinks").
    *   Calculate the sentiment (e.g., using NRC or Bing lexicons) of *only* those identified sentences.
    *   This sentiment of the relevant sentences becomes the aspect-wise sentiment score for that review. (A pilot study was done to validate this approach).
5.  **Overall Sentiment and Emotion:** Calculated overall sentiment and the scores for 8 specific emotions (using NRC lexicon) for the entire review text, as discussed in previous lectures.
6.  **Final Data Structure:** The final dataset included:
    *   **Y Variables:** Overall Rating (1-10), Recommendation (0/1).
    *   **X Variables:**
        *   Quantitative Attribute Ratings (e.g., Value for Money rating, Seat Comfort rating, etc. - 5-point scale).
        *   Overall Sentiment.
        *   8 Emotion scores.
        *   6 Aspect-wise Sentiment scores.

## Model Specification

Three models were developed to explain the outcome variables (Overall Rating and Recommendation):

*   **Model 1:** Uses only the quantitative attribute ratings as predictors.
*   **Model 2:** Uses quantitative attribute ratings + overall sentiment + 8 emotion scores as predictors.
*   **Model 3:** Uses quantitative attribute ratings + overall sentiment + 8 emotion scores + 6 aspect-wise sentiment scores as predictors.

*   **Model Type:** Linear Regression for predicting Overall Rating (continuous 1-10 scale); Logistic Regression for predicting Recommendation (binary 0/1).
*   **Multicollinearity Check:** VIF values were checked and were generally below 4, indicating no significant multicollinearity issues.

## Findings

Looking at the regression results (coefficients and significance levels):

*   **Model 1 (Quantitative Ratings Only):**
    *   For Overall Ratings, **Value for Money**, **Cabin Staff Rating**, and **Seat Comfort Rating** were the most important attributes. These appear to be core attributes.
    *   This suggests that **core attributes are generally more important** than augmented attributes for explaining overall satisfaction (as measured by ratings).
    *   Adjusted R-squared was around 0.56.

*   **Model 2 (Adding Overall Sentiment & Emotions):**
    *   Overall positive emotions are positively related to satisfaction, negative emotions negatively related (as expected).
    *   For explaining **Overall Ratings**, **Disgust** emerged as a highly significant and impactful negative emotion.
    *   For explaining **Recommendation**, **Joy** was more related than for satisfaction.
    *   This highlights the significant role of emotions (both positive and negative) beyond just structured ratings.

*   **Model 3 (Adding Aspect-wise Sentiment):**
    *   When aspect-wise sentiments are included, they also contribute significantly.
    *   For explaining Overall Ratings, **Comfort Sentiment** (core) and **Punctuality Sentiment** (core) showed high importance.
    *   For explaining Recommendation, **Food and Beverage Sentiment** (augmented) and **In-flight Entertainment Sentiment** (augmented) also became quite important, comparable to core service sentiments like Staff Behavior Sentiment.
    *   This indicates that while core service ratings are crucial, the *sentiment* expressed about augmented services in reviews also strongly influences the likelihood of recommendation.

*   **Contextual Differences (Low-cost vs. Full-service Airlines - mentioned as being in the paper):**
    *   The relative importance of attributes changes depending on the airline type.
    *   For Low-Cost Airlines, concrete aspects like **Value for Money** are relatively more important.
    *   For Full-Service Airlines, abstract aspects like **Seat Comfort**, **Staff Behavior**, **Food and Beverages**, and **In-flight Entertainment** are relatively more important.

## Conclusions

Based on the analysis:

1.  **Core service aspects** (e.g., value for money, seat comfort, staff) have a **stronger positive relationship with customer satisfaction** (overall rating) than augmented aspects.
2.  However, **sentiment expressed in text about both core and augmented aspects** is important, especially for explaining recommendation behavior. Augmented services' sentiment can be quite influential for recommending a service.
3.  The **relative importance of service aspects varies** between low-cost and full-service airlines. Concrete aspects are more critical for low-cost carriers, while abstract/augmented aspects are more critical for full-service carriers.

## Managerial Implications

Organizations can use these findings to:

*   **Prioritize Investment:** Focus resources and attention on the service aspects that are most important for customer satisfaction and recommendation, considering the specific service model (low-cost vs. full-service).
*   **Monitor and Address Feedback:** Develop automated systems using text mining to continuously monitor reviews, identify specific aspects and emotions causing dissatisfaction (especially disgust), and address them proactively.
*   **Enhance Communication:** Tailor communication strategies based on the emotions identified as most influential for their customer base.
*   **Go Beyond Ratings:** Understand that textual reviews provide deeper insights into customer experience and emotional state than ratings alone.

This paper demonstrates how text mining can convert large volumes of unstructured text data into quantitative features that can be integrated with traditional quantitative data (ratings) to build predictive models and gain deeper insights into customer behavior and preferences at scale.

Thank you very much for your attention. I will see you in the next video.

```markdown
# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 60: Text Mining and Sentiment Analytics (Contd.)

Hello everybody. Welcome to Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM, IIT Kharagpur. We are in week 11 and this is the last video in week 11.

This video discusses another paper titled: **Drivers of helpfulness of online hotel reviews for sentiment and emotion mining approach.**

You can find this paper on ScienceDirect and ResearchGate.

## Paper Highlights

*   Textual reviews and quantitative ratings are used to explain review helpfulness.
*   Text mining is done to find the polarity, sentiment, and emotions from the text.
*   Econometrics and Machine Learning techniques were used.
*   Contributes towards review performance literature and review management strategy.

## Understanding Helpfulness of Online Hotel Reviews

Online hotel reviews are important performance indicators. They are the second most trustworthy platform after recommendations from family and friends. They serve as a crucial source of information for prospective customers.

As a hotel, it's vital to understand which reviews are more likely to contribute to customer decision-making (more helpful) and which are less likely.

If a review is predicted to be highly helpful:
*   If it's positive, the hotel should promote it further through digital marketing.
*   If it's negative, the hotel needs to take immediate action, potentially contacting the reviewer or implementing service recovery mechanisms to mitigate its negative impact.

Conversely, if a negative review is unlikely to be helpful (e.g., vague complaints without reasoning), focusing resources on it might not be the best strategy.

It is more important to identify potentially viral reviews and manage them based on their sentiment.

## Why Study Review Helpfulness?

*   **Predict potentially popular reviews:** Helps both hotels and consumers.
    *   **For Hotels:** Take corrective measures, get genuine feedback to enhance quality, prioritize review solicitation from satisfied customers.
    *   **For Consumers/Reviewers:** Understand what makes a review impactful, learn how to write helpful reviews to get their voice heard. Review writing can even be a business for influencers.
*   **Shortlist reviews for customers:** Helps customers make purchase decisions quickly.

## Hypotheses

Based on the importance of review helpfulness, several hypotheses were developed:

*   **H1:** The higher the total sentiment content (positive + negative words) in the text of the review, the more helpful the review is perceived. (Opinionated reviews are more helpful than neutral ones).
*   **H2:** The higher the polarity (bias towards overwhelmingly positive or negative) of the text of the review, the less helpful the review is perceived. (Extremely biased opinions might be distrusted).
*   **H3:** The interaction of total sentiment content and polarity will have a negative impact on review helpfulness. (If a review is highly opinionated *and* highly polarized, it's even less helpful).
*   **H4:** The same relationships (as H1, H2, H3) apply to the polarity of the title of the review.

Regarding emotions, recall the 8 basic emotions (4 positive, 4 negative) and the concept of arousal (high/low):

*   High Arousal Negative (e.g., Anger, Fear, Disgust)
*   Low Arousal Negative (e.g., Sadness)
*   High Arousal Positive (e.g., Joy, Surprise)
*   Low Arousal Positive (e.g., Trust, Anticipation)

*   **H5:** The higher the emotions associated with negative valence and low arousal (e.g., sadness), the more helpful the review is perceived. (Sadness might be linked to expectation disconfirmation, providing useful insights).
*   **H6:** The higher the emotion associated with negative valence and high arousal (e.g., anger, fear, disgust) expressed, the *less* helpful the review is perceived. (High arousal negative emotions might be seen as personal or extreme experiences).

Other review/reviewer characteristics:

*   **H7:** The higher the length of the review, the more helpful the review is. (Vivid descriptions are expected to be helpful).
*   **H8:** The higher the overall quantitative rating (e.g., star rating), the more helpful the review is. (Positivity bias suggests higher ratings are read more).
*   **H9:** The higher the time distance between the experience date and review posting date, the less helpful the review is. (Fresh memories are considered more helpful).
*   **H10:** The higher the number of past helpful votes received by the author, the more helpful the current review is. (Reviewer trustworthiness).

## Model Overview

The model aims to predict **Review Helpfulness** (the dependent variable). The independent variables include:

*   **From Text:** Total sentiment content, text polarity, title polarity, interaction effects, counts of different emotions (negative valence low arousal, negative valence high arousal), length of review.
*   **From Quantitative Data:** Overall rating, time distance between experience and review date, author's past helpful votes.

## Methodology

*   **Data Source:** tripadvisor.com
*   **Observations:** 942 reviews from 40 hotels.
*   **Rating Scale:** 1 to 5 points.
*   **Text Mining Tools:**
    *   **Afinn:** Used to find sentiment scores.
    *   **NRC Emolex:** Used to find counts of 8 basic emotions.
*   **Statistical Models:**
    *   **Poisson Regression:** Initially considered for the count variable (number of helpful votes).
    *   **Negative Binomial Regression:** Used because the helpful votes data showed high variance (mean 1.63, standard deviation 3.58), indicating overdispersion which violates the Poisson assumption (mean ≈ variance). Negative Binomial is suitable for count variables with many zeros.

## Findings and Results

The Negative Binomial regression results indicated the following:

*   **H1 (Total Sentiment Content):** Not supported (practically not significant).
*   **H2 (Text Polarity):** Supported (negative effect).
*   **H3 (Interaction):** Supported (negative impact).
*   **H4 (Title Polarity):** Supported (negative effect).
*   **H5 (Negative Valence Low Arousal - e.g., Sadness):** Supported (positive impact).
*   **H6 (Negative Valence High Arousal - e.g., Fear, Disgust):** Supported (positive impact for disgust, negative for fear).
*   **H7 (Review Length):** Opposite of hypothesis (negative significant impact). *This is a key finding.*
*   **H8 (Overall Rating):** Supported (positive relationship).
*   **H9 (Time Distance):** Supported (negative relationship).
*   **H10 (Author's Past Helpful Votes):** Supported (positive relationship).

### Discussion of Key Findings

*   **Review Length:** The finding that *higher length leads to lower helpfulness* is counter-intuitive but significant. This suggests that mere length or "blabbering" about one's personal experience without providing useful information does not make a review helpful. Readers seek opinions and information relevant to their decision-making, not a personal travel blog entry. Providing concise, relevant information is more valuable than a long description of personal activities.
*   **Polarity:** Polarizing titles and text reduce helpfulness, suggesting readers prefer balanced or credible-sounding opinions over extreme ones.
*   **Emotions:** Low arousal negative emotions (like sadness, perhaps reflecting unmet expectations) and surprisingly, some high arousal negative emotions (like disgust), can increase helpfulness, while others (like fear) decrease it. This complex relationship highlights the nuanced impact of specific emotions.
*   **Quantitative Factors:** Higher overall ratings, recent reviews (lower time distance), and reviews from authors with a history of writing helpful reviews are perceived as more helpful.

## Managerial Implications

*   **Predictive Modeling:** The models developed (regression, random forest, boosting, ANN were explored) can be used by hotels to predict potentially popular reviews and manage them proactively.
*   **Review Solicitation:** Hotels can use these findings to guide customers on writing helpful reviews, encouraging them to focus on specific aspects (staff, food quality, amenities) rather than just personal anecdotes. This helps solicit reviews that are likely to be impactful.
*   **Reviewer Guidance:** Review platforms can guide reviewers on how to write reviews that are considered helpful by the community, emphasizing substance, balanced opinion, and relevant details over mere length or extreme emotion.

In essence, this study provides insights into the drivers of review helpfulness, empowering both businesses and reviewers to optimize their engagement with online review platforms.

We will briefly discuss topic modeling in the next video before moving to Week 12.

Thank you very much. I will see you in the next video.
```

# Marketing Analytics
## Lecture 61: Social Network Analysis and Excel Dashboards

This lecture, part of the Marketing Analytics course led by Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur, covers Social Network Analysis for Marketing Management.

### Course Journey So Far

We have covered a significant path in this course, understanding:
*   What customers want (customer value, conjoint analysis).
*   Demand forecasting and pricing.
*   Recommendation engines and RFM analysis in retail.
*   Customer lifetime value calculation.
*   Text mining and its applications (in the last two weeks).

### Introduction to Social Network Analysis (SNA)

This lecture focuses on Social Network Analysis (SNA), which is distinct from Social Media Marketing or digital marketing. We will not be discussing how to place ads on platforms like Facebook.

Instead, the core concern of SNA is **how the connections between people** can aid in various business decision-making processes.

#### Understanding Social Connections

Every person has social connections:
*   **Professional:** Colleagues, industry contacts, students, professors.
*   **Personal:** Family, friends, acquaintances, neighbors.

Some connections can be common between individuals, while others are unique to a person's network.

#### Strength and Direction of Relationships

*   **Strength:** Relationships vary in strength (e.g., family ties vs. a professor-student relationship).
*   **Direction:** Relationships can be unidirectional or bidirectional. For example, a customer might feel a strong connection to a bank teller they always interact with, but the teller might not have a strong connection to that specific customer among many others. Similarly, students might know their professors well, but a professor with many students might not know every student by name.

These nuances in strength and direction are crucial in Social Network Analysis.

### History and Applications of SNA

The term "Social Network Analysis" was coined by J.A. Barnes in 1954, originating in sociology, sociometrics, and computer science. It was first used by Harvard's schools of business and sociometrics and later adopted by other institutions like The Manchester School. While old in its core concept, its application in marketing is relatively newer.

#### Common Uses of SNA

SNA is commonly used in:
*   **Diffusion of ideas and diseases:** How information or illness spreads through a network.
*   **Social influence:** Identifying key individuals who can influence others.
*   **Social support systems:** Ensuring support reaches the intended recipients.
*   **Organizational structure:** Understanding formal and informal connections within a company.
*   **Marketing management:** Identifying influential nodes for viral marketing or understanding customer relationships.
*   **Social movements:** Analyzing how movements gain traction and spread.

### Core Components of a Social Network

A social network consists of:
*   **Nodes (or Actors):** The individual entities in the network (e.g., people, organizations).
*   **Paths (or Ties):** The connections or relationships between the nodes. Paths can be:
    *   **Unidirectional:** Flowing in one direction (e.g., A influences B, but B doesn't influence A).
    *   **Multidirectional (or Bidirectional):** Flowing in both directions (e.g., A and B influence each other).

The **strength** of a path or tie can be represented by its width or a numerical weight, indicating how strong or weak the relationship is.

### Representing a Social Network

A network can be represented:
*   **Graphically:** A visual diagram with nodes as points and paths as lines/arrows connecting them.
*   **Matrix:** A connection or adjacency matrix where rows and columns represent nodes. The cell value indicates whether a connection exists (e.g., 1 for exists, 0 for doesn't) and possibly the strength of the connection.

Example Matrix:

|      | A   | B   | C   | D   | E   |
| :--- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| A    | 0   | 1   | 1   | 0   | 0   |
| B    | 1   | 0   | 0   | 0   | 0   |
| C    | 1   | 0   | 0   | 1   | 2   |
| D    | 0   | 0   | 2   | 0   | 0   |
| E    | 0   | 0   | 2   | 0   | 0   |

*Note: '1' indicates a standard connection, '2' indicates a stronger connection (e.g., thicker line in a graph).*

### Network Structure and Diffusion

The structure of a network significantly impacts diffusion.
*   **Cohesive Networks:** Networks where nodes are highly interconnected. Information spreads easily within the network once it penetrates. However, penetrating such a closed network can be difficult initially.
*   **Less Cohesive Networks:** Networks with sparser connections. Information might spread slower but might be easier to introduce initially.

This applies directly to marketing; understanding network structure helps determine the best strategy for information dissemination or campaign penetration.

### Types of Ties

The definition of a tie or connection can be broad:
*   **Evaluation:** Friendship, liking, respect.
*   **Transfer:** Business transactions, payment, lending, borrowing.
*   **Association/Affiliation:** Belonging to the same club, joint attendance.
*   **Behavioral Interaction:** Talking, sending messages.
*   **Movement:** Between places or statuses.
*   **Biological or Formal Relationships:** Family, employee/employer.

Defining the specific type of tie relevant to the marketing problem is crucial.

### Units of Analysis

*   **Dyads:** A pair of actors and their connection. The tie is a property of the pair. Analyzing dyads can reveal insights into relationships between two individuals (e.g., reciprocity). Hypotheses can be tested based on the number or strength of dyads (e.g., does a higher number of strong family dyads correlate with higher consumption?).
*   **Triads:** Three actors and their connections. Triads can reveal more complex relationship patterns (e.g., balance or conflict). The number and type of triads (e.g., positive/negative relationships) can potentially predict things like conflict within a network.

### Network Measures

*   **Centrality:** A measure of how central or important a node is within the network. Central nodes typically have more connections and are positioned closer to the network's core. Various centrality measures exist (e.g., degree centrality, betweenness centrality, closeness centrality). Centrality helps identify popular or influential individuals (the "stars").
*   **Structural Hole:** An absence of connections between two nodes that connect otherwise disconnected or weakly connected parts of the network. A structural hole acts as a barrier to information flow between different clusters. The person who bridges a structural hole holds significant power or influence as they control information flow.

Examples:
*   A person connecting two distinct friend groups.
*   A manager connecting two different departments that don't interact directly.
*   **Real-world example:** COVID-19 lockdowns and quarantine measures are attempts to create structural holes in the global network of people to prevent the virus from spreading between previously connected populations.

### Types of People in Networks

Networks often contain specific types of individuals important for diffusion and influence:
*   **Connectors:** People with a large number of connections.
*   **Mavens:** People with deep knowledge and expertise who share information.
*   **Salespersons:** People persuasive in convincing others.

Identifying these roles is vital for marketing strategies like viral marketing.

### Example: Organizational Network

Analyzing a network within an organization might show different departments (e.g., Marketing, Manufacturing, Finance) forming clusters. A key individual (like "Paul" in the example given) might connect different departments, acting as a bridge across structural holes and facilitating communication that might otherwise not occur. If this individual leaves, the structural hole reappears, potentially hindering cross-departmental interaction.

### Conclusion

This lecture introduced the foundational concepts of Social Network Analysis, distinguishing it from social media marketing and explaining core components, representations, measures, and applications relevant to marketing.

The next video will delve into a case study to demonstrate how these concepts can be applied in a real-life marketing scenario.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 62 : social network analysis and excel dashboards (contd.)

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 62: Social Network Analysis and Excel Dashboards (Contd.)

Hello everybody. Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur, who is taking this course. We are discussing Social Network Analysis, and in this particular class, I will discuss a Manufacturing Company Case Study in the context of marketing.

## Case Study 1: Manufacturing Company Complaint Handling

This particular case study has been taken from one of the published cases. The company is a global leader in manufacturing integrated circuits, a high-tech product. Their products are applied in various sectors like automotive, electronic products, and IT companies. They have two plants in Thailand with around 6,000 employees.

The company is trying to be **customer-oriented**. This is an important term in marketing. Customer orientation means that *everyone* in the organization is responsible for handling the customer, not just the marketing person. The idea is that the customer is king, and everyone – finance, manufacturing, marketing, sales, HR – should understand the importance of making customers happy and satisfied. When the entire organization's culture shifts to focus on customer happiness, irrespective of position or job role, the organization becomes customer-oriented.

However, achieving this is not easy because different departments have different goals and performance metrics:
*   Marketing: Brand image contribution
*   Sales: Revenue generation
*   Finance: Financial ratios
*   HR: Attrition, attendance, employee engagement, cost to hire, etc.
*   Manufacturing: Productivity, cost reduction
*   Supply Chain: Cost reduction, timeliness

These departments often operate in silos with different priorities. This can lead to problems, especially when dealing with cross-functional processes like complaint handling. The lack of social connection between people from different departments means they don't understand each other's challenges or contributions. A marketing manager might not even know a manufacturing person in a large organization. This lack of communication can lead to misunderstandings and inefficiencies.

In this particular organization, such difficulties were evident in the **complaint handling process**. There were numerous complaints, and they were not being handled efficiently.

### Formal Complaint Handling Process

The standard process for handling a customer complaint was described as follows:
1.  The Sales Department receives a written customer complaint.
2.  The Customer Service (CS) Manager is notified by the Sales Department.
3.  The CS Manager discusses the complaint with the CS Engineer (a customer-facing engineer).
4.  The CS Engineer holds a discussion session with the QA Engineer and Production Operator.
5.  The Production Operator discusses with the Maintenance Technician and the Process Engineer.
6.  Once all relevant parties have discussed, the Process Engineer (PE) conducts an investigation and engineering analysis on the complaint.
7.  The PE verifies their findings with the PE Leader and PE Manager.
8.  A formal statement is presented to the Maintenance Manager, Production Supervisor, and Production Manager.
9.  A formal statement is also given to the Manufacturing Director and QRA Directors.
10. Once the Directors finalize the response, the Customer Service Manager and MD (Managing Director) are informed of the findings.
11. The Sales Department receives the report and forwards it to the customer.

This is a lengthy process involving many handoffs and discussions across different departments.

### The Problem

The core problem was:
*   **80% of complaints** took longer than the targeted time frame during the past 2 years.
*   On average, there were **15 complaints quarterly** since the last plant expansion.

The initial evaluation pointed to the **ineffectiveness of information sharing** as the major cause for the slow responses. People weren't effectively sharing information with each other across the organizational structure.

### Social Network Analysis (SNA) Application

To solve this problem, the company hired consultants (professors) who decided to run an SNA study.

The objective was:
1.  To examine the existing information flow (formal and informal) within the complaint handling process.
2.  To identify possible improvement areas to strengthen and improve the effectiveness of the process.

### Data and Methodology

The study involved the key people involved in the complaint handling process (around 20 people). For each person (node), their designation, functional unit, and responsibility (direct or indirect) for complaints were noted.

A **20x20 matrix** was created to represent the connections and their strengths (weights). The connection strength was defined subjectively based on discussions, using weights:
*   **1**: Between directly and directly responsible persons.
*   **0.67**: Between a directly responsible person and an indirectly responsible person.
*   **0.33**: For other connections.
This corresponds to a ratio of 3:2:1.

### Results and Interpretation

The network visualization of the social connections revealed insights into the information flow.

*(Based on the description, the key observations from the network were:)*
*   The formal process dictates information flow, but the social network shows disconnects.
*   Specifically, CS Engineers and PE Engineers, who are crucial in the formal process flow (CS Engineer -> PE Engineer), did not have strong direct social connections. Their communication might only happen through formal channels or via intermediate nodes (like the PE Leader).
*   Many PE Engineers were not well-connected within the network, or with other crucial roles like Maintenance Technicians, except for possibly one PE Engineer. This means information might not be effectively disseminated among the PE team.
*   The PE Leader appeared to be a **structural hole** – a crucial bridge connecting different parts of the network. If this person were absent, communication between groups might break down entirely.

### Recommendations based on SNA

The SNA highlighted bottlenecks in information flow due to poor social connections between critical roles. Possible solutions could include:
1.  **Improve connections for isolated roles:** Actively encourage or facilitate connections for PE Engineers or other less-connected individuals who are key to the process.
2.  **Leverage central nodes:** Utilize the PE Leader, QA Engineer, or CS Engineer (who appeared central in the network) as key points for disseminating information. For instance, information could bypass the current CS Engineer -> PE Engineer direct handoff and go to the PE Leader first, who is better connected to the PE team.
3.  **Incentivize central nodes:** Consider incentivizing central persons like the CS Engineer or QA Engineer to proactively ensure information is passed effectively to all relevant parties.

By aligning the informal social network with the formal process flow, or by leveraging the existing strong connections, the company could potentially improve the speed and effectiveness of complaint handling.

## Case Study 2: Alphexo Technology Adoption

Another case study discussed involved **Alphexo**, a major telecom manufacturing company.

### Problem

Alphexo faced a significant number of injuries at its distribution center due to **problems in supplier palletizing**. Suppliers were using out-of-specification pallets, leading to poor quality, breakage, and injuries during handling, disposal, and re-palletizing.

### Proposed Solution

To address this, Alphexo decided to implement an **ER system** for real-time reporting of discrepancies and quality issues related to inbound deliveries (like pallet problems) to both Alphexo internal teams and its suppliers. This system was seen as a proper solution for better tracking supplier performance and communicating quality issues.

### The Challenge: System Adoption

The key challenge was whether the employees would actually **adopt** this new ER system.

### SNA Application

An SNA study was conducted to understand the social network among employees and predict system adoption.

The objective was:
*   To identify key individuals or groups who could influence the adoption of the new ER system.

### Data and Methodology

The study involved identifying the actors (employees) and mapping their social network based on connections.

*(Based on the description of the network visualization:)*
*   The network showed groups of employees and some central individuals.
*   One part of the network formed a highly connected **triad** (a group of three strongly connected individuals).
*   Another individual appeared to be a single person but also well-connected to many others.

### Model and Findings

They created a model to predict system adoption using factors like:
*   **Network Centrality:** How central a person is in the social network.
*   **Years in Alphexo:** Length of service in the organization.
*   **Organizational Rank:** Position in the organizational hierarchy.

The findings revealed:
*   **Network centrality had a positive impact** on predicting who would adopt the system. People who were more central in the social network were more likely to adopt.
*   **Years in Alphexo and organizational rank did not significantly matter** (P-values were high).

This suggests that in this organization, influencing the **central individuals** in the social network would be the most effective strategy for driving technology adoption, rather than targeting people based on their experience or formal position. The triad and the other well-connected individual identified in the network visualization would be prime candidates for incentivizing or influencing to promote the ER system adoption.

## Conclusion

We have discussed two case studies demonstrating how Social Network Analysis can be applied to solve marketing-related problems, specifically focusing on process improvement and technology adoption within organizations. SNA helps identify bottlenecks, crucial individuals (structural holes), and influential nodes within the informal network, providing insights beyond the formal organizational structure.

In the next videos, we will discuss how social network analysis can be actually implemented in the market to solve certain problems, including the codes and technical details.

Thank you very much. I will see you in the next video.

# Marketing Analytics: Social Network Analysis and Excel Dashboards (Contd.) - Lecture 63

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Dr. Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur. We are currently in Week 12, discussing Social Network Analysis (SNA). In the previous two videos, we covered the basics of SNA and its potential applications. In this session, we will work through a case study to see how SNA can be applied in a real-life scenario.

## Case Study: Club Member Churn

The case study involves a club where members subscribe to use various facilities and participate in activities. Entertainment is a major focus, and entertainment often happens in groups. When people subscribe and start attending the club, they become part of a social group. Decisions about arranging parties, competitions, or participating in events are generally collective decisions within these groups.

Similarly, the decision to switch from one club to another can also be a collective decision. This particular club suddenly noticed a significant number of members leaving in large chunks. This was a churn problem, but unlike churn in telecom or banking where it's often a personal decision, the club recognized it as a collective decision.

The goal was to identify the influential people within the club's social network who were driving this churn. By identifying these individuals, the club could connect with them, offer incentives, implement service recovery measures, or create switching barriers to prevent further churn.

## Data Collection

To address this, the club collected data on member interactions. They called members and asked who they had met or talked with in the last month and how many times. This "how many times" served as the strength of the connection. This recent interaction data was deemed more relevant than historical connections, as the churning decisions were happening within a short timeframe.

The data collected was in the form of a matrix representing the number of interactions between members.

## Analyzing the Network using R

The analysis of this social network was performed using R, specifically the `igraph` package.

### Setting up R and Loading Data

1.  **Set Working Directory:** Set the working directory to the location of the source file.
2.  **Read Data:** The data was in a text file formatted as a matrix. It was read using `read.csv`.
    *   The separator was a space (`sep = " "`).
    *   There was no header row (`header = FALSE`).

    ```R
    # Set working directory (replace with your path)
    setwd("path/to/your/directory")

    # Read the data
    term_matrix <- read.csv("your_data_file.txt", sep = " ", header = FALSE)
    ```
    *Initial observation showed an extra column, likely due to how the file was read.*

3.  **Clean the Data:** The extra column (column 35) which contained NA values was removed.

    ```R
    # Remove the last column with NA values
    term_matrix <- term_matrix[, -35]
    ```
    *This resulted in a 34x34 matrix.*

4.  **Set Row and Column Names:** The members were identified as V1, V2, ... V34. Row and column names were set accordingly.

    ```R
    # Create names for nodes (members)
    node_names <- paste0("V", 1:nrow(term_matrix))

    # Set row and column names of the matrix
    rownames(term_matrix) <- node_names
    colnames(term_matrix) <- node_names
    ```
    *This ensures that member V1 is connected to member V2 with a certain strength, etc., represented by the matrix cells.*

### Creating and Manipulating the Graph

1.  **Install and Load igraph:** The analysis requires the `igraph` package. If not installed, it needs to be installed.

    ```R
    # Install igraph if not already installed
    install.packages("igraph")

    # Load the igraph library
    library(igraph)
    ```

2.  **Create Graph from Adjacency Matrix:** The graph object `g` was created from the `term_matrix` using `graph_from_adjacency_matrix`.
    *   `weighted = TRUE` was set because the numbers in the matrix (e.g., 3, 5) represent the strength (frequency of interaction) and should be treated as weights.
    *   `mode = "undirected"` was used because the connections were considered bidirectional (if A talked to B, B also talked to A with the same frequency in this data).

    ```R
    # Create the graph object
    g <- graph_from_adjacency_matrix(as.matrix(term_matrix), weighted = TRUE, mode = "undirected")
    ```
    *The data type was confirmed as matrix before passing it.*

3.  **Simplify the Graph:** The `simplify()` function was used. For an undirected, weighted graph created this way, `simplify()` typically removes loops (edges from a node to itself, which are already zero in the data) and might handle multiple edges between the same two nodes (though in this specific data, the matrix directly gives the total weight). The instructor mentioned it helps visualize by potentially considering only one half of the matrix, which is relevant for undirected graphs where the adjacency matrix is symmetric.

    ```R
    # Simplify the graph (remove loops and consolidate parallel edges if any)
    g <- simplify(g)
    ```

4.  **Set Vertex Labels:** The labels displayed on the nodes (vertices) in the plot were set to the member names (V1, V2, etc.).

    ```R
    # Set vertex labels to their names (V1, V2, ...)
    V(g)$label <- V(g)$name
    ```

5.  **Calculate and Set Vertex Degree:** The degree of each vertex (the number of connections it has) was calculated using `degree()` and stored as a vertex attribute. This degree can be used to influence the visual representation (e.g., size) of the nodes.

    ```R
    # Calculate degree for each vertex
    V(g)$degree <- degree(g)

    # Display degrees (optional)
    print(V(g)$degree)
    ```
    *The degree indicates how many *other* members each member has interacted with (non-zero entries in their row/column in the matrix).*

### Plotting the Network

Different plotting methods and layouts were explored to visualize the network structure.

1.  **Simple Plot with Fruchterman Reingold Layout:** This is a common force-directed layout algorithm that tries to position nodes such that connected nodes are close together and unconnected nodes are far apart.

    ```R
    # Choose a layout algorithm (Fruchterman Reingold)
    layout1 <- layout_with_fr(g)

    # Plot the graph
    plot(g, layout = layout1)
    ```
    *Initial plots might not show labels clearly or nodes with varying sizes.*

2.  **Interactive Plot with Kamada Kawai Layout (tkplot):** `tkplot` provides an interactive window where the graph can be manipulated (nodes moved, parameters changed). The Kamada Kawai layout is another force-directed algorithm aiming for aesthetically pleasing layouts.

    ```R
    # Choose a layout algorithm (Kamada Kawai)
    layout2 <- layout_with_kk(g)

    # Create an interactive plot
    tkplot(g, layout = layout2)
    ```
    *In the `tkplot` window, properties like vertex color and size can be changed interactively to improve visibility.*
    *Steps shown in the video:*
    *   Select all vertices.
    *   Right-click on a vertex.
    *   Change vertex color to white (makes all nodes the same color).
    *   Right-click again, change vertex size to a larger value to see them better.
    *   Use "Fit view" to zoom to fit the screen.
    *   Visually inspect the layout to identify potentially central nodes based on position and connections. Nodes V34 and V1 appeared prominent, V20, V29, V31 less so.

3.  **Plotting with Enhanced Visual Attributes:** To make the plot more informative directly, vertex size, label size, label color, and edge width were explicitly set based on graph properties (like degree or weight).

    ```R
    # Set vertex size based on degree (scaled)
    V(g)$size <- V(g)$degree * 0.8 # Scale factor might need adjustment

    # Set vertex label color
    V(g)$label.color <- "black"

    # Set edge width based on weight (scaled)
    E(g)$width <- E(g)$weight * 0.5 # Scale factor might need adjustment

    # Set edge color
    E(g)$color <- "grey" # Make edges grey for better visibility

    # Plot again with enhanced attributes using Fruchterman Reingold
    plot(g, layout = layout_with_fr(g))
    ```
    *This plot clearly showed nodes with larger sizes (like V1, V33, V34) indicating higher degrees (more connections).*

    *Using `tkplot` again with these attributes loaded:*
    *   Nodes V33, V34 appeared very large, V1 also large.
    *   Visually, the network seemed to show two denser clusters connected by some intermediate nodes.

## Analysis and Insights

From the visual analysis of the network graph, especially with node sizes reflecting degree:

*   Nodes **V33** and **V34** stood out as very large, indicating they are connected to a high number of other members.
*   Node **V1** was also large, suggesting it's another highly connected individual.
*   The network appeared to have two main clusters of members. One cluster seemed more connected to V33 and V34, and another cluster seemed more connected to V1.
*   Node **V3** (and possibly V9 based on initial inspection) appeared to be located in between these two main groups, potentially acting as a bridge connecting the clusters.

These visual insights suggest that V33, V34, and V1 are key influencers due to their high number of direct connections. V3 might be a critical connector between different segments of the network.

For the club's marketing managers, this means:
*   If one of the main clusters is responsible for the churn, then the highly connected individuals in that cluster (e.g., V33, V34, or V1) are the primary targets for intervention.
*   If conflict or disagreement between the two main clusters is leading to churn, then the bridge individual (V3) might be a crucial person to engage with.

Identifying these central figures allows the club to direct their efforts (incentives, communication, service recovery) towards the individuals who have the most influence within the network and are likely driving the collective decision-making regarding churn.

## Conclusion

Social network analysis provides a powerful exploratory technique to understand the social structure of a group. By visualizing the network and analyzing measures like centrality (degree, betweenness, etc., although only degree was explicitly used for size in this demo), organizations can identify key influencers and connectors.

In this case study, visual inspection based on member interaction data highlighted specific individuals (V33, V34, V1 as central, V3 as a potential bridge) who are crucial to understanding and potentially mitigating the collective churn behavior in the club.

This type of analysis, while not a hardcore machine learning prediction, provides actionable insights by identifying *whom* to target for marketing or retention efforts based on their position and influence within the social structure.

We will explore another case study in the next video. Thank you for being with me. See you in the next video.
'''

# Marketing Analytics
## Lecture 64: Social Network Analysis and Excel Dashboards (Contd.)

Hello everybody. Welcome to Marketing Analytics course. This is Doctor Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur who is taking this course.

This video might look a little bit different from the normal NPTEL videos because of this Corona virus that is coming up. I could not go to the studio and I have recorded this particular video from my house.

So we are in week 12, last week and we have been discussing about **Social Network Analysis**. And in this particular class I will give you a very nice example of social network analysis, how that can be applied in a real life situation.

### Case Study: Bullwhip Effect and Organizational Hierarchy

I do not know whether you have heard about something called **Bullwhip Effect** in logistics or not. Bullwhip Effect is basically an effect that can be seen in a supply chain context where, let us say, there are some mispredictions. There is basically the retailer and then comes the supplier, then comes the warehouse guy, then comes the manufacturer. If this small whip here, which is basically an error in estimation of the demand, and this information gets passed to this guy who is a supplier, then this becomes much further higher. Here it goes further higher. So it looks like a bullwhip. Getting a small whip in this portion, it becomes a big, huge whip in this portion. That is what we generally try to find out.

There was a training program that I was taking with an organization on oil and gas and there I was taking this particular program. And there I have demonstrated a supply chain game in that particular thing. There is a supplier, there is, let us say, in a supply chain of this particular company there are retailers and then the wholesalers and then the manufacturers. Manufacturer is the organization, but this organization also takes into account the dealing part. And in the whole chain there are many various people who can take a call on how much has to be produced, how much has to be delivered and etc.

So what I found, by taking this particular test was that, we made two groups. And these two groups played and the major goal to this particular groups was, tell that okay, you have to reduce the costs of this product, this estimation and misprediction. The higher you do misprediction, the higher will be your costs. And obviously one group did less misprediction than another group and that group won.

Now till now, each group had 4 members or 4 groups. Each group has actually 4 subgroups in them. One subgroup is the retailer, one subgroup is the wholesalers and manufacturers and warehouse persons. But I did not let them talk with each other. Means the subgroups will talk within themselves but retailer will not talk with the wholesaler; wholesaler will not talk to the manufacturer and etc. They will give the orders based on, or make their predictions based on their idea of whatever the order coming up and etc. And that created a Bullwhip effect.

Now generally this Bullwhip effect will come down when we allow some sort of data sharing between them because the more information sharing happens, the uncertainty reduces and when the uncertainty reduces, people make better decisions.

So what we found from this kind of an analysis is that after we broke this particular thing, after we allowed them to talk with each other, the group which was bad in the first round did pretty well and actually they could reduce their cost in the second round. But the group that did good in the first round, it was very astonishing that even after data sharing, information sharing, they did worse than the first round. Not only that they failed in the second round, they did worse than the first round.

So then we tried to analyze that what happened, why they failed? And we could probably find out that in the, these are, these are all the guys are coming from the same company. Now in the same company though they are in various roles, they have hierarchy. They know that okay this person is General Manager, whatever he is saying I have to follow that. If he is saying that order 10 bottles or 15 bottles, I have to order 10, 15 bottles because even outside this training program, he is my boss.

So that kind of a **social pressure**, **social connection** comes into these kinds of games as well.

### Social Network Analysis: Data Collection and Implementation

Now we tried to see that whether things are there in terms of the, so this is the, so what we collected at the early means even before the game started, we started collecting this data and we did a survey with them actually before the games were even started.

The survey question was something like: "Please let us know the top 5 persons in this group with whom you communicated the most. Also let us know in a 1 to 5 point scale how much was that communication? 1 means few communication and 5 means high communication."

So this will be creating something like this kind of a matrix where there will be around, I do not know, 24 people here in the row and 24 people there in the column and a matrix will be created. Let it open.

The matrix will get created. 0 means no connections, see diagonal elements is still 0 and then 4 means high connection, then 5 means big so on. And we took an average. So just, or the highest, we took the highest. If 1 is saying that I have talked 3 times with 4, and 2 is saying I talked 4 times with 1 then we took the highest of them. This is our choice, you can take anything else.

And then with our R programming, we tried to create these kinds of charts, or graphs and let us see what these graphs are. Let the R program open.

Yeah it is opening.

```R
# Set working directory to source file location
setwd(dirname(rstudioapi::getActiveDocumentContext()$path))

# Read the communication data
data <- read.csv("communication.csv")

# Remove first row (assuming it's header/description)
data <- data[-1,]

# Read batch details (for node attributes like designation)
batch_details <- read.csv("batch details.csv")
batch_details <- batch_details[-1,] # Remove first row

# Load igraph library for network analysis
library(igraph)

# Create a graph object from the data frame
# Assuming 'data' is an edge list or matrix representing communication links
# (The specific format of communication.csv is not shown, but this is a common igraph function)
g <- graph_from_data_frame(d=data, vertices=batch_details, directed=FALSE) # Assuming data is edge list

# Simplify the graph (remove loops, multiple edges)
g <- simplify(g, remove.multiple = TRUE, remove.loops = TRUE)

# Set vertex labels from batch_details name column
V(g)$label <- as.character(batch_details$name)

# Plot the graph
plot(g)

# More advanced plotting with layout and attributes
# Get layout for visualization
lay <- layout_with_fr(g) # Fruchterman-Reingold layout

# Plot with initial layout
plot(g, layout=lay)

# Example of setting vertex shapes based on designation
# Assuming 'batch_details' has a column 'Designation' and mapping to shapes
# shapes <- c("circle", "raster", "square", "pie") # Example shapes
# V(g)$shape <- shapes[as.factor(batch_details$Designation)]

# Plot with shapes and colors based on attributes
```

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 65: Social Network Analysis and Excel Dashboards (Contd.)

**Instructor:** Dr. Swagato Chatterjee, VGSOM IIT Kharagpur

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. We are in week 12, and this is the last session. In this session, we will discuss something called a **Dashboard**.

We have likely encountered dashboards in college or professional settings. While interactive dashboards often require specialized tools like Tableau, QlikView, or Power BI, this video will demonstrate how to create a simple dashboard using Microsoft Excel.

## Understanding the Data

We typically work with datasets like the one shown.

The example data here has each **row representing one order**. It contains various details about each order:

*   Order Id
*   Order Date
*   Customer Id
*   Customer Name and City/Place
*   Salesperson Name
*   Region of the salesperson
*   Shipper Name
*   Shipping Date
*   Ship Name
*   Product Category
*   Product Name
*   Unit Price
*   Quantity
*   Revenue
*   Shipping Fees

This kind of data can often be downloaded from systems like SAP or CRM within an organization. Marketers often need to create regular reports or dashboards for managers based on this data. The goal is to show how to create such reports using Excel without relying solely on the MIS or IT team or dedicated BI tools.

It's important to note that Excel can handle data up to approximately 6 lakh (600,000) rows, although handling text data makes it more challenging.

## The Purpose of a Dashboard

In analytics, we distinguish between different types:

*   **Exploratory Analytics**
*   **Descriptive Analytics**
*   **Diagnostic Analytics**
*   **Predictive Analytics**
*   **Prescriptive Analytics**

Descriptive analytics, often visualized in dashboards, aims to generate **basic insights** from the data. These insights can then be used in diagnostic analytics to form **hypotheses** or **checks** about the data.

The primary purpose of a dashboard, especially in descriptive analytics, is to generate:

*   Certain hypotheses
*   Certain hunches
*   Certain thought processes

These are crucial for the data analyst or manager to **further probe** and **deep dive** into the data to find reasons behind observed patterns.

A dashboard that is just flat, without any intriguing patterns or "undulations," is a boring dashboard. Good dashboards should have **curves** and **undulations** that trigger the observer's inquisitiveness. They should prompt questions and encourage further investigation.

## Exploring the Example Dashboard

The dashboard we will demonstrate looks like this:

*   **Chart 1: Revenue by Months** - This is a line chart showing the total revenue for each month.
*   **Chart 2: Revenue Distribution / Ticket Size** - This is likely a histogram or bar chart showing the count of orders within different revenue ranges (e.g., 1-1000, 100-200, 200-300, etc.). This shows the distribution of order values.

Looking at the "Revenue by Months" chart, we can observe:

*   A spike in revenue in **October** and **December**.
*   Another spike in **June**.

These spikes trigger questions. Why are these months performing better?

*   October and December spikes might be related to **festive seasons** (Thanksgiving in November, Winter Holidays/Christmas in December).
*   The June spike might be related to **summer**.

This leads to hypotheses that can be tested using filters.

## Using Filters for Diagnostic Analysis

The dashboard includes **filters** (like slicers in Excel, though not explicitly named). We can filter the data by **Region** or **Salesperson**.

**Example 1: Filtering by Region (North vs. South)**

*   **Hypothesis:** If the June spike is due to summer, it might be more prominent in regions where the summer temperature difference from winter is significant (e.g., Northern regions compared to Southern regions which might have milder winters).
*   Selecting the **North** region shows a jump in May and June, but it's not exceptionally high.
*   Selecting the **South** region shows a pretty high jump in June, July, and August.

This initial check suggests that summer alone might not be the *only* reason for the June jump across all regions. There might be other contributing factors.

**Example 2: Filtering by Salesperson**

*   We can select a specific salesperson, e.g., **Andrew**.
*   The dashboard updates to show Andrew's performance:
    *   His monthly sales pattern (lots of sales in Jan, June, and December/January).
    *   His revenue distribution (mostly lower values, but also a few big-ticket invoices).
    *   His working region (East).
*   We can also select multiple salespersons (e.g., Jan Kotas, Laura, Anne) to compare their performance metrics side-by-side.

These filters allow for quick comparisons and deeper drilling down into the data based on different dimensions.

## Conclusion and Next Steps

These interactive dashboards are important because they don't just present data; they **generate questions** that lead to further probing. This is essential for diagnostic and predictive analytics.

Before diving into complex models, starting with this kind of exploratory analysis using dashboards is highly beneficial.

In the next video, we will demonstrate how to **create this specific dashboard from scratch** using Excel.

Thank you for being with me. See you in the next video.
'''


Lecture Name: Lecture 66 : social network analysis and excel dashboards (contd.)

Okay, here is the transcript of the video in a structured markdown format.

# Marketing Analytics - Lecture 66: Social Network Analysis and Excel Dashboards (Contd.)

## Introduction

Welcome to the Marketing Analytics course. This is Doctor Swagato Chatterjee from VGSOM IIT Kharagpur. This video is likely the last one in the course. In this video, we will demonstrate how to create an interactive dashboard in Excel from a raw dataset.

## Starting Fresh

The first step is to start with a clean slate. We will delete any existing dashboards and individual sheets created for previous visualizations to build a fresh one.

## Visualizing the Dashboard

Before starting, it's helpful to visualize the components needed for the dashboard. Based on previous discussions, the dashboard should include:

*   Sales per Month (at the top)
*   Sales per Region
*   Sales per Salesperson
*   Invoice Size Distribution (as a histogram)

## Creating Charts using Pivot Tables and Pivot Charts

We will create each required chart one by one using Excel's Pivot Table and Pivot Chart features.

### What is a Pivot Chart?

A pivot chart is a dynamic chart linked to a pivot table. It allows you to summarize and analyze data by placing categorical variables in rows/columns and numeric values in the data area (which can be summed, averaged, counted, etc.).

### Step-by-step Chart Creation

1.  **Monthly Revenue and Invoice Count Chart**
    *   Select any non-blank cell in the raw data table.
    *   Go to `Insert` -> `Pivot Table`.
    *   Excel will automatically capture the table range. Confirm the range and click `OK`. A new sheet with a blank pivot table will be created.
    *   **Add Fields:**
        *   Drag `Order Date` to the `Rows` area.
        *   Drag `Revenue` to the `Values` area. By default, it will show `Sum of Revenue`.
        *   Drag `Revenue` to the `Values` area *again*. This second instance will also show `Sum of Revenue`.
    *   **Change Second Value Field:**
        *   Click on the second `Sum of Revenue` field in the `Values` area.
        *   Select `Value Field Settings`.
        *   Change `Sum` to `Count` and click `OK`. This gives the count of invoices per date.
    *   **Group Dates by Month:**
        *   In the pivot table, right-click on any date value in the row labels.
        *   Select `Group`.
        *   In the grouping dialog, select `Months` (unselect `Days` if needed). Click `OK`. The pivot table now shows `Sum of Revenue` and `Count of Revenue` per month.
    *   **Create Pivot Chart:**
        *   With the pivot table selected, go to `Analyze` (or `Options` in older versions) -> `PivotChart`.
        *   Select the desired chart type. A clustered column chart is often the default.
    *   **Adjust for Different Scales:**
        *   Notice that the `Count of Revenue` is much smaller than `Sum of Revenue` and appears almost flat on the chart.
        *   Select the data series for `Count of Revenue` on the chart.
        *   Right-click and select `Format Data Series`.
        *   Choose `Secondary Axis` under `Plot Series On`.
    *   **Change Chart Type (Combo):**
        *   Go back to the chart's design options (e.g., `Design` -> `Change Chart Type`).
        *   Select `Combo`.
        *   For `Sum of Revenue`, choose a `Clustered Column`.
        *   For `Count of Revenue`, choose a `Line` chart and ensure `Secondary Axis` is checked. Click `OK`. This often looks better.
    *   **Format the Chart:**
        *   Right-click on field buttons (`Month`, `Total`) on the chart and select `Hide All Field Buttons on Chart`.
        *   Adjust the bar width: Right-click on the bars, `Format Data Series`, reduce `Gap Width` (e.g., to 25%).
        *   Change colors for better visual appeal (e.g., change column fill color).
        *   Add a chart title: Go to `Chart Elements` (+) -> `Chart Title`. Enter a title like "Monthly Revenue and Number of Invoices".
        *   Move the legend: Go to `Chart Elements` (+) -> `Legend` -> `Bottom`.
    *   Rename the pivot table sheet (e.g., "Monthly").

2.  **Salesperson Revenue and Invoice Count Chart**
    *   Right-click on the "Monthly" sheet tab and select `Move or Copy...`.
    *   Check `Create a copy` and select `(move to end)`. Click `OK`.
    *   Rename the new sheet (e.g., "Salesperson").
    *   In the pivot table fields list (or by clicking the pivot table), remove `Order Date` from the `Rows` area.
    *   Drag `Salesperson` to the `Rows` area. The pivot table and chart will update for each salesperson.
    *   Update the chart title (e.g., "Revenue and Invoice Count per Salesperson").
    *   Format the chart (e.g., change column color to differentiate from the monthly chart).

3.  **Region Revenue and Invoice Count Chart**
    *   Repeat the process: Right-click on the "Salesperson" sheet, `Move or Copy...`, `Create a copy`.
    *   Rename the new sheet (e.g., "Region").
    *   In the pivot table fields list, remove `Salesperson` from `Rows`.
    *   Drag `Region` to the `Rows` area. The pivot table and chart will update per region.
    *   Update the chart title (e.g., "Revenue and Invoice Count per Region").
    *   Format the chart (e.g., change column color).

4.  **Invoice Size Distribution (Histogram) Chart**
    *   Go back to the original data sheet.
    *   Go to `Insert` -> `Pivot Table`. Create a new pivot table on a new sheet.
    *   Drag `Revenue` to the `Rows` area.
    *   Drag `Revenue` to the `Values` area. Change the `Value Field Settings` to `Count`. The table shows the count for each specific revenue amount.
    *   **Group Revenue Values:**
        *   Right-click on any revenue value in the row labels.
        *   Select `Group`.
        *   Excel will suggest a starting value (e.g., 0), ending value (the max revenue), and grouping interval (e.g., 1000). Confirm or adjust as needed (e.g., interval of 1000). Click `OK`. The table now shows count of invoices within each revenue bin.
    *   **Create Pivot Chart:**
        *   With the pivot table selected, go to `Analyze` -> `PivotChart`.
        *   A column chart is suitable here, representing a histogram.
    *   **Format the Chart:**
        *   Hide field buttons.
        *   Adjust the bar width (e.g., to 0% or a small value like 5%) to make it look more like a histogram, or keep a gap (e.g., 25%) if preferred.
        *   Add a chart title (e.g., "Histogram of Ticket Size").
    *   Rename the pivot table sheet (e.g., "Histogram").

## Building the Dashboard Sheet

1.  **Create Dashboard Sheet:**
    *   Insert a new sheet and rename it "Dashboard".
    *   Select all cells (Click the corner button above row 1 and left of column A).
    *   Apply a background fill color to the entire sheet.

2.  **Copy and Paste Charts:**
    *   Go to each chart sheet ("Monthly", "Salesperson", "Region", "Histogram").
    *   Select the pivot chart (click on the chart border).
    *   Copy the chart (`Ctrl+C` or Right-click -> `Copy`).
    *   Go to the "Dashboard" sheet.
    *   Paste the chart (`Ctrl+V` or Right-click -> `Paste`).
    *   Repeat for all four charts.

3.  **Arrange and Resize Charts:**
    *   Position and resize the charts on the dashboard sheet as desired to create a layout.

## Adding Interactivity with Slicers

Slicers provide easy-to-use filter buttons to dynamically change the data displayed in charts.

1.  **Insert Slicers:**
    *   Select *any* one of the pivot charts on the dashboard.
    *   Go to `Analyze` -> `Insert Slicer`.
    *   A dialog box will appear showing the available fields from the associated pivot table.
    *   Select the fields you want to use as filters (e.g., `Salesperson`, `Region`).
    *   Click `OK`. The slicers will appear on the dashboard sheet.

2.  **Connect Slicers to All Charts:**
    *   Initially, a slicer only filters the chart it was inserted from. To connect it to *all* charts:
    *   Right-click on a slicer (e.g., the Salesperson slicer).
    *   Select `Report Connections...`.
    *   A list of pivot tables in the workbook will appear. Check the boxes next to *all* the pivot tables that feed the charts on your dashboard. Click `OK`.
    *   Repeat this process for the other slicer (e.g., the Region slicer), connecting it to all relevant pivot tables.

3.  **Test Interactivity:**
    *   Click on different buttons within the slicers. All charts connected to the slicers should update simultaneously based on the selected filter criteria.
    *   Use `Shift+Click` or `Ctrl+Click` to select multiple items in a slicer.
    *   Use the `Clear Filter` button (top right of the slicer) to remove the filter.

## Final Touches

Arrange the slicers and charts neatly on the dashboard sheet. The dashboard is now interactive and can be used to explore sales data by month, salesperson, region, and invoice size, filtered by selected salespeople and regions.

## Conclusion

Creating interactive dashboards in Excel using pivot charts and slicers is relatively straightforward for simple to moderately complex datasets. For larger or more complex data and reporting needs, dedicated Business Intelligence (BI) tools might be used, but the principles are often similar. This course has covered various marketing analytics concepts and tools. Keep practicing what you have learned.

Thank you for being part of this course. Safe, happy learning!
